- Information architecture - Information in this document is structured based on Mrunal sir's economy architecture which arranges information into 6 pillars. - Here are the pillars in this document: - Pillar 1 - Pillar 1A - Pillar 1A1 - Pillar 1A2 - Pillar 1A3 - Pillar 1B - Pillar 1B1 - Pillar 1B2 - Pillar 1C - Pillar 1C1 - Pillar 1C2 - Pillar 1D - Pillar 1D1 - Pillar 1D2 - Pillar 1D3 - Pillar 2 - Pillar 2A - Pillar 2A1 - Pillar 2A1-i - Pillar 2A1-ii - Pillar 2A2 - Pillar 2B - Pillar 2B1 - Pillar 2B2 - Pillar 2B3 - Pillar 2D - Pillar 2D1 - Pillar 2D2 - Pillar 2D3 - Pillar 3 - Pillar 3A - Pillar 3B - Pillar 3B1 - Pillar 3B2 - Pillar 3C - Pillar 3C1 - Pillar 3C2 - Pillar 3C3 - Pillar 4 - Pillar 4A - Pillar 4A1 - Pillar 4A2 - Pillar 4B - Pillar 4B1 - Pillar 4B1-i - Pillar 4B1-ii - Pillar 4B1-iii - Pillar 4B2 - Pillar 4B2-i - Pillar 4B2-ii - Pillar 4C - Pillar 4C1 - Pillar 4C2 - Pillar 4D - Pillar 4E - Pillar 5 - Pillar 5A - Pillar 5A1 - Pillar 5A2 - Pillar 5A3 - Pillar 5B - Pillar 5B1 - Pillar 5B2 - Pillar 5B3 - Pillar 5C - Pillar 5C1 - Pillar 5C2 - Pillar 5C3 - Pillar 5C4 - Pillar 6 - Pillar 6A - Pillar 6A1 - Pillar 6A2 - Pillar 6B - Pillar 6B1 - Pillar 6B2 - Pillar 6C - Pillar 6D - Pillar 6D1 - Pillar 6D2 - Pillar 6E - Pillar 6E1 - Pillar 6E2 - Pillar 1A1: Money and Payment Systems - Definition: - An economy is a system of production, distribution, and consumption of goods and services within a region, driven by interactions among individuals, businesses, and governments. Money and payment systems are critical for facilitating transactions, enabling trade, investment, and financial inclusion in modern economies. - Context: - Money and payment systems have evolved from barter to digital platforms, addressing inefficiencies and supporting India’s economic growth by promoting efficiency, transparency, and access to financial services. - Significance for India: - India’s shift to a digital economy, through initiatives like UPI and CBDC, aligns with goals of financial inclusion, reducing black money, and enhancing global competitiveness, crucial for a developing economy aiming for self-reliance (Atmanirbhar Bharat). - Barter System and Its Disadvantages - Definition: - The barter system is a method of exchange where goods or services are traded directly without using money as a medium. - Historical Context: - Originated with Mesopotamian tribes around 6000 BCE, serving as a foundational trade mechanism before monetary systems. - Disadvantages: - Double Coincidence of Wants: - Both parties must desire each other’s goods or services, making trade cumbersome and often unfeasible (e.g., a farmer with grain needing shoes must find a shoemaker needing grain). - High Search and Transaction Costs: - Locating a willing trading partner and negotiating terms requires significant time and resources, hindering efficient trade. - Storage Challenges: - Perishable goods, such as food or livestock, deteriorate quickly, leading to loss of value and impracticality for long-term trade. - Hinders Specialization: - The system discourages division of labor and specialization, limiting economic efficiency and productivity growth by restricting individuals to produce only what they can directly trade. - Functions of Money - Definition: - Money is a universally accepted medium that facilitates economic transactions by overcoming the limitations of the barter system, serving as a cornerstone of modern economies. - Functions: - Medium of Exchange: - Enables indirect trade, eliminating the need for double coincidence by allowing goods and services to be exchanged through money (e.g., buying groceries with rupees). - Unit of Account: - Provides a standardized measure to express the value of goods, services, and assets, facilitating price comparison and accounting (e.g., pricing a car at Rs. 10 lakh). - Store of Value: - Retains purchasing power over time, enabling savings for future use, though subject to inflation risks (e.g., saving Rs. 5000 for emergencies). - Standard of Deferred Payment: - Supports credit transactions by serving as a measure for settling debts over time, critical for loans and mortgages (e.g., repaying a loan in installments). - Evolution and Types of Money - Definition: - Money has evolved from physical commodities to digital forms, reflecting advancements in trade, technology, and economic systems, enhancing transaction efficiency and accessibility. - Types of Money: - Commodity Money: - Objects with intrinsic value used as money (e.g., grains, livestock). - Metallic Money: - Coins made from precious metals with standardized value. - Paper/Token/Representative/Fiat Money: - Currency valued by government backing rather than intrinsic worth. - Bank Money/Deposit Money: - Funds in bank accounts, accessible via cheques or digital transfers. - Cryptocurrency: - Digital or virtual currency secured by cryptographic technology. - Additional Subtypes: - Electronic Money: - Stored in digital wallets or prepaid cards (e.g., Paytm wallet, AmazonPay). - Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC): - Digital fiat currency issued by central banks, combining fiat reliability with digital efficiency. - Significance: - The evolution reflects India’s transition to a digital economy, with systems like UPI and CBDC reducing cash dependency and promoting financial inclusion. - Commodity Money - Definition: - Commodity money consists of objects with intrinsic value used as a medium of exchange due to their inherent worth, accepted in trade for their utility or rarity. - Examples: - Iron nails, bear pelts, cocoa beans, whale teeth, gold nuggets, used historically in various cultures. - Challenges: - Perishability: - Items like food or livestock degrade quickly, reducing their utility as a reliable medium of exchange. - Non-Uniformity: - Variations in quality and quantity (e.g., size of pelts or purity of gold) complicate standardization and fair trade. - Non-Acceptance: - Foreign traders may not value specific commodities (e.g., cocoa beans irrelevant in non-agricultural regions), limiting international trade. - Metallic Money - Definition: - Metallic money refers to coins made from precious metals like gold or silver, standardized by weight and purity to ensure trust and uniformity in transactions. - Historical Context: - Traders and kings stamped gold nuggets to certify purity and weight, fostering trust (e.g., Lydian coins, 7th century BCE). - In India, Indo-Greek, Kushana, and Gupta kings issued gold coins; Gupta coins were notable for artistic designs (e.g., king playing veena, standing with wife). - Delhi Sultanate introduced silver tanka; Sher Shah Suri’s rupiyah (1540s) and Akbar’s muhr were significant silver and gold coins, standardizing trade. - Note: - Refer to Indian coinage history in standard history texts for detailed study, including numismatic evolution and economic impact. - Metallic Money: Full-Bodied vs. Token Coins - Definition: - Full-bodied coins have intrinsic value equal to or greater than their face value, while token coins have intrinsic value less than their face value, relying on legal backing. -
Full-Bodied Coins Token Coins
- Intrinsic value matches or exceeds face value (e.g., Gupta coin: ~8 gm gold, worth its nominal value). - Intrinsic value less than face value (e.g., 50 paisa coin: ~4 gm steel, negligible material value).
- Challenges: - Debasement: - Reducing metal content in coins, often during weak governance (e.g., Aurangzeb, Roman emperors), eroding trust and causing inflation. - Tughlaq’s token coin experiment (1330s) failed due to public rejection of low-value bronze coins (refer to history texts). - Modern Indian coins are token coins, made of cupronickel to deter melting for metal value. - Coinage Act 2011 prohibits coin melting to maintain currency circulation and prevent economic disruption.
- Intrinsic Value: Coins vs. Paper Currency - Definition: - Intrinsic value is the inherent worth of the material used in money, independent of its nominal or face value. -
Precious Metal Coins Paper Currency
- Have intrinsic value; can be melted for use in jewelry or weapons (e.g., gold coins used for ornaments). - Lack intrinsic value; paper has no significant alternative use (e.g., cannot be repurposed like metal).
- Fiat Money - Definition: - Fiat money is currency without intrinsic value, deriving its value from government decree or legal backing, accepted due to trust in the issuing authority. - Conditions: - Exists as physical coins/notes, virtual coins, or digital coins capable of measuring value. - Issued by a government, monarch, or central bank (e.g., RBI in India). - Examples in India: - Coins (50 paisa to Rs. 20) issued under Coinage Act 2011 (e.g., Rs. 20 coin is a 12-sided dodecagon for tactile identification). - Rs. 1 note, signed by the Finance Secretary, issued by Government of India, excludes “I promise to pay bearer” clause. - RBI issues other currency notes (Rs. 2 to Rs. 2000) under RBI Act 1934, signed by the RBI Governor with “I promise to pay bearer.” - Significance: - Fiat money ensures universal acceptability within India, supporting economic stability and transactions. - Legal Tender - Definition: - Legal tender is currency legally mandated for acceptance in settling all debts and transactions within a country, ensuring universal acceptability and trust. - Conditions: - Qualifies as fiat money (issued by government or central bank). - Cannot be refused by creditors for transactions or debt repayment, as per law. -
Item Physical/Digital Form? Issued by Central Bank/Govt? Fiat Money? Legal Tender in India?
Indian Rupee: Coins, Notes, CBDC Yes Yes Yes Yes
Demonetized Rs. 500, Rs. 1000 Yes Yes No No
Foreign Currencies (Dollar, Euro, etc.) Yes No No No
G-Sec, T-Bill No Yes No No
Shares, Bonds, Cheques, Cards No No No No
Bitcoin Yes No No No (Exception: Legal tender in El Salvador)
Commemorative Coins Yes Yes Yes No, unless notified by RBI/Government
- Limited vs. Unlimited Legal Tender - Definition: - Limited legal tender can be refused beyond a specified limit, while unlimited legal tender must be accepted for any amount, as per RBI regulations. -
Limited Legal Tender Unlimited Legal Tender
- Recipient can refuse payment beyond a specified limit. - Coinage Act 2011: - Coins Rs. 1 and above: Acceptable up to Rs. 1000 per transaction. - Coins 50 paisa: Acceptable up to Rs. 10 (20 coins). - Coins below 50 paisa withdrawn in 2011 (e.g., 10 paisa, 25 paisa no longer valid). - Rs. 1 paper note: Limited legal tender, rarely used. - No restriction on acceptance amount. - RBI Act 1934, Section 26: - All bank notes (Rs. 2 to Rs. 2000) are unlimited legal tender. - Cannot be refused for settling any debt, ensuring universal acceptability.
- Note: Finance Act 2017 restricts cash transactions to Rs. 2 lakh to curb tax evasion; higher amounts require non-cash methods like cheques or NEFT.
- RBI’s App for the Visually Impaired - Definition: - Tools developed by the Reserve Bank of India to assist visually impaired individuals in identifying currency notes, promoting financial inclusion and accessibility. - Context: - Supports approximately 80 lakh blind individuals in India, ensuring equitable access to financial transactions. - Features: - Old Gandhi Series Notes: - Include tactile shapes for identification (e.g., square for Rs. 50, triangle for Rs. 100) to aid visually impaired users. - New Gandhi Series Notes: - Feature bleed lines (raised lines) and raised Gandhi printing for tactile recognition. - Mobile Aided Note Identifier (MANI) App (Launched January 2020): - Scans currency notes (even if half-folded) using a mobile camera. - Provides audio output in Hindi and English, identifying denomination, works offline for accessibility. - Cannot verify note authenticity (genuine vs. counterfeit), limiting its use for fraud detection. - Offers vibration-based notifications for deaf-blind users, enhancing inclusivity. - Significance: - Aligns with India’s commitment to inclusive financial systems, ensuring all citizens can participate in the economy. - Currency-Related General Knowledge - Indian Currency Notes: - Feature 17 languages (15 on reverse, Hindi and English on obverse) from the 8th Schedule of the Constitution (22 languages), reflecting India’s linguistic diversity. - Rupee Symbol (₹): - Designed by D. Udaya Kumar in 2010, selected via a Department of Economic Affairs competition, combining Devanagari “Ra” and Roman “R.” - Lion Emblem: - Derived from the Sarnath Pillar, Uttar Pradesh, symbolizing India’s historical and cultural heritage. - Slogan “Satyamev Jayate”: - From Mundaka Upanishad, meaning “Truth Alone Triumphs,” adopted as India’s national motto, printed on currency notes. - Numismatics Museum: - Located in Kolkata (RBI Mint), showcases the evolution of coins and trade in India, valuable for understanding monetary history. - Additional Facts: - Currency notes include security features like watermarks, security threads, and micro-text to prevent counterfeiting. - RBI’s clean note policy ensures damaged notes are replaced to maintain quality in circulation. - “I Promise to Pay the Bearer” Clause - Definition: - A clause on currency notes indicating the RBI’s obligation to provide value equivalent to the note’s denomination upon demand, reinforcing trust in fiat money. - Historical Context: - Colonial Era: - Notes were convertible to gold or silver coins/bars under the gold standard (e.g., 1 US Dollar ≈ 14 grams gold). - Modern Era: - Convertible to other bank notes or token coins of equal face value (e.g., Rs. 2000 = 4 x Rs. 500 notes). - Features: - Notes are zero-interest, anonymous bearer bonds, not adjusted for inflation or linked to precious metals. - Clause appears on RBI-issued notes (Rs. 2 to Rs. 2000), except Rs. 1 note issued by the Government of India. - Significance: - Enhances public confidence in fiat currency by guaranteeing value, critical for economic stability. - Currency Note vs. Promissory Note - Definition: - A promissory note is a legal instrument promising payment between parties, while currency notes, despite similarities, are distinct as legal tender. - Perspectives: - Academic: - Currency notes resemble promissory notes due to the “I promise to pay” clause, implying an obligation by the RBI. - Legal: - Under the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881, currency notes are not promissory notes, as they are legal tender with universal acceptability, not negotiable instruments. - Comparison: - Currency Notes: - Issued by RBI/Government, universally accepted, backed by legal tender status. - Promissory Notes: - Issued by individuals/entities, require agreement, not legal tender. - Significance: - Clarifies the legal status of currency in financial transactions, relevant for UPSC understanding of monetary systems. - Demonetization of Fiat Money - Definition: - Demonetization is the process of stripping a currency unit of its legal tender status, rendering it invalid for transactions, often to curb black money or counterfeiting. - Historical Instances in India: - 1946: - High-denomination notes (Rs. 1000, Rs. 10000) demonetized post-World War II to address black money. - 1978: - Rs. 1000, Rs. 5000, Rs. 10000 notes demonetized to target unaccounted wealth. - 8 November 2016: - Rs. 500 and Rs. 1000 notes of Mahatma Gandhi series demonetized. - 2016 Demonetization: - Initiated by Finance Ministry’s Department of Economic Affairs via gazette notification on 8 November 2016. - Objectives: - Curb black money, counterfeit currency, and terror financing. - Promote digital payments and formalize the economy. - Specified Bank Notes (Cessation of Liabilities) Act 2017: - RBI not obligated to honor “I promise to pay” for demonetized Rs. 500 and Rs. 1000 notes. - Holding banned notes is illegal, except for research, numismatics, or museum purposes with RBI permission. - Impact: - Disrupted cash-based transactions, boosted digital payments (e.g., UPI adoption surged). - Supreme Court (2023) upheld demonetization’s legality, citing economic policy prerogative (refer to Pillar #2: Taxation and Black Money). - Significance: - Highlights India’s efforts to combat black money and transition to a digital economy, a key UPSC topic. - 2000 Note: Withdrawal but Continued Legal Tender - Definition: - Withdrawal is the phased removal of a currency note from circulation without revoking its legal tender status, aimed at reducing its usage over time. - Timeline: - November 2016: - RBI introduced Rs. 2000 note post-demonetization to address currency shortages in the economy. - 2018–19: - Printing of Rs. 2000 notes stopped as lower denominations (e.g., Rs. 100, Rs. 200, Rs. 500) became sufficient. - May 2023: - RBI ordered exchange of Rs. 2000 notes with other denominations at banks, encouraging their return. - Status (July 2025): - Remains fiat money and legal tender, not demonetized, but withdrawn from active circulation. - Can still be used for transactions, though rarely accepted due to limited circulation. - Reasons for Withdrawal: - Temporary measure to address 2016 currency shortage; lower denominations now adequate. - Low usage in daily transactions due to high denomination, inconvenient for small purchases. - Aligns with RBI’s clean note policy to ensure quality banknotes in circulation. - Lifespan of Rs. 2000 notes (printed 2016–17) is 4–5 years; paper quality may deteriorate by 2022–23, prompting replacement. - Significance: - Reflects RBI’s strategy to optimize currency circulation and promote digital transactions, a key aspect of India’s monetary policy. - Bank Money and Deposit Money - Definition: - Bank money (deposit money) refers to funds held in bank accounts, accessible via cheques, debit cards, or digital transfers, forming a significant part of the money supply (M1, M2 in monetary aggregates). - Types of Bank Accounts: - Savings Account: - For individuals, offers interest (e.g., 3–4% p.a.), suitable for regular transactions and savings, with withdrawal limits. - Current Account: - For businesses, no interest, supports high-volume transactions, offers overdraft facilities for cash flow management. - Fixed Deposit Account: - Locks funds for a fixed term (e.g., 1–5 years), offers higher interest (e.g., 6–7% p.a.), ideal for long-term savings. - Recurring Deposit Account: - Allows regular deposits (e.g., monthly) with fixed tenure, earns interest similar to fixed deposits, encourages disciplined saving. - Non-Resident External (NRE) Account: - For Non-Resident Indians (NRIs), fully repatriable, tax-free interest, used for foreign earnings (e.g., salaries abroad). - Non-Resident Ordinary (NRO) Account: - For NRIs, non-repatriable (with limits), taxable interest, used for India-based income (e.g., rent, dividends). - Salary Account: - For employees, linked to employer payroll, often zero-balance with benefits like free transactions. - Senior Citizen Account: - For individuals above 60, offers higher interest rates (e.g., 0.5% above regular savings) and additional benefits like priority banking. - Significance: - Bank accounts facilitate financial inclusion, enabling savings, credit access, and digital transactions, critical for India’s economic growth. - Paper Orders: Cheque and Demand Draft - Definition: - Paper orders are written instructions to transfer funds from a bank account, governed by the Negotiable Instruments Act, 1881, ensuring legal enforceability. - Cheque: - Definition: - A cheque is a written order by an account holder (drawer) instructing their bank (drawee) to pay a specified amount to a recipient (payee). - Process: - Drawer issues the cheque, specifying payee and amount, and signs it. - Payee presents the cheque to their bank, which forwards it for clearance via the Cheque Truncation System (CTS). - Funds are transferred after verification, typically within 1–3 days (T+1 cycle post-2024). - Types: - Stale Cheque: - Invalid if not deposited within 3 months of issuance, ensuring timely clearance. - Post-Dated Cheque: - Depositable only after a specified future date, used for deferred payments (e.g., loan EMIs). - Open/Bearer Cheque: - Payable to anyone without crossing; crossing (two parallel lines) restricts payment to bank accounts for security. - Codes: - IFSC (Indian Financial System Code): - 11-digit alphanumeric code (e.g., SBIN0001234) identifying bank branches for electronic transfers. - MICR (Magnetic Ink Character Recognition): - 9-digit code printed in magnetic ink for automated cheque clearance, ensuring accuracy. - Use Cases: - Payments for goods, services, or bills, widely used in business and personal transactions. - Example: - Paying rent or settling supplier invoices. - Significance: - Offers a paper-based, secure payment method, though declining with digital payment adoption. - Demand Draft: - Definition: - A demand draft is a prepaid instrument issued by a bank, guaranteeing payment to the payee upon presentation, ensuring secure and reliable transactions. - Process: - Customer requests a draft from their bank, paying the amount upfront plus a nominal fee (e.g., Rs. 50–200). - Bank issues the draft, drawable at a specified branch or correspondent bank, backed by pre-paid funds. - Payee presents the draft to their bank for clearance, typically within 1–3 days, with guaranteed payment. - If expired (e.g., after 3 months), drafts can be revalidated by the issuing bank. - Use Cases: - Secure payments for education fees (e.g., university admissions), property purchases, or government applications. - Preferred for inter-city or inter-bank transactions where cheques may risk non-payment (e.g., bounced cheques). - Example: Paying for a competitive exam application or property registration. - Features: - Safer than cheques as funds are pre-paid, eliminating the risk of bouncing. - Issued in favor of a specific payee, reducing fraud risks. - Can be canceled or reissued if lost, with proper documentation. - Significance: - Widely used in India for high-value, secure transactions, especially where digital payments are not feasible, critical for UPSC understanding of payment systems. - Overdraft - Definition: - An overdraft is a credit facility allowing account holders to withdraw funds beyond their account balance, treated as a short-term loan with interest, supporting immediate financial needs. - Process: - Pre-approved by banks based on account holder’s creditworthiness, account type, or relationship (e.g., current account holders). - Funds withdrawn are repaid with interest, typically higher than regular loans (e.g., 10–15% p.a.), on demand or per agreement. - Features: - Used for short-term expenses, such as business cash flow or emergency personal needs. - Example: - Pradhan Mantri Jan-Dhan Yojana offers up to Rs. 10,000 overdraft for eligible account holders, promoting financial inclusion (refer to Pillar #1D: Financial Inclusion). - Comparison with Loans: - Overdrafts are flexible, short-term, and for smaller amounts; loans are for long-term, high-value needs (e.g., home or car loans). - Significance: - Enhances liquidity for businesses and individuals, supporting economic activity and financial inclusion in India’s banking system. - Cheque Truncation System (CTS) and Positive Pay Mechanism - Definition: - Cheque Truncation System (CTS) is a digital cheque clearance system that transmits scanned cheque images online, replacing physical cheque movement for faster processing. -
Mechanism Benefits
Cheque Truncation System (CTS) - Faster clearance than physical delivery (within hours, T+1 cycle post-2024). - Reduces logistics costs and risks of loss or damage during transport. - Enhances efficiency in banking operations, supporting India’s digital banking ecosystem.
Positive Pay Mechanism - Requires drawer to submit cheque details (e.g., amount, date, payee) to bank for verification before clearance. - Prevents fraud, such as amount tampering or unauthorized issuance. - Mandatory for high-value cheques (e.g., above Rs. 50,000, varies by bank), enhancing security.
- Significance: - CTS and Positive Pay strengthen India’s banking system by improving efficiency and reducing fraud, aligning with digital payment goals. - Electronic Orders and Digital Payments - Definition: - Digital payments involve electronic fund transfers using online platforms, mobile apps, or cards, regulated by the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, to promote a cashless economy. - Types: - Online Banking: - Transactions via internet banking portals (e.g., fund transfers, bill payments). - Mobile Payments: - Via apps like Unified Payments Interface (UPI) or digital wallets (e.g., Paytm, Google Pay). - Card Payments: - Using debit/credit cards at Point of Sale (PoS) terminals or online platforms. - Significance: - Drives India’s cashless economy, reduces black money, enhances transparency, and promotes financial inclusion, especially in rural areas. - Core Banking Solution (CBS) - Definition: - Core Banking Solution (CBS) is centralized banking software enabling seamless operations across bank branches, supporting branch-less banking and digital services. - Examples: - Finacle (Infosys), BaNCS (TCS), E-Kuber (RBI’s proprietary platform). - Process: - Integrates customer accounts, transactions, and services on a centralized server. - Enables real-time access to account details across branches, ATMs, and online platforms. - Benefits: - Facilitates services like mobile banking, ATMs, and online transfers. - Centralizes customer data for efficient management and fraud detection. - Enhances customer experience with seamless banking access nationwide. - Significance: - Critical for India’s banking modernization, supporting digital payments and financial inclusion. - Digital Payment Systems: NEFT, RTGS, IMPS - Definition: - National Electronic Funds Transfer (NEFT), Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS), and Immediate Payment Service (IMPS) are electronic fund transfer systems managed by RBI (NEFT, RTGS) and NPCI (IMPS). -
Feature RTGS NEFT IMPS
Transfer Amount Min Rs. 2 lakh, max Rs. 2000 crore (SBI) Rs. 1 to Rs. 5 lakh Up to Rs. 10 lakh (SBI)
Target Customers Businesses for high-value transactions Retail customers Retail customers
Settlement Instant, transaction-by-transaction Net settlement every 30 minutes Instant
Availability 24/7 (post-2020 reforms) 24/7 (post-2019 reforms) 24/7
Fees Zero post-RBI reforms Zero post-RBI reforms Fee + GST (some banks waive for small transactions)
Providers Banks, non-banks (e.g., AmazonPay post-2021) Banks, non-banks Banks, mobile wallets (e.g., PhonePe)
- Process: - RTGS: - Real-time, gross settlement for high-value transfers, ideal for business deals (e.g., property purchases). - NEFT: - Batch processing every 30 minutes, suitable for retail payments (e.g., salary transfers). - IMPS: - Instant transfers via mobile apps, ideal for urgent retail transactions (e.g., paying a vendor). - Significance: - These systems form the backbone of India’s digital payment infrastructure, supporting economic transactions and financial inclusion. - Lightweight Payment and Settlement System (LPSS) - Definition: - Lightweight Payment and Settlement System (LPSS) is an emergency backup system for digital payments, designed for use during disasters or war when connectivity is disrupted. - Features: - Operates with minimal staff and infrastructure, ensuring continuity of payments. - Supports temporary transactions in low-connectivity areas (e.g., rural regions during floods). - Uses offline or low-bandwidth technologies for resilience. - Significance: - Enhances India’s payment system robustness, ensuring financial access during crises, a key aspect of economic resilience for UPSC. - Beneficiary Account Name Look-Up Facility (2024) - Definition: - A facility allowing senders to verify the beneficiary’s name before initiating NEFT or RTGS transfers, reducing errors and fraud. - Process: - Sender inputs beneficiary’s account number and IFSC code; system displays the registered name for confirmation. - Implemented post-October 2024 for enhanced security. - Benefits: - Prevents transfers to incorrect accounts due to typographical errors. - Reduces fraud risks (e.g., phishing scams redirecting funds). - Significance: - Strengthens trust in digital payments, critical for India’s cashless economy drive. - 1.13 National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) - Definition: - NPCI is a not-for-profit company under the Companies Act, 2013, established in 2008 to develop cost-effective, inclusive payment solutions for India. - Background: - Founded by 10 banks with Rs. 100 crore capital; non-banks (e.g., Paytm, PhonePe) added as shareholders in 2020. - Operates under RBI oversight, aligning with India’s financial inclusion goals. - Objectives: - Develops technologies like UPI, RuPay, and IMPS to streamline payments. - Promotes financial inclusion by expanding banking access to rural and underserved populations. - Significance: - NPCI’s innovations have transformed India’s payment ecosystem, making it a global leader in digital transactions (e.g., UPI’s 100 billion+ annual transactions). - 1.14 Unified Payments Interface (UPI) - Definition: - Unified Payments Interface (UPI) is a real-time payment system developed by NPCI, enabling instant fund transfers via mobile apps, revolutionizing retail payments. - Features: - QR code scanning for merchant payments (e.g., at shops, street vendors). - Direct bank account linkage, eliminating wallet storage, ensuring funds remain in banks. - Supports push (sender-initiated) and pull (recipient-initiated) transactions. - Examples: BHIM, SBI Yono, AxisPay, Google Pay, PhonePe. - Process: - Users link bank accounts to UPI apps using a Virtual Payment Address (VPA, e.g., name@upi). - Transactions are authenticated via UPI PIN or biometrics, completed instantly. - International Reach: - Available in Bhutan, Nepal, Singapore, Mauritius, Sri Lanka, etc., via NPCI agreements, enabling Indian tourists to use UPI abroad. - Transaction Limits: - Normal transactions: Rs. 1 lakh/day. - Capital markets, credit card payments, loans: Rs. 2 lakh/day. - G-Sec, IPO, medical/educational fees, tax payments: Rs. 5 lakh/day (post-August 2024). - Significance: - UPI’s low-cost, instant transfers have driven India’s cashless economy, with over 50% of global real-time payment volume, a key UPSC topic. - UPI: Push vs. Pull Transactions - Push Transactions: - Initiated by the sender (e.g., sending Rs. 500 to a friend via UPI). - Pull Transactions: - Initiated by the recipient (e.g., utility company deducting bill payments automatically). - Significance: - Enables flexible payment models, supporting both individual and automated transactions. - UPI Delegated Payments (2024) - Definition: - Allows primary account holders (e.g., parents) to create secondary UPI accounts with spending limits for others (e.g., children). - Process: - Primary user sets up a delegated account via UPI app, specifying transaction limits (e.g., Rs. 5000/month). - Secondary user transacts within limits, enhancing controlled access. - Significance: - Promotes financial literacy and safety, especially for minors or dependents, aligning with financial inclusion goals. - UPI Transaction Limits: UPI123, UPI Lite (2024) -
UPI Service Before October 2024 After October 2024
UPI Lite (Per Transaction) Rs. 500 Rs. 1000
UPI Lite (Wallet Capacity) Rs. 2000 Rs. 5000
UPI 123Pay (Feature Phones) Rs. 5000 Rs. 10000
- UPI Lite: - On-device wallet for small transactions without PIN, reducing server load. - UPI 123Pay: - Enables UPI payments on feature phones via IVR or USSD (*99#), promoting rural access. - UPI Lite and UPI Lite-X - Definition: - UPI Lite is an on-device wallet for small transactions; UPI Lite-X extends this with offline payment capabilities. -
Feature UPI Lite UPI Lite-X
Transaction Limit Up to Rs. 1000 without PIN Up to Rs. 1000 without PIN
Connectivity Requires internet Offline via NFC; sync within 4 days
Benefits - Faster transactions for small payments. - Works despite bank server issues. - Same as UPI Lite. - Offline functionality for low-connectivity areas.
- Significance: - Enhances accessibility for rural and low-connectivity regions, supporting India’s digital inclusion agenda. - Conversational Payments - Definition: - UPI payments initiated via AI-powered voice commands, similar to Siri or Alexa, integrated into UPI apps. - Process: - Users issue voice commands (e.g., “Pay Rs. 500 to Ravi”) via UPI app, authenticated via PIN or biometrics. - Benefits: - Enhances accessibility for visually impaired or less tech-savvy users. - Speeds up transactions with natural language processing. - Significance: - Aligns with India’s focus on inclusive digital payments, a key UPSC topic for social equity. - Pre-Sanctioned Credit Line - Definition: - A pre-sanctioned credit line is a pre-approved borrowing limit offered by banks, allowing account holders to access funds without applying for a loan, repayable with interest. - UPI Overdraft (April 2023): - Definition: - Enables UPI payments even with insufficient account balance, using a pre-approved overdraft, repaid later with interest. - Process: - Banks offer overdraft limits based on creditworthiness (e.g., Rs. 5000–10,000 for PMJDY accounts). - Users transact via UPI, with overdrawn amounts deducted from future deposits. - Features: - Supports “Buy Now, Pay Later” (BNPL) model, popular for retail purchases. - Promotes financial inclusion by providing small loans to rural users, reducing reliance on high-interest moneylenders. - Example: - A small vendor uses UPI overdraft to buy inventory, repaying when sales revenue is deposited. - Significance: - Enhances credit access for underserved populations, supporting India’s financial inclusion goals. - Interoperable Card-less Cash Withdrawal (ICCW) - Definition: - ICCW allows cash withdrawal from ATMs using UPI without a physical debit card, enhancing convenience and security. - Process: - User selects “Card-less Withdrawal” on ATM, scans UPI QR code, and authenticates via PIN. - Cash is dispensed, debited from the linked bank account or overdraft. - Benefits: - Reduces risks of card cloning or theft. - Enables access for users without cards, especially in rural areas. - Significance: - Strengthens India’s digital payment ecosystem, aligning with cashless economy objectives. - UPI for Cash Deposits/Withdrawals - Definition: - UPI enables cash deposits and withdrawals at ATMs or Cash Deposit Machines (CDMs) using QR codes, reducing reliance on physical cards. - Features: - Cash Withdrawals: - Scan QR code at ATMs or coin-dispensing machines, authenticate via UPI PIN. - Cash Deposits: - Deposit cash into CDMs by scanning UPI QR code, credited to linked account. - Example: - A user deposits Rs. 5000 into their account via a CDM using PhonePe’s UPI interface. - Benefits: - Enhances accessibility for cash-based transactions in a digital framework. - Reduces dependency on bank branches or ATMs with card readers. - Significance: - Bridges cash and digital economies, critical for India’s semi-urban and rural populations. - BHIM App - Definition: - Bharat Interface for Money (BHIM) is NPCI’s UPI-based mobile app for seamless bank-to-bank transfers, promoting digital payments. - Features: - Supports smartphones and feature phones via *99# USSD code for low-tech users. - Uses three-factor authentication (e.g., UPI PIN, mobile number, bank account) for security. - Funds remain in bank accounts, earning interest, unlike wallet-based apps. - No card-related fees (e.g., Merchant Discount Rate), making it cost-effective. - Process: - Users link bank accounts, create a VPA (e.g., name@bhim), and transact via QR codes or mobile numbers. - Significance: - A flagship initiative for India’s cashless economy, widely adopted for its simplicity and accessibility. - RuPay Card Payment Gateway - Definition: - RuPay is NPCI’s card payment scheme, launched in 2012, competing with global networks like Visa and Mastercard. - Features: - Operates via ATMs, Point of Sale (PoS) terminals, and online payment gateways. - Offers lower transaction fees than competitors, benefiting merchants and users. - Free RuPay debit cards provided with PMJDY accounts (refer to Pillar #1D: Financial Inclusion). - International agreements enable use in Singapore, Japan, UAE, etc., for limited global transactions. - Process: - Users swipe RuPay cards at PoS or enter details online, authenticated via PIN or OTP. - Significance: - Reduces India’s reliance on foreign card networks, promoting self-reliance in payment systems. - Credit Card Network Portability (2024) - Definition: - Allows customers to choose or switch credit card networks (e.g., RuPay, Visa, Mastercard) for better offers or compatibility. -
Before 2024 After 2024
- Customers locked to one network (e.g., SBI Visa card). - New customers choose preferred network at card issuance. - Existing customers switch networks at card renewal or via bank request.
- Significance: - Enhances consumer choice and competition, reducing costs and improving service quality in India’s credit card market. - NPCI Initiatives - Definition: - NPCI initiatives are technology-driven payment solutions developed to enhance India’s financial infrastructure, promoting efficiency and inclusion. -
Initiative Description
Bharat Bill Payment System (BBPS) - Facilitates utility bill payments (e.g., electricity, gas, water) via a single platform, accessible through UPI, cards, or net banking.
Unified Presentment Management System (UPMS) - Launched by Bharat BillPay Ltd. (2022), manages bill presentation and payments, enhancing BBPS efficiency.
National Financial Switch (NFS) - Powers ATM network operations, enabling inter-bank ATM transactions nationwide.
Cheque Truncation System (CTS) - Digital cheque clearance system for faster, secure processing (see 1.11.3).
National Electronic Toll Collection (NETC) - Supports FASTag for automated toll collection on highways.
PAI Chatbot - AI-based chatbot for resolving queries on NPCI products (e.g., UPI, RuPay).
DigiSaathi Helpline (1800-891-3333) - 24/7 helpline for resolving digital payment issues, enhancing user support.
DakPay (2020) - Payment app by India Post Payments Bank, offering UPI, bill payments, and banking services, especially in rural areas.
- Significance: - NPCI’s initiatives have positioned India as a global leader in digital payments, supporting economic formalization and inclusion. - Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT): E-RUPI - Definition: - E-RUPI is a prepaid, cashless electronic voucher (delivered via SMS or QR code) developed by NPCI using UPI for targeted delivery of benefits and subsidies. - Supported by: - Ministry of Finance (Department of Financial Services). - Ministry of Health (National Health Authority). - Partner banks and NPCI. - Process: - Issued by banks or authorized entities to beneficiaries, specifying purpose (e.g., medical expenses). - Beneficiary scans QR code or uses SMS code at designated merchants to redeem benefits. - Funds are transferred directly to the merchant’s account, ensuring targeted usage. - Uses: - Government Schemes: - Subsidies for food, medicines, fertilizers, or education (e.g., PM Kisan, Ayushman Bharat). - Private Sector: - Employee benefits (e.g., meal vouchers), corporate social responsibility (CSR) programs. - Expanded Scope (2023): -
Feature Before 2023 After 2023
Who Can Buy Government, companies Government, companies, individuals
Who Can Sell Banks only Banks, non-bank PPIs (e.g., AmazonPay)
Maximum Value Rs. 10,000 Rs. 1 lakh
- Significance: - Ensures leak-proof delivery of subsidies, reduces misuse, and promotes digital payments, a key UPSC topic for welfare schemes. - QR Codes - Definition: - Quick Response (QR) codes are 2D barcodes storing payment information, enabling quick and secure digital transactions via mobile apps. - Background: - Developed in Japan (1990s) by Denso Wave, widely adopted in India for digital payments. - Types: -
Type Description
Proprietary QR Code - Exclusive to specific apps (e.g., Paytm); not interoperable. - RBI banned new proprietary codes to promote standardization.
UPI QR Code - Generated by NPCI’s UPI; scannable by all UPI apps (e.g., BHIM, PhonePe).
Bharat QR Code - Developed by NPCI with Visa/Mastercard; interoperable with UPI and card apps.
- Mandate: - From 2022, apps must use UPI QR or Bharat QR for interoperability, ensuring seamless payments across platforms. - Significance: - QR codes have revolutionized retail payments in India, enabling small merchants and street vendors to accept digital payments, supporting the cashless economy. - Credit and Debit Cards (Plastic Money) - Definition: - Plastic money refers to credit and debit cards used for electronic payments at PoS terminals, ATMs, or online platforms, reducing cash dependency. -
Credit Card Debit Card
- Borrowing facility; incurs interest on unpaid balances or ATM withdrawals (e.g., 2–3% monthly). - Offers rewards (e.g., cashback, points) for spending. - Uses existing bank balance; no borrowing involved. - No rewards; linked directly to account funds.
- Additional Types: - Hybrid/Duo Card: - Combines credit and debit functions (e.g., IndusBank Hybrid Card). - Prepaid Card: - Subtype of debit card, usable without a bank account (e.g., IRCTC’s UBI Prepaid Card for ticket payments).
- Process: - Credit Card: - User spends within credit limit, repays bank with interest if not cleared by due date. - Debit Card: - Transactions deduct directly from bank account, authenticated via PIN or OTP. - Significance: - Facilitates digital transactions, especially for online shopping and travel, aligning with India’s cashless economy goals. - Card Tokenization (October 2022) - Definition: - Tokenization replaces sensitive card details (e.g., 16-digit number) with a unique token to enhance security for online and PoS transactions. - Process: - Tokens generated via card issuer’s website/app, linked to user’s device or merchant. - During payment, token is used instead of card details, reducing hacking risks. - Benefits: - Protects against data breaches by not sharing actual card details. - Simplifies recurring payments (e.g., subscriptions) with secure tokens. - Significance: - Enhances trust in digital payments, critical for India’s growing e-commerce sector. - CVC-Less Transactions - Definition: - Post-tokenization, Cardholder Verification Code (CVC) may not be required for tokenized online transactions, improving user convenience. - Process: - Tokenized cards use device authentication (e.g., OTP) instead of CVC for payments. - Benefits: - Faster checkout process for online shopping. - Maintains security through tokenization and alternate authentication. - Significance: - Balances convenience and security, supporting India’s digital payment adoption. - Cards and Technologies for Transport/Travel - Definition: - Specialized cards and technologies designed for transport-related payments (e.g., tolls, metro fares) and travel services, enhancing mobility and convenience. - National Common Mobility Card (NCMC, 2019) - Definition: - NCMC is a multi-purpose card for transport and retail payments under the “One Nation One Card” initiative, promoting seamless mobility. - Developed by: - Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs, NPCI, Bharat Electronics Limited, and banks. - Components: - NCMC Card: - Debit/credit/prepaid card used for metro, bus, railways, tolls, parking, and retail shopping. - SWEEKAR: - Automatic Fare Collection System for efficient ticketing. - SWAGAT: - Automated gates at transport stations for quick access. - Process: - Card is tapped at compatible terminals (e.g., metro gates, PoS), deducting fares or payments instantly. - Significance: - Integrates transport and retail payments, reducing cash use and enhancing urban mobility in India. - SBI Nation First Transit Card (2023) - Definition: - A prepaid card by State Bank of India for transport and retail payments, compatible with NCMC infrastructure. - Uses: - Toll payments, parking fees, public transport fares, and retail shopping. - Process: - Rechargeable via UPI, net banking, or bank branches; tapped at terminals for payments. - Significance: - Supports India’s smart mobility initiatives, aligning with digital payment goals. - FASTag/NETC (2017) - Definition: - FASTag is a prepaid, rechargeable RFID tag for automated toll collection on highways, using NPCI’s National Electronic Toll Collection (NETC) technology. - Developed by: - National Highways Authority of India (NHAI) with NPCI support. - Features: - Launched with MyFASTag and FASTag Partner apps for recharging and management. - Mandatory for vehicles since 2021, affixed on windshields for automatic toll deduction. - Process: - RFID reader at toll plaza scans FASTag, deducts toll from linked account or wallet. - Benefits: - Reduces congestion at toll plazas, saving time and fuel. - Enables tracking of stolen vehicles via RFID data. - Future: - Satellite-based toll collection planned for 2025, using GPS for distance-based tolling (refer to Pillar #5: Infrastructure). - Significance: - Transforms India’s highway infrastructure, promoting efficiency and digital payments. - Merchant Discount Rate (MDR) - Definition: - Merchant Discount Rate (MDR) is the fee merchants pay to banks for accepting card or UPI transactions, shared among the card issuer, acquiring bank, and payment gateway. - Challenges: - High MDR reduces merchant profits, discouraging PoS adoption, especially for small businesses. -
Initiative Description
RBI (2017) - Caps MDR to a percentage of transaction value (e.g., 0.4–0.9% for cards, varies by transaction size).
MEITY (2021) - Subsidizes MDR up to Rs. 2000 for RuPay debit cards and UPI transactions to encourage adoption.
- MDR Status: - RuPay Debit Cards and UPI: - Zero MDR, making them cost-effective for merchants. - RuPay Credit Cards, Visa/Mastercard: - Non-zero MDR, impacting merchant profitability. - Significance: - MDR policies balance merchant affordability with payment system sustainability, critical for India’s cashless economy. - Measures to Promote Cashless Economy - Definition: - A cashless economy relies on digital payments to reduce physical currency use, enhancing transparency and efficiency. - Initiatives: - RBI’s Vision 2019–2021: - Set goals for increasing digital payment adoption, improving infrastructure, and ensuring security. - Nandan Nilekani Committee (2019): - Recommended measures to promote digital payments, including zero MDR for UPI and RuPay. - Budget Restrictions: - Finance Act 2017 limits cash transactions to Rs. 2 lakh to curb tax evasion (refer to Pillar #2: Black Money). - Payments Infrastructure Development Fund (PIDF): - Rs. 500 crore fund (RBI + banks) to subsidize PoS machines in Tier 3–6 cities and North-East. - Supports PM SVANidhi (street vendors) and PM Vishwakarma (artisans) schemes (refer to Pillar #1D). - Significance: - Promotes financial inclusion, reduces black money, and aligns with India’s Digital India initiative, a key UPSC focus. - Global Money Transfer - Definition: - Global money transfer systems enable cross-border payments for tourism, trade, and investments, reducing reliance on traditional methods like SWIFT. - Initiatives in India: - UPI for Foreign Tourists (2023): - G20 nation tourists can use UPI for shopping from NRE/NRO accounts in India, enhancing tourism. - RuPay Cards International Acceptance (2023): - RBI allows RuPay cards for ATM withdrawals and shopping in countries like Mauritius, Singapore (2024). - UPI in Sri Lanka, Mauritius, etc. (2024): - Available in Bhutan, Oman, UAE, Nepal, France, etc., for Indian tourists’ mobile payments via UPI apps. - India-UAE Local Currency Settlement System (2023): - Enables payments in INR and AED, bypassing US dollars and SWIFT, reducing costs. - UPI-IPP (Instant Payment Platform) linkage under development for seamless settlements. - UAE’s JAYWAN and AANI: - JAYWAN: UAE’s RuPay-based domestic card scheme. - AANI: UAE’s UPI-like payment system, developed with NPCI support. - Project Nexus (2024): - Connects India’s UPI with fast payment systems in Malaysia, Philippines, Singapore, Thailand. - Coordinated by Bank for International Settlements (BIS) for 60-second cross-border P2P/P2M payments. - Financial Messaging Systems: -
SWIFT (Global) SFMS (India)
- Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication (1973, Belgium). - Facilitates international bank transactions via secure messaging. - Banned nations: Iran, Russia (post-2022 Ukraine invasion). - Structured Financial Messaging System by TCS for IDRBT (RBI’s research arm). - Supports NEFT, RTGS, and intra-bank transactions within India.
- Significance: - Enhances India’s global financial integration, reduces dependency on Western systems, and supports economic diplomacy. - Payment System Operators (PSOs) - Definition: - PSOs are entities licensed by RBI under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, to operate digital payment systems, ensuring efficiency and security. -
Type Examples
Umbrella Entity NPCI
Card Payment Gateway Mastercard, Visa, RuPay
Cross-Border Remittance Western Union, MoneyGram
Prepaid Payment Instrument (PPI) AmazonPay, PhonePe, Mobikwik
Payment Aggregators Paytm, Google Pay, Razorpay
ATM Operators SBI, White Label ATMs (Tata Indicash, Muthoot)
Financial Market Infrastructure Clearing Corporation of India (CCI)
- Significance: - PSOs drive India’s digital payment ecosystem, ensuring diverse, secure, and accessible transaction platforms. - New Umbrella Entity (NUE, 2019) - Definition: - NUE is a proposed RBI initiative to license new entities competing with NPCI for retail payment systems, fostering innovation. - Objectives: - Increase competition, reduce NPCI’s monopoly, and lower transaction costs. - Promote new technologies for digital payments. - Status (2023): - Applications (e.g., Amazon, Google-led consortiums) rejected; licensing on hold due to regulatory concerns. - Significance: - Reflects RBI’s balancing act between innovation and regulatory control, a potential UPSC discussion point. - Third-Party Application Providers (TPAPs) - Definition: - TPAPs are apps (e.g., Google Pay, WhatsApp Pay) that use NPCI’s UPI platform under permission, not statutory licensing. - Process: - TPAPs integrate with UPI via NPCI’s APIs, enabling transactions without direct RBI licensing. - Significance: - Expands UPI’s reach through private sector apps, driving adoption but raising concerns about data privacy and market dominance. - Digital Payment Regulatory Bodies - Definition: - Regulatory bodies oversee digital payment systems to ensure security, efficiency, and consumer protection, critical for India’s financial ecosystem. - Digital Transactions Ombudsman (DTO, 2019) - Definition: - DTOs are senior RBI officials at 21 locations resolving digital payment complaints up to Rs. 20 lakh. - Functions: - Handles issues with PPIs, mobile wallets, NEFT, RTGS, and UPI transactions. - Can order compensation up to Rs. 1 lakh for customer distress (e.g., fraud losses). - Appeals go to RBI Deputy Governor. - Process: - Complaints filed via RBI’s online portal or DTO offices; resolved within 30 days. - Significance: - Strengthens consumer trust in digital payments by providing accessible grievance redressal. - Board for Payment and Settlement Systems (BPSS) - Definition: - BPSS is a statutory RBI body under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, regulating all payment system providers (banks, non-banks, PPIs). - Origin: - Recommended by Narsimham-II Committee (1998) to oversee e-banking and digital payments. - Functions: - Formulates policies for payment systems, licenses PSOs, and ensures compliance. - Monitors innovations like UPI, CBDC, and FASTag for stability and security. - Significance: - Central to India’s digital payment governance, ensuring a robust and secure financial ecosystem. - Digital Payment Indices and Initiatives - Definition: - Indices and initiatives measure and promote digital payment adoption, reflecting India’s progress toward a cashless economy. -
Initiative/Index Description
RBI Vision 2019–2021 - Outlined goals for enhancing digital payment infrastructure, security, and adoption.
Digital Payments Index (DPI) - RBI’s index measuring digital payment adoption across India, based on transaction volume and value.
Financial Inclusion Index - Measures access to banking, loans, and insurance, especially for underserved populations.
Harbinger Hackathon - RBI’s 2024 hackathon with themes like Zero Financial Frauds and Divyang-Friendly systems, fostering innovation.
- Significance: - Tracks India’s progress in digital financial inclusion, a key UPSC topic for economic policy evaluation. - Cryptocurrency and Blockchain Technology - Definition: - Cryptocurrency is a digital or virtual currency secured by cryptography, operating on blockchain technology, a decentralized ledger for tamper-proof transactions. - Background: - Launched in 2009 by Satoshi Nakamoto with Bitcoin, motivated by distrust in fiat money post-2007 US subprime crisis. - Examples: - Bitcoin (1 BTC = 10^8 Satoshi), Ethereum, Ripple, Dogecoin (meme coin). - Blockchain Features: - Decentralized, transparent, and secure, used for cryptocurrencies, supply chain tracking, and smart contracts. - Types of Cryptocurrencies: - Utility Tokens: - Used for specific ecosystems (e.g., Ethereum for smart contracts). - Stablecoins: - Pegged to assets like fiat or gold (e.g., Tether, USDC). - Meme Coins: - Based on internet trends (e.g., Dogecoin, $TRUMP). - Significance: - Offers decentralized finance (DeFi) alternatives but poses regulatory challenges for India. - Hot Wallet vs. Cold Wallet - Definition: - Hot wallets are online crypto storage (e.g., mobile apps), while cold wallets are offline (e.g., USB drives). -
Hot Wallet Cold Wallet
- Connected to internet, convenient for frequent transactions. - Vulnerable to hacking (e.g., phishing attacks). - Offline storage, highly secure against cyber threats. - Less convenient for daily use, suited for long-term storage.
- Crypto-Winter (2021–2022) - Definition: - A period of prolonged low cryptocurrency prices, with Bitcoin falling from $70,000 (Nov 2021) to $20,000 (Jun 2022), a 70% drop. - Causes: - Market speculation, regulatory crackdowns, and economic uncertainty. - Impact: - Investor losses and reduced trust in crypto markets. - Bitcoin Price Surge (2024) - Context: - Bitcoin reached $100,000 in December 2024, driven by pro-crypto policies in the US (e.g., Trump administration, Elon Musk’s influence). - Significance: - Highlights crypto’s volatility and global policy impact on markets, relevant for UPSC economic trends. - US Bitcoin Strategic Reserve Fund (2025) - Definition: - A proposed US fund using 200,000 seized Bitcoins to generate profits for welfare and debt reduction without tax hikes. - Significance: - Reflects global acceptance of cryptocurrencies as strategic assets, contrasting India’s cautious approach. - Crypto-Scams - Examples: - Binance, FTX collapses, WazirX $230 million loss (2024 cyber-attack). - Risks: - Fraud, investor losses, money laundering, and lack of traceability. - Significance: - Underlines need for robust regulation, a key UPSC issue for financial governance. - Economic Survey 2023 Observations - Challenges: - Price manipulation by large holders (“whales”). - High electricity consumption for blockchain (e.g., Bitcoin mining). - Tax evasion, price volatility, fraud risks, and lack of intrinsic value. - Recommendations: - Global coordination for crypto regulation (e.g., G20, OECD frameworks). - Significance: - Highlights India’s cautious stance on cryptocurrencies, balancing innovation and risks. - Taxation in India - Budget 2022: - 30% tax on cryptocurrency profits, 1% TDS on transfers (refer to Pillar #2A: Direct Taxes). - Significance: - Integrates crypto into India’s tax framework, addressing revenue and black money concerns. - Cryptocurrency: Global Status - Status: - Banned: - Nepal, Algeria, Egypt, Morocco, Turkey, Iran, China (restricts bank services for crypto). - Legal Tender: - Venezuela: - Bolivar and Petro (cryptocurrency). - Marshall Islands: - US Dollar and SOV (2018). - El Salvador: - US Dollar and Bitcoin (2021). - Permitted Uses: - UNICEF: - Accepts crypto donations (2019). - World Bank: - Issued blockchain bond (2018, Australia). - Significance: - Reflects diverse global approaches to crypto, relevant for UPSC’s international economic policy discussions. - EU’s MiCA Regulation (2023) - Definition: - Markets in Crypto Assets (MiCA) is an EU framework to regulate cryptocurrencies, excluding CBDCs and NFTs. - Objectives: - Protect against fraud, tax evasion, money laundering, and terror financing. - Standardize crypto regulations across EU nations. - Significance: - Sets a global benchmark for crypto regulation, influencing India’s policy considerations. - OECD’s CARF Framework (2022) - Definition: - Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework (CARF) is an OECD initiative for transparent reporting of crypto transactions to combat tax evasion and fraud. - Significance: - Supports global coordination, relevant for India’s alignment with G20 regulatory efforts. - Stablecoins - Definition: - Stablecoins are cryptocurrencies pegged to stable assets like fiat (e.g., USD) or gold to reduce volatility (e.g., Tether, USDC). - Challenges: - Risks of money laundering, terror financing, and lack of consumer protection. - Potential destabilization if backing assets fail (e.g., Tether’s reserve concerns). - Significance: - Offers stability in crypto markets but requires regulation, a key UPSC issue. - World Coin Project (2021–22) - Definition: - A global digital ID system using blockchain, similar to India’s Aadhaar, for identity verification and access to services. - Pros: - Simplifies access to government schemes, education, and passports. - Enhances financial inclusion via blockchain-based identity. - Cons: - Privacy concerns due to biometric data collection (e.g., iris scans). - Risks of misuse by governments or corporations. - Significance: - Highlights blockchain’s potential beyond currency, relevant for UPSC’s technology and privacy discussions. - Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC) - Definition: - Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC) is a digital form of fiat currency issued and backed by a central bank, using blockchain or distributed ledger technology, recognized as legal tender with the same value as physical currency. - Global Examples: -
Year Nation Paper Currency CBDC
2020 Bahamas Bahamian Dollar (BSD) Digital BSD (“Sand Dollar”)
2021 Nigeria Naira eNaira
2022 Jamaica Jamaican Dollar JAM-DEX
2023 China Renminbi (Yuan) Digital Yuan (e-CNY)
- India’s CBDC (e₹): - Symbol: - e₹ or eINR. - Launched via amendments to the RBI Act, 1934, in December 2022 as a pilot project. - Issued by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) on behalf of the Government of India. - Represents legal tender as a digital token, distributed through banks and financial institutions. - No interest earned, similar to physical cash, ensuring parity with notes and coins. - Features: - Uses blockchain or distributed ledger technology for secure, transparent, and tamper-proof transactions. - Offers trust, safety, and settlement finality, akin to physical currency, backed by RBI’s credibility. - Supports offline transactions (e.g., via NFC) for accessibility in low-connectivity areas. - Interoperable with UPI and other digital payment systems for seamless integration. - Process: - RBI issues e₹ tokens to banks, which distribute them to customers via digital wallets or apps. - Users transact using e₹ for P2P (person-to-person) or P2M (person-to-merchant) payments, authenticated via secure protocols. - Transactions are recorded on a centralized ledger managed by RBI, ensuring traceability and security. - Significance: - Reduces dependency on physical cash, lowering printing and logistics costs (e.g., Rs. 500 note costs ~Rs. 4 to print). - Enhances financial inclusion by enabling digital access in rural areas, aligning with Digital India. - Combats black money and counterfeiting through traceable digital transactions. - Positions India as a leader in digital currency innovation, competing with global CBDC initiatives. - CBDC: Wholesale vs. Retail -
Type Purpose
CBDC-Retail (e₹-R) - For Person-to-Person (P2P) and Person-to-Merchant (P2M) transactions (e.g., retail purchases, bill payments). - Targets individual consumers and small businesses for daily transactions. - Example: Paying for groceries or utilities using e₹ via a mobile app.
CBDC-Wholesale (e₹-W) - For interbank settlements and large-value transactions (e.g., government bond settlements, corporate transfers). - Targets financial institutions, central banks, and large corporations. - Example: Banks settling interbank loans using e₹-W for faster, secure transactions.
- Process: - e₹-R: - Distributed via banks to customer wallets, used like UPI for retail payments, with optional offline capabilities. - e₹-W: - Used in RBI’s Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS) system for instant, high-value interbank transfers. - Significance: - e₹-R promotes financial inclusion and digital payments among the public, reducing cash reliance. - e₹-W enhances efficiency in financial markets, reducing settlement times and costs for banks. - CBDC Pilot in India - Timeline: - December 2022: - RBI launched e₹-W pilot for wholesale transactions, focusing on government securities settlement. - February 2023: - e₹-R pilot launched for retail transactions in select cities (e.g., Mumbai, Delhi). - 2024–25: - Expanded to more cities, integrated with UPI, and tested for offline transactions. - Participating Banks: - State Bank of India, ICICI Bank, HDFC Bank, and others, distributing e₹ via digital wallets. - Features: - e₹-R pilot supports payments at merchants, peer transfers, and government scheme disbursements. - Offline mode tested for rural areas using NFC or QR codes, ensuring access without internet. - Progress (July 2025): - Over 1 million daily e₹ transactions reported in pilot phase, with growing merchant adoption. - RBI aims for nationwide rollout by 2026, integrating e₹ with existing payment systems. - Significance: - Demonstrates India’s proactive approach to digital currency, balancing innovation with regulatory control, a key UPSC topic. - Benefits of CBDC - Financial Inclusion: - Enables access to digital payments for unbanked populations, especially in rural India, via offline capabilities. - Cost Reduction: - Reduces costs of printing, transporting, and securing physical currency (e.g., RBI spends ~Rs. 6000 crore annually on currency management). - Transparency: - Traceable transactions reduce black money and tax evasion, supporting India’s economic formalization. - Efficiency: - Faster settlements for wholesale transactions (e.g., e₹-W reduces clearing time in RTGS). - Security: - Blockchain ensures tamper-proof records, reducing fraud and counterfeiting risks. - Global Competitiveness: - Aligns India with global CBDC trends, enhancing cross-border payment efficiency (e.g., integration with Project Nexus). - Challenges of CBDC - Privacy Concerns: - Centralized ledger may allow RBI to track transactions, raising data privacy issues. - Solution: - RBI exploring anonymized tokens for e₹-R to balance privacy and traceability. - Cybersecurity Risks: - Digital currency vulnerable to hacking or system failures, requiring robust security measures. - Infrastructure Gaps: - Limited internet and smartphone penetration in rural areas may hinder e₹ adoption. - Solution: - Offline transaction modes (e.g., NFC, QR codes) being developed. - Public Awareness: - Low awareness of CBDC among masses requires education campaigns, especially in non-urban areas. - Impact on Banks: - Direct CBDC access may reduce bank deposits, affecting lending capacity. - Solution: - RBI’s two-tier model (banks as intermediaries) mitigates disintermediation risks. - Significance: - Addressing these challenges is critical for CBDC’s success, a potential UPSC discussion point on monetary policy. - CBDC vs. Cryptocurrency -
Feature CBDC (e₹) Cryptocurrency (e.g., Bitcoin)
Issuer Central Bank (RBI) Decentralized (no single issuer)
Legal Tender Yes No (except in El Salvador)
Value Stability Pegged to INR, stable Volatile (e.g., Bitcoin’s 2021–24 fluctuations)
Regulation Fully regulated by RBI Limited regulation in India
Technology Centralized blockchain/ledger Decentralized blockchain
Use Case Retail and wholesale transactions Investment, DeFi, limited transactions
- Significance: - CBDC offers a regulated, stable alternative to cryptocurrencies, addressing RBI’s concerns about volatility and fraud while promoting digital payments. - Additional Economic Concepts for UPSC - Definition: - Fundamental economic concepts related to money and payment systems, critical for a holistic understanding of India’s monetary framework. - Money Supply: - Definition: - Money supply is the total amount of money available in an economy, categorized into M0, M1, M2, M3, and M4 by RBI. - Components: -
Category Description
M0 (Monetary Base) Currency in circulation + bank reserves with RBI
M1 Currency with public + demand deposits + other deposits with RBI
M2 M1 + short-term time deposits (e.g., savings accounts)
M3 M2 + long-term time deposits (e.g., fixed deposits)
M4 M3 + all post office savings deposits
- Significance: - Money supply influences inflation, interest rates, and economic growth, a key UPSC topic for monetary policy. - Monetary Policy and RBI: - Definition: - Monetary policy is the RBI’s strategy to control money supply and interest rates to achieve economic stability, using tools like repo rate, CRR, and SLR. - Role in Payment Systems: - RBI regulates payment systems (e.g., UPI, CBDC) to ensure liquidity, security, and inclusion. - Example: Zero MDR for UPI and RuPay to promote digital payments. - Significance: - Aligns payment systems with macroeconomic goals like inflation control and financial inclusion. - Financial Inclusion: - Definition: - Financial inclusion ensures access to banking, credit, and payment systems for all, especially underserved populations. - Initiatives: - Pradhan Mantri Jan-Dhan Yojana (PMJDY): - Zero-balance accounts with overdraft, RuPay cards. - UPI 123Pay, UPI Lite: - Payments for feature phone users and rural areas. - E-RUPI: - Targeted subsidy delivery for welfare schemes. - Significance: - Critical for India’s inclusive growth, reducing economic disparities, a core UPSC theme. - Regulatory Framework: - Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007: - Governs all payment systems, empowering RBI to license and regulate PSOs (e.g., NPCI, Paytm). - Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999: - Regulates cross-border payments, impacting UPI’s international expansion and remittances. - Coinage Act, 2011: - Defines legal tender status for coins, prohibits melting to maintain circulation. - Significance: - Provides legal backbone for India’s payment systems, ensuring stability and trust. - Demonetization and Digital Push - Context: - The 2016 demonetization accelerated India’s shift to digital payments, with UPI transactions growing from 0.1 billion (2016) to over 100 billion annually (2024). - Impact: - Increased adoption of UPI, mobile wallets, and card payments. - Reduced cash dependency, formalizing the economy and curbing black money. - Significance: - A landmark event in India’s economic policy, highlighting the role of payment systems in economic reform. - Cross-Border Payment Challenges - Issues: - High costs of remittances via SWIFT (e.g., 3–5% fees for small transfers). - Delays in international settlements due to intermediary banks. - Currency conversion losses for Indian workers abroad. - Solutions: - UPI’s international expansion (e.g., Singapore, UAE) for low-cost transfers. - India-UAE Local Currency Settlement (2023) to bypass USD and SWIFT. - Project Nexus (2024) for instant cross-border payments with ASEAN nations. - Significance: - Enhances India’s global financial integration, supporting diaspora remittances and trade. - Future Trends in Payment Systems - Emerging Technologies: - Artificial Intelligence: - Conversational payments, fraud detection (e.g., NPCI’s PAI chatbot). - Internet of Things (IoT): - Wearable devices for contactless payments. - 5G and Satellite Connectivity: - Enhancing offline payment systems like UPI Lite-X. - Policy Goals: - RBI’s Vision 2025: - Aims for 3x digital payment growth, full CBDC rollout, and zero financial frauds. - Global Leadership: - India’s UPI and CBDC as models for developing nations. - Significance: - Positions India at the forefront of global payment innovation, a key UPSC topic for economic and technological advancements. - Pillar 1A2: Money Supply and Monetary Policy - Definition: - Money supply refers to the total stock of money available in an economy at a given time, encompassing currency in circulation and various types of bank deposits. Monetary policy involves actions by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) to regulate money supply, credit availability, and interest rates to achieve economic stability, control inflation, and promote growth. - Context: - Money supply and monetary policy are critical for managing liquidity, stabilizing prices, and fostering economic development in India, aligning with objectives like financial inclusion and sustainable growth. - Significance for India: - Effective monetary policy supports India’s economic goals by controlling inflation, ensuring financial stability, and facilitating credit access, crucial for a developing economy aiming for self-reliance (Atmanirbhar Bharat). - Demand for Money: Liquidity Preference Theory - Definition: - Liquidity Preference Theory, proposed by John Maynard Keynes in his 1936 book *The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money*, explains why individuals prefer to hold money in liquid form (cash) based on three motives. - Motives for Holding Money: - Transaction Motive: - Individuals hold money for routine transactions, such as purchasing daily necessities like milk or vegetables, facilitating trade as a medium of exchange. - Precautionary Motive: - Money is held to meet unexpected or emergency expenses, such as medical costs or impulsive purchases during travel, ensuring financial preparedness. - Speculative Motive: - Also known as asset demand, individuals hold cash to capitalize on future investment opportunities, such as buying gold or land when prices fall. - Factors Affecting Total Demand for Money: - Total demand = Transaction demand + Precautionary demand + Speculative demand. - Inverse Relationship with Interest Rates: - Higher deposit interest rates encourage people to invest in banks rather than hold cash, reducing liquidity preference, and vice versa. - Other Factors: - Income levels, consumption patterns, and economic uncertainty influence the amount of money held for each motive. - Liquidity of Assets - Definition: - Liquidity refers to the ease with which an asset can be converted into cash without significant loss of value. - Types of Assets: - Highly Liquid Assets: - Cash, gold, demand deposits, government securities (G-Sec/T-Bills), and shares/bonds of reputed companies, easily convertible to cash. - Relatively Illiquid Assets: - Real estate, paintings, or sculptures, which are harder to sell quickly at the desired price due to limited buyer availability. - Liquidity Injection: - Occurs when RBI purchases financial assets (e.g., G-Sec, T-Bills) from banks or NBFCs, providing them with cash to enhance market liquidity. - Deposit Types - Definition: - Deposits are funds placed in banks, categorized based on accessibility and purpose, forming a significant component of money supply. - Types of Deposits: - Demand Deposits: - Funds in current accounts, savings accounts, and salary accounts, accessible on demand without penalty. - Examples: - Corporate salary accounts (e.g., Infosys opening accounts for employees) and individual savings accounts. - Time Deposits: - Funds in fixed deposits, cumulative/recurring deposits, or staff security deposits, accessible only at maturity or with a penalty for early withdrawal. - Green Deposits: - Introduced by RBI in 2023, these are fixed deposits where funds are loaned to environmentally friendly projects (e.g., renewable energy, waste management). -
Permitted Investments Prohibited Investments
- Renewable energy, clean transportation, energy efficiency, afforestation. - Fossil fuels, nuclear power, tobacco-related businesses.
- Notes: - Issued in rupees only, covered by DICGC (up to Rs. 5 lakh refund if bank fails). - Not mandatory for banks/NBFCs to offer green deposits. - If no green borrowers are found, funds can be invested in liquid instruments (e.g., T-Bills) with maturity up to 1 year.
- Bulk Deposits: - Large fixed deposits, defined by RBI as Rs. 3 crore or higher (post-2024, previously Rs. 2 crore). - Banks may offer different interest rates based on their business strategy. - Inoperative and Unclaimed Deposits: -
Inoperative Accounts Unclaimed Deposits
- No transactions for 2+ years. - Banks must pay interest on savings accounts. - Savings/current accounts unoperated for 10 years or fixed deposits unclaimed 10 years post-maturity. - Over Rs. 42,000 crore stuck (2023 data).
- UDGAM Portal (2023): - Launched by RBI as the Unclaimed Deposits Gateway to Access Information, allowing beneficiaries to search unclaimed deposits across banks using Aadhaar or PAN. - Funds unclaimed for 10 years are transferred to RBI’s Depositor Education and Awareness Fund (DEA). - Full Reserve vs. Fractional Reserve Banking - Definition: - Banking systems differ in how they manage deposits and lend funds, impacting money creation and economic stability. - Comparison: -
Aspect Full Reserve Banking Fractional Reserve Banking
Lending from Demand Deposits No; demand deposits not used for loans. Yes; banks lend both demand and time deposits.
Lending from Time Deposits Yes Yes
Vulnerability to Bank Runs Low; demand deposits are safe. High; lending both deposit types increases risk.
Economic Growth Contribution Low; limited loan availability. High; more loans fuel economic activity.
- India’s System: - India uses fractional reserve banking, allowing banks to lend a portion of deposits, increasing money supply but requiring careful regulation to prevent bank runs. - Measures of Money Supply (M0-M4) - Definition: - Money supply is measured by RBI through indicators M0, M1, M2, M3, and M4, reflecting different levels of liquidity and components of money in circulation. - Components: - M0 (Reserve Money/Monetary Base): - Currency in circulation (public and vault cash) + bankers’ deposits with RBI (e.g., CRR) + other deposits with RBI. - Also called high-powered money due to its role in money creation. - M1 (Narrow Money): - Currency with public + demand deposits (CASA) + other deposits with RBI. - M2: - M1 + savings deposits with post office savings banks. - M3 (Broad Money/Aggregate Monetary Resources): - M1 + time deposits with commercial banks; most commonly used for monetary policy analysis. - M4: - M3 + total deposits with post office savings organizations (excluding National Savings Certificates). - Significance: - M3 is the primary indicator for RBI’s monetary policy, influencing price levels (inflation) and interest rates. - Narrow money (M1, M2) is smaller and more liquid; broad money (M3, M4) is larger due to inclusion of time deposits. - Notes: - Only net demand and time deposits (public deposits) are counted, excluding inter-bank deposits. - NCERT formulas are used for exams, differing slightly from RBI’s technical formulas for simplicity. - Money Multiplier - Definition: - The money multiplier (MM) measures how much M3 (broad money) is generated from each unit of M0 (reserve money) through bank lending in a fractional reserve system. - Formula: - MM = M3 / M0; theoretically, MM = 1 / CRR (e.g., 4% CRR yields MM of 25x). - Factors Affecting MM: - Cash Holdings: - More cash held by individuals reduces bank deposits, acting as a leakage and lowering MM. - Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR): - Higher CRR requires banks to hold more reserves with RBI, reducing lendable funds and lowering MM. - Banking Penetration: - Increased financial inclusion and digital economy boost deposits, raising MM. - Economic Conditions: - Boom periods with high loan demand increase MM; expansionary monetary policy (low CRR) also raises MM. - Historical Trends: - 1960s: MM < 2x; 1990s: MM > 3x; 2023: MM > 5x, driven by LPG reforms, banking penetration, and digital economy growth. - 2000 Note Withdrawal (2023): - Increased bank deposits (as people retained funds in accounts) boosted MM and liquidity. - Significance: - Higher MM indicates greater liquidity, supporting economic growth but requiring careful management to avoid inflation. - Creation of Money (M0) - Definition: - M0 (reserve money) is created by RBI’s Issue Department, ensuring assets match liabilities as per the RBI Act. - Assets of Issue Department: - Rupee coins (RBI circulates as government’s agent). - Gold coins (minimum Rs. 200 crore). - Gold bullion (minimum Rs. 115 crore). - Foreign securities (no minimum post-1995). - Government securities (used for government borrowing). - Liabilities of Issue Department (M0): - Currency in circulation (public and vault cash). - Bankers’ deposits with RBI (e.g., CRR). - Other deposits (e.g., public, NBFCs, government, international organizations). - Notes: - Rs. 1 notes and coins are government liabilities, circulated by RBI as an agent. - M0 increases when RBI’s assets (e.g., foreign securities, G-Sec) increase, boosting money supply. - Money Supply and FRBM Act - Definition: - The Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act, 2003, limits government borrowing and fiscal deficits to ensure fiscal discipline. - Key Provisions: - Government must control fiscal deficit within a specified percentage of GDP. - Government cannot borrow directly from RBI (buying G-Sec in primary market) except in: - Short-term Ways and Means Advances (WMA). - Emergencies (war, disaster, agricultural crisis, significant GDP decline). - RBI can buy/sell G-Sec in the secondary market via Open Market Operations (OMO). - Monetization of Deficit: - Involves RBI printing money to lend to the government, restricted by FRBM to prevent inflation. - Allowed only in exceptional circumstances outlined above. - Currency in Circulation (CIC) - Definition: - CIC measures the total cash (notes and coins) used as a payment instrument, excluding digital payments like cheques or UPI. - Trends: - 2021: - CIC growth declined due to reduced precautionary cash holdings post-COVID, with more deposits in banks. - 2022: - Temporary CIC increase due to Russia-Ukraine war, prompting precautionary cash holdings. - Significance: - Indicates reliance on cash versus digital payments, influencing RBI’s monetary policy. - Velocity of Money Circulation - Definition: - Velocity of money is the average number of times a unit of money is used in transactions over a period, reflecting economic activity. - Example: - A Rs. 10 note used to buy a pen, then used by the shopkeeper for tea, performs Rs. 20 worth of transactions. - Factors Affecting Velocity: - Income Distribution: - Money with lower-income groups has higher velocity due to frequent spending. - Borrowing Habits: - Higher borrowing (common in developed economies with consumerist lifestyles) increases velocity. - Economic Boom: - Increased purchases of raw materials and hiring during boom periods raise velocity. -
