Significance: - High velocity indicates robust economic activity; low velocity may signal economic slowdown. - Liquidity Trap - Definition: - A liquidity trap occurs when low interest rates fail to stimulate borrowing and spending, as people prefer to hold cash due to economic uncertainty or expectation of falling asset prices. - Comparison with Liquidity Overhang: -
Aspect Liquidity Trap Liquidity Overhang
Definition Excess cash holding due to low interest rates and economic pessimism. Excess liquidity in the system due to high money supply or low loan demand.
Impact Reduces effectiveness of monetary policy; limits economic growth. Increases inflation risk if not absorbed by economic activity.
- Significance: - In a liquidity trap, RBI’s efforts to boost money supply (e.g., lowering CRR) may not stimulate demand, requiring alternative fiscal measures. - Pillar 1A3: Monetary Policy - Definition: - Monetary policy is a macroeconomic strategy designed by a country’s central bank to manage money supply and interest rates, influencing variables such as inflation, consumption, savings, investment, and capital formation. - Context: - Monetary policy has evolved as a vital tool for central banks to stabilize economies, control inflation, and promote sustainable growth, adapting to domestic and global economic challenges. - Significance for India: - The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) employs monetary policy to maintain price stability (2-6% CPI), foster economic growth, generate employment, ensure exchange rate stability, and promote financial inclusion, aligning with India’s vision of becoming a developed economy by 2047. - Objectives of Monetary Policy - Definition: - The objectives of monetary policy are the goals set by the RBI to guide its actions in managing money supply and interest rates to achieve macroeconomic stability. - Primary Objectives: - Price Stability: - Maintains inflation within 2-6% CPI to preserve purchasing power and ensure economic predictability. - Economic Growth: - Encourages investment and consumption through favorable monetary conditions to support sustainable GDP growth. - Employment Generation: - Stimulates economic activity to create jobs, reducing unemployment. - Exchange Rate Stability: - Stabilizes the rupee’s external value to promote trade and attract foreign investment. - Secondary Objectives: - Financial Inclusion: - Enhances access to financial services, supporting initiatives like Jan Dhan Yojana. - Social Justice: - Ensures equitable credit distribution to priority sectors (e.g., agriculture, MSMEs) to reduce economic disparities. - Quantitative Tools - Definition: - Quantitative tools, also known as general or indirect tools, regulate the overall volume of money supply and credit in the economy, impacting all sectors uniformly. - Types: - Statutory Reserve Requirements: - Definition: - Mandates banks to hold a portion of their deposits as reserves, either with the RBI or in liquid assets, to ensure financial stability and control money supply. - Subtypes: - Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR): - The percentage of Net Demand and Time Liabilities (NDTL) held as cash with the RBI (4% as of February 2025). - Impact: - Higher CRR reduces lendable funds, contracting money supply; lower CRR increases liquidity, expanding money supply. - Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR): - The percentage of NDTL held in government-approved securities (e.g., G-Secs, gold) (18% as of February 2025, capped at 40% legally). - Impact: - Locks funds in safe assets, restricting credit expansion and supporting government borrowing. - Additional Measures: - Incremental CRR (I-CRR): - Temporary measure (e.g., 10% on additional deposits post-₹2000 note withdrawal in 2023) to absorb excess liquidity and prevent liquidity overhang. - Impact: - Reduces money supply to control inflation and ensure bank run safety. - Open Market Operations (OMOs): - Definition: - The RBI’s buying or selling of government securities (G-Secs) in the secondary market to adjust liquidity. - Subtypes: - Repo OMO: - RBI buys G-Secs with a repurchase agreement, injecting liquidity. - Outright OMO: - RBI buys/sells G-Secs without repurchase agreements, increasing (buying) or decreasing (selling) money supply. - Operation Twist (2019): - Simultaneous buying of long-term G-Secs and selling of short-term G-Secs to lower long-term yields, reducing borrowing costs for corporates and government. - Government Securities Acquisition Programme (G-SAP): - Pre-announced G-Sec purchases (e.g., ₹1.0 lakh crore in Q1 2021-22, ₹1.2 lakh crore in Q2 2021-22) to inject liquidity post-COVID. - Impact: Buying G-Secs increases money supply, fighting deflation; selling G-Secs reduces money supply, controlling inflation. - Key Rates: - Definition: - Interest rates set by the RBI to influence borrowing and lending in the economy. - Subtypes: - Repo Rate: - Rate at which RBI lends short-term funds to banks against G-Secs (6.25% as of February 2025). - Impact: - Primary policy rate to control inflation; lower rates increase liquidity, higher rates reduce it. - Reverse Repo Rate: - Rate at which RBI borrows from banks against G-Secs (3.35% as of February 2025, fixed). - Impact: - Higher rates incentivize banks to park funds with RBI, reducing money supply. - Variable Rate Reverse Repo (VRRR): - Reverse repo conducted via auction, with rates determined by bids, used to absorb excess liquidity. - Marginal Standing Facility (MSF): - Rate for emergency borrowing by Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) and Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) using SLR-quota G-Secs (6.50% as of February 2025, Repo + 0.25%). - Impact: - Provides liquidity during crises. - Standing Deposit Facility (SDF): - Rate at which banks park surplus funds with RBI without collateral (6.00% as of February 2025, Repo - 0.25%). - Impact: - Absorbs excess liquidity to control inflation. - Bank Rate: - Rate for long-term RBI lending, now primarily used for penalties (6.50% as of February 2025, aligned with MSF). - Impact: - Dormant tool, less used for lending. - Policy Corridor: - Definition: - The range between MSF (upper bound) and SDF (lower bound, replaced Reverse Repo in April 2022) around the repo rate (width: 0.50%). - Impact: - Guides liquidity management; narrower corridor enhances policy precision. - Qualitative Tools - Definition: - Qualitative tools, also known as selective or direct tools, control the distribution of credit to specific sectors or segments to address economic imbalances. - Types: - Moral Suasion: - Definition: - Informal persuasion by the RBI Governor to influence banks’ lending behavior without punitive measures. - Methods: - Conferences, letters, seminars, or public statements (e.g., urging banks to reduce lending rates or expand rural branches). - Impact: - Encourages compliance through moral pressure and public opinion. - Direct Action: - Definition: - Punitive measures (e.g., fines, restrictions) on banks for non-compliance with RBI directives under acts like the RBI Act or Banking Regulation Act. - Example: - Clawback provisions for bank CEOs involved in fraud (2019). - Impact: - Enforces regulatory compliance. - Margin Requirements/Loan to Value (LTV): - Definition: - Specifies the percentage of collateral value banks can lend against (e.g., 90% for gold loans post-2020 reforms). - Impact: - Higher LTV boosts credit for sectors like gold loans; lower LTV curbs speculative lending. - Selective Credit Control: - Definition: - Regulates credit to specific sectors to manage demand or supply imbalances. - Subtypes: - Credit Rationing: - Limits credit to specific sectors or borrowers (e.g., 1960s Credit Authorization Scheme required RBI approval for loans above ₹1 crore). - Impact: - Curbs speculative lending or prioritizes essential sectors. - Consumer Credit Control: - Relaxes down payment/EMI norms during deflation to boost consumption (e.g., for vehicles, electronics). - Impact: - Stimulates demand in targeted sectors. - Priority Sector Lending (PSL): - Definition: - Mandates banks to allocate a minimum percentage of loans to priority sectors (e.g., 40% for SCBs, 75% for RRBs/SFBs/UCBs by 2025-26). - Sectors (Post-2020 Reforms): - Weaker Sections (12%): - SC/ST, women, minorities, PM-Jan Dhan overdrafts (up to ₹10,000). - Agriculture (10%): - Marginal (up to 1 hectare) and small farmers (1-2 hectares). - Micro Enterprises (7.5%): - Khadi, village industries. - Others (2.5%): - Affordable housing, renewable energy, education loans (up to ₹10 lakh), social infrastructure. - Mechanisms: - PSL Certificates (PSLC): - Overachieving banks sell excess PSL compliance to underachieving banks. - Shortfall Funds: - Underachieving banks deposit shortfalls in RIDF (NABARD, since 1995-96) or UIDF (NHB, since 2023) for rural/urban infrastructure. - Impact: - Enhances credit access for underserved sectors, supporting inclusive growth. - Monetary Policy Framework - Definition: - The framework outlines the RBI’s approach to setting monetary policy, guided by the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC), to achieve macroeconomic objectives. - Key Features: - Flexible Inflation Targeting (FIT): - Adopted in 2016, mandates CPI inflation within 2-6% (4% ± 2%), extended to March 2026. - Failure: - Inflation exceeding 6% or below 2% for three consecutive quarters requires a report to the government. - Monetary Policy Committee (MPC): - Composition: - Six members (RBI Governor as ex-officio chair, Deputy Governor for monetary policy, one RBI-nominated executive, three government-appointed experts). - Tenure: - Four years for external members (no re-appointment); RBI members tied to their roles. - Process: - Meets bi-monthly, requires a quorum of four (including Governor), decides repo rate by majority vote (Governor has casting vote in ties). - Transparency: - Publishes meeting minutes on the 14th day and a Monetary Policy Report every six months. - Policy Stances: - Accommodative: - Lowers rates to boost growth during slowdowns. - Neutral: - Maintains balanced rates for stability. - Calibrated Tightening: - Increases rates to control inflation without committing to cuts. - Withdrawal of Accommodation: - Gradually tightens policy to reduce liquidity. - Historical Context and Economic Theories - Milton Friedman: - Monetarist theory emphasized controlling money supply to manage inflation, earning a Nobel Prize in 1976. - Phillips Curve: - Suggests an inverse relationship between inflation and unemployment, guiding policy trade-offs (detailed in Pillar 4C). - Optimal Inflation: - Moderate inflation (2-6% CPI) supports economic stability by encouraging spending and investment. - Cantillon Effect: - When money supply increases, early recipients gain purchasing power at the expense of others, potentially causing stagflation (high inflation, high unemployment, low growth). - Liquidity Trap: - Coined by John Maynard Keynes, occurs when low interest rates fail to stimulate demand due to consumer preference for holding cash, limiting monetary policy effectiveness. - Limitations of Monetary Policy - Definition: - Factors that restrict the RBI’s ability to control inflation and boost growth despite bi-monthly policy updates. - Key Limitations: - Savings Culture: - High household savings in India reduce banks’ reliance on RBI borrowing (e.g., repo), slowing policy transmission compared to Western economies. - Non-Performing Assets (NPAs): - Bad loans and poor management in banks (especially public sector) hinder effective transmission of rate cuts. - Supply-Side Issues: - External factors like poor monsoons, geopolitical tensions, or rising global commodity prices drive inflation beyond RBI’s control. - Government Policies: - Fiscal deficits, subsidy leakages, and loan waivers undermine monetary policy efficacy. - Structural Issues: - Lack of ease of doing business and inadequate infrastructure (e.g., electricity, roads) affect production and inflation trends. - Informal Economy: - Presence of informal moneylenders and low financial inclusion in rural areas limit banking sector penetration. - Black Swan Events: - Unpredictable events (e.g., 2007 subprime crisis, 2020 COVID-19) disrupt monetary policy effectiveness, potentially causing capital outflows (e.g., $100 billion FPI pullout risk). - Lazy Banking: - Banks’ reluctance to seek new loan clients hampers money multiplier effects. - Transmission Delays: - Pre-2019, banks delayed passing repo rate cuts to borrowers (6-12 months for customers, 24 months for inflation impact), improved by the External Benchmark System (2019). - External Benchmark System - Definition: - Introduced in October 2019, mandates banks to link loan interest rates to an external benchmark for faster policy transmission. - Components: - Benchmark Options: - Repo rate, 91-day or 182-day T-bill yields, or benchmarks by Financial Benchmarks India Ltd. - Formula: - External Benchmark + Spread (profit) + Risk Premium = Loan Interest Rate. - Update Frequency: - Banks must update benchmark data at least every three months. - Eligible Loans: - Personal, retail (home, vehicle), micro/small enterprises, and medium enterprises (from April 2020). - Applies to existing loans if borrowers apply for inclusion. - Benefits: - Faster transmission of RBI rate changes, enhancing transparency and accountability. - Recent Developments - Green Deposits: - SBI proposed lower CRR on green deposits (2023) to increase loanable funds for green projects, not yet implemented. - Net Interest Margin (NIM): - Difference between loan and deposit interest rates; NIM >5% is risky for financial stability (Indian banks below 5%, per Economic Survey 2024). - Liquidity Overhang: - Excess money supply over loan demand (e.g., post-₹2000 withdrawal in 2023), addressed by I-CRR to prevent cheap loans fueling inflation. - Loan Interest Rates: - Pre-2019 Systems: - Administered rates (pre-1991), BPLR (2003), MCLR (2016) were slow in transmitting rate cuts. - External Benchmark (2019): - Links loan rates to repo or T-bill yields, reducing delays. - Floating vs. Fixed Rates: - Borrowers can switch from floating to fixed rates or opt for bullet repayment (single payment) to reduce EMI burden. - PSL Reforms (2020): - Increased quotas for weaker sections (10% to 12%), agriculture (8% to 10%), and RRBs/SFBs/UCBs (40% to 75% by 2025-26). - Added sectors: - Startups, renewable energy, healthcare. - Weightage to backward districts for balanced regional development. - Global Context: - Post-2022, RBI raised repo rates (4% to 6.50%) less aggressively than US Federal Reserve (4.25%), ECB (3%), or Bank of England (2.50%) to achieve a soft landing (balancing inflation and growth) rather than a hard landing (sharp growth slowdown). - Pillar 1B1: Financial Intermediaries - Banks - Definition: - Financial intermediaries are entities facilitating financial transactions between lenders and borrowers, investors and entrepreneurs, or households and businesses, channeling funds to promote economic activity. - Context: - Banks, as formal financial intermediaries, are critical to India’s economy, supporting credit creation, financial inclusion, and economic growth under the regulatory oversight of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). - Significance for India: - Banks drive India’s economic development by mobilizing savings, providing credit, and supporting government initiatives like financial inclusion and infrastructure development, aligning with the vision of a developed economy by 2047. - Reserve Bank of India (RBI) - Definition: - The RBI, established under the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, is India’s central bank, responsible for monetary policy, financial regulation, and economic stability. - Historical Context: - Recommended by the Hilton Young Commission (1926) post the 1929 Great Depression, which led to the collapse of over 450 banks in India. - Operational since April 1, 1935; fully nationalized in 1948 under the RBI Transfer of Ownership Act. - Central Board Composition: - Comprises official directors (RBI Governor, up to four Deputy Governors) and non-official directors (two government officials, ten government-nominated directors, four from RBI’s regional boards: North, East, South, West). - Governor and Deputy Governors: - Appointed by the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointment Search Committee (FSRASC), led by the Cabinet Secretary, with final approval by the Appointments Committee of the Cabinet, chaired by the Prime Minister. - Tenure typically three years, with re-appointment possible. - Notable Governors: - Sir Osborne Smith (1935–37, first Governor), C.D. Deshmukh (1943–49, first Indian Governor), Manmohan Singh (1982–85), Raghuram Rajan (2013–16), Shaktikanta Das (2018–24), Sanjay Malhotra (since December 2024). - Offices and Departments: - Headquartered in Mumbai, with regional offices in Delhi (North), Kolkata (East), Chennai (South), and Mumbai (West). - Departments oversee banks, NBFCs, payment systems, foreign exchange, and more. - Functions: - Monetary Policy: - Controls money supply (M0) and interest rates (Pillar 1A3). - Foreign Exchange Management: - Regulates via the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA). - Banker to Governments: - Manages public debt and accounts for Union and State governments. - Banker’s Bank: - Acts as lender of last resort and advises banks on monetary matters. - Regulation: - Oversees banks (Banking Regulation Act, 1949), All India Financial Institutions (AIFIs), and deposit-taking NBFCs. - Promotional Roles: - Enhances financial inclusion (e.g., PSL norms, 25% rural branches), customer protection (Ombudsman), and data publication (e.g., Financial Stability Report). - International Cooperation: - Engages with BASEL, IMF, G20’s Financial Stability Board, and the Network for Greening the Financial System (NGFS, joined 2021). - Notable Portals (2022–2024): - UDGAM Portal (2023): - Facilitates access to unclaimed deposits (transferred to RBI’s Depositor Education and Awareness Fund after 10 years). - PRAVAAH Portal (2023): - Streamlines licensing for banks, NBFCs, and digital payment firms. - Daksh Web Portal (2022): - Enables RBI to monitor banks and NBFCs for compliance (e.g., credit card fraud). - E-Kuber Portal: - Manages RBI’s core banking, NEFT/RTGS, repo operations, and G-Sec purchases for retail investors. - RBI-CRIS (2024): - Provides climate change and pollution data to banks and NBFCs. - Sachet Portal (2024): - Handles complaints about investment fraud, redirecting to authorities (e.g., SEBI). - Connect 2 Regulate: - Collects public feedback for RBI. - Digital Payments Intelligence Platform (2024): - Reduces payment fraud risks (A.P. Hota Committee). - MuleHunter AI (2024): - Detects mule accounts used for money laundering (developed by RBI’s Innovation Hub). - FREE-AI Framework (2024): - Promotes ethical AI use in finance (Pushpak Bhattacharyya Committee). - Podcast (2024): - Disseminates RBI information to the public. - Domain Names (2025): - Introduces .bank.in for banks and .fin.in for NBFCs to reduce phishing, managed by IDRBT. - Scheduled Banks - Definition: - Banks listed in the Second Schedule of the RBI Act, 1934, meeting specific criteria for financial stability and depositor safety. - Criteria: - Minimum paid-up capital and reserves of ₹5 lakh. - Operations not detrimental to depositors’ interests. - ₹5 Lakh Capital Sufficiency: - The ₹5 lakh capital requirement, set in 1934, is outdated due to inflation and economic growth. Modern banks require significantly higher capital (e.g., ₹500 crore for new private banks under 2016 on-tap licensing). - Remains a historical criterion for scheduled status but is insufficient for operational viability today. - Features: - Must maintain Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) with RBI. - Eligible for RBI’s liquidity facilities (e.g., repo, MSF). - Subject to RBI regulations for depositor protection. - Preferred for government schemes (e.g., PM Jan Dhan Yojana). - Types: - Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs): Includes public sector banks (e.g., SBI, PNB), private sector banks (e.g., ICICI, Axis), foreign banks, and differential banks (RRBs, SFBs, PBs). - Scheduled Cooperative Banks: Includes urban and rural cooperative banks (e.g., Haryana Rajya Sahakari Bank). - Non-Scheduled Banks: - Primarily cooperative banks not meeting RBI’s criteria, maintaining CRR internally and ineligible for RBI liquidity facilities. - Commercial Banks - Definition: - Banks accepting demand and time deposits, providing loans, and operating under RBI regulation to support economic activity. - Pre-Independence Evolution: - Early banks (e.g., Bank of Hindustan, 1770; Presidency Banks, 1806–42) lacked regulation. - Imperial Bank of India (1921) became SBI in 1955. - Indian-owned banks emerged (e.g., PNB, 1894; Bank of Baroda, 1908). - Post-Independence Nationalization: - 1969: - 14 banks with deposits over ₹50 crore nationalized (e.g., PNB, Canara). - 1980: - Six banks with deposits over ₹200 crore nationalized (e.g., Vijaya, Corporation). - Issues: - Elite control, reckless lending, rural neglect prompted nationalization to enhance financial inclusion and economic planning. - Public Sector Banks (PSBs): - Consolidation and Mergers: - 2008: - State Bank of Saurashtra merged with SBI. - 2010: - State Bank of Indore merged with SBI. - 2017: - Five SBI associate banks (State Bank of Bikaner & Jaipur, State Bank of Hyderabad, State Bank of Mysore, State Bank of Patiala, State Bank of Travancore) and Bharatiya Mahila Bank merged with SBI. - 2019–20: - Ten PSBs merged into four anchor banks: - Punjab National Bank (PNB) merged with Oriental Bank of Commerce and United Bank of India (effective April 1, 2020). - Canara Bank merged with Syndicate Bank (effective April 1, 2020). - Union Bank of India merged with Andhra Bank and Corporation Bank (effective April 1, 2020). - Indian Bank merged with Allahabad Bank (effective April 1, 2020). - Benefits: - Enhanced geographical and technological synergies (e.g., ATM networks, IT costs). - Privatization: - Government sells over 51% shares, converting PSBs to private banks (e.g., Axis, IDBI). - Challenges: - Political interference, low loan recovery, union resistance, and low profitability due to administered rates. - Private Sector Banks (PvBs): - Post-1991 Reforms: - M. Narasimham Committee (1991) recommended reduced government ownership, lower CRR/SLR, liberalized interest rates, and new bank licenses. - Licensing Rounds: - 1993–95 (e.g., ICICI, HDFC), 2001–04 (e.g., Kotak, Yes), 2013–16 (e.g., Bandhan, IDFC First). - On-Tap Licensing (2016): - Allows applications anytime with ₹500 crore capital and 10 years’ banking experience. - Features: - Higher foreign investment (up to 49% automatic, 74% with approval), innovative services, and competition with PSBs. - IPO Norms: - New private banks must list within three years via Initial Public Offerings (IPOs) to raise capital and ensure public shareholding. - SEBI regulates IPOs, requiring 25% public shareholding within three years for banks under RBI’s 2016 guidelines. - Foreign Banks: - Post-1991 liberalization allowed banks like Citibank, HSBC to operate subsidiaries. - Regulation: - Subject to CRR, SLR, and modified PSL norms; licensed on reciprocity. - Investment Limits: - Up to 20% in PSBs, 49–74% in PvBs. - Differential Banks: - Definition: - Specialized banks with restricted operations to promote financial inclusion. - Types: - Regional Rural Banks (RRBs): - Established under RRB Act, 1976, for rural credit (M. Narasimham Committee, 1970s). - Shareholding: - Union (50%), State (15%), Sponsor Bank (35%); 2015 amendment allows 49% private investment. - Regulation: - RBI (ultimate), NABARD (immediate); 75% PSL, restricted to specific districts. - Data (ES25): - Reduced from 133 (2006) to 43 (2023) due to mergers; improved profitability and lower NPAs. - Local Area Banks (LABs): - Introduced in 1996, privately established under Banking Regulation Act. - Operate in three contiguous districts, primarily rural, with one urban center. - Non-scheduled, subject to tailored RBI norms. - Small Finance Banks (SFBs): - Approved in 2015 (Nachiket Mor Committee) for financial inclusion. - Examples: - Capital SFB, Ujjivan, Utkarsh; require ₹100 crore capital, 25% rural branches, 75% PSL, 50% loans under ₹25 lakh. - Regulation: - Same as PvBs for CRR, SLR, and BASEL-III; can become universal banks after five years. - Payments Banks (PBs): - Approved in 2015 for small savings and remittances; examples: Airtel, IPPB, Paytm. - Restrictions: - Accept demand deposits up to ₹2 lakh (2021), no loans or credit cards, invest in G-Secs/T-Bills. - Regulation: - Same as PvBs, with modified SLR terms; can upgrade to SFB after five years. - Paytm Case (2024): - RBI restricted operations due to KYC non-compliance, multiple accounts per PAN, data security issues, and money laundering concerns; UPI services allowed to continue. - India Post Payments Bank (IPPB): - Launched in 2018, 100% owned by Department of Posts; operates under Banking Regulation Act. - Features: - Zero-balance accounts, doorstep banking, ₹2 lakh deposit limit, no loans; aims to become SFB. - Comparison with Universal Banks: - Universal banks (e.g., SBI, ICICI) operate nationwide, accept all deposits, and provide unrestricted loans (40% PSL). - Differential banks face geographical or operational restrictions, targeting underserved segments. - Banking Investment Company (BIC): - Proposed by RBI in 2010 as a holding company model to manage multiple banking and financial subsidiaries under one entity, improving risk management and capital allocation. - Not implemented due to regulatory complexities and lack of consensus; remains a theoretical concept. - Cooperative Banks - Definition: - Member-owned banks prioritizing community welfare over profit, regulated under the Banking Regulation Act (since 1966) and cooperative society laws. - Types: - Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs): - Operate in urban areas, single or multi-state, with scheduled/non-scheduled status. - Regulation: - RBI (since 2020 amendment); 40–75% PSL. - Option to convert to SFBs (2018). - PMC Bank Scam (2019): - Punjab and Maharashtra Cooperative (PMC) Bank collapsed due to fraudulent lending (₹4,355 crore to HDIL), hiding 70% NPAs. - RBI imposed restrictions, capped withdrawals at ₹1 lakh, and facilitated its merger with Unity SFB in 2022. - Rural Cooperative Banks: - Include State Cooperative Banks, District Central Cooperative Banks (DCCBs), and Primary Agricultural Credit Societies (PACS). - Regulation: - Dual (RBI and State Registrar, except PACS by State Registrar only). - Features: - 75% PSL, lower lending rates than commercial banks, member-focused loans. - Features: - Voting: - One member, one vote, unlike share-based commercial banks. - Challenges: - Politicization, casteism, poor loan recovery, scams, and money laundering. - National Urban Cooperative Finance and Development Corporation (NUCFDC, 2024): - Type: - RBI-regulated NBFC, launched by Ministry of Cooperation. - Funding: - ₹300 crore from NCDC and investors. - Functions: - Develops IT for UCBs, acts as SRO, provides advisory services, and facilitates regulator dialogue. - Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs) - Definition: - Financial intermediaries providing loans and investments without accepting demand deposits, regulated by RBI or other authorities (e.g., SEBI, IRDAI). - Types (RBI-Regulated): - Investment and Credit Companies: - Finance asset purchases (e.g., SREI Equipment Finance). - Consumer Durable Loan/BNPL: - Support EMI-based purchases (e.g., Bajaj Finserv). - Core Investment Companies: - Long-term investments (e.g., Tata Capital). - Infrastructure Finance Companies: - Fund infrastructure (e.g., REC). - Infrastructure Debt Funds: - Infrastructure loans (e.g., L&T IDF). - Asset Reconstruction Companies: - Manage NPAs (e.g., Reliance ARC). - Factoring Companies: - Short-term lending against invoices (e.g., IFCI Factors). - Gold Loan Companies: - Collateral-based loans (e.g., Muthoot, regulated LTV by RBI). - Housing Finance Companies: - Home loans (e.g., DHFL, regulated by RBI since 2019). - Micro Finance Institutions (MFIs): - Small loans to low-income households (e.g., Bandhan, max ₹1.25 lakh loan, regulated by RBI and Ministry of Corporate Affairs). - MUDRA: - Non-deposit-taking NBFC under SIDBI, supports micro-enterprises via PM Mudra Yojana. - Money Changers/Authorized Dealers: - Foreign currency exchange under FEMA. - Residuary NBFCs: - Unregulated by other authorities, fall under RBI. - Features: - Deposits: - Only NBFC-D (deposit-taking) accept time deposits, not insured by DICGC. - Regulation: - CRR not applicable; SLR applies to NBFC-D; BASEL-III for NBFC-D and systemically important NBFCs (assets over ₹500 crore). - Credit Cards: - Since 2022, NBFCs can issue credit cards directly or co-branded. - Recovery: - Housing Finance NBFCs have SARFAESI powers; others vary. - Complaints: - Handled by RBI’s Ombudsman. - Shadow Banking: - Includes HFCs, liquid debt mutual funds, and retail NBFCs operating partially outside RBI regulation. - Risks: - High asset risk, potential default (e.g., IL&FS crisis, 2019). - Islamic Banking: - Prohibited in India due to RBI opposition (2017), despite financial inclusion arguments, citing manpower needs and terror financing concerns. - FinTech Companies - Definition: - Technology-driven firms offering financial services like loans, payments, and investments. - RBI-Regulated: - P2P Lenders: - Connect borrowers and lenders (e.g., Faircent, CRED Mint). - Account Aggregators: - Manage financial data (e.g., NeSL, Cookiejar). - Loan Aggregators: - Facilitate loans (e.g., Loanadda, Paisabazaar). - Payment Aggregators: - Enable digital payments (e.g., Razorpay). - Mobile Wallets: - Support digital transactions (e.g., PhonePe, Paytm). - Non-RBI Regulated: - Discount Brokers: - Online trading platforms (e.g., Zerodha, SEBI-regulated). - Insurance Web Aggregators: - Compare insurance policies (e.g., Policybazaar, IRDAI-regulated). - Digital Lenders: - Categories: - RBI-regulated (e.g., banks, P2P), SEBI-regulated (e.g., VCFs), and unregulated (illegal, subject to closure). - Self-Regulatory Organization (SRO): - Fintech Association for Consumer Empowerment (FACE, 2024) regulates fintech for transparency, ethics, and consumer protection. - Benefits: - Reduces RBI’s workload, enhances coordination, and sets standards for data privacy and cybersecurity. - Development Finance Institutions (DFIs) - Definition: - Specialized institutions providing medium- to long-term loans for infrastructure and industrial projects, not accepting public deposits. - Features: - Offer loans, guarantees, and technical support (e.g., project feasibility studies). - Objective: - Boost GDP through infrastructure development. - All India Financial Institutions (AIFIs): - EXIM Bank (1982): - Supports cross-border trade with loans and foreign currency. - NABARD (1982): - Regulates and finances rural banks, operates RIDF. - NHB (1988): - Supports housing finance, monitors RESIDEX. - SIDBI (1990): - Finances MSMEs, operates udyamimitra.in for schemes like Mudra. - NaBFID (2022): - Fifth AIFI, funds infrastructure (₹5 lakh crore over three years), supports National Infrastructure Pipeline. - Regulation: - RBI oversees AIFIs with tailored BASEL norms. - Credit Information and Rating - Credit Information Companies (CICs): - Regulator: - RBI. - Examples: - CIBIL, Equifax, Experian. - Function: - Assess individual creditworthiness (e.g., CIBIL score 300–900). - Utility: - Influences loan approval and interest rates. - Credit Rating Agencies (CRAs): - Regulator: - SEBI. - Examples: - CRISIL, ICRA, Moody’s. - Function: - Rate companies, NBFCs, and governments (e.g., AAA, D-). - Utility: - Impacts investment decisions and borrowing costs. - Informal Financial Intermediaries - Definition: - Entities providing loans without formal application processes, often requiring collateral such as property, vehicles, or gold, operating outside RBI regulation. - Features: - Charge high compound interest rates and may use coercive recovery methods. - Source funds from personal savings or, in some cases, illicit money. - Account for approximately 33% of rural household borrowing (2012 data), alongside banks and personal networks. - Types: - Indigenous Bankers: - Historically known as Shroffs, Chettiars, or Marwaris; mobilize funds via hundis (bills of exchange) or commercial bills. - Moneylenders/Brokers: - Include pawnbrokers, village banias, or mahajans, lending from personal or borrowed funds. - Regulation: - Governed by state laws (e.g., Bombay Moneylenders Act, 1947; Kerala, 1958; Gujarat, 2011), requiring registration, capping interest rates, and prohibiting strong-arm tactics. - Significance: - Fill credit gaps in underserved areas but pose risks due to high costs and unregulated practices, impacting financial inclusion efforts. - Pillar 1B2: Bad Loans & Other Burning Issues in Banking - Loan Types - Loan Types: Depending on Guarantee/Collateral - Secured Loans - Borrower pledges an asset as security (e.g., home loans, gold loans, automobile loans, business loans against fixed deposits, shares, bonds, or property). - Lower risk for bankers, often resulting in lower interest rates. - Typically higher loan amounts and longer repayment tenures (e.g., 15–20 years). - Unsecured Loans - No collateral required (e.g., overdrafts, credit cards, personal loans for wedding/travel, student loans, farmer loans). - Higher risk for bankers, leading to higher interest rates. - Shorter repayment tenures to recover funds quickly. - Exception: Some unsecured loans (e.g., education loans, micro-enterprise loans, farmer loans) may have lower interest rates due to government-backed credit guarantees (Ref: Pillar#1D). - Loan Types: Based on Interest Rate - Fixed Interest Loan - Interest rate remains constant throughout the loan tenure (e.g., short-term crop loans up to ₹3 lakh for farmers at 7% fixed interest). - Floating Interest Loan - Interest rate varies based on an external benchmark (e.g., repo rate) plus spread and risk premium (Ref: Pillar#1A2-Monetary Policy). - Rate may increase or decrease during the loan tenure. - Teaser Loan: A subtype with low initial interest rates that increase later. RBI introduced stricter regulations in 2011 but has not banned them. - Loan Types: Based on Borrowers - Prime Borrower - Has the capacity to repay loans, posing lower risk to lenders. - Subprime Borrower (Individual) - Lacks repayment capacity. Offering teaser loans to such borrowers contributed to the 2007-08 US Subprime Crisis, leading to the Global Financial Crisis (Ref: Pillar#3). - Overleveraged Borrower (Company) - Has borrowed beyond repayment capacity, with a high debt-to-equity ratio (Ref: Pillar#1C). - Zombie Lending - Weak banks provide new loans to subprime/overleveraged borrowers to keep them afloat, increasing risk. - Impaired Loan - A loan not performing as per original terms, typically due to delayed or non-repayment. - Loan Types: Syndicated Lending and Credit Line - Syndicated Lending - Multiple lenders share the risk of a borrower’s default, spreading financial exposure. - Can be a fixed lump sum or a credit line (contrary to some misconceptions). - Credit Line - A pre-approved loan amount that borrowers can draw upon as needed, repaying only the used portion with interest. - Loan Types: Financial vs. Non-Financial Debt - Financial Debt - Includes loans from banks, NBFCs, bonds, and other debt securities (e.g., housing loans, credit card dues). - Non-Financial Debt - Includes treasury bills and other government securities, not typically considered household debt. - RBI’s Authority on Credit Card Interest Rates (SC Ruling, 2024) - Banking Regulation Act, 1949, grants RBI exclusive authority to set loan interest rates, including for credit cards. - 2008: - National Consumer Disputes Redressal Commission (NCDRC) capped credit card interest at 30%. - 2024: - Supreme Court overturned NCDRC’s ruling, affirming only RBI can regulate interest rates, as some banks charged 36–49% on late payments. - NPA/Twin Balance Sheet (TBS) Problem: Three Stages - Stage 1: Mid-2000s Boom - Global economic growth led Indian corporates to take large loans, becoming overleveraged. - Stage 2: 2007-08 Subprime & Global Financial Crisis - Indian exports declined, coupled with UPA government’s policy paralysis, judicial activism, and environmental restrictions, delaying projects and loan repayments. - Stage 3: By 2013 - ~1/3rd of bank loans were held by companies with an Interest Coverage Ratio (ICR) < 1, indicating insufficient revenue to cover interest payments. - Resulted in weakened balance sheets for both large corporates and Public Sector Banks (PSBs), termed Twin Balance Sheet Syndrome (TBS) by Economic Survey 2015-16. - Non-Performing Assets (NPAs) - Definition - A loan becomes an NPA if principal or interest remains unpaid for over 90 days from the due date. - For farm loans, NPA status is based on cropping seasons, not days. - NPA Decline (Economic Survey 2024) - Significant reduction in NPAs due to loan recoveries and write-offs. - NPA-Related Definitions in Banking - Standard Assets - Loans where borrowers repay principal and interest on time. - Special Mention Accounts (SMA) - SMA-0: - Principal or interest unpaid for 1–30 days. - SMA-1: - Unpaid for 31–60 days. - SMA-2: - Unpaid for 61–90 days. - Provisioning - RBI mandates banks to set aside funds to cover potential NPA losses, reducing bank profitability. - Gross NPA (GNPA) - Total value of all NPA loans. - Net NPA - GNPA minus provisioning. - Substandard Asset - Loan classified as NPA for 12 months or more. - Doubtful Asset - Substandard asset for an additional 12 months or more. - Loss Asset - Declared by bank, auditor, or RBI as having little to no salvageable value. - One-Time Settlement (OTS) with Haircut - Borrower settles dues at a reduced amount (e.g., pays 60% of dues, bank forgoes 40% as a loss). - Evergreening of Loans - Issuing new loans to pay off old loans, masking NPAs. - Loan Write-Off - Process - Bank removes NPA from its balance sheet’s asset side to claim tax benefits. - Does not waive the bank’s right to recover the loan; it’s an accounting exercise. - Impact - Reduces NPA percentage by removing bad loans from the balance sheet. - 2022: Banks wrote off ₹11.17 lakh crore in loans over six years (Government to Parliament). - Restructured Loans & Stressed Assets - Restructured Loan - Loan terms (principal, interest rate, or tenure) modified to aid borrower repayment. - Stressed Assets - Comprises NPAs, written-off loans, and restructured loans. - Atmanirbhar Loan/EMI/NPA Relief (Moratorium) - 2020-March: - RBI allowed a six-month optional moratorium (March–August) for loan repayments due to COVID-19. - Borrowers could skip EMIs if income was affected, but repayment was not compulsory if financials were strong. - Loans were not waived, only temporarily suspended. - RBI Frameworks for NPAs/Bad Loans - Frameworks (2015–2021) - 3R Framework (2015–19): Revitalizing Stressed Assets. - Prudential Framework (2019): For stressed assets. - Resolution Frameworks 1.0 and 2.0 (2020–21): Allowed extra loans, extended tenures, or reduced interest rates for genuine cases. - Note: - These are outdated for 2025-26, removed from detailed study. - NPA: Household Loan Stress (K-Shaped) - Household loans are for consumption (e.g., marriage, iPhone) or asset creation (e.g., home, shop). - Rising defaults in consumption loans (e.g., credit cards, gold loans) but lower in asset creation loans, forming a K-shaped trend (Nomura research, 2024). - ES25 on NPA Data & Observations - NPA reduction attributed to loan recoveries and write-offs. - ES25: Credit-to-GDP Gap - Definition: - Difference between current credit-to-GDP ratio and its long-term trend. - Positive/High Gap: - Indicates looming stress/crisis (e.g., 2006–12 US subprime crisis impacting India’s TBS). - Negative Gap: - Suggests sustainable banking sector with growth potential. - ES25: Bank Credit-to-GDP Ratio (India vs. Others) - India’s ratio is lower than the US, UK, and Japan, indicating less risk of an economic crisis compared to these nations. - SARFAESI Act 2002: Origin, Objective & Limitations - Origin - 1991: - Narasimham-I Committee noted borrowers obtained court stay orders, delaying NPA recovery. Led to Debt Recovery Tribunals (DRTs, 1993). - 1998: - Narasimham-II Committee recommended strengthening DRTs, resulting in the SARFAESI Act 2002. - Objective - Enables lenders to attach mortgaged assets for unpaid loans, change company boards, auction assets, or sell to Asset Reconstruction Companies (ARCs). - Not applicable to farm loans. - Process - Defaulters cannot approach ordinary courts for stay orders; must go to DRT, then Debt Recovery Appellate Tribunal (DRAT), requiring a 50% loan dues deposit. - Appeals escalate to High Court and Supreme Court. - Lenders with SARFAESI Powers - All banks (commercial and cooperative). - Housing Finance Companies (HFCs, e.g., DHFL). - NBFCs with asset size ≥ ₹100 crore and loans ≥ ₹20 lakh (e.g., Bajaj Finserv, Reliance Capital). - Legal Rights of Loan Defaulters
RightDescription
Right to Ample NoticeLenders must provide 60 days’ notice before auctioning assets.
Right to Be HeardDefaulter can appeal to the lender during the notice period.
Right to Ensure Fair ValueAuction must reflect fair market value (e.g., cannot auction a ₹5 crore bungalow at ₹2 crore).
Right to Balance ProceedsExcess auction proceeds (e.g., ₹1 crore if ₹6 crore fetched for a ₹5 crore loan) returned to defaulter.
Right to Humane TreatmentRecovery agents cannot harass; contact restricted to 7 AM–7 PM.
- Limitations of SARFAESI Act - Understaffed DRTs/DRATs lead to case backlogs (over 1 lakh pending in 2016), delaying recovery. - Asset value erosion (e.g., machinery, vehicles) during prolonged cases. - Auctions may yield low returns in niche sectors (e.g., remote hotels). - Act does not facilitate loan restructuring, necessitating the IBC Code. - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) 2016 - Overview - Classifies creditors into Financial Creditors (FCs: banks, NBFCs, bondholders, homebuyers) and Operational Creditors (OCs: suppliers, contractors, employees). - FCs can participate in the Committee of Creditors (CoC); OCs cannot. - IBC Appeal Structure
CriteriaIndividual/Partnership FirmCompany
RegistrationShops and Establishments Act, Indian Partnership Act, 1932Companies Act, 2013
Adjudicating AuthorityDebt Recovery Tribunal (DRT)National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT)
Appellate AuthorityNational Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT)NCLAT
Next AppealSupreme CourtSupreme Court
- IBC Resolution Process - Insolvency Professional (IP) creates a resolution plan (e.g., reducing interest, extending tenure, finding new investors). - Plan presented to CoC (FCs), with voting power based on loan amounts. - If approved by required FC percentage, the plan is implemented; otherwise, assets are liquidated. - Budget-2024: IBC resolved over 1,000 companies, recovering ₹3.3 lakh crore for lenders. - Statutory Bodies Related to IBC
OrganizationFounded UnderHeadquarters
National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT)Companies Act, 2013Delhi (with benches in multiple cities)
National Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT)Companies Act, 2013Delhi
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI)IBC, 2016Delhi
- Insolvent vs. Wilful Defaulter
CriteriaInsolvent DefaulterWilful Defaulter
DefinitionIncapable of repaying loans due to financial shortage.Has capacity but deliberately avoids repayment.
Loan Restructuring EligibilityEligible under IBC, unless NPA for over a year with no repayment capacity, then assets auctioned under SARFAESI.Not eligible; assets auctioned under SARFAESI.
Contest Elections/Government JobsNot explicitly barred by Constitution.Not allowed (Bankers’ Association proposed to Election Commission).

- RBI Proposal (2023) - Banks must declare wilful defaulters within 6 months of NPA classification to avoid delays (e.g., Vijay Mallya case, delayed from 2012 to 2017). - Compromise Settlement (RBI, 2023) - Wilful defaulters can negotiate a compromise settlement with banks. - Wilful Defaulter Characteristics (RBI) - Deliberate non-payment despite adequate cash flow and net worth. - Siphoning funds between companies. - Misusing loan funds or selling pledged assets. - Disposing of collaterals without bank consent. - Falsifying records or engaging in fraudulent transactions. - Guarantor - A person (e.g., friend, relative) legally responsible for repaying a loan if the borrower defaults. - Required for large loans or borrowers with poor credit scores. - Consequences: Bank can attach guarantor’s assets, downgrade their credit score. - IBC Pre-Packs - A pre-arranged insolvency resolution for MSMEs, introduced via IBC 2021 Ordinance. - Common in the UK and US; faded from current affairs for 2025-26. - Atmanirbhar IBC Ordinance/Act 2020 - Suspended new IBC cases for 6 months (until March 24, 2021) due to COVID-19 challenges. - Increased minimum default threshold for IBC cases from ₹1 lakh to ₹1 crore to protect small businesses. - Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI) - Statutory body under IBC 2016, under Ministry of Corporate Affairs. - Comprises 1 Chairman (current: Ravi Mittal, 2022), 1 RBI nominee, and 8 government nominees. - Selects Insolvency Professional Agencies (IPAs) like ICAI, ICSI, and Institute of Cost Accountants. - IBBI Information Utility - Maintains borrower database (e.g., National E-Governance Services Ltd., NeSL, 2017). - Aids lenders in assessing creditworthiness and provides evidence for legal proceedings. - Indian Institute of Corporate Affairs (IICA) - Autonomous body under Ministry of Corporate Affairs. - Offers a two-year Graduate Insolvency Programme, allowing graduates to become IPs without 10 years’ experience. - Cross-Border Insolvency - Involves insolvency proceedings across national borders; details not critical for prelims. - IBC Observations from Economic Survey 2025 - Increased Forex Hedging - Pre-IBC: - Borrowers avoided forex hedging due to weak SARFAESI enforcement. - Post-IBC: - Fear of losing company control encourages hedging, improving loan access for exporters. - Reduced Bond Credit Spreads - Bond credit spread = Corporate Bond Yield – Treasury Bond Yield. - IBC reduces spreads by increasing investor confidence in corporate bonds. - Miscellaneous Benefits - Recovered ₹3.6 lakh crore by September 2024. - Improved borrower behavior, reducing need for government bailouts during slowdowns. - Future Suggestions - NCLT should penalize frivolous complaints. - Streamline court procedures to maintain IBC’s non-adversarial approach. - Budget-2025: On Loans/NPA - NaBFID to establish Partial Credit Enhancement Facility for infrastructure corporate bonds. - PSBs to develop Grameen Credit Score for SHG members and villagers. - Revamped Central KYC Registry rollout in 2025. - Voluntary Liquidation of Companies - Liquidation: Process of shutting down a company. - Types - Involuntary: - Due to loan default, via IBC or SARFAESI. - Voluntary: - Owner-initiated without default, via Companies Act, 2013, or IBC (former more popular). - Budget-2022: - Centre for Processing Accelerated Corporate Exit (C-PACE) to expedite voluntary liquidation. - Credit Rating/Monitoring Initiatives
PortalDescription
Credit Information Companies (CICs) & Credit Rating Agencies (CRAs)Provide credit scores and ratings (Ref: Pillar#1B1).
Information Utility (IU)IBBI’s borrower database, managed by NeSL.
Central Repository of Information on Large Credits (CRILC)RBI’s portal for loans above ₹5 crore, with weekly updates.
Public Credit Registry (PCR)Proposed by RBI’s Deosthalee Committee, not yet implemented.
- National Financial Information Registry (NFIR, Budget-2023) - Designed with RBI to provide comprehensive borrower data (individuals and companies). - Enhances loan processing speed and accuracy. - Public-Tech Platform for Frictionless Credit (2023) - Collects data from government, CICs, and aggregators to streamline loan approvals. - Unified Lending Interface (2024-September) - Extension of Public-Tech Platform, initially for farmers and MSMEs, managed by RBI’s Innovation Hub. - eBkray Auction Platform/IBAPI (2019) - Indian Banks Association’s portal for e-auction of PSB-attached properties. - Baanknet (2025) - PSB e-auction portal for defaulted loan properties, managed by Department of Financial Services. - CEIB Unified Automated Search Portal (2024) - Assists PSBs in background checks for borrowers seeking over ₹50 crore, managed by Central Economic Intelligence Bureau. - IFTAS Cloud Computing Facility - RBI-owned Indian Financial Technology & Allied Services provides secure cloud storage for banks/NBFCs. - Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH) Repository - RBIH’s digital repository for blockchain, AI/ML, and fintech innovations. - AI and Banks - Covered in Mains Handout (ES25). - Legal Entity Identifier (LEI) Number - 20-digit global code for companies, mandated by G20’s Financial Stability Board post-2008 crisis. - Indian coordinator: Clearing Corporation of India. - Mandatory for cross-border transactions ≥ ₹50 crore (RBI, 2022). - Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018 - Targets offenders involved in scams ≥ ₹100 crore (e.g., cheque dishonor, loan fraud, money laundering) who flee India. - Special PMLA courts order appearance within 6 weeks; non-compliance leads to “Fugitive Economic Offender” status and asset attachment. - Appeals limited to High Court and Supreme Court. - 14.20.1 Bombay HC Ruling - PSBs cannot request Look Out Circulars (LOCs) against defaulters, as only the Ministry of Home Affairs can issue them. - 14.20.2 CARIN Asset Recovery Group (2024) - India’s Enforcement Directorate joined the Camden Asset Recovery Inter-Agency Network to trace criminal assets globally. - DICGC Act: Objective & Limitations - Objective - Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation (DICGC, 1961) mandates banks to insure deposits, paying up to ₹5 lakh (increased from ₹1 lakh, Budget-2020) per depositor if a bank fails. - DICGC, 100% RBI-owned, is headquartered in Mumbai. - Limitations - Covers only ₹5 lakh per depositor; excess requires liquidation. - Excludes Primary Agricultural Credit Societies (PACS) and NBFCs. - Historical case: UTI’s 2002 losses not covered, requiring government bailout of ₹14,500 crore. - Proposed Financial Resolution and Deposit Insurance (FRDI) Bill (2017) withdrawn in 2018 due to public opposition. - Bad Bank (NARCL, IDRCL-2021)

- Prompt Corrective Action (PCA) Framework - RBI classifies Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) into Risk Thresholds #1–3 based on capital and asset quality. - Actions for Higher Risk - Strict warnings, audits, and supervision. - Restrictions on salaries, dividends, branch expansion, and lending. - Potential merger or shutdown (Banking Regulation Act, 1949). - Exit PCA: Reduce NPAs, increase capital, and improve profitability. - PCA for NBFCs (2022) - Monitors NBFCs using NPAs, BASEL-III capital adequacy, and Tier 1 capital. - Applies to deposit-taking NBFCs (e.g., Bajaj Finserv) and government-owned NBFCs (e.g., PFC, REC). - Supervisory Action Framework (SAF) for Urban Co-operative Banks - Outdated, removed from study. - Four-Tiered Regulatory Framework for Urban Co-operative Banks (2022)

- Higher tiers face stricter BASEL-III norms. - Regulations Review Authority (RRA 2.0) - Established in 2021 to simplify RBI regulations for banks/NBFCs. - Chairman: - M. Rajeshwar Rao (RBI Deputy Governor). - RBI Supervision: Utkarsh-2022 Roadmap - Three-year framework (2019) to improve RBI regulation and supervision based on global best practices. - Digital Lending Norms (RBI, 2022-November) - Regulates Loan Service Providers (LSPs) like LazyPay, ensuring no exorbitant interest rates and mandatory grievance redressal officers. - First Loss Default Guarantee (FLDG) Norm - FinTechs must cover a portion of bank losses if borrowers default. - Digital Lending Apps Repository (RBI, 2024-August) - Lists approved digital lending apps; unlisted apps are illegal. - BASEL-III Norms - Set by Bank for International Settlements (BIS) to ensure global financial stability. - Implemented in India from 2013, requiring: - Minimum Capital to Risk-Weighted Assets Ratio (CRAR) of 9% by 2019. - Capital Conservation Buffer (CCB), Counter Cyclical Capital Buffer (CCCB), Leverage Ratio (LR), Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR), and High-Quality Liquid Assets (HQLA). - Non-compliance leads to PCA listing, potential merger, or shutdown. - RBI Increasing CRAR on Unsecured Loans (2023)

- Implications - Encourages prudent loan approvals. - Increases loan costs (up to 4% higher interest rates). - Reduces bank/NBFC profitability due to higher capital requirements. - Capital Tiers - BASEL-III divides capital into tiers (e.g., Tier 1, Tier 2) to meet risk-weighted asset requirements. - Additional Tier 1 (AT1) Bonds - No maturity date, perpetual interest payments; can be written off or converted to shares in case of bank losses. - Zero Coupon Bonds - Covered in Pillar#1C (Sharemarket). - Domestic Systemically Important Banks (D-SIBs) - Banks too big to fail (e.g., SBI, ICICI, HDFC), requiring additional capital. - BASEL Norms/Deadlines Postponed (COVID-19) - Deadlines extended due to economic disruptions; specific dates not critical. - Banks, Balance Sheet/Audit Norms - Indian Accounting Standards (IndAS) - Mandated by Ministry of Corporate Affairs for standardized financial reporting. - RBI sets implementation deadlines for banks; details not critical. - Recapitalization of PSBs - PSBs needed ₹1.8 lakh crore by 2019 for BASEL-III compliance. - Indradhanush Plan (2015–18): - ₹70,000 crore allocated, later deemed insufficient. - Bank Recapitalization Bonds (2017): - 7% interest, mature 2028–33, non-transferable. - Budget-2021: - ₹20,000 crore for PSB recapitalization. - Post-2022: - No further funds, as PSB profitability improved. - Banking Sector: Governance/Administrative Reforms - Gyan-Sangam-I, 2015 - Outcomes: Bifurcated PSB CMD roles, Indradhanush Plan, and Bank Board Bureau (BBB). - Bank Board Bureau (BBB, 2016) - Non-statutory body selecting top PSB officials; replaced by FSIB in 2022. - Financial Services Institutions Bureau (FSIB, 2022) - Replaces BBB to comply with court ruling, with similar functions. - PSB Personnel Reforms (ES20) - Shifted to Mains Handout. - Enhanced Access and Service Excellence (EASE) Agenda 2018 - Six pillars: - Customer responsiveness, responsible banking, credit outreach, MSME support, financial inclusion, and HRM. - EASE 6.0 (2023–24) and 7.0 (2024–25) focus on digitalization, risk management, and customer service. - Staff Accountability Framework for PSB-NPA - Protects PSB bankers from undue inquiries for bona fide loan decisions (up to ₹50 crore, 2022). - B.P. Kanungo Committee (2022) - Reforms to prevent harassment by loan recovery agents. - Terms

- Negative News: IL&FS & Shadow Banking - Shadow Banking - Non-regulated financial activities outside traditional banking (e.g., HFCs, Liquid Debt Mutual Funds, Retail NBFCs). - Risky, illiquid assets; vulnerable to bank-run-like situations. - IL&FS & NBFC Liquidity Crisis - IL&FS (1987, Mumbai), a systemically important NBFC, faced defaults due to poor lending practices and fraud. - Triggered NBFC liquidity crisis (2019), affecting investors (e.g., ₹4,000 crore stuck in LDMFs). - Atmanirbhar: RBI Support for NBFCs - TLTRO and special liquidity window (Ref: Pillar#1A2). - RBI Remedies - Tightened Asset-Liability Management (ALM) norms. - Proposed four-tier NBFC regulation (Base, Middle, Upper, Top Layers). - Partial Credit Guarantee Scheme (PCGS) 2.0 - Budget-2019: - Government covers 20% of PSB losses on NBFC/MFI bond defaults. - Government & SEBI Reforms - Shifted to Mains Handout. - Negative News: PNB, LoU, SWIFT, Nirav Modi - PNB Scam (2018): Corrupt officials issued Letters of Undertaking (LoUs) to Nirav Modi and Mehul Choksi without due diligence, worth ₹12,000 crore. - LoUs facilitated foreign currency loans; not integrated with PNB’s Core Banking Solution, enabling fraud. - RBI Dividend, Reserves, Economic Capital Framework - Income Sources - Interest on G-Secs, foreign bonds, loans to banks/NBFCs, seigniorage, penalties. - Allocations - Staff funds, provisions, contingency reserves (₹2.5 lakh crore), exchange rate stability reserves (₹7 lakh crore). - Bimal Jalan Panel (2018) - Set principles for higher dividend transfers to the government (2019). - Record Dividend (2023–24) - ₹2.1 lakh crore transferred to the government. - RBI Financial Year - Shifted to April–March from 2021, aligning with government’s fiscal year. - Section-7 Directive & Urjit Patel’s Resignation - Section 7(1) allows government consultation with RBI Governor; Section 7(2) enables binding orders. - 2018: - Government initiated consultation, leading to Patel’s resignation before term completion. - Pillar 1C1: Bonds and Debt Instruments - Definition: - Debt instruments are financial securities representing a loan made by an investor to a borrower, typically a government or corporation, entitling the holder to periodic interest payments and principal repayment at maturity, playing a critical role in capital markets. - Context: - In India, debt instruments like bonds and debentures facilitate government and corporate financing, supporting infrastructure, economic growth, and financial inclusion, with the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) and Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) ensuring regulatory oversight. - Significance for UPSC: - Understanding debt instruments is essential for UPSC aspirants, as they are frequently tested in Prelims for their role in financial markets, monetary policy, and economic development, with recent questions focusing on innovative instruments like green bonds and electoral bonds. - Capital: Types and Arrangements - Definition: - Capital refers to financial resources used by businesses or governments to fund operations, investments, or projects, categorized based on purpose and duration. - Types of Capital: - Fixed Capital: - Funds allocated for long-term assets like land, machinery, or buildings, essential for establishing or expanding business infrastructure (e.g., purchasing factory equipment). - Working Capital: - Funds used for day-to-day operations, such as raw materials, salaries, rent, and marketing, ensuring smooth business functioning (e.g., paying employee wages). - Capital/Funding Arrangements: - Trade Credit: - Short-term credit extended by suppliers allowing deferred payment for goods or services (e.g., a retailer paying a supplier 30 days after delivery). - Accounts Receivable and Payable: - Receivables are amounts owed to a company by its customers; payables are amounts a company owes to suppliers, both critical for managing cash flow. - Lease Financing (Medium-Term): - A company rents assets (e.g., machinery) instead of purchasing, reducing upfront costs and preserving capital for other uses, typically for 1-5 years. - Retained Earnings (Long-Term): - Profits reinvested into the business after distributing dividends, decided by the board of directors, used for expansion or debt repayment (e.g., reinvesting profits to open new branches). - Significance: - Efficient capital management enhances business sustainability and growth, critical for India’s economic development and job creation. - Securities: Definition and Types - Definition: - Securities are tradable financial instruments representing ownership (equity) or debt (bonds/debentures), facilitating capital raising in financial markets. - Types of Securities: -
Equity Securities Debt Securities
- Shares representing ownership in a company.
- Holders receive dividends from profits, but only if profits are made.
- Last claim during liquidation (residual claimants).
- Attractive during economic booms due to higher profit potential. 		- Bonds/debentures representing loans to the issuer.
- Holders receive fixed interest and principal, regardless of profits.
- First claim during liquidation.
- Attractive during slowdowns for assured returns.
- Note: - Bonds and debentures have technical differences (e.g., security backing), but for UPSC purposes, they are treated similarly as debt instruments. - Financial/Securities Market: Meaning and Types - Definition: - Financial markets are platforms (physical or virtual) where securities like bonds and shares are bought and sold, enabling capital flow between investors and issuers. - Types of Financial Markets: - By Tenure: - Money Market: - Trades short-term securities (<1 year), e.g., T-Bills, Commercial Papers, highly liquid and low-risk. - Capital Market: - Trades long-term securities (≥1 year), e.g., bonds, shares, facilitating long-term investments. - By Freshness: - Primary Market: - New securities are issued to raise fresh capital (e.g., Initial Public Offerings), connecting issuers with investors. - Secondary Market: - Existing securities are traded (e.g., BSE, NSE), providing liquidity and confidence to investors. - By Settlement: - Spot Market: - Securities are bought/sold for immediate delivery. - Future Market: - Contracts to buy/sell securities at a future date at a predetermined price. - By Asset: - Includes Bond Market, Share Market, G-Sec Market, Foreign Currency Market, Commodity Market. - Universal Exchange: - A platform (e.g., BSE, NSE since 2018) trading multiple asset types. - Significance: - Financial markets drive India’s economic growth by mobilizing savings, supporting infrastructure, and enhancing liquidity. - Debt Instruments: Overview - Definition: - Debt instruments are financial contracts where the borrower promises to repay the lender principal and interest, traded in money or capital markets. - Characteristics: - Bondholders are creditors with first claim during liquidation. - Assured interest payments, regardless of issuer’s profitability. - Classified into short-term (<1 year) and long-term (≥1 year) based on tenure. - Short-Term Debt Instruments - Definition: - Short-term debt instruments have a maturity of less than one year, typically unsecured, traded in the money market, and considered "near money" due to high liquidity. - Characteristics: - Sold at a discount and repurchased at face value, with the difference as interest. - Negotiable and transferable, allowing resale to third parties. - Types by Issuer: - Government: -
Instrument Issuer Tenure Details
Treasury Bills (T-Bills) Union Government 14, 91, 182, 364 days Issued at discount, no state T-Bills since 2001.
Cash Management Bills (CMBs) Union Government 91 days Addresses temporary cash mismatches.
Ways and Means Advances (WMA) RBI to Union/State 3 months Short-term loans at repo rate, not counted in fiscal deficit.
Overdraft RBI to Union/State Short-term At repo + 2-5%, used when WMA quota is exhausted.
Special Drawing Facility (SDF) RBI to States Short-term Loans against G-Secs/T-Bills as collateral, at repo - 1%.
- Companies/Merchants: - Bill of Exchange: - A written order for payment at a specified time (e.g., Hundi in historical India). - Commercial Papers: - Unsecured promissory notes issued by corporations for short-term financing. - Commercial Bills: - Bills issued by merchants for trade financing. - Certificates of Deposit (CDs): - Unsecured, negotiable instruments issued by banks, surged in 2023 due to deposit shortages and high repo rates. - Financial Intermediaries: - Call Money: - One-day interbank loans at a specified interest rate. - Notice Money: - Interbank loans for 2-14 days. - Collateralized Borrowing and Lending Obligation (CBLO): - Short-term loans facilitated by Clearing Corporation of India Ltd (CCIL). - Inter-Corporate Deposits (ICDs): - Unsecured short-term loans between companies. - Tri-Party Repo: - Repo transactions facilitated by a third-party agent (e.g., NSE, BSE), not a monetary policy tool. - Factoring and TReDS: - Factoring: - MSMEs sell unpaid invoices to banks/NBFCs for immediate cash, regulated by the Factoring Regulation (Amendment) Act, 2021, which allows all NBFCs to participate and mandates invoice registration to prevent fraud. - Trade Receivables Electronic Discounting System (TReDS): - Online platforms (e.g., RXIL, M1xchange) connecting MSMEs with financiers, owned by entities like SIDBI and NSE, enhancing loan access for small businesses. - Interest Rate Benchmarks: - LIBOR: - London Interbank Offered Rate, average rate for short-term interbank loans in London. - MIBOR: - Mumbai Interbank Offered Rate, India’s equivalent to LIBOR. - SORR: - Secured Overnight Rupee Rate, proposed by RBI’s Ramanathan Subramanian Committee (2024) as a new benchmark, calculated by Financial Benchmarks India Limited (FBIL). - Long-Term Debt Instruments - Definition: - Long-term debt instruments have a maturity of one year or more, used by governments and companies for significant investments, traded in capital markets. - By Issuer: - Government: -
Instrument Details
Government Securities (G-Secs) Long-term bonds, also called gilt-edged due to low default risk, rated ~BAA by global agencies.
State Development Loans (SDLs) Bonds issued by state governments for development projects.
Kisan Vikas Patra Savings certificates for retail investors, promoting financial inclusion.
- Colonial-Era Instruments: - Coupon Bonds: - Bonds with detachable coupons for claiming interest. - Zero Coupon Bonds: - Sold at discount, no periodic interest, repurchased at face value. - Bearer Bonds: - Unregistered bonds payable to the holder, used during wartime. - Special Purpose Bonds: - Inflation Indexed Bonds (IIBs): - Launched in 1997, 2004, 2013, 2018, protect principal and interest against inflation using CPI (post-2013), ensuring positive real interest rates. - Capital Indexed Bonds (CIBs): - Older variant of IIBs, used WPI, less relevant today. - Sovereign Gold Bonds (SGBs): - Introduced in 2015, denominated in gold grams, pay 2.5-2.75% interest, mature in 8 years with payout linked to gold prices, aimed at reducing physical gold imports. - Sovereign Green Bonds: - Issued via RBI’s E-Kuber platform, funds allocated to renewable energy, clean transport, and green buildings, reviewed by the Green Finance Working Committee. - Greenium: - Lower interest rates accepted by investors for sustainable projects, though Indian green bonds faced low uptake in 2025 due to transparency and liquidity issues. - Companies: - Bonds/Debentures: - Long-term debt instruments, rated by agencies (e.g., junk bonds for high-risk issuers like IL&FS). - Redeemable Bonds: - Pay interest and principal at maturity. - Irredeemable Bonds: - Pay only interest, often used by PSBs for Basel capital requirements, with redemption options after 5-10 years. - Secured vs. Unsecured: - Secured bonds are backed by assets; unsecured are not. - Non-Convertible Debentures (NCDs): - Cannot be converted into shares, offer higher interest (e.g., 12%). - Hybrid Instruments: - Optionally Fully Convertible Debentures (OFCDs): - Convertible into shares, used in Sahara Refund Portal (2023) for investor refunds. - Foreign Currency Convertible Bonds (FCCBs): - Issued in foreign currency (e.g., Tata’s $1 billion FCCB in 2010), convertible at a predetermined exchange rate, lower interest due to conversion option. - Contingent Convertible Bonds (CoCos)/Enhanced Capital Notes (ECNs): - Hybrid of AT1 bonds and OFCDs, convertible into shares or written off under specific conditions. - By Issuer (Special Types): - Urban Local Bodies (ULBs): - Municipal Bonds: - First issued by Ahmedabad (1996, Rs. 100 cr), Bengaluru (1997, Rs. 125 cr), Lucknow (2020, Rs. 100 cr), Ghaziabad (2021, green bonds), Indore (2023, retail green bonds), Ahmedabad (2024, green bonds). - Global Issuers: - BRICS Bonds: - Issued by New Development Bank (2014) for infrastructure, in US dollars. - World Bank’s Bond-i: - First blockchain-based bond (2018), issued in Australian dollars. - Masala Bonds: - Rupee-denominated bonds issued abroad (e.g., IFC, NHAI, Kerala’s KIIFB in 2019), high interest for lower-rated issuers. - Other Currency-Based Bonds: -
Bond Type Issuer Borrowing From Currency
