Dim Sum Bonds Non-Chinese Hong Kong Renminbi
Kangaroo Bonds Non-Australian Australia Australian Dollar
Maharaja Bonds Non-Indian India Rupee
Formosa Bonds Non-Taiwanese Taiwan Other than Taiwan Dollar
- Special Purpose Bonds: - Surety Bonds: - Regulated by IRDAI (2022), used for contract guarantees. - Blue Bonds: - For marine and fisheries projects (e.g., Seychelles, 2018). - ESG Bonds: - Invested in companies with strong environmental, social, and governance records. - Catastrophe Bonds: - High interest, principal at risk if a disaster occurs. - Consol Bonds: - Perpetual bonds with no maturity, proposed for India’s post-COVID recovery. - Elephant Bonds: - Proposed (2019) for black money investment in infrastructure, 25-year tenure. - Social Impact Bonds: - E.g., SIDBI’s Women’s Livelihood Bonds (2019), NSDC’s Skill Impact Bond (2021), NABARD’s Social Bonds (2023) for social projects. - Zero Coupon Zero Principal (ZCZP) Bonds: - Issued by NGOs/NPOs, no interest or principal repayment, akin to donations, regulated by SEBI. - Electoral Bonds - Definition: - Introduced in 2017 by the Finance Ministry, issued by SBI in denominations of Rs. 1,000 to Rs. 1 crore, for political donations. - Characteristics: - Physical, bearer, interest-free promissory notes, valid for 15 days. - Non-transferable, non-pledgeable, ensuring donor confidentiality. - Purchase Details: - Available for 10 days each quarter (January, April, July, October), extended during Lok Sabha elections. - Eligible buyers: - Indian citizens and companies, requiring KYC compliance. - Donations limited to political parties with ≥1% votes in recent elections. - Benefits: - Enhances transparency, reduces cash-based political funding, protects donor anonymity. - Controversy and Ban (2024): - Supreme Court banned electoral bonds in February 2024, citing potential corruption, violation of voter rights under Article 19(1)(a), and non-compliance with RPA, Companies Act, and Income Tax Act. - SBI ordered to disclose donor and recipient details (2019-2024) by March 2024. - Electoral Trusts (2013): - Allows companies to create trusts for anonymous political donations (e.g., Prudent Electoral Trust), not banned as of 2024. - Buffer Funds for Bond Repayment - Debenture Redemption Reserve (DRR): - Mandatory fund for companies issuing bonds, relaxed in 2019 for banks, NBFCs, and public-listed companies, with others at 10%. - Consolidated Sinking Fund (CSF): - State governments maintain for bond repayment; no clear data on Union government’s equivalent fund. - Miscellaneous Bond Types - Market-Linked Debentures/Bonds: - Interest tied to market indices (e.g., SENSEX, G-Sec yield). - Bond Yield and Related Concepts: - Yield: - Return on bond investment, covered in Pillar 1A2. - Yield Inversion and Negative Yield: - Outdated, less relevant for UPSC. - Pillar 1C2: Share Market and SEBI Regulations - Definition: - The share market is a regulated platform where securities like equity shares, bonds, derivatives, and other financial instruments are traded, enabling companies to raise capital and investors to gain ownership or returns. The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) oversees these markets to ensure transparency, investor protection, and financial stability. - Context: - India’s share market, primarily operated through the Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE) and National Stock Exchange (NSE), is a cornerstone of the economy, facilitating capital formation, wealth creation, and economic growth, with SEBI ensuring alignment with global standards. - Significance for India: - SEBI’s regulatory framework fosters investor confidence, curbs malpractices, and supports initiatives like Make in India and financial inclusion, positioning India as the 4th-largest equity market globally by January 2024 (ES25). - Equity Instruments - Definition: - Equity instruments represent ownership in a company, entitling holders to a share of profits (dividends) and residual assets during liquidation, primarily in the form of shares. - Characteristics: - Ownership: - Equity holders are proprietors with voting rights on corporate decisions (e.g., electing board members). - Dividends: - Receive dividends if the company earns profits, though not guaranteed. - Liquidation: - Last claim on assets after creditors and preference shareholders. - Types of Equity Investments: - Private Equity (PE): - Investments in unlisted companies by wealthy individuals or firms for growth or restructuring (e.g., PE funding in Byju’s). - Venture Capital (VC): - A subtype of PE focusing on early-stage startups with high growth potential (e.g., Ratan Tata’s investment in Urban Ladder). - Angel Investors: - High-net-worth individuals investing in startups for profit or passion, often in seed stages (e.g., angel funding in Ola). - Corporate Strategic Investors: - Corporations investing for technology, market access, or buyouts (e.g., Google’s investments in Indian startups). - Share Types by Risk/Quality: - Blue-Chip Shares: - Low-risk, high-quality shares of established companies (e.g., TCS, Reliance). - Growth Shares: - High-risk shares of companies with growth potential (e.g., tech startups). - Value Shares: - Undervalued shares with strong fundamentals (e.g., underperforming PSUs). - Penny Stocks: - High-risk, low-priced shares of small companies, prone to speculation. - Share Types: Floating vs Restricted Stocks: - Floating Stocks: - Shares available for public trading, enhancing market liquidity. - Restricted Stocks: - Shares held by insiders (e.g., promoters) with trading restrictions, often under SEBI-mandated lock-in periods post-IPO. - Share Types: Preference Shares vs Ordinary Shares - Definition: - Shares represent ownership units, divided into preference shares (fixed returns, priority in liquidation) and ordinary shares (variable returns, voting rights). - Comparison: -
Feature Preference Shares Ordinary Shares
Dividend Fixed rate (e.g., 10% on ₹100 = ₹10 annually) Variable, based on remaining profits
Liquidation Priority Paid before ordinary shareholders Paid after creditors and preference shareholders
Voting Rights Generally none, except in specific cases Full voting rights
Convertibility Convertible to ordinary shares if specified Non-convertible
Issuance Optional Mandatory for registered companies
Risk Lower, due to fixed returns Higher, due to variable returns
- Subtypes: - Preference Shares: Cumulative (unpaid dividends accumulate) and non-cumulative (unpaid dividends lapse). - Ordinary Shares: Also called equity shares, traded on BSE/NSE. - Shares for Employees and VIPs - Definition: - Special shares or options issued to employees and key personnel to incentivize performance and retention. - Types: - Sweet Equity: - Discounted shares for directors/employees (e.g., shares at ₹50 vs market price ₹100). - Sweat Equity: - Shares for non-cash contributions (e.g., intellectual property), regulated under Companies Act, 2013. - Employee Stock Option Plan (ESOP): - Allows employees to buy shares at a predetermined price (e.g., Infosys ESOPs). - Equity Warrants: - Options to buy shares at a future date, often issued to VIPs or strategic investors. - Significance: - Aligns employee and company goals, common in startups; SEBI regulates ESOP disclosures. - Account Types: Goodwill - Definition: - Goodwill is an intangible asset reflecting the premium paid for a company’s brand, reputation, or customer base during acquisitions (e.g., Tata’s acquisition of Jaguar Land Rover). - Significance: - Impacts valuation and financial statements; SEBI ensures transparent reporting in mergers/acquisitions. - Valuation of Companies - Definition: - Valuation determines a company’s worth using financial metrics, critical for investments, IPOs, and mergers. - Methods: - Price-to-Earnings (P/E) Ratio: - Share price divided by earnings per share (EPS), indicating growth expectations. - Discounted Cash Flow (DCF): - Estimates future cash flows discounted to present value. - Book Value: - Net asset value (total assets minus liabilities) divided by shares. - Market-Based Valuation: - Reflects supply-demand-driven stock prices. - Startup Valuation (Unicorns): - Unicorns are startups valued at $1 billion+ (e.g., Byju’s, Paytm), using venture capital or comparable company analysis. - Listed Companies: - Small Cap (< ₹5,000 cr), Mid Cap (₹5,000–20,000 cr), Large Cap (> ₹20,000 cr). - Reliance Industries hit ₹20 lakh crore market cap in February 2024, a milestone (ES25). - Market Froth & Asset Bubble: - Excessive speculation in small/mid-cap stocks can lead to bubbles, as cautioned by ES25. - Tulip Mania: - Definition: - A speculative bubble (1634–1637) in the Netherlands where tulip bulb prices soared (e.g., Semper Augustus at 10,000 guilders) and collapsed in 1637. - Context: - Driven by herd behavior and futures contracts, it’s a cautionary tale for market froth (e.g., small-cap bubbles). - Relevance: - Parallels modern bubbles (e.g., Bitcoin, dot-com); SEBI’s circuit breakers and regulations prevent such excesses. - Volatility/ViX: - Volatility measures price fluctuations; India VIX (NSE) indicates market uncertainty, guiding SEBI’s circuit breaker policies. - Other Metrics: - Leverage Ratio: - Debt relative to assets/equity. - Debt to Equity Ratio: - Total debt divided by shareholders’ equity. - ROCE: - Return on capital employed, measuring profitability. - Net Worth: - Total assets minus liabilities. - EBITDA: - Earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. - Depreciation: - Allocation of tangible asset costs over time. - Amortization: - Allocation of intangible asset costs (e.g., goodwill). - Significance: - SEBI mandates transparent disclosure of valuation metrics to protect investors. - Methods of Issuing Shares - Definition: - Companies issue shares to raise capital, regulated by SEBI to ensure transparency. - Methods: - IPO: - Initial Public Offering, first-time share sale to the public. - FPO: - Follow-on Public Offering, additional share issuance by listed companies. - QIP: - Qualified Institutional Placement, shares issued to institutional investors. - Offer for Sale (OFS): - Promoters sell shares to the public. - Share Pledging: - Using shares as loan collateral. - Open Offer: - Mandatory offer during takeovers. - Equity Dilution: - Ownership percentage reduction due to new shares. - Bonus Shares: - Free shares to existing shareholders. - Share Splitting: - Dividing shares to increase liquidity. - Share Buyback: - Company repurchasing shares to boost value. - SPAC: - Special Purpose Acquisition Company, raising funds for acquisitions. - Direct Listing (2023): - Indian companies listing on foreign exchanges (e.g., GIFT City’s IFSC). - ICO: - Initial Coin Offering, unregulated crypto fundraising. - Other Terms: - Underwriters/Lead Managers: - Banks ensuring IPO/FPO success. - Grey Market: - Unofficial pre-listing trading of IPO shares. - Share-Swapping: - Exchanging shares during mergers. - De-listing: - Removing shares from exchanges. - Significance: - SEBI regulates issuance to prevent fraud and ensure investor access (e.g., UPI-ASBA reforms). - Stock Exchanges/Secondary Market - Definition: - Stock exchanges (e.g., BSE, NSE) facilitate trading of listed securities, ensuring liquidity and price discovery. - Major Exchanges: - BSE: - Asia’s oldest, hosts Sensex (30 companies). - NSE: - Largest by volume, hosts Nifty 50. - Social Stock Exchange (SSE): - Platform for non-profits to raise funds via zero-coupon, zero-principal instruments. - DEMAT and Depositories: - DEMAT accounts hold securities electronically; - depositories (NSDL, CDSL) manage them. - ASBA (Applications Supported by Blocked Amount): - Locks funds for IPO applications. - UPI-ASBA (2024): - Enables UPI-based applications in secondary markets. - ISIN Number: - Unique identifier for securities. - CAS System: - Consolidated account statement for transactions. - Significance: - SEBI ensures transparent trading and investor protection. - Central Counterparties (CCPs) - Definition: - CCPs (e.g., BSE’s ICCL) act as intermediaries, guaranteeing trade settlements. - Regulation: - SEBI oversees CCPs; ESMA (European regulator) controversy (2023) involved recognition disputes. - Significance: - Reduces counterparty risk, enhancing market stability. - Types of Investors - Definition: - Investors are categorized by capacity, behavior, and trading style, influencing market dynamics. - By Buying Capacity: - Retail Investors: - Individuals with limited funds (e.g., SIP investors). - Institutional Investors: - FIIs/FPIs, mutual funds, banks. - Accredited Investors: - High-net-worth individuals meeting SEBI criteria. - By Buying Behavior: - Value Investors: - Seek undervalued stocks (e.g., ELSS). - Growth Investors: - Target high-growth companies (e.g., small-caps). - Disposition Effect: - Tendency to sell winners early, hold losers. - By Trading Style: - Day Traders: - Trade within a day. - Long-Term Investors: - Hold for years. - Retail vs Proprietary Traders: - Retail: - Individual traders; Proprietary: Firms trading own capital. - Significance: - SEBI’s investor charter (2021) promotes participation and protection across investor types. - SENSEX and Other Indices - Definition: - Indices like Sensex (BSE, 30 companies) and Nifty 50 (NSE) reflect market performance. - Movement: - Rises with positive economic news, corporate earnings; falls with crises (e.g., Yen carry trade, 2024). - Force Majeure: - Unforeseen events (e.g., disasters) halting trading, regulated by SEBI. - Significance: - Indices guide investor sentiment; SEBI ensures transparent calculations. - Sharemarket 2020–2024 - India as 4th-Largest Equity Market (2024-Jan): - Surpassed Hong Kong, driven by IPOs and retail participation (ES25). - FPI Exit Impact (ES25): - Limited impact due to strong domestic investment. - US Sharemarket Crisis (ES25): - Potential US volatility affects India via FPI flows. - India-US Market Relation: - Correlated due to global capital flows; SEBI monitors FPI activities. - Market Theories - Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH): - Prices reflect all available information, making consistent outperformance difficult. - Random Walk Theory: - Prices move randomly, unpredictable by past trends. - Alpha and Beta Values: - Alpha: - Excess returns compared to a benchmark (e.g., Nifty). - Beta: - Stock volatility relative to the market (Beta > 1 = higher risk). - Analysis Types: - Fundamental: - Evaluates financials; Technical: Analyzes price patterns. - DEMAT Portfolio Diversification: - Spreading investments to reduce risk, promoted by SEBI. - SEBI and Reforms - Definition: - SEBI, established under the SEBI Act, 1992, regulates securities markets, protecting investors and ensuring stability. - Key Functions: - Investor Protection: - Enforces disclosures, prevents insider trading. - Market Regulation: - Oversees exchanges, brokers, and companies. - Market Development: - Promotes financial literacy, innovation (e.g., REITs). - Reforms: - T+1 Settlement (2023): - Reduced settlement cycle to one day. - T+1 Hour/T+0 (2024): - Piloting instantaneous settlements. - Circuit Breakers: - Halts trading during extreme volatility. - Investor Protection Fund (IPF): - Compensates investors for broker defaults. - Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF): - Ensures trade settlements. - Illegal Trading: - Curbs dabba trading, insider trading, front running. - Algo Trading & Co-Location: - Regulates high-frequency trading (TAP case). - SEBI LAMA: - IT monitoring framework for market surveillance. - IRRA Portal (2023): - Investor Risk Reduction Access for broker failures. - Adani-Hindenburg (2023): - SEBI investigated short-selling allegations. - Finfluencers: - Regulates financial influencers to prevent misinformation. - Investor Charter (2021): - Promotes retail investor participation; mandates 25% public holding in PSUs. - Market Insights (ES25): - Capital market deepened, but debt/bond market lags. - G-Sec Trading - Definition: - Government Securities (G-Secs) are debt instruments issued by RBI, traded via Retail Direct Gilt (RDG) and NDS-OM platforms. - Key Terms: - Bid-Ask Spread: - Difference between buying and selling prices. - Arbitrage: - Profiting from price differences across markets. - Yen Carry Trade (2024): - Borrowing in low-interest Yen to invest in India, causing market volatility. - Significance: - SEBI/RBI promote retail G-Sec trading for financial inclusion. - Investment Funds - Mutual Funds: - Types: - Open-ended (flexible redemption), Close-ended (fixed term), Hybrid (equity-debt mix), ESG, Shariya-compliant, ELSS (tax-saving). - Investment Modes: - Lumpsum or Systematic Investment Plan (SIP). - Risk-o-Meter: - SEBI’s tool to indicate fund risk levels. - Backstop Entity (Proposed): - Supports stressed mutual funds. - Hedge Funds: - High-risk funds for accredited investors, using complex strategies. - REITs/InvITs: - Real estate/infrastructure trusts for retail/institutional investors. - Sovereign Wealth Funds: - State-owned funds investing globally (e.g., GIC of Singapore). - ETFs: - Track indices/commodities (e.g., Bharat Bond ETF, Gold ETF). - Bitcoin ETF (USA, 2024): Approved, not in India. - CPSE ETF: - Tracks public sector enterprises. - AIFs: - Alternative Investment Funds for real estate, startups (e.g., 2019 real estate AIF). - Forward/Future Contracts & Call/Put Options - Definition: - Futures: - Contracts for future delivery at set prices (e.g., wheat futures). - Options: - Right to buy (call) or sell (put) at a set price. - Significance: - Enable risk management; SEBI regulates to prevent speculation. - Over-Financialization & Sachetisation - Over-Financialization: - Excessive financial sector growth harms productivity (ES25), risking bubbles. - Bell-Shaped Relationship (ES25): - Moderate financialization boosts GDP in developing economies (e.g., India); over-financialization harms growth. - Sachetisation: - Small-ticket F&O trading increases retail losses, cautioned by ES24. - Derivatives and Swaps - Definition: - Derivatives derive value from underlying assets (e.g., shares, commodities). - Types: - Futures, Options, Currency Swaps, Credit Default Swaps (CDS), Interest Rate Swaps. - Securitization: - Pooling assets to create securities (e.g., NHB’s Mortgage-Backed Securities). - Participatory Notes (P-Notes) - Definition: - Offshore derivative instruments issued by FIIs/FPIs for investing in Indian securities without SEBI registration. - Risks: - Anonymity enables tax evasion, money laundering. - SEBI Regulations: - Restricts issuance by Category I FPIs; Category II FPIs deposit $1,000. - Tax on Investment/Financial Assets - Taxable Assets: - Bank savings interest, G-Secs, shares, bonds, futures, options, P-Notes, cryptocurrencies (30% tax + 1% TDS). - Tax-Exempt Assets: -

- Discontinued: - RGESS (2012–2017): Tax benefits for first-time equity investors. - Commodity Market - Definition: - Trades bulk goods (e.g., grains, metals, energy); SEBI regulates since FMC merger (2015). - Gold Exchanges (Budget 2021): - SEBI regulates; - WDRA oversees warehousing. - Electronic Gold Receipts (EGRs): - Issued for gold deposited in WDRA warehouses, traded on exchanges. - India International Bullion Exchange (IIBX, 2022): - First bullion exchange at GIFT City, trading gold/silver. - Financial Stability and Development Council (FSDC, 2010) - Definition: - Non-statutory body chaired by the Finance Minister, coordinating regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA). - Functions: - Supervises conglomerates, promotes financial literacy/inclusion. - Other Financial Bodies - FSB (2009, Basel): - G20 body for global financial monitoring; India has three seats. - FATF (1989, Paris): - Combats money laundering; India joined 2010. - IOSCO: - Global securities regulator body; SEBI is a member. - FSAP: - IMF-World Bank survey (2011–12 for India), covering Basel-III, derivatives. - NISM (2006, Mumbai): - SEBI’s educational institute, expanded in Budget 2023. - Corporate Governance - Definition: - Rules ensuring ethical, transparent company operations, mandated by Companies Act, 2013, and SEBI. - Provisions: - Independent directors, woman director, e-voting, whistleblower protection. - Uday Kotak Committee (2017): - Enhanced governance standards. - NSE Prime (2022): - Voluntary high governance standards for NSE-listed companies. - CSR: - 2% of average profits for social causes (e.g., education, health). - Small Companies (Budget 2021): - Paid-up capital ≤ ₹2 crore, turnover ≤ ₹20 crore, with relaxed compliance. - Company Types - By Incorporation: - Chartered: - By royal charter (e.g., East India Company). - Statutory: - By parliamentary acts (e.g., RBI, LIC). - Registered: - Under Companies Act, 2013 (e.g., Tata Motors). - By Members: -

- By Ownership: - Public Sector: - Government owns ≥51% (e.g., SAIL). - Private Sector: - Private entities own ≥51% (e.g., Reliance). - Holding/Subsidiary: - Tata Sons (holding) owns TCS (subsidiary). - Miscellaneous: - OPC: - Single-member company, eased for NRIs (Budget 2021). - Not-for-Profit: - Reinvests profits (e.g., GSTN). - Dormant: - Inactive or for future use. - LLP: - Shields partners from joint liability (LLP Act, 2008). - Partnership: - Joint liability under Partnership Act, 1932. - Statutory Bodies under Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA) - CCI: - Prevents cartelization, monopolies (Competition Act, 2002). - NCLT: - Handles company law, insolvency cases. - NFRA: - Sets auditing standards for listed companies. - IEPF: - Manages unclaimed shares/dividends; IT portal (Budget 2023). - SFIO: - Investigates financial frauds. - ICAI, ICSI, ICMAI: - Regulate CAs, company secretaries, cost accountants. - MCA Initiatives: - MCA21, SPICe+: - E-governance for company compliance. - Central Data Processing Centre (Budget 2023): - Enhances ease of doing business. - Takeover-Related Terms - Friendly Takeover: - Board-approved (e.g., Ranbaxy-Sun Pharma). - Hostile Takeover: - Without board consent (e.g., Musk-Twitter, 2022). - Leveraged Buy-Out (LBO): - Using borrowed funds (e.g., Musk-Twitter). - Poison Pill: - Defensive share issuance to deter takeovers. - De-Merger: - Dividing a company (e.g., ITC Hotels split). - Pillar 1D1: Insurance, Pension & Financial Inclusion - Definition: - Insurance is a legal contract where an insurer compensates an insured party for losses due to specified risks (e.g., death, damage, illness) in exchange for premiums, promoting financial stability and inclusion. - Significance: - Provides security to households against death, disability, or property damage, and to entrepreneurs against risks like fire or natural disasters, supporting economic resilience. - Insurance companies channel premiums into public and private sector investments, fostering capital formation and economic growth, aligning with India’s financial inclusion goals (e.g., Jan Dhan Yojana). - Insurance Principles - Uberrima Fides: - Principle of utmost good faith; insured must disclose all material facts (e.g., HIV status) to avoid policy invalidation. - Indemnity: - Compensation limited to actual financial loss, not imaginary losses (e.g., cannot claim for missed opportunities like IIM admission due to fire-damaged books). - Subrogation: - Insurer can recover compensation paid from a negligent third party (e.g., recovering from a boiler manufacturer for a factory fire). - Causa Proxima: - Covers losses directly linked to the insured event (e.g., cannot insure against emotional distress from a celebrity’s death). - Insurable Interest: - Insured must have a financial stake; loss impacts their financial position (e.g., cannot insure a lottery ticket’s failure). - History of Insurance in India - 1818: - European insurers charged higher premiums for Indian clients, reflecting colonial biases. - 1870: - Bombay Mutual Life Insurance, the first Swadeshi life insurance company, offered equitable premiums. - 1912: - Life Insurance Companies Act introduced lax regulations, inadequate during the Great Depression. - 1938: - Insurance Act enforced stricter norms to address financial instability. - Post-Independence: - Nationalization addressed scams and supported financial inclusion: - 1956: - LIC Act nationalized ~245 private life insurance companies. - 1972: - GIC Act nationalized ~107 private general insurance companies, forming GIC and four subsidiaries (National Insurance, New India Assurance, United India, Oriental). - Reforms: - 1993: - Malhotra Committee recommended private sector entry and FDI liberalization. - 1999: - IRDAI established as a statutory regulator. - 2025: - Dinesh Khara Committee proposed reforms to the Insurance Act, 1938. - Safeguards: - Investment norms mandate minimum investment in G-Secs and limit exposure to low-rated private companies. - Rural and social obligation norms require selling policies to rural/marginalized groups and investing in affordable housing. - Delivery Channels: - Agents, brokers, bancassurance, surveyors/loss assessors, third-party administrators (e.g., hospitals). - Life Insurance Types - Definition: - Life insurance provides financial protection against death or disability, with premiums varying by policy type. - Types: -
Type Duration Return at Maturity Return at Death
Whole Life Long-term (e.g., 35–40 years) Savings with interest Yes
Endowment Short-term (e.g., 10–20 years) Savings with interest Yes
Term Life Short-term, low premium (e.g., PMJJB) No Yes
ULIP Variable Savings with mutual fund profits Yes
- ULIP (Unit Linked Insurance Plan): - Combines life insurance with investment in mutual funds, offering dual benefits but with higher risk due to market linkage (ES25 notes insurance gap risk). - IRDAI Reform: Surrender Value - Definition: - Surrender value is the refund paid if a policyholder discontinues a whole-life or endowment policy after 1–2 years. - Reform (2024): -
Period Surrender Value
Before 2024 Zero or minimal
After 2024 80–85% of premium refunded
- Life Insurance: Public Sector - Postal Life Insurance (PLI): - Started 1884; Rural PLI (1995) for villagers (e.g., Gram Suraksha, Gram Suvidha). - Eligibility: - Public sector employees, professionals (doctors, engineers), listed company employees. - DARPAN PLI App: - Facilitates digital enrollment and premium payments. - Post Office Services: - G2C: - Application forms for PMFBY, PMJAY, NPS, PAN eKYC, e-Stamp. - B2C: - Bill payments (Bharat Bill Payment System), premium collection for life/general insurance. - Life Insurance Corporation of India (LIC) - Establishment: - LIC Act, 1956; statutory corporation, nationalized ~245 private insurers. - HQ: - Mumbai; - Magazine: - Yogakshema; - Motto: - “Yogakshemam Vahamyaham” (Gita, ensuring safety). - Regulation: - Governed by LIC Act (1956), Insurance Act (1938), IRDAI Act (1999). - Aam Aadmi Bima Yojana (AABY, 2007): - For BPL/APL citizens (e.g., carpenters, cobblers). - Benefits: - ₹30,000 (death), ₹37,500–75,000 (disability), ₹1,200/year child scholarship (Class 9–12, ITI). - Premium: - ₹200 (₹100 Union, ₹100 beneficiary/state). - Disinvestment (2020): - Budget 2020 amended LIC Act for IPO; 2022 SEBI-approved IPO sold 5% government shares (see Pillar 1C2). - PM Schemes for Insurance (2015) -

- Auto-debited premium from bank accounts. - General Insurance - Definition: - Covers non-life risks (e.g., accident, health, crop, fire, vehicle, marine). - Public Sector Entities: - 1948: - ESIC (Labour Ministry) for workers; ECGC (Commerce Ministry) for exporters. - 1961: - DICGC (not a general insurer, provides deposit insurance). - 1972: - GIC Act nationalized ~107 companies; GIC and four subsidiaries. - 2002: - Agriculture Insurance Company Ltd. (GIC, subsidiaries, NABARD). - 2018: - Budget proposed merging National, United, Oriental (not implemented by 2025). - General Insurance Business (Nationalisation) Amendment Bill, 2021: - Removed 51% government shareholding rule, enabling privatization. - Pros: - Enhances professionalism, profitability, customer responsiveness. - Cons: - Shifted to Mains handout. - Seasonal Establishment and Casual Worker -

- Employees’ State Insurance Corporation (ESIC, 1948) - Established under ESIC Act, 1948; Labour Ministry. - Applicability: - Pre-2020: - Non-seasonal establishments (10+ employees), hazardous industries (voluntary if <10). - Post-2020: - Compulsory for hazardous industries regardless of size; non-hazardous <10 remains voluntary. - Subscribers: - Employees with salary < ₹21,000/month. - Premium: - Shared by employee and employer (exact % not critical for UPSC). - Benefits: - Medical insurance (worker/family, from day 1). - Maternity benefits for women. - Pension for family (employment-related death, extended to COVID deaths in 2020–21). - Sickness benefit (partial wages during medical leave). - Disability payment. - Unemployment allowance (Atal Bimit Vyakti Kalyan Yojana). - One IP-Two Dispensaries: - Migrant workers access treatment at different locations. - E-Pehchan: - Digital ID cards. - Abhiyan Indradhanush: - Daily bedsheet changes in ESIC hospitals. - ESIC 2.0/Nirman Se Shakti: - Modernizes hospital infrastructure. - Project Panchdeep: - Digitization by WIPRO (2017). - Investment Reform (2022): - Pre-2022: - Premiums in FDs, G-Secs, bonds. - Post-2022: - Up to 15% in equities for higher returns, 85% in fixed instruments. - General Health Insurance Schemes - Standalone Health Insurers (SAHI): - Focus solely on health insurance (e.g., Star Health, Niva Bupa). - Policy Types: - Individual vs Floater: -

- Subtypes: - Cashless (free at empanelled hospitals) vs Non-Cashless (reimbursement). - Corona Kavach vs Rakshak: - Kavach: - Indemnity-based, ₹4,848 premium, up to ₹5 lakh coverage. - Rakshak: - Fixed benefit, ₹700 premium, ₹50,000 payout. - Arogya Sanjeevani & Saral Jeevan Bima: - Standardized policies by public/private insurers; low-cost, basic coverage. - Niramya Health Insurance: - For persons with disabilities, by Oriental Insurance (Dept. of Empowerment of PwD). - Premium: - ₹250–500; Coverage: Up to ₹1 lakh. - Ayushman Bharat / PM Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY, 2018) - Components: - Health & Wellness Centres: - 1.5 lakh PHCs transformed for free drugs, checkups, mother-child care. - PMJAY: - National Health Protection Scheme, renamed with motto “Swasthya Aapka, Saath Hamara.” - PMJAY Details: - Free ₹5 lakh/family/year for secondary/tertiary hospitalization. - Covers pre-existing diseases from day 1, 3 days pre-hospitalization, 15 days post-hospitalization. - Cashless, paperless access via NITI Aayog portal. - Beneficiaries: - ~50 crore people (8 crore rural, 2 crore urban, SECC data). - No cap on family size or age. - Treatment at public and empanelled private hospitals. - Pradhan Mantri Aarogya Mitras (PMAMs): Trained by Skill Development Ministry to assist patients. - Sub-Schemes: - PM SEHAT (2020): - Extends ₹5 lakh coverage to all 21 lakh J&K residents. - Ayushman CAPF (2021): - Covers CAPF forces (BSF, CRPF, etc.) and families. - Senior Citizens (2024): - All aged 70+ eligible for ₹5 lakh coverage, even with private insurance/ESIC. - Poor families’ seniors get additional ₹5 lakh top-up. - National Health Authority (NHA): - Restructured as an authority (2019), under Health Ministry. - Oversees PMJAY implementation, guidelines, and collaboration with insurers. - Governing Board: Chaired by Health Minister, includes NITI Aayog CEO, state representatives. - Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission: - Digital storage of health records (see Pillar 6). - State Health Agency (SHA): - Each state forms SHA for PMJAY implementation. - Funding: -

- Post-Article 370 (2019), J&K as UT with legislature gets 60:40; proposed “Hill UT” may retain 90:10. - Challenges: - Shifted to Mains handout. - Hospital Funding: - PPP model, funded by health cess on imported medical devices (see Pillar 2). - General Insurance: Non-Health - Pradhan Mantri Fasal Bima Yojana (PMFBY, 2016): - Nodal: - Agriculture Ministry; implemented by public/private insurers. - Covers natural calamities, pests, diseases, pre/post-harvest losses. - Premium: - Rabi (1.5%), Kharif (2%), Horticulture/Commercial (5%); balance by Union/State. - Reforms (2020): - Voluntary for farmers (previously mandatory with crop loans). - Union contribution reduced (25–30%), states’ burden increased; NE states retain 90:10. - Updated crop loss assessment methodology. - Tech Solutions: - WINDS Portal: - Gram Panchayat-level weather data. - AIDE/Sahayak App: - Door-to-door farmer enrollment. - YES-Tech Manual: - Crop damage assessment procedure. - Other Agriculture Schemes: - Restructured Weather-Based Crop Insurance Scheme (RWBCIS, 2016): - Covers weather risks only. - National Agricultural Insurance Scheme: - Subsumed by PMFBY. - NIRVIK Scheme (2019): - By ECGC (Commerce Ministry); covers 90% of exporters’ loan defaults (previously 60%). - Third-Party Motor Insurance: - Mandatory under Motor Vehicles Act, 1988; covers victim compensation; IRDAI regulates premiums. - Own Damage (OD) Insurance: - Protects vehicle owners against theft, vandalism, accidents, fire. - Road Accident Victims (2024): - Pilot in Chandigarh; cashless treatment up to ₹1.5 lakh/accident. - Nodal: - Ministry of Road Transport; implemented by NHA. - Tech Add-Ons for Motor OD: - IRDAI allows “pay as you drive,” “pay how you drive” for two-wheelers/cars. - De-tariffication (China, ES23): -

- Title Insurance: - Protects property buyers from title disputes; mandatory for builders under RERA, 2016. - Clinical Trial Liability Insurance: - Covers pharma companies’ legal costs for clinical trial damages. - Bharat Griha Raksha, Sookshma/Laghu Udyam Suraksha: - Standardized fire and perils policies (from 2021) for homes/MSMEs, covering fire, riots, terrorism up to ₹50 crore. - Property Insurance: -

- Catastrophe Insurance (Proposed): - Covers natural/manmade disasters; aims to reduce government compensation burden. - Catastrophe/Surety Bonds: - See Pillar 1C2 for details. - Cyber Insurance: - Covers malware, phishing, data theft, ransomware; demand surged post-COVID (e.g., Bajaj Allianz, HDFC ERGO). - e-Insurance Account (eIA): - Digital storage of policies (e.g., NSDL’s NIR), similar to DEMAT for shares. - Re-Insurance - Definition: - Insurance for insurers to mitigate large-scale risks; mandatory under Insurance Act, 1938. - Evolution: - Pre-2015: - GIC monopoly. - Post-2015: - Private (e.g., ITI Reinsurance) and foreign reinsurers (e.g., Swiss Re, Lloyds) allowed. - Benefits: - Breaks GIC monopoly, reduces costs, enables innovative products. - Pandemic Risk Pool (Proposed): - Fund by insurers/government to cover future pandemic losses. - IRDAI: Insurance Sector Regulator - Establishment: - 1996 as IRDA; statutory status in 1999; renamed IRDAI in 2014; HQ: Hyderabad. - Structure: - 1 Chairman (5 years/65 age), 9 members (5 years/62 age), re-appointable. - Functions: - Licenses life, general, re-insurance companies. - Sets norms for accounting, solvency, audits, agent commissions. - Penalizes violations; appeals to Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT). - Regulates intermediaries (agents, brokers, bancassurance, hospitals). - Handles consumer grievances via Insurance Ombudsman. - Member of FSDC (see Pillar 1C2). - Domestic Systemically Important Insurers (D-SIIs): - “Too big to fail” insurers (LIC, GIC, New India Assurance) under stricter monitoring. - Reforms (2024): - Senior Citizens/HIV-Cancer Patients: -

Cancer/HIV Patients Buying Policy Often rejected Cannot be rejected

- Premium Hike Regulation: - 2025: - Caps senior citizen premium increases at 10%/year due to rising hospital costs (14%) and air quality issues. - 30-Day Free-Look Period: - Allows policy review/refund without penalty within 30 days. - Bima Sakhi (LIC): - Women agents (18–70, 10th pass) for rural sales; stipend ₹5,000–7,000 for 3 years, plus commissions. - Bima Vahak (2023–24): - Village-level salespersons selling multiple insurers’ policies, assisting with KYC/claims. - Bima Vistaar (Proposed, 2024): - All-in-one policy (life, health, property); guidelines pending. - Bima Sugam: - Digital platform for comparing/buying/renewing policies, KYC via Aadhaar. - National Health Claim Exchange (NHCX): - Connects hospitals/insurers for faster claim processing (within 3 hours). - Insurance for All (2024): - Mandates coverage targets: -

Other Insurance Companies 49% 74% (2021), 100% (2025, premium invested in India)

- Safeguards (2021): - Majority directors and key management must be resident Indians. - 50% directors independent. - Specified profit percentage retained as general reserve. - Arguments for/against FDI: Shifted to Mains. - Parliamentary Committee Suggestions (2023) - Composite License: - Current: - Separate licenses for life, general, health insurance. - Proposed: - Single license for all, reducing costs for companies and documentation for clients (not implemented by 2025). - GST Reduction: - Current: - 18% GST on insurance products. - Proposed: - Reduce to 5% (not implemented). - Insurance Intermediaries - Agent vs Broker: -
Type Role
Agent Sells policies of one company (e.g., LIC agent)
Broker Sells policies of multiple companies (e.g., Policybazaar)
- Types: - Direct Broker: - Sells life/general/health policies. - Reinsurance Broker: - Sells reinsurance policies. - Composite Broker: - Handles both. - Bancassurance: - Banks selling insurance policies. - Pillar 1D2: Pension & Financial Inclusion - Definition: - Pension: - A monthly payment to a retired person (post-58/60) or their spouse upon death, ceasing thereafter, unlike insurance which compensates for death/damage (e.g., PMJJB’s ₹2 lakh). - Financial Inclusion: - Ensures access to pensions, insurance, and banking for all, especially unorganized and poor sections, aligning with Jan Dhan Yojana goals. - Significance: - Provides post-retirement financial security, reducing poverty among the elderly. - Channels savings into investments (e.g., G-Secs, bonds), supporting economic growth. - Employee Provident Fund Organisation (EPFO) - Establishment: - Set up in 1951–52 via ordinance, later EPF & Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952; nodal: Labour Ministry; HQ: New Delhi. - Governance: - Central Board of Trustees (tripartite): 15 government nominees (Union + States), 10 employer nominees, 10 employee nominees. - Decides investment allocations (G-Secs > corporate bonds > shares, within prescribed slabs) and subscriber interest rates. - Applicability: - Covers establishments with 20+ employees; excludes cooperatives with <50 workers not using power (e.g., electricity, coal). - Subscriber Identity: - Universal Account Number (UAN): - Unique, portable ID across job changes. - 2019 Reform: - Employees can generate UAN online using Aadhaar and mobile number, bypassing employer dependency. - Employer Identity: - Labour Identification Number (LIN): - Used by employers for EPFO transactions on Shram Suvidha portal (Labour Ministry). - Transaction Platforms: - Public/private banks and MeitY’s UMANG App (Unified Mobile Application for New-age Governance). - EPFO Schemes - Employee Provident Fund (EPF, 1952): - Principal + interest returned at retirement (age 58) or death. - Interest Rates: - 8.5% (2020–21), 8.1% (2022–23, 40-year low), 8.25% (2023–24, 2024–25). - Partial Withdrawal: - Up to a percentage allowed for education, marriage, illness, house construction. - Employees Deposit Linked Insurance Scheme (EDLI, 1976): - Employer pays premium; family receives insurance if worker dies. - 2020 Reform: - Extended to COVID-related deaths (₹2.5–7 lakh coverage). - Employee Pension Scheme (EPS, 1995): - Monthly pension post-retirement (age 58) or permanent disability. - Not linked to share market performance, unlike NPS. - If worker dies pre-retirement, spouse gets minimum ₹1,000/month pension. - EPF Commutation (2020): - Allows partial pension withdrawal before age 58; reduces subsequent pension amount. - Centralized Pension Payments System (CITES-CPPS): - IT modernization project; centralizes pension disbursal. - Pre-CITES: - Retirees visited banks for physical verification; zonal EPFO offices tied to 3–4 banks, causing issues for pensioners without accounts in those banks. - Post-CITES: - All banks connected online; no need for physical verification or zonal office visits for Pension Payment Order (PPO) updates. - EPFO 3.0 (2025): - Upgraded digital portal, akin to e-banking. - Features: - ATM withdrawals, online updates (e.g., address), complaint filing without visiting EPFO offices. - Government Schemes for EPFO Contribution - Pradhan Mantri Rojgar Protsahan Yojana (PMRPY, 2016): - Outdated (deleted from 2023 handout); government contributed to EPFO for new hires by factory owners. - Atmanirbhar Bharat Rozgar Yojana (ABRY, 2020): - Launched under Atmanirbhar Bharat 3.0 (Nov 2020); nodal: Labour Ministry. - Eligibility: - Workers earning <₹15,000/month, either new EPFO-registered hires or those who lost jobs (Mar–Sep 2020) and rejoined post-Oct 2020. - Benefits: -
Firm Size Worker Contribution Employer Contribution
Up to 1,000 employees 12% of wages (Govt to EPFO) 12% of wages (Govt to EPFO)
>1,000 employees 12% of wages (Govt to EPFO) 12% (Employer pays)
- Outcomes: - Formal job creation, increased worker income, post-COVID economic revival. - Validity: - Registration till Jun 2021; benefits for 2 years (e.g., Jan 2021–Jan 2023). - Employment Linked Incentive Scheme (ELI, Budget 2024): - Nodal: - Labour Ministry; Target: >2 crore jobs in 2 years. - ELI-Scheme A (First Timers, >2 crore jobs): - First-time EPFO employees get ₹15,000 DBT (one month’s salary) in three installments. - Excludes employees earning >₹1 lakh/month. - ELI-Scheme B (Manufacturing, 30 lakh jobs): - Government contributes to EPFO for worker and employer for 4 years for new manufacturing jobs (details pending). - ELI-Scheme C (Employer Support, 50 lakh jobs): - For jobs paying up to ₹1 lakh/month, government contributes up to ₹3,000/month to worker’s EPFO for 2 years. - Coal Miners’ Pension (CMPFO): - Coal Mines Provident Fund & Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1948; nodal: - Coal Ministry. - Coal Mines Provident Fund Organisation (CMPFO): - Statutory body, separate from EPFO. - C-CARES Portal: - Facilitates pension access for coal miners/pensioners. - FAQ: Why Not Merge EPFO with ESIC? - Separate laws (EPF Act, 1952 vs ESIC Act, 1948); merger requires legislative changes. - Pension for Government Employees - Types: - Old Pension Scheme (OPS): - Defined benefit, fixed pension; phased out for new hires post-2004. - National Pension System (NPS): - Defined contribution, market-linked; introduced 2004. - Unified Pension Scheme (UPS): - Hybrid model, introduced 2024. - National Pension System (NPS, 2004): - For government employees joining post-01/01/2004 (except armed forces); opened to all citizens (18–55) in 2009. - Mechanism: - Employee: - 10% of basic pay; Government: 14% (since Interim Budget 2019). - Funds managed by PFRDA via NPS Trust, invested in G-Secs, bonds, shares based on subscriber preference (Tier 1/Tier 2 accounts). - Subscriber ID: - Permanent Retirement Account Number (PRAN), akin to EPFO’s UAN. - T V Somanathan Committee (2023): - Reviewed NPS for government employees; proposed UPS. - Unified Pension Scheme (UPS, 2024): - Notified Aug 2024, effective 01/04/2025; optional for government employees (stay in NPS or switch to UPS). - Contribution: -
Contributor NPS UPS
Employer (Govt) 14% 18.5%
Employee 10% 10%
- Entitlements: - Minimum 10 years’ service: ₹10,000/month pension. - 25 years’ service: 50% of average last 12 months’ salary. - Family Pension: - 60% to spouse upon pensioner’s death. - Inflation-linked: - Adjusted via Consumer Price Index for Industrial Workers (CPI-IW). - Lump-sum payment at retirement (amount not critical for UPSC). - Old Pension Scheme (OPS) in Non-BJP States: - Union Government: - OPS unsustainable due to fiscal constraints; prefers taxpayer funds for poor welfare. - Non-BJP States (e.g., Rajasthan): - Reverting to OPS; experts argue it’s fiscally unsustainable. - Andhra Guaranteed Pension Scheme (GPS): - For state government employees: -
Contributor Contribution Pension
Employee 10% 33% of last salary
State Govt 14% 40% of last salary
- NPS Penny Drop Verification (2023): - Mandatory test: - Pension company transfers ₹1 to verify bank account details before full disbursal. - Ensures accuracy of account number/IFSC code to prevent errors. - NPS for Non-Government Employees - NPS-Lite (Swavalamban, 2010): - For unorganized sector poor; government contributed ₹1,000/year for 5 years. - NPS-Vatsalya (Budget 2024): - Pension account for minors (<18); - parents/guardians contribute. - Contribution: - Minimum ₹1,000/year, no upper limit. - Converts to standard NPS account at age 18. - Minimum Assured Return Scheme (MARS): - Proposed for non-government NPS subscribers; PFRDA to guarantee minimum return (e.g., 8%). - If market returns fall short, PFRDA/other entity covers the loss, encouraging NPS uptake. - Jeevan Pramaan (2014): - Aadhaar-based Digital Life Certificate by MeitY; eliminates need for physical certificate submission. - Process: - Pensioner’s Aadhaar + biometric (fingerprint/iris) or facial recognition (2022 reform for elderly) generates certificate. - 2020: - Extended to EPFO subscribers. - Pension for Senior Citizens with Capacity to Invest - Pradhan Mantri Vaya Vandana Yojana (PMVVY, 2017): - Nodal: - Dept. of Financial Services; implemented by LIC. - Eligibility: - Senior citizens (60+); closed for new registrations post-Mar 2023. - Similar Schemes: - Senior Citizen Savings Scheme, LIC Varistha Pension Bima Yojana (outdated, low UPSC relevance). - Pension for Poor with Capacity to Invest - Maan Dhan Yojanas: -

- Economic Survey 2025 (ES25) Observations - Pension Sector Challenges: - Global elderly population surpasses children; increased life expectancy strains pension funds. - Pensioners save less than active workers, reducing national savings in aging countries. - Rising retirement ages (e.g., Japan, France) cause worker resentment. - Inflation increases pension amount needs. - Pension Market Development: - OECD nations: - Pension assets ~80% of GDP; India: EPFO (17%), NPS (4.5%). - 80% of Indian pensioners under APY, receiving ₹1,000–5,000/month. - Micro-Pension Issues: - Schemes like APY, Shram Yogi provide small pensions; 93% APY subscribers opt for ₹1,000 plan due to lower contributions. - Poor prioritize immediate consumption over pension savings. - Behavioral interventions (simplified enrollment, renewal reminders) needed. - Britain’s PAYGO Schemes: - Pay-As-You-Go: - Young workers’ contributions directly fund pensioners, not invested (unlike EPFO/NPS). - Sustainability requires more young workers and inflation-linked inflows (indexation). - Asset-liability mismatch in Greece, Italy; Britain faces similar risks in 2 decades. - Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA) - Establishment: - 2003 via executive order; statutory status in 2013; HQ: New Delhi. - Structure: - 1 Chairman (5 years/65 age) + 5 members (5 years/62 age), re-appointable. - Functions: - Implements NPS, selects fund managers. - Regulates public/private pension funds (excludes EPFO, seamen, coal miners, Assam tea plantations due to separate acts). - Prescribes liquidity, auditing, investment norms. - Protects clients/pensioners; holds civil court powers. - Promotes awareness via pensionsanchay.org.in. - Pension FDI aligned with insurance FDI (49%), not set by PFRDA. - Social Security for Overseas Indians - Pravasi Bharatiya Bima Yojana (PBBY, 2017): - Nodal: - Ministry of External Affairs. - Mandatory for Indian workers in Emigration Check Required (ECR) countries (e.g., Saudi, UAE). - Benefits: - ₹10 lakh for accidental death/permanent disability, maternity expenses, family hospitalization. - Totalisation Pact (India-USA): - Addresses issues where NRIs face difficulties accessing US pension/social security contributions after returning to India. - Negotiations ongoing; no further details critical for UPSC. - Pillar 1D3: Financial Inclusion - Definition: - Financial Inclusion (FI): - Providing access to banking, investment, pension, insurance, and credit facilities to all citizens, especially marginalized and unorganized sectors, ensuring social, economic, and transaction (S-E-T) security. - Social Security (SS): - Government payments/assistance to ill, handicapped, poor, aged, or unemployed citizens, rooted in DPSP Articles 41 (public assistance) and 42 (humane work conditions, maternity relief). - Social Justice (SJ): - Equitable distribution of wealth, opportunities, and privileges via reservations, legal safeguards, and protection of civil rights. - Significance: - FI, SS, and SJ promote human development, inclusive growth, and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). - Reduces cash-based economy, enhances women’s empowerment, and supports targeted subsidy delivery (e.g., via JAM Trinity). - Bank Accounts for Everyone - Historical Context: - 1955, - 1969, 1980: Bank nationalizations to expand banking access. - 1961: - Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation (DICGC) Act. - 1966: - Cooperative banks under RBI regulation. - 1969: - Lead Bank Scheme—Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) prepare district credit plans using Service Area Approach, coordinating with government, banks, NBFCs. - 1971: - State-Level Bankers’ Committee to monitor FI progress. - 1976: - Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) established via RRB Act. - 2005: - RBI permits no-frills (zero-balance) savings accounts. - 2006: - Banking Business Correspondent Agents (BCAs) allowed. - 2011: - Swabhiman scheme to boost rural banking. - 2013: - e-KYC using Aadhaar and biometrics, eliminating photocopy needs. - 2014: - PM Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY), new private banks (e.g., Bandhan, IDFC First). - 2015: - Small Finance Banks and Payment Banks introduced. - 2017–18: - India Post Payments Bank (IPPB) launched (see Pillar#1B1). - RBI Norms: - Commercial banks must open 25% of branches in unbanked rural areas. - Similar norms for White Label ATM companies. - Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY, 2014) - Nodal: - Finance Ministry, Dept. of Financial Services (DFS); Motto: “Mera Khaata, Bhagya Vidhaata.” - Phases and Objectives: - Phase I (2014–15): - Financial literacy programs. - Banking access within 5 km. - Accounts for every family with overdraft and RuPay ATM-cum-debit card. - Phase II (2015–18): - Credit Guarantee Fund for overdraft defaults. - Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) for subsidies/scholarships. - Selling micro-insurance/pension products via banks. - Features: - Accounts in SCBs/Cooperative Banks with Core Banking System (CBS) and RuPay Payment Gateway. - Basic Savings Bank Deposit Account (BSBDA): - Age 10+, zero-balance, no penalty; chequebook requires minimum balance. - Overdraft: - Up to ₹10,000 (originally ₹5,000) based on 6-month balance history; one account per household (preferably woman); repayable with interest in 3 years (bank sets rate). - Free Accident Insurance: - ₹1 lakh, premium by NPCI; requires regular card use (e.g., balance check); excludes Union government employees, income taxpayers. - Withdrawal Limits: - Restrictions on monthly withdrawals. - Significance: - JAM Trinity (Jan Dhan, Aadhaar, Mobile) enables targeted DBT, reducing leakages. - 2020: - PM Garib Kalyan—₹500/month for 3 months to 20 crore women PMJDY accounts. - 2023: - Total deposits crossed ₹2 trillion. - Criticism: - Accounts used as money mules during 2016 demonetization. - 1.2.1 Jan Dhan Darshak App (2018): - Developed by DFS and National Informatics Centre (NIC). - Locates nearby financial touchpoints (bank branches, ATMs, post offices). - Investments Other Than Bank - Overview: - Small savings schemes offer higher returns than bank deposits for poor/lower-middle-class individuals. - Funds flow to National Small Savings Fund (NSSF), used for loans to Union/selected states. - Interest rates set quarterly by Finance Ministry’s Dept. of Economic Affairs. - Schemes: -

- Mahila Samman Bachat Patra (2023): - Announced in Budget 2023; Tenure: 2 years (up to Mar 2025). - Max Deposit: - ₹2 lakh per girl/woman; Interest: 7.5%. - Senior Citizen Savings Scheme (SCSS): - Deposit Limit: - Increased from ₹15 lakh to ₹30 lakh (Budget 2023). - Sukanya Samriddhi Yojana (2015): - Fixed deposit for girls (0–10 years); Annual Deposit: ₹250–₹1.5 lakh until age 14. - Interest: - ~8.5% (originally 9.1%), set by Dept. of Economic Affairs. - Withdrawal: - At age 18–21 (based on marriage status); prevents child marriage, supports education/business. - Limits: - One account per daughter; max two daughters per family. - Five Star Village Scheme (2020): - Nodal: - Ministry of Communications, Dept. of Post. - Post offices promote small savings (e.g., PPF, NSC, Sukanya Samriddhi) and insurance (e.g., RPLI, PMJJB, PMSBY) in villages. - Sampoorna Bima Gram Yojana (2017): - Nodal: - Dept. of Post. - Ensures at least one Rural Postal Life Insurance (RPLI) policy per household in one village per district. - Model Insurance Villages (MIV, 2021): - Proposed by IRDAI; aims for complete village insurance coverage (life, health, crop, livestock, vehicle). - Funding: - Union, states, CSR, NABARD, other AIFIs. - Chit Funds: - Collective investment scheme; members contribute monthly, access loans from the fund. - Concurrent List: - Regulated by Union (RBI, SEBI) and states; scams due to lax supervision. - Banning of Unregulated Deposit Schemes Act, 2019: - Prohibits unregistered entities (e.g., builders, jewelers) from soliciting deposits/investments. - Regulators: - RBI (banks, NBFCs), SEBI (MF, REITs), IRDAI, PFRDA, MCA (NIDHI), states (chit funds), EPFO. - Penalties: - Up to ₹50 crore fine, 10 years imprisonment, asset attachment for refunds. - Union to create online database of deposit-taking activities. - Financial Inclusion: Credit (Loans) - Context: - Links to Pillar#1A2 (Priority Sector Lending) and Pillar#1B1 (Microfinance). - Refinance: - AIFIs (e.g., NABARD, SIDBI) or MUDRA provide new finance to banks/NBFCs based on loans already given to end-borrowers, often via securitization. - Credit Guarantee: - Covers bank/NBFC losses if borrowers default, enabling collateral-free lending. - Earlier by DICGC; now by specialized organizations (e.g., CGTMSE, NCGTC). - Credit Guarantee Organizations: -

- RBI’s Integrated Ombudsman (2021): - Consolidated Banking, NBFC, and Digital Transaction Ombudsman into One Nation One Ombudsman Scheme. - Web Portals - PaiSA Portal (2018): - Outdated, deleted for 2024–25. - psbloansin59minutes.com (2018): - By SIDBI + 5 PSBs; GST-registered MSMEs get ₹10 lakh–₹1 crore collateral-free loans; ₹1,000 fee if approved. - Jan Samarth Portal (2022): - Nodal: - Finance Ministry; single platform for education, agriculture, livelihood loans. - Connects borrowers to 125+ banks/NBFCs; verifies Aadhaar, GST, UDYAM, CIBIL for faster processing. - SARTHI Portal (2024): - Nodal: - Agriculture Ministry, with UNDP India. - Facilitates crop, life, health, tractor, shop insurance for farmers/villagers. - PM-SURAJ Portal (2024): - Nodal: - Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. - Targets 1 lakh entrepreneurs from SC/ST/OBC/nomadic tribes for loans; interest subsidy/credit guarantee by ministry (details pending). - 1.12 Financial Inclusion Reports - Global Microscope Report: - By Economist Intelligence Unit, Accion, Gates Foundation. - Global Findex Database: - World Bank, Gates Foundation. - Global Pension Index: - Mercer CFA Institute; India’s score: 45.9 (2023) to 44 (2024, per ES25). - India’s FI Index: - By DFS; measures access, usage, quality. - RBI’s FI Index (2021): - Score 0–100; 60 (2023) to 64.2 (Mar 2024). - National Strategy for Financial Inclusion: By RBI. - Pillar 2A1-i: Budget - Fiscal Policy: Meaning & Significance - Definition: - Fiscal policy involves government decisions on taxation, expenditure, subsidies, and other financial operations to influence savings, investment, and consumption, achieving national goals like income redistribution, socio-economic welfare, economic development, and inclusive growth. - Etymology: - Derived from Greek “fiscus” (basket), symbolizing the public purse. - Significance: - Employment: - Funds welfare schemes (e.g., MGNREGA, Pillar#6) to create jobs. - Inflation Control: - Higher income taxes reduce disposable income, curbing demand; lower taxes during deflation boost demand (Pillar#4). - Economic Growth: - Tax benefits on savings (e.g., LIC, Mutual Funds) provide capital for industries, leading to factory expansion, jobs, and GDP growth (Pillar#4). - Inclusive Growth: - Higher taxes on the rich fund health, education, and poverty alleviation (Pillar#6). - Regional Balance: - Tax incentives for industries in backward areas (e.g., North East, Naxal-affected regions). - Exchange Rate Stability: - Tax benefits for exporters and higher import duties reduce Current Account Deficit (CAD), stabilizing INR:USD rates (Pillar#3). - Budget: Meaning - Definition: - Annual financial statement detailing estimated revenues and expenditures for the next financial year, serving as the primary tool for implementing fiscal policy. - Constitutional Basis: - Article 112 mandates the Annual Financial Statement (AFS). - Three Funds Related to Budget -
Fund Article Description
Consolidated Fund of India (CFI) 266 Includes taxes, loans raised/recovered; withdrawals require Parliament approval, except for charged expenditures (e.g., judges’ salaries).
Public Account of India 266 Holds provident funds, small savings, postal deposits; government acts as banker, no Parliament approval needed for transfers. New funds (e.g., Central Road Fund, 2000) require Parliament approval.
Contingency Fund of India 267 For unforeseen events; managed by Finance Secretary (DEA) on behalf of President; post-expenditure Parliament approval required; replenished from CFI. Budget 2021: Increased from ₹500 crore to ₹30,000 crore (40% Expenditure Dept, 60% DEA).
- Donation Funds: PMNRF vs. PM CARES -
Aspect PMNRF (1948) PM CARES (2020)
Purpose Initially for Pakistan refugees; now for floods, cyclones, earthquakes, medical aid (e.g., cancer, transplants), acid attacks, riots. Emergency situations (e.g., COVID-19 pandemic).
Funding Donations from individuals, institutions, foreigners; no budget support. Same as PMNRF.
Tax Benefits Donors get income tax exemptions; company donations count as CSR (Pillar#1C). Same as PMNRF.
Operation Managed by PMO; PM as ex-officio chairman, assisted by officers. Same; PM as ex-officio chairman, includes Defence, Home, Finance Ministers as ex-officio trustees, 3 nominated trustees (health, science, social work experts).
Legal Status Not set up by Parliament; registered under Indian Trust Act, 1882. Same as PMNRF.
Controversy - RTI applicability debated; officials refused information, leading to court cases.
- Note: - National Defence Fund (1962) supports military/paramilitary families, similar to PMNRF/PM CARES. Critics suggest moving donation funds to Public Account/CFI for transparency. - Three Documents Related to Budget - Etymology: - “Budget” from French “bougette” (leather bag); FM traditionally carried budget documents in a suitcase, replaced in 2019 by Nirmala Sitharaman with a “Bahi-Khata” (red cloth folder). - Documents: -
Document Article Description
Annual Financial Statement (AFS) 112 Details last year’s receipts/expenditures and next year’s projections; separates revenue and capital expenditures.
Finance Bill 117, 265 Seeks Parliament’s approval to impose taxes; Parliament can reduce/abolish taxes but cannot increase beyond government’s proposal.
Appropriation Bill 114 Authorizes withdrawals from CFI; includes charged (non-votable, e.g., judges’ salaries) and voted expenditures (e.g., scheme funding).
- Note: - Finance and Appropriation Bills are Money Bills (Article 110); Rajya Sabha can discuss for 14 days but cannot reject/amend; Lok Sabha Speaker’s decision on Money Bill status is final (Article 110(3), 122). - Six Stages of Passing the Budget in Parliament - Stages: - Presentation of Budget. - General Discussion. - Scrutiny by Departmental Committees. - Voting on Demands for Grants, Cut Motions, Guillotine. - Passing of Appropriation Bill. - Passing of Finance Bill. - Reference: - Self-study from Indian Polity by M. Laxmikanth (Parliament chapter). - Financial Year (FY) - Period: - April 1 to March 31, inherited from British India (1867) to align with their fiscal calendar. - Constitution: - No specific FY months mandated. - Shankar Acharya Committee (2016–17): - Explored changing FY (e.g., Jan–Dec or Rabi-Kharif seasons) for better tax/expenditure estimation; states opposed due to accounting/software changes; Modi government retained April–March FY. - 2020: - Fake news about FY change due to COVID; debunked. - Vote on Account - Purpose: - Allows government to withdraw funds from CFI for routine expenses (e.g., salaries, electricity) before the Appropriation Act is passed for the new FY. - Process: - Parliament (effectively Lok Sabha) approves funds (typically one-sixth of budget estimate) for two months until Appropriation Act is passed. - Pre-2017: - Budget presented on last February working day, requiring Vote on Account due to delayed six-stage process. - Post-2017: - Budget presented on first February working day, completing six stages by March; Vote on Account unnecessary except in election years (2019, 2024) for interim budgets to keep options open post-elections. - Interim Budget - Definition: - Presented in election years or crises (e.g., coalition collapse) to avoid drastic/populist measures; includes AFS, Finance Bill, and Appropriation Bill but avoids major policy changes. - Validity: - Covers entire FY; new government may present a full (General) Budget to revise provisions. - Examples: - 2004 (Yashwant Sinha), 2009 (Pranab Mukherjee), 2014 (P. Chidambaram), 2019 (Piyush Goyal), 2024 (Nirmala Sitharaman). - 2014 Case: - Interim Budget (Feb) by Chidambaram; post-election, full Budget (July) by Arun Jaitley. - Terminology: - “Full Budget” used in handout for revision; use “General Budget” in mains. - Budget’s Theme - Not constitutionally required; used for media attention. -
Year Type Theme/Features
2021 General Paperless (Samsung tablet, red cover); no explicit theme, focus on AtmaNirbhar Bharat.
2024-Feb Interim Paperless; no tax changes, minimal new schemes; White Paper comparing UPA vs. Modi government.
2024-July General Paperless; no explicit theme.
- Economic Survey - Definition: - Annual document, typically prepared by Chief Economic Adviser (CEA), tabled in Parliament a day before the Budget (no constitutional mandate). - Labeling: - Named after previous FY (e.g., ES 2023-24 tabled in July 2024). - Exceptions: - No survey before interim budgets (Feb 2019, Feb 2024); presented before General Budgets (July 2019, July 2024). - Formats: -

2014-15 2 Vol 1: Future suggestions; Vol 2: Past data; Theme: Creating opportunity, reducing vulnerability via JAM Trinity.

2022-23 1 By CEA; no explicit theme.
2024 1 July presentation; no theme.
2025 1 31/Jan/2025; no explicit theme; focus: geopolitical issues, over-financialisation, AI job losses, deregulation, energy transition, GDP growth 6.3–6.8%.
- Chief Economic Adviser (CEA) - Role: - Under DEA, oversees Indian Economic Service (IES) officers; 3-year tenure, reappointment possible; not a constitutional/statutory body. - Notable CEAs: - Manmohan Singh, Raghuram Rajan, Arvind Subramanian (2014–18), Krishnamurthy Subramanian (2018–21). - Current: - Dr. V. Anantha Nageswaran (2022–March 2027); IIM-A MBA; Books: *Can India Grow*, *The Rise of Finance*. - Finance Ministry and Its Departments - Department of Economic Affairs (DEA) - Functions: - Formulates fiscal policy, prepares Union Budget (including Railways), budgets for Union Territories without legislatures, and states under President’s Rule. - Sets interest rates for small savings schemes (Pillar#1D). - Manages www.pppinindia.gov.in for Public-Private Partnership (PPP) information (Pillar#5). - Organizations: - Finance Commission (Article 280). - Prints currency, coins, stamps, passports. - Infrastructure Finance Secretariat (IFS). - Department of Expenditure - Functions: - Controller General of Accounts (CGA, Indian Civil Accounts Service) estimates CFI spending. - Implements Finance/Pay Commission reports, pensions (old scheme, Pillar#1D). - Arun Jaitley National Institute of Financial Management (AJNIFM), Faridabad: Training/research. - Portals: - Public Financial Management System (PFMS): - Disburses funds to ministries/states. - Bharatkosh (NTRP): - Non-tax receipts (e.g., India Yearbook, Yojana magazine). - PARAS: - Pension accounts. - Department of Revenue - Functions: Oversees taxation via: - Central Board of Direct Taxes (CBDT): Income Tax. - Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs (CBIC): GST (post-2017, 101st Amendment), earlier CBEC. - Authorities for Advance Rulings (IT, Customs, GST). - Taxation tribunals/appellate bodies. - Subordinate Bodies: - Enforcement Directorate (PMLA, FEMA). - Central Economic Intelligence Bureau. - Central Bureau of Narcotics. - Financial Intelligence Unit. - Companies: - Goods and Services Tax Network (GSTN). - Government Opium and Alkaloid Factories (GOAF): Produces morphine, codeine, thebaine from opium in MP, Rajasthan, UP. - Department of Financial Services (DFS) - Functions: - Oversees financial inclusion schemes (Pillar#1D). - Supervises/recapitalizes Public Sector Banks (PSBs). - Manages public sector financial intermediaries (except EPFO/ESIC). - Organizations: - National Bank for Financing Infrastructure and Development (NABFID). - E-Bikray Portal: Auctions assets for PSB loan defaults. - Department of Investment and Public Asset Management (DIPAM) - Functions: - Manages disinvestment/privatization of Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs, Pillar#2D). - Department of Public Enterprises (DPE) - Pre-2021: - Under Ministry of Heavy Industries. - Post-2021: - Shifted to Finance Ministry for synergy with DIPAM. - Functions: - Oversees CPSE administration, expenditure, financial health, performance monitoring. - Awards ‘Ratna’ status to profitable CPSEs (Pillar#2D). - Manages CPSE employee training, Voluntary Retirement Scheme (VRS, Pillar#1B1). - Note: - CPSE employee selection by Public Enterprises Selection Board (PESB), not DPE; regulator chiefs (RBI, SEBI) selected by Cabinet Secretary-led committee. - National Land Monetization Corporation (NLMC, 2022) - Announced: - Budget 2021; established March 2022 under DPE. - Structure: - 100% government-owned, ₹150 crore capital. - Functions: - Rents/sells surplus/non-core land assets of government departments/CPSEs; hires private-sector professionals. - Related: - PM Gati Shakti, National Monetization Pipeline (Pillar#5). - Indian Audit and Accounts Department (IAAD) - Headed by: - Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG, constitutional body). - Functions: - Audits government accounts, ensuring transparency. - IAAD-OIOS - One Indian Audit & Accounts Department One System: Paperless administration system by CAG. - Budget: Revenue Part – Receipts – Tax Receipts - Types of Taxes: Direct vs. Indirect -
Parameter Direct Tax (e.g., 5% Income Tax) Indirect Tax (e.g., 18% GST on Biscuits)
Impact (Collection Point) Income tax assessee deposits tax. Shopkeeper/seller deposits tax.
Incidence (Burden) Assessee bears burden; not transferable. Customer/buyer bears burden; transferable from seller.
Relation Impact and incidence on same person. Impact and incidence on different persons.
- Types of Taxes: Progressive vs. Regressive - Progressive: - Higher tax rates for higher incomes (e.g., 5–30% income tax slabs); direct taxes are progressive. - Regressive: - Flat tax rate disproportionately affects lower incomes (e.g., 18% GST on biscuits impacts poor more). - Types of Direct Taxes - Progressive: - As above. - Proportional: - Single flat rate (e.g., 10%) regardless of income level. - Degressive: - Progressive up to a limit, then flat rate (e.g., 5–10% up to a point, then 10% thereafter); income tax is progressive for MCQs. - Adam Smith’s Four Canons of Taxation - Equality: - Taxes proportionate to income; rich pay more. - Certainty: - Clear dates, slabs, rates announced in advance. - Convenience: - Simple tax payment process (no long queues or complex forms). - Economy: - Low administrative cost relative to tax collected. - Pillar 2A1-ii: Direct Taxes - Direct Taxes: Types - Definition: Taxes levied directly on income, assets, or transactions by the Union or State Governments. -
Category Union Government State Government Abolished
On Income Corporation Tax, Minimum Alternate Tax (MAT), Income Tax (non-agricultural), Capital Gains Tax (CGT) Agricultural Income Tax, Professional Tax (max ₹2,500/year, constitutional ceiling) Dividend Distribution Tax (DDT)
On Assets/Transactions Securities Transaction Tax (STT), Commodities Transaction Tax (CTT) Land Revenue, Stamp/Registration Duty, Urban Property Tax Wealth Tax, Banking Cash Transaction Tax, Estate Duty
On Expenditure - Road Tax (debatable, sometimes collected by seller) Hotel Receipt Tax, Gift Tax, Fringe Benefit Tax (e.g., employer-provided gym subscriptions)
- Estate Duty, Wealth Tax, Inheritance Tax (Abolished) -
Tax Description Abolished Reason
Estate Duty/Inheritance Tax 1953 onwards: Paid by legal heirs on deceased’s property (5–40% rates). 1985 (FM: V.P. Singh) Low revenue, high administrative cost, evasion via property undervaluation.
Wealth Tax Paid by owner on assets above ₹30 lakh (1% rate). 2015 (FM: Arun Jaitley) Low revenue, high administrative cost, evasion via asset undervaluation.
Gift Tax 1958 onwards: Tax on gifts above a threshold. 1998 (FM: Yashwant Sinha) Low revenue, high administrative cost.
- Note: - NCERT terms these as “Paper Taxes” due to low revenue yield. - Reintroduction of Inheritance/Wealth/Estate Taxes - Reference: - Mains Handout Pillar#2 for detailed discussion. - Direct Taxes: Merits and Demerits - Merits: - Progressive: - Higher taxes on richer individuals reduce income inequality. - Civic Consciousness: - Direct tax payment creates awareness of tax burden. - Savings/Investment: - Deductions (e.g., NPS, LIC) encourage savings. - Elasticity: - Tax revenue rises with income levels. - Certainty: - Clear tax slabs and payment schedules. - Exchange Rate Stability: - Taxes like Tobin Tax reduce currency volatility (Pillar#3). - Demerits: - Externalities Ignored: - Equal tax rates for socially beneficial (e.g., authors) vs. harmful (e.g., cigar promoters) activities. - Hardship Ignored: - Same tax rate for active workers (e.g., carpenters) vs. passive income earners (e.g., landlords). - High Rates: - May discourage work or foreign investment. - Narrow Base: - Excludes poor, requiring large administrative staff if included. - Evasion/Loopholes: - Prone to litigation, tax avoidance (Pillar#2B). - Union Tax, Cess, and Surcharge -
Type Description Destination State Sharing
Union Tax Computed on taxable income/profit/transactions. Consolidated Fund of India (CFI). Shared with states per Finance Commission formula (IGST differs, Pillar#2B).
Surcharge Tax on tax; e.g., 10% Social Welfare Surcharge on customs duty for welfare schemes. CFI. Not shared with states.
Cess Computed on (tax + surcharge); specific objectives (e.g., Health & Education, Road Cess). CFI, then Public Account (sometimes separate funds like Prarambhik Shiksha Kosh). Not shared via Finance Commission; some indirectly reaches states via schemes.
- Cess: FAQs - Purpose: - Enables Union to fund schemes without sharing with states. - Mandatory: - Not compulsory; depends on government discretion. - Applicability: -
Cess Applied On
Health & Education Cess Direct Taxes (Income Tax, Corporation Tax)
Health Cess Customs Duty on Medical Devices
Agriculture Infrastructure Cess Customs/Excise Duty on select products
Road & Infrastructure Cess Excise Duty on Petrol/Diesel
GST Compensation Cess GST on specific goods (e.g., gutkha, cars)
Solid Waste Management Cess Urban Local Bodies (₹30–50/month, per 2016 SWM Rules)
- Health vs. Health & Education Cess: Distinct (see table). - Cess: Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN) - Details: - 4% Health & Education Cess on direct taxes (Income Tax, Corporation Tax) funds PMSSN, a non-lapsable Public Account fund (2021). - Uses (Pillar#6): - Ayushman Bharat (PM-JAY: ₹5 lakh health insurance). - Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (AIIMS-like institutions, medical college upgrades). - National Health Mission (NHM). - Health emergencies (e.g., COVID-19). - Laffer Curve, Tax Elasticity, Tax Buoyancy - Reference: Pillar#2B (Black Money Handout). - Corporation Tax - Definition: Levied on company profits under Income Tax Act, 1961 (Corporate Income Tax). - Rates (Budget 2024): -

- Minimum Alternate Tax (MAT) & Alternate Minimum Tax (AMT) - MAT: - Introduced 1996 (FM: Chidambaram) at 18.5%, reduced to 15% by Modi government; applied to “zero-tax” companies using exemptions/depreciations to show no taxable income. - AMT: - 15% tax on cooperative societies (e.g., Amul, IFFCO). - Purpose: - Ensures minimum tax liability despite accounting adjustments. - Corporation Tax on Startups - Definition: - Companies <10 years old, turnover <₹100 crore, engaged in innovation (Startup India Scheme, Pillar#4B). - Benefit: - 100% tax holiday on profits for 3 years within first 10 years of incorporation. - Angel Tax on Startup Investments - Introduced: - 2012 (UPA) under DPIIT; 30% tax + penalty on angel investments to startups. - Purpose: - Curb suspicious investments. - Abolished: - Budget 2024 (July) due to concerns about discouraging startup growth. - Equalisation Levy/Google Tax - Status: - Abolished (Budget 2024, July). - Related Concepts (Pillar#2B): - Global Minimum Tax. - Significant Economic Presence (SEP): Taxes foreign companies (e.g., Netflix) earning from India via digital services. - OECD’s Tax Challenges of Digitization: Addresses MNC tax avoidance. - GAFA Tax (France, 2019): Taxes tech giants (Google, Apple, Facebook, Amazon). - Dividend Distribution Tax (DDT) - Introduced: - 1997 (FM: Chidambaram); 15% + cess + surcharge (20.56%) on shareholder dividends. - Abolished: - Budget 2020; dividends now taxed as shareholder income. - Benefits: - Lower tax rates (0–5%) for middle-class shareholders. - Increases disposable income, boosting demand and economic growth. - Buyback Tax - Reference: Video lecture for details (not exam-critical). - Capital Gains Tax (CGT) - Definition: Tax on profits from selling capital assets (e.g., non-agricultural land, property, jewelry, shares, bonds, cryptocurrencies). - Types: - Short-Term Capital Gains Tax (SCGT): Assets sold within 12 months (financial assets) or 24 months (other assets). - Long-Term Capital Gains Tax (LCGT): Assets sold after 12/24 months. - Budget 2022: - 30% CGT + 1% TDS on virtual digital assets (VDAs) like Bitcoin (Pillar#1A1). - CGT on Share Market Profits (Budget 2024, July) -
Type Previous Rate New Rate Rationale
SCGT (sold within 12 months) 15% 20% Increased profits in rising markets; funds welfare schemes; reduces speculation.
LCGT (sold after 12 months) 10% 12.5% Prevents over-financialization (Pillar#1C2); encourages long-term holding.
- Indexation Benefit/Cost Inflation Index (CII) - Definition: Adjusts profits for inflation, reducing LCGT liability (not applicable to SCGT). -

- Note: - Budget 2024 initially removed indexation; restored for pre-23/7/2024 properties after backlash. - Income Tax on Individuals - History: - Introduced by James Wilson (1860) to fund British losses post-1857 Sepoy Mutiny; 24 July celebrated as Income Tax Day. - Old Tax Regime (OTR) vs. New Tax Regime (NTR) - NTR Introduced: 2020. -
Parameter OTR NTR (2025)
Gross Income (age <60) ₹7.5 lakh ₹12.75 lakh
Standard Deduction ₹50,000 ₹75,000
NPS Deduction ₹50,000 N/A
LIC/Home Loan Deduction ₹1.5 lakh N/A
Taxable Income ₹5 lakh ₹12 lakh
Income Tax ₹12,500 ₹60,000
Rebate (up to ₹5L OTR/₹12L NTR) ₹12,500 ₹60,000
Tax Payable ₹0 ₹0
Surcharge (>₹50L) N/A N/A
Health & Education Cess 4% (₹0) 4% (₹0)
- Note: - Deductions (NPS, LIC) capped; NTR excludes most deductions except standard deduction. - NTR Increases Disposable Income - Disposable Income: - Income after direct taxes and social security deductions (e.g., LIC, NPS). - NTR Benefit: - Fewer deductions increase disposable income, boosting spending and economic growth. - Switching Between OTR and NTR - Taxpayers choose annually; - NTR is default for salaried individuals unless OTR opted. - Surcharge on Income Tax -
