- Note: - NTR has no separate slabs for senior citizens. - Revenue Forgone/Tax Expenditure - Definition: - Tax revenue sacrificed due to exemptions/deductions. - Example: - OTR vs. NTR for ₹5.5Cr income; NTR saves ₹20.94 lakh, termed “revenue forgone” by government. - Budget 2025: - ₹1 lakh crore (direct taxes), ₹2,600 crore (indirect taxes) forgone to stimulate economy. - Direct Taxes: Miscellaneous Concepts - Hindu Undivided Family (HUF) - Definition: - Hindu, Buddhist, Jain, or Sikh families pool assets under Income Tax Act; taxed separately, reducing tax liability via provisions/loopholes. - Presumptive Taxation - Definition: - Simplified method for self-employed professionals (e.g., lawyers, doctors, freelancers) to calculate income tax without detailed accounting. - Distinction: - Not a separate tax; applies within Income Tax, unlike Professional Tax (state-levied, max ₹2,500/year). - Advance Tax - Purpose: - Ensures timely tax collection; payable quarterly if annual liability ≥₹10,000 (Income Tax, Corporation Tax). - TDS & TCS - Purpose: - Tracks income, reduces tax evasion/black money. -
Type Collector From Examples
TDS Buyer/Issuer Seller/Investor Salary, royalty, interest, dividends, service fees
TCS Seller Buyer Luxury cars, foreign currency
- Note: - TDS/TCS are administrative mechanisms, not separate taxes; credited to income tax. - Tax Deducted at Source (TDS) - Mechanism: - Deducted by payer (e.g., college, bank) and deposited to IT department with recipient’s PAN; forces tax return filing. - Budget Updates: - 2019: - 2% TDS on cash withdrawals >₹1 crore to promote digital payments. - 2022: - 1% TDS on VDA transfers. - 2024 (July): - Reduced TDS on select transactions. - TDS on Influencers - Issue: - Non-monetary payments (e.g., phones) complicate TDS; not exam-critical. - Tax Collected at Source (TCS) - Mechanism: - Seller (e.g., car showroom) collects extra tax (e.g., 1% on ₹25 lakh SUV) to ensure buyer files tax return. - Budget 2025: - TCS relief on select transactions. - Tax Refund - Eligibility: - Paid tax exceeds liability (e.g., 10% TDS but 0% slab); refunded with interest. - GST Refund: - Claimed via GSTN portal. - Deferred Tax Assets - Concept: - Tax benefits from writing off NPAs (Pillar#1B2); not exam-critical. - Financial Transaction Taxes - Tobin Tax/Robinhood Tax - Proposed: - 1970s by James Tobin; small tax on currency conversions to reduce speculation and stabilize global economy (Pillar#3A). - India: - Foreign currency conversions subject to GST (indirect tax). - Securities Transaction Tax (STT) & Commodities Transaction Tax (CTT) - STT: - Levied on share, ETF, derivative transactions (0.001–2%); Budget 2024 increased STT on futures/options (Pillar#1C2). - CTT: - Levied on non-agricultural commodities (~0.01%). - Administered by: - CBDT under Income Tax Act (direct taxes). -
Tax By Description
Capital Gains Tax Union On profit from selling assets (e.g., property, shares, Bitcoin).
Securities Transaction Tax Union On sale/purchase of shares, bonds, securities; regardless of profit/loss.
Dividend Distribution Tax Union Abolished 2020; previously on dividends paid.
Stamp Duty State On securities, properties, agreements; uniform rates post-2020 Indian Stamp Act amendment; collected by Union, distributed to states.
- Pillar 2A2: Indirect Taxes - Definition: - Indirect taxes are levies imposed on goods and services, collected by intermediaries (e.g., traders, manufacturers) and passed on to consumers through the price of the product, administered by the Ministry of Finance, Department of Revenue, through the Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs (CBIC), renamed from Central Board of Excise and Customs (CBEC) in Budget-2018. - Context: - Indirect taxes are a critical revenue source for the Union and State governments, funding public services and infrastructure while influencing consumption patterns and economic behavior through targeted taxation. - Significance for India: - Indirect taxes, particularly GST, have unified India’s tax structure, reduced cascading effects, and promoted ease of doing business, aligning with India’s goals of economic integration, transparency, and global competitiveness under initiatives like Make in India and Atmanirbhar Bharat. - Indirect Taxes: Types - Definition: - Indirect taxes are categorized based on their method of imposition, either on the value of goods/services or on their quantity. - Types: - Ad-Valorem Tax: - Levied as a percentage of the value of goods or services (e.g., 35% customs duty on imported orange juice worth ₹1000 results in ₹350 tax). - Advantages: - Easier to administer due to standardized valuation methods. - Disadvantages: - Leads to inspector-raj and litigation; small rate increases significantly impact consumer prices. - Specific Tax per Unit: - Levied based on quantity or volume (e.g., ₹260 excise duty per 1000 cigarettes of 65-70mm length, regardless of price). - Advantages: - Effective in reducing harmful consumption by passing greater burden to consumers. - Disadvantages: - Difficult to administer due to quantity-based assessments. -
TypeBasisExampleMeritsDemerits
Ad-ValoremValue-based35% duty on ₹1000 juice = ₹350 taxEasier to administerInspector-raj, litigation
Specific TaxQuantity-based₹260 per 1000 cigarettesReduces harmful consumptionComplex administration
- Burden of Indirect Tax – Forward vs. Backward Shifting - Definition: - The burden of indirect taxes can shift to consumers (forward) or suppliers/producers (backward), depending on market dynamics. - Forward Shifting: - Sellers increase product prices to maintain profit margins (e.g., Vadilal raises ice cream prices after GST increase from 18% to 28%). - Backward Shifting: - Sellers absorb tax increases by cutting costs (e.g., Vadilal reduces worker salaries to maintain ice cream prices and market share). - Factors Influencing Shifting: - Determined by supply and demand elasticity (e.g., forward shifting is common for essential medicines due to inelastic demand). - Example (CAPF-2024 MCQ Context): - The burden of a tax on a commodity is independent of who it is imposed upon but depends on the slope of demand and supply curves. - Indirect Taxes: Merits and Demerits - Merits: - Convenient to collect as traders act as unpaid tax collectors, ensuring a wide tax base (e.g., 18% GST on biscuits). - Elastic, generating significant revenue from small rate increases, impacting all consumers. - Reduces harmful consumption through high taxes on sin goods (e.g., tobacco, alcohol, soft drinks). - Demerits: - Regressive, disproportionately affecting the poor, who pay a higher income share (e.g., same GST rate on biscuits for all income groups). - Hidden in product prices, reducing civic awareness of tax contributions. - Increases product costs, creating demand uncertainty and variable government revenue. - High risk of corruption and tax evasion without input tax credit, leading to cascading effects. -
MeritsDemerits
Convenient, wide tax baseRegressive, impacts poor more
Elastic, high revenue potentialHidden, reduces civic awareness
Reduces harmful consumptionIncreases costs, demand uncertainty
Corruption, cascading effect without ITC
- Pigouvian Tax - Definition: - A tax imposed to correct negative externalities, where third parties bear unintended costs of economic activities (e.g., pollution from cement factories affecting local communities). - Concept: - Proposed by Arthur C. Pigou to tax activities causing negative externalities (e.g., pollution, passive smoking, alcohol-related social issues). - Examples in India: High indirect taxes on petroleum, tobacco, and alcohol; Clean Environment Cess on coal (₹400/tonne, abolished under GST). - Sin Tax: - Taxes on morally undesirable goods (e.g., alcohol, tobacco); distinction from Pigouvian tax is minor and not exam-critical. - Customs Duty in Budget – Normal Patterns - Definition: - Taxes levied on imported goods, separate from GST, with additional cess/surcharges. - Patterns: - Budget-2020 introduced 5% Health Cess on imported medical devices to fund hospitals in Aspirational Districts under Ayushman Bharat. - Customs duty remains a significant revenue source, unaffected by GST regime. - Customs Duty on Imported Lab Chemicals (Budget-2024-July) - Changes: - Increased from 10% to 150%, reverted to 10% after protests by scientists. - Industrial-grade ethanol imports retain 150% duty to prevent misuse in liquor production. - Customs Duty on Gold and Other Precious Metals - Changes (Budget-2024): -
ItemBeforeAfter
Gold and Silver Bars15%6%
Platinum, Palladium, Osmium, Ruthenium, Iridium15.4%6.4%
- Justification: - High customs duty on gold encourages smuggling; reduction aims to curb black money and enhance compliance. - Customs Duty-Free Gold Baggage Allowance - Allowance: -
Passenger CategoryJewellery Weight LimitMax Value LimitCustoms Duty

- Excess Gold: - Subject to 3%-10% customs duty based on weight. - Issues: - NRIs bringing gold for events like weddings face harassment by customs officials; Delhi High Court (2025) ordered CBIC to review baggage limits. - Cess and Surcharges on Excise & Customs - Types: -
Cess/SurchargeDescription

- Justification: - Response to international criticism (e.g., US claims of India’s high import tariffs). - Indirect Tax – Petrol & Diesel Price High - Tax Structure (Mid-2021): -

- Comparison: - Global tax rates: EU (45-60%), Canada (15-30%), USA (15%). - Impact of GST: - Applying 28% GST would reduce taxes to ~₹5-6/litre, causing a revenue loss of over ₹4 lakh crore, making cheap fuel unfeasible without significant revenue cuts. - Context: - Post-COVID, direct tax collections fell, reducing government willingness to lower fuel taxes. - Special and Additional Excise Duty Not Shared with States - Structure: -

- Windfall Tax on Export of Petrol/Diesel/ATF (2022) - Definition: - A tax on unexpectedly large profits, termed “windfall tax” by media, applied to fuel exports during high global prices. - Context (2022): - Sharp rise in global fuel prices increased profits for Indian refineries exporting petrol/diesel. - Excessive exports reduced domestic supply, contributing to inflation. - Implementation: - Special Additional Excise Duty (SAED): - ₹6/litre (petrol), ₹12/litre (diesel), ₹6/litre (ATF). - Gradually reduced/eliminated from August 2022 as global prices fell. - GST on Petrol, Diesel, Electricity: NITI Aayog 6-Year Roadmap - Proposal (2021-Aug): - 28% GST on petrol/diesel + compensation cess for states (6 years). - 18% GST on electricity. - Status: - Under consideration; case pending in Kerala High Court. - Petrol-Diesel: Dynamic Fuel Pricing System & Oil Bond Repayment - Evolution: - 1970s-2002: - Administered Price Mechanism (APM) with government-fixed prices and subsidies via oil bonds. - 2002-2014: - Gradual decontrol to reduce subsidy burden. - Current: - Dynamic Fuel Pricing System (since 2017), where Oil Marketing Companies (OMCs) set daily prices based on international Trade Parity Price (TPP). - Benefits: - Aligns domestic prices with global markets, theoretically lowering prices when global prices fall. - Challenges: - High taxes (Union and State) keep prices elevated despite global price drops. - Oil bond repayments (from UPA-II era) limit Union’s ability to reduce excise duties (FM Sitharaman, 2021). - Miscellaneous Issues Related to Indirect Taxes - Minerals: Royalty Not Tax (SC, 2024) - Ruling: - Royalty is a contractual payment for mineral rights, not a tax. - States can impose taxes on mines and minerals from April 1, 2005, under State List Entry 49 (taxes on lands and buildings). - States cannot demand interest/penalties for 2005-2024 tax dues; payable in installments over 12 years. - Context: - India Cement case (Tamil Nadu) clarified states’ taxing powers despite Union’s Mines and Minerals (Development and Regulation) Act, 1957. - Urban Local Bodies (ULB) Solid Waste Management (SWM) Cess - Levied under Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016, typically ₹30-50/month, collected with property tax. - Himachal Water Cess on Hydel Projects (Unconstitutional) - Issue: - Himachal Pradesh imposed 10-50 paise/cubic meter water cess on hydel projects (2023-24). - Union challenged, citing existing electricity duty; additional cess violates constitutional taxation provisions. - Ruling (Himachal HC, 2024): - Water cess declared unconstitutional. - Cascading Effect of Indirect Taxes - Definition: - Without input tax credit (ITC), taxes are levied at each stage of production/sale, leading to tax-on-tax, increasing final product prices. - Impact: - Raises consumer costs and complicates pricing; mitigated by GST’s ITC mechanism. - Indirect Taxes: A Timeline of Reforms - Evolution: -
SystemPeriodITC on Union TaxesITC on State Taxes
MODVAT1986-2004Yes (excise)No
CENVAT2004-2017Yes (excise, service tax)No
State VAT2005-2017NoYes (VAT)
GST2017-PresentYes (CGST)Yes (SGST)
- Significance: - GST unified Union and State indirect taxes, providing ITC for both, reducing cascading effects and simplifying tax administration. - Indirect Taxes → GST: Timeline - Key Milestones: - 2004: - Vijay Kelkar Task Force on FRBM recommends GST. - 2006: - P. Chidambaram announces GST launch by 2010. - 2011: - 115th Constitutional Amendment Bill (lapsed). - 2014-16: - 122nd Constitutional Amendment Bill passed, leading to 101st Constitutional Amendment Act, 2016. - 2017: - GST implemented (July 1). - Constitutional Amendments: - 101st Amendment enabled GST, amending Articles 246-A, 268-A, 269-A, 270, 279-A, 366. - GST: 101st Constitutional Amendment Act, 2016 - Key Provisions: - Article 246-A: - States empowered to tax goods and services; Union retains IGST for inter-state trade. - Article 268-A: - Deleted, as service tax subsumed under GST. - Article 269-A: - IGST distribution formula set by GST Council. - Article 270: - CGST distribution between Union and States per Finance Commission. - Article 279-A: - Established GST Council, chaired by Finance Minister. - Article 366: - Excluded alcoholic liquor for human consumption from GST. - Implementation: - Effective from July 1, 2017, with ITC for CGST and SGST. - GST Council: Composition - Structure: -
Union Representatives (2)States’ Representatives (31)
Finance Minister (Chairman), Union Minister of State for Finance/RevenueOne minister per state/UT with legislature (J&K, Delhi, Puducherry); one selected as Vice-Chairman
- Voting: - Union: 1/3rd voting power; States: 2/3rd. - 3/4th majority required for decisions; quorum of 50% membership. - GST Council: Functions - Responsibilities: - Decide taxes/cess/surcharges subsumed under GST. - Set date for including crude oil, petrol, diesel, ATF, and natural gas in GST. - Determine standard and special GST rates (e.g., 1% calamity cess for Kerala, 2019). - Establish IGST norms for inter-state trade. - Set GST registration norms (e.g., turnover thresholds: ₹40 lakh for general states, ₹20 lakh for special category states). - Protect special category states’ interests (e.g., rejecting Sikkim’s COVID cess proposal). - Settle disputes between Union and States or among States. - Recommend compensation for states’ revenue losses. - GST Council Decisions Not Binding - Supreme Court (2022): - GST Council decisions are advisory, not mandatory, potentially causing compliance issues with non-BJP states. - GST Input Tax Credit (ITC) - Definition: - ITC allows businesses to offset GST paid on inputs against GST collected on outputs, reducing tax burden. - Mechanism: - Intra-state: - CGST (Union) + SGST (State) or UTGST (UT without legislature). - Inter-state: - IGST (CGST + SGST/UTGST), distributed to destination state/UT. - Example: - Infosys pays 18% GST on laptops (input) but gets ITC for 0% GST on exported software services (output). - Inverted Duty Structure Problem in GST on Textile - Issue: - Higher GST on inputs (e.g., 18% on fiber) than outputs (e.g., 5% on apparel < ₹1000), leading to ITC accumulation and refund burdens. - Reform (Postponed): - GST Council proposed uniform 12% GST on manmade fiber, yarn, fabrics, and apparel; deferred after industry protests. - Popcorn – 3 Rates of GST - Rates: -
ItemGST Rate

Pre-packaged/Branded Popcorn12%
Caramelised Popcorn18% (confectionery)
- Justification: - Prevents inverted duty structure by aligning rates with product processing. - GST on Used Cars – Margin Value (2024 Clarification) - Rules: -

- States’ Control/Tax on Industrial Alcohol (SC, 2024) - Ruling: - States can regulate and tax industrial alcohol (denatured ethanol) in addition to GST (18% GST: 9% CGST + 9% SGST). - Overturns 1991 SC ruling limiting Union control. - Context: - Industrial alcohol used in pharmaceuticals, cosmetics; can be misused for potable liquor, prompting state oversight. - GST Revenue Collection Figures - Trends: - Monthly GST collections vary due to seasonality; significant growth from FY18 to FY24. - FY24: - ~₹1.51 lakh crore (indicative, exact figures fluctuate). - Tax Collection: Highest to Lowest (Budget-2025) - Order: - Income Tax > GST > Corporation Tax > Excise > Customs > Service Tax (₹100 crore from old cases). - Historical Trends: - Budget-2023/2022: GST > Corporation Tax > Income Tax > Excise > Customs. - Pre-2019: - Corporation Tax > GST > Income Tax > Excise > Customs. - GST Rates on Services - Zero-Rated/Exempt Services: - Services by Union/State governments, local bodies, RBI, ESIC, EPFO. - Religious/charitable activities, public libraries, toilets, crematoriums. - Residential rent, aviation services in North-Eastern states, milk/newspaper transport. - Educational services (schools, colleges, not coaching institutes). - Exported services (zero-rated, ITC eligible). - Taxable Services: -

- Context: - Aligned with International Year of Millets (FAO/UN, 2023). - GST Composition Scheme - Definition: - Allows small traders (turnover ≤₹1.5 crore) to deposit GST quarterly instead of monthly, simplifying compliance. - Note: - Technical rules exist but are not exam-critical. - 1% GST Deposit in Cash for Fake ITC Claims - Status: - Outdated, not relevant for prelims. - Reverse Charge Mechanism - Definition: - Buyer deposits GST to government if seller is unregistered but buyer is registered. - Significance: - Associated with GST, similar to E-way bill mechanism. - E-way Bill System (2018 Onwards) - Definition: - Electronic document generated on GSTN portal for goods movement (≥₹50,000) intra-state or inter-state. - Benefits: - Reduces bribery, delays, and harassment at checkposts, ensuring hassle-free transport. - E-Invoice (2022 Onwards) - Comparison: -
AspectE-BillE-Invoice
GeneratorTransport companyBusiness selling goods/services
ObjectiveEase transportTax surveillance, prevent fake ITC
- Implementation: - Announced 2020, postponed due to COVID, effective 2022. - Compensation to States: Why - Rationale: - GST, a destination-based tax, shifts revenue to consuming states, reducing income for industrialized states (e.g., Gujarat loses SGST on cars sold in UP). - States feared revenue loss from VAT subsumption into GST. - Compensation to States: How - Mechanism: - GST Compensation to States Act, 2017, imposes GST Compensation Cess on luxury/sin goods (e.g., pan masala 60%, tobacco, vehicles 3-22%). - Cess funds compensate states for revenue shortfall (14% annual growth over 2015 VAT base) for 5 years (2017-2022). - Formula: - Shortfall = Projected Revenue (14% CAGR from 2015 VAT) – Actual SGST. - GST Compensation & Back-to-Back Loans - Issue (2020): - COVID lockdown reduced SGST; states demanded ₹2.35 lakh crore compensation. - Union cited “Act of God” (COVID) to limit liability to ₹97,000 crore; Attorney General ruled full compensation mandatory. - Solution: - States accepted modified Option 1: Union facilitated ₹1.10 lakh crore in back-to-back loans. - Back-to-Back Loans: Beneficiary States - Beneficiaries: - States/UTs with SGST shortfall (e.g., Andhra Pradesh, Bihar, Delhi, J&K). - Excluded: Some North-Eastern states with no shortfall. - GST Compensation Cess Extended - Extension: - Originally set to expire June 30, 2022; extended to March 31, 2026, due to COVID disruptions. - GST-Related Organizations - Group of Ministers (GoM): - Temporary committees under GST Council to address specific issues (e.g., 2021 GoM under Meghalaya CM reviewed COVID-related GST rates). - National Anti-Profiteering Authority (NAA): - Established under CGST Act, 2017, to penalize businesses not passing ITC benefits to consumers (e.g., Dominos, Nestle). - Ceased in 2022; functions transferred to Competition Commission of India. - Authority for Advance Ruling (AAR): - Provides advance GST rate clarifications to businesses (e.g., Amul’s camel milk: 0% or 12% GST). - Appellate Authority for Advance Ruling (AAAR) handles appeals. - GST Appellate Tribunal (GSTAT, 2023): - Composition: President (SC/HC judge), 1 judicial member, 2 technical members (Union, State). - Reduces case pendency in HCs/SC; handles CGST, SGST, IGST, UTGST disputes. - Customs, Excise, and Service Tax Appellate Tribunal (CESTAT): - Handles appeals for non-GST indirect taxes (customs, excise). - GST Fitment Committee: - Revenue officials review GST rate change proposals; recommendations non-binding (e.g., rejected tobacco GST reduction in 2023). - GSTN Network: - Not-for-profit company (50% Union, 50% States since 2018) managing GST portal for registration, ITC, E-way bills. - Supports tax compliance, data analytics; integrates with RBI’s Public Credit Registry. - Project Saksham (2016): - CBIC’s digital integration of ACES (excise), SWIFT (customs), and GSTN portals. - HSN and SAC Codes: - HSN (goods, e.g., tobacco: 24039930, 28% GST) and SAC (services, e.g., coaching: 999293, 18% GST) codes aid digital tax administration. - PAN vs. GSTIN vs. Aadhaar - Comparison: -

- Notes: - PAN mandatory for GSTIN; Aadhaar linkage with PAN required by June 30, 2023. - PAN 2.0 & TAN 2.0 (2024) - Reforms: - Single portal for PAN/TAN services, paperless applications, free e-PAN, QR-coded cards. - Penalty: ₹10,000 for non-compliance or multiple PANs. - GST: Benefits - Unified tax structure with standard rates, minimal cess/surcharges. - GSTN portal and E-way bills reduce harassment, enhancing ease of doing business. - ITC reduces cascading effects, lowers production costs, and deters tax evasion. - Anti-profiteering ensures consumer benefits (e.g., reduced MRP). - Uniform rates across states prevent rate arbitrage, fostering a national market. - Supports ancillarisation, job creation, and Make in India. - GST Benefit: Zero-Rated Exports - 0% IGST on exports; ITC refundable, reducing production costs and enhancing global competitiveness. - GST: Ease of Doing Business (EoD) – Decriminalisation - Measures (Budget-2023): -

- Pillar 2B1: Finance Commission - Definition: - The Finance Commission is a constitutional body established under Article 280 of the Indian Constitution to recommend the distribution of financial resources between the Union and State governments, ensuring fiscal federalism by addressing taxation and expenditure imbalances. - Context: - Fiscal federalism involves dividing taxation and expenditure responsibilities across government levels (Union, States, Local Bodies). States have significant expenditure responsibilities under the 7th Schedule but limited taxation powers, making the Finance Commission critical for equitable revenue sharing. - Significance for India: - The Finance Commission promotes fiscal equity, supports state development, and ensures financial stability by recommending tax devolution, grants, and fiscal discipline measures, aligning with India’s goals of cooperative federalism and balanced regional growth. - Introduction to Finance Commission - Definition: - The Finance Commission is a quasi-judicial body constituted by the President of India every five years (or earlier) under Article 280 to recommend the division of net proceeds of taxes between the Union and States, grants-in-aid, and measures to augment state finances. - Structure: - Comprises a Chairman and four members, appointed by the President, eligible for re-appointment. Recommendations are advisory but rarely rejected by the government. - Functions: - Recommends vertical tax devolution (Union to States) and horizontal distribution (among States). - Suggests grants-in-aid to States for revenue deficits, local bodies, and disaster management. - Advises on augmenting State Consolidated Funds for Panchayats and Municipalities. - Addresses additional matters specified in Terms of Reference (ToR) by the President, such as fiscal discipline or specific state needs. - Membership: 15th vs. 16th Finance Commission -
Aspect 15th Finance Commission 16th Finance Commission

- Note: - The Constitution mandates FC formation every five years, but gaps (e.g., 6 years between 10th and 11th, 15th and 16th) occur due to the time taken for analysis and recommendations. - Terms of Reference: 15th vs. 16th Finance Commission - Definition: - Terms of Reference (ToR) are directives from the President outlining the scope of FC recommendations, including tax devolution, grants, and fiscal policy measures. -
Terms of Reference 15th Finance Commission 16th Finance Commission
Tax Devolution and Grants Vertical devolution and horizontal distribution of Union taxes (excluding cess, surcharge, IGST); grants-in-aid to States Same as 15th FC
Local Bodies Augment State Consolidated Funds for Panchayats and Municipalities Same as 15th FC
Disaster Management Suggest funding for disaster management Same as 15th FC
Performance Incentives Reward States for population control, tax collection efficiency (incorporated in horizontal devolution formula) Propose performance-based incentives/grants
Union Expenditure Needs Consider Union’s expenses for defense, internal security, infrastructure, railways, climate change, and UTs (reduced States’ share from 42% to 41% for J&K, Ladakh) Not mentioned
National Development Align with programs like Swachh Bharat, Digital India; conditional grants for water/sanitation (criticized by non-BJP States) Not mentioned
Census Data Use Census-2011 data (criticized by Southern States for penalizing population control) Not mentioned
Fiscal Discipline Recommend measures for Union and State fiscal consolidation (e.g., curb populist schemes); no specific formula provided Not mentioned
Defense/Security Funds Suggest non-lapsable funds for defense and internal security Not mentioned
J&K Award Address J&K needs post-reorganization (2019) Not mentioned
- Vertical Tax Devolution - Definition: - Vertical tax devolution is the process of distributing the divisible pool of Union taxes (excluding cess, surcharge, and IGST) between the Union and States, as recommended by the Finance Commission. - Historical Trend: -
Finance Commission Chairman Period States’ Share
12th FC C. Rangarajan 2005–10 30.5%
13th FC Vijay Kelkar 2010–15 32%
14th FC Y.V. Reddy 2015–20 42%
15th FC N.K. Singh 2020–26 41% (1% reserved for J&K, Ladakh UTs)
- Note: - The 15th FC reduced the States’ share to 41% to account for Union expenses in managing J&K and Ladakh as Union Territories post-Article 370 revocation. - Horizontal Tax Devolution - Definition: - Horizontal tax devolution determines how the States’ share of Union taxes is distributed among individual States based on a formula considering economic and demographic factors. - 15th FC Formula: -
Component Description Weight
Income Distance Measures a State’s per capita GSDP relative to the highest (Haryana as benchmark); poorer States receive more funds 45%
Area Larger geographical area receives more funds 15%
Population Based on Census-2011; higher population receives more funds 15%
Demographic Performance Rewards States with lower Total Fertility Rate (TFR) to incentivize population control 12.5%
Forest and Ecology States with more forest cover receive more funds due to opportunity costs (e.g., restricted industrial development) 10%
Tax Effort States improving per capita tax collection over the last three years receive more funds 2.5%
- 14th FC Formula: -
Component Description Weight
Population (1971 Census) Higher population receives more funds 17%
Demographic Change (2011 Census) Considers population growth and migration 10%
Income Distance Based on per capita income (GSDP/population); poorer States receive more funds 50%
Area Larger area receives more funds 15%
Forest Cover More forest cover receives more funds due to opportunity costs 8%
- Key States’ Shares (15th FC): - Top: - Uttar Pradesh (17.931%), Bihar (10.061%), Madhya Pradesh (7.886%), West Bengal (7.519%), Maharashtra (6.135%). - Bottom: - Goa (0.386%), Sikkim (0.388%), Mizoram (0.506%), Nagaland (0.573%), Tripura (0.709%). - Union Territories: - 0% in horizontal devolution; funded directly by Union. - Union Territories: J&K and Ladakh - Context: - Until the 10th FC, FCs prescribed revenue-sharing formulas for Union Territories (UTs). Since the 11th FC, the Union Ministry of Finance decides UT funding at its discretion. - Post-Article 370 revocation (31 October 2019), Jammu & Kashmir was split into two UTs: J&K and Ladakh. - Provisions: - J&K Reorganization Act, 2019, mandates that J&K and Ladakh receive funds equivalent to J&K’s share under the 14th FC (31/10/2019–31/3/2020), distributed based on population and other parameters. - The 15th FC allocated 1% of the divisible pool to the Union for J&K and Ladakh, reducing States’ share to 41%. - Grants from Union to States - Definition: - Grants-in-aid are non-repayable funds provided by the Union to States to address revenue deficits, support local bodies, disaster management, and sector-specific needs. - 14th FC Grants: - Local Bodies Grants: - For Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs) and Urban Local Bodies (ULBs), split into basic grants and performance-based grants (10–20%). - Disaster Management Grants: - For National and State Disaster Response Funds. - Post-Devolution Revenue Deficit Grants: - For 14 States with revenue deficits post-tax devolution. - 15th FC Grants (2021–26): -
Type Amount (₹ Lakh Crore) Description
Local Bodies Grants 4.36 Rural (2.36) and Urban (1.00) for PRIs and ULBs
Post-Devolution Revenue Deficit Grants 2.94 For 14 States (e.g., Assam, Himachal Pradesh) to cover deficits after tax devolution
Disaster Management Grants 1.90 National Disaster Funds (0.68) and State Disaster Funds (1.22)
Sector-Specific Grants 1.00+ Health, education, agriculture, rural roads, judiciary, statistics, aspirational districts
State-Specific Grants 0.50 For tourism, historical monuments, infrastructure, water
Defense and Internal Security Fund 2.38 Non-lapsable fund for Union’s defense and security needs
Total 10.00+ Comprehensive support for States and Union
- Post-Devolution Revenue Deficit Grants - Definition: - Grants provided to States with revenue deficits after receiving their share of Union taxes to ensure fiscal stability. - Mechanism: - Calculated as: State’s Revenue Expenditure – Revenue Income – Union Tax Share = Deficit. - Example: Andhra Pradesh’s 2020–21 deficit (₹41,000 cr expenditure – ₹35,000 cr tax share = ₹6,000 cr) is covered by a ₹6,000 cr grant. - Eligibility: - 14 States, including Assam, Himachal Pradesh, Manipur, and Meghalaya. - Special Grants - Definition: - One-time grants for States receiving less in 15th FC devolution (2020–21) compared to 14th FC to prevent perceived injustice. - Details: - 2020–21: - ₹6,764 cr for Karnataka, Telangana, Mizoram. - 2021–26: - No special grants announced. - Local Bodies Grants - Definition: - Grants to strengthen Panchayati Raj Institutions and Urban Local Bodies, supporting local governance and service delivery. - Structure: - Basic Grants: - For general needs under the 11th Schedule (except salaries). - Performance-Based Grants: - For sanitation, open defecation-free status, and waste management. - 15th FC Allocation (2021–26): - Rural: - ₹2.36 lakh crore. - Urban: - ₹1.00 lakh crore. - Disaster Management Grants - Definition: - Funds allocated under the Disaster Management Act, 2005, for National and State Disaster Risk Management Funds. - Structure: -
Fund Allocation (₹ Lakh Crore) Breakdown
National Disaster Risk Management Fund (NDRMF) 0.68 80% National Disaster Response Fund (NDRF), 20% National Disaster Mitigation Fund (NDMF)
State Disaster Risk Management Fund (SDRMF) 1.22 Based on past disasters, risk exposure (area, population); States contribute to SDRMF
- Sector-Specific Grants - Definition: - Grants for targeted sectors to address critical development needs, often with performance-based incentives. - 15th FC Allocations (2021–26): -
Sector Amount (₹ Crore) Description
Healthcare (Local Bodies) 70,000 Convert Primary Health Centres to Health and Wellness Centres
Healthcare (Critical Care) 15,000 Establish critical care hospitals (ICU, ventilators, dialysis)
Healthcare (Training) 13,000 Train allied healthcare workforce (lab technicians, ASHA workers)
Healthcare (Doctor Training) 2,000 Doctor training courses in district hospitals
Agriculture Reforms 45,000 Groundwater conservation, oilseed/pulse production, agricultural exports
Rural Roads 27,000 Maintenance of PMGSY roads
Judiciary 10,000 Extra courts for property cases, marginalized groups, POSCO cases
Higher Education 6,000 Online learning, regional language course translations
School Education Statistics 4,800 Data collection for education
- Additional Recommendations: - Union and States to spend 2.5% of GDP on healthcare by 2025. - Propose All India Medical and Health Service under All-India Services Act, 1951, for doctor recruitment via UPSC. - Address concentration of medical colleges and super-specialty hospitals in Western/Southern India. - Defense and Internal Security Fund - Definition: - A non-lapsable fund in the Public Account of India for Union’s defense and internal security modernization. - Details: - Amount: - ₹2.38 lakh crore (2021–26). - Purpose: - Modernization Fund for Defence and Internal Security (MFDIS) to support defense infrastructure and security needs without annual parliamentary re-approval. - Permanent Status for Finance Commission - Context: - Proposals to make the Finance Commission a permanent body to ensure continuous fiscal coordination have been discussed but not implemented. - Current Status: - Remains a temporary body constituted every five years or earlier, as per Article 280. - Finance Commission vs. Planning Commission vs. NITI Aayog -
Aspect Finance Commission Planning Commission NITI Aayog
Status Constitutional body (Article 280) Non-constitutional, non-statutory (executive resolution, 1951–2014) Non-constitutional, non-statutory (executive resolution, 2015)
Formation 1951 (1st FC, K.C. Neogy) 1951; dissolved 2014 2015
Role Recommends tax devolution, grants, fiscal discipline Allocated funds for Centrally Sponsored Schemes, State Five-Year Plans (Gadgil-Mukherjee formula) Policy think tank, monitors schemes, does not allocate funds
Key Outputs Vertical/horizontal devolution formulas 12 Five-Year Plans (1951–2017) Three-Year Action Agenda, Seven-Year Strategy, Fifteen-Year Vision (2017–32)
- Special Category States - Definition: - States with unique challenges (e.g., hilly terrain, low population density, strategic border location) designated by the National Development Council (NDC) for additional financial support. - Criteria: - Hilly and difficult terrain. - Low population density or significant tribal population. - Strategic border location. - Economic/infrastructural backwardness. - Non-viable state finances. - Examples: - Eight North-Eastern States, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand (J&K removed post-Article 370). - Telangana granted status in 2014. - Benefits: - Higher Union contribution in Centrally Sponsored Schemes (e.g., 90:10 ratio). - Tax incentives for industries. - Previously, FCs assigned extra weightage; stopped by 14th FC. - Current Status: - 15th FC: - Special category status not part of ToR; States receive benefits at Union’s discretion. - Vertical Fiscal Imbalance - Definition: - A mismatch between revenue and expenditure responsibilities across government levels, making States dependent on Union funds. - Factors Responsible: -
Union States
- Earns over ₹20 lakh crore from direct taxes (income, corporation).
- Collects over ₹2 lakh crore from customs duty (States lack this power).
- Cess and surcharges not shared with States. 		- Limited by Constitution to ₹2,500 professional tax per year.
- Avoid taxing agricultural income for electoral reasons.
- Delays in GST compensation cess release by Union.
- Fiscal Health Index and Panchayat Devolution Index - Fiscal Health Index (NITI Aayog, Jan 2025): - Definition: - Ranks States on fiscal performance based on quality of expenditure, revenue mobilization, fiscal prudence, debt index, and debt sustainability. - Top-5: - Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Goa, Jharkhand, Gujarat. - Bottom-5: - Haryana, Kerala, West Bengal, Andhra Pradesh, Punjab. - Panchayat Devolution Index (Ministry of Panchayati Raj, Feb 2025): - Definition: - Monitors transfer of funds and powers to Panchayats under the 73rd Amendment, based on framework, functions, finances, functionaries, capacity enhancement, and accountability. - Top-3: - Karnataka, Kerala, Tamil Nadu. - Conducted by: - Indian Institute of Public Administration (IIPA, Delhi). - Pillar 2B2: Black Money & Allied Issues - Definition: - Black money refers to income or transactions that are taxable but deliberately concealed from tax authorities to evade taxes. It operates within a parallel economy, undermining fiscal integrity and reducing government revenue for welfare and development. Unlike legal tax planning (e.g., investing in LIC or PPF for deductions), which is ethical and lawful, black money involves illegal practices like tax evasion or money laundering. - Context: - Black money distorts economic equity, fuels corruption, and hampers India’s ability to fund public services. India has implemented stringent laws, international agreements, and digital reforms to combat black money, aligning with global efforts to enhance financial transparency. - Significance for India: - Tackling black money is crucial for increasing tax compliance, boosting revenue for infrastructure and social programs, and strengthening India’s global financial standing. It supports economic stability and aligns with initiatives like Atmanirbhar Bharat by fostering a transparent, inclusive economy. - Black Money → Notable Organisations - Definition: - Several specialized agencies in India and globally work to detect, prevent, and penalize activities related to black money, tax evasion, and money laundering, ensuring compliance with financial regulations. - Key Organisations: -
Organisation Details
Enforcement Directorate (ED) Established in 1956, headquartered in Delhi under the Ministry of Finance (Department of Revenue). It enforces the Prevention of Money Laundering Act (PMLA, 2002), Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA, 1999), and Conservation of Foreign Exchange and Prevention of Smuggling Activities Act (COFEPOSA, 1974). The ED conducts investigations, searches, seizures, and arrests to combat money laundering, forex violations, and smuggling.
Directorate of Revenue Intelligence (DRI) Operates under the Ministry of Finance (Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs - CBIC). It investigates smuggling and illegal activities related to customs, narcotics, wildlife, and arms, ensuring compliance with relevant laws.
Central Bureau of Narcotics (CBN) Headquartered in Gwalior under the Department of Revenue (CBIC). Also known as the National Opium Agency, it supervises opium and poppy cultivation in Madhya Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh, and Rajasthan and combats drug trafficking under the Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances Act, 1985.
Central Economic Intelligence Bureau (CEIB) Under the Ministry of Finance, CEIB serves as a central repository for economic offense data, receiving inputs from agencies like CBI, ED, SFIO, and state police to coordinate intelligence on financial crimes.
Economic Intelligence Council (EIC) Chaired by the Finance Minister with the Director General of CEIB as member-secretary. Includes representatives from CBDT, CBIC, ED, SEBI, RBI, and R&AW to strategize and oversee economic intelligence efforts.
Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU-India) Established in 2004, it analyzes suspicious financial transactions (domestic and cross-border) and reports to the EIC, aiding in the detection of money laundering and terror financing.
Customs, Excise and Service Tax Appellate Tribunal (CESTAT) Handles appeals related to Union’s indirect taxes (excluding GST). The appeal process involves Junior Officers (Stage 1), Commissioner (Stage 2), CESTAT (Stage 3), and High Court/Supreme Court (Stage 4).
Financial Action Task Force (FATF) An international body (HQ: Paris, est. 1989 by G7) to combat money laundering and terror financing. India joined in 2010. Maintains a greylist (e.g., Syria, Yemen) and blacklist (e.g., Iran, North Korea) for non-compliant nations. Mauritius and Pakistan were removed from the greylist in 2022.
Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) Established in 1961 (HQ: Paris), OECD promotes international cooperation on economic and tax policies, known for Base Erosion and Profit Shifting (BEPS) norms. India is not a member but collaborates on tax initiatives.
- Tax Evasion (Hiding Income/Transactions) - Definition: - Tax evasion involves illegally concealing income or transactions from tax authorities to avoid paying taxes, a criminal act punishable by fines and imprisonment. - Prevention of Money Laundering Act (PMLA, 2002): - Enacted following the 1998 UN General Assembly declaration on money laundering, PMLA empowers the ED to conduct searches, seizures, arrests, and impose penalties. Cases are adjudicated by the PMLA Adjudicating Authority, with appeals to the PMLA Appellate Tribunal, High Court, and Supreme Court. - Regulators like RBI, SEBI, and IRDAI enforce KYC (Know Your Customer) and AML (Anti-Money Laundering) norms for banks and NBFCs. For instance, in 2013, a Cobrapost sting operation exposed ICICI, HDFC, and Axis Bank flouting norms, leading to RBI penalties. - PMLA Reporting Norms for Crypto-Exchanges (2023): - Crypto-exchanges (e.g., Unocoin, Coinswitch) must comply with KYC requirements, collecting ID proof (PAN/Aadhaar) and verifying addresses. Suspicious transactions must be reported to authorities under PMLA, enhancing transparency in cryptocurrency trading. - Burden of Proof & PMLA - Definition: - Burden of proof refers to the obligation to substantiate claims with evidence. In standard criminal cases, the prosecution bears this burden, but PMLA introduces a reverse burden of proof, requiring the accused to prove their innocence. -
Aspect Standard Burden Reverse Burden (PMLA)
Responsibility Prosecution must prove the accused committed the crime. Accused must prove they did not commit the crime.
Examples Indian Penal Code (IPC), Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS). PMLA (e.g., Rahul Gandhi in National Herald case), NDPS Act (e.g., Aryan Khan case).
- PMLA Amendments via Finance Bill (2015-19): - Enhanced ED’s powers for summons, arrests, and raids. The reverse burden makes bail nearly impossible, as the accused must prove innocence. - Amendments were passed via Finance Bills (Money Bills) to bypass Rajya Sabha scrutiny, raising concerns about fundamental rights violations. In 2022, the Supreme Court upheld the amendments, but in 2023, CJI D.Y. Chandrachud announced a seven-judge bench to revisit the issue. - Undisclosed Foreign Income & Assets Act (UFIA, 2015): - Mandates Indian residents to disclose foreign assets (e.g., Dubai property, Swiss bank accounts) and income (e.g., from shell companies in tax havens) in tax returns. - Foreign income is taxed at 30% with no deductions, exemptions, or rebates. Violations attract penalties and up to 7 years imprisonment. Company officials are liable unless they prove ignorance of violations. - Empowers the Union to sign tax information exchange agreements with other nations. - Benami Transactions Prohibition Act (BTPA, 1988, amended 2016): - Targets properties registered in the name of relatives, staff, or fictitious persons to evade taxes (e.g., Misa Bharti’s alleged farmhouse purchase in 2018). - Administered by the Income Tax Department, violations lead to property confiscation, penalties, and jail. Cases follow the PMLA adjudication process. - Tax Evasion → Government Announcements - Tax Surrender Schemes: - Allow tax evaders to declare undisclosed income, pay taxes and penalties, and avoid prosecution by the Income Tax Department (though police may pursue criminal cases like narcotics). -
Scheme Details
Income Declaration Scheme (IDS, 2016) From June to September 2016, evaders paid ~45% (30% tax + surcharge + penalty) of undisclosed income. Declared ~Rs. 67,000 crore in black money.
Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Yojana (PMGKY, 2016-17) Post-demonetization (Dec 2016–Apr 2017), ~50% of undisclosed income was taxed (tax + penalty + cess), with 25% deposited in a zero-interest RBI scheme for 4 years. Funds supported irrigation, housing, and health. Declared ~Rs. 5,000 crore, less successful.
Sabka Vishwas Legacy Dispute Resolution Scheme (2019) Allowed businesses to settle service tax and excise duty disputes by admitting fault, with tax officials waiving penalties/late fees to avoid prolonged litigation.
- Vivad se Vishwas Scheme (Budget 2020): - Targets direct tax disputes (income tax, corporation tax) pending as of 31 January 2020 before appellate forums (IT Commissioner, ITAT, HC, SC). - Taxpayers pay 50–100% of disputed tax (case-specific) for full waiver of interest and penalties. Extended beyond March 2020 under Atmanirbhar Bharat, relaunched in Budget 2024. Not applicable for criminal prosecutions or foreign black money cases. - Dispute Resolution Committee (DRC, Budget 2021): - For taxpayers with taxable income up to Rs. 50 lakh and disputed income up to Rs. 10 lakh. The committee can reduce penalties or grant immunity, reducing legal costs and time compared to regular appeals. Operates facelessly online for transparency. - Interim Budget 2024 – Amnesty for Small Taxpayers: - Withdraws small direct tax cases: Rs. 25,000 (1962–2010) and Rs. 10,000 (2010–15), benefiting ~1 crore taxpayers by clearing old disputes. - Other Initiatives (Pre-2020): - Banking Cash Transaction Tax (BCTT, 2005–09): - Levied 0.1% tax on cash withdrawals to promote a less-cash economy and track transactions. Withdrawn due to limited impact. - Special Investigation Team (SIT) on Black Money (2014): - Chaired by Justice M.B. Shah, recommended measures to curb black money in India and overseas (e.g., P-Notes). Supreme Court mandated implementation. - Operation Clean Money (2017): - Post-demonetization, the Income Tax Department verified large bank deposits to identify tax evaders. - Restrictions on Cash Transactions (Budget 2017): - Penalty of 100% on cash transactions above Rs. 2 lakh per day/event. Political parties pay tax on cash donations above Rs. 2,000, encouraging digital payments (cheques, NEFT, electoral bonds). - Tax Avoidance - Definition: - Tax avoidance involves legally disclosing income and transactions but exploiting loopholes to minimize tax liability. While not always illegal, it is often considered unethical (e.g., Bollywood stars registering companies in tax havens). - Double Taxation Avoidance Agreement (DTAA): - Bilateral treaties to prevent taxing the same income twice in different countries. For example, the India-Mauritius DTAA (1982) exempted Mauritius-based investors from Indian Capital Gains Tax (CGT). - Loophole Exploitation: - India’s CGT (12.5–20%) was higher than Mauritius (~0–3%), leading to round-tripping: Indian money sent via hawala to Mauritius shell companies, reinvested in India to avoid CGT. The India-Singapore DTAA had similar issues. - Amendments (2016): - Modi government revised DTAA with Mauritius and Singapore, subjecting investments to Indian taxes to curb round-tripping. - Principal Purpose Test (PPT, 2024): - Introduced in the India-Mauritius DTAA (March 2024), PPT denies tax benefits if tax avoidance is the primary purpose of a transaction, reducing BEPS and round-tripping. This led to foreign investors exiting Indian markets, impacting SENSEX. - International Law: Monism vs. Dualism: -
Aspect Monism Dualism (India’s Approach)
Supremacy International law prevails over domestic law. International and domestic laws are separate; international law applies only after domestic law amendments.
Application International law applies automatically without local law changes. Requires notification/amendment under domestic laws (e.g., Income Tax Act for DTAA).
- 2023 Supreme Court Ruling: - DTAA applies only after notification under India’s Income Tax Act, reinforcing India’s dualist approach. - Tax Avoidance via Non-Resident Status: -
Status Tax on Indian Income Tax on Global Income
Ordinarily Resident Yes Yes
Non-Resident Yes No (unless DTAA alters)
- Budget 2020 Changes: - Redefined residency: Non-residents must stay outside India for 246 days (previously 182 days) to avoid global income tax, discouraging tax avoidance through prolonged absence. - Place of Effective Management (POEM): - Determines a company’s tax residency based on where key management decisions are made. For example, a company operating in India but managed from a tax haven may be taxed as an Indian resident to prevent tax avoidance. - Base Erosion and Profit Shifting (BEPS): - MNCs shift profits to tax havens to reduce tax liability (e.g., registering in Luxembourg). India signed the OECD’s Multilateral Convention (MLI) to implement BEPS prevention measures, ensuring taxes are paid where profits are earned. - Transfer Pricing & Authority for Advance Rulings (AAR): - Transfer pricing involves setting prices for transactions between related entities (e.g., parent and subsidiary) to shift profits to low-tax jurisdictions. AAR provides advance rulings to clarify tax liabilities, reducing disputes. - General Anti-Avoidance Rules (GAAR, 2017): - Introduced under the Income Tax Act to empower tax officials to challenge suspected tax avoidance (distinct from evasion, covered by PMLA/UFIA). Critics argued GAAR could lead to tax terrorism, delaying its implementation until 2017. - Safe Harbour Rules: - Provide legal immunity for certain transactions (e.g., transfer pricing for foreign investors) to avoid harassment by tax officials, ensuring ease of doing business. - Retrospective Taxation - Definition: - Retrospective taxation involves levying taxes on past transactions that were not taxable at the time they occurred (e.g., taxing a 2007 transaction in 2012). - Vodafone Case: - Vodafone acquired Hutchison’s Indian assets via a Cayman Islands shell company in 2007, avoiding Indian CGT. In 2012, the Income Tax Department demanded retrospective CGT, leading to a legal dispute. - Cairn Energy Case: - Similar to Vodafone, Cairn faced retrospective CGT demands. In 2020, a Netherlands Arbitration Court ordered India to pay $1.2 billion for tax harassment. Cairn attempted to seize Indian assets abroad (e.g., Air India planes). - Taxation Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2021: - Ended retrospective taxation for indirect asset transfers before May 28, 2012. Companies like Cairn and Vodafone dropped foreign and Indian lawsuits, and India withdrew tax demands (2021). - Global Minimum Tax - Definition: - A global framework to ensure MNCs pay a minimum tax rate (15%) on profits, preventing tax avoidance via low-tax jurisdictions (tax havens like Cayman Islands, 0.2% tax). - G7 Framework (2021): - Agreed at the Cornwall G7 Summit, companies must pay taxes in countries where they operate, with a 15% minimum corporate tax to discourage tax-reduction races. India supported; Trump withdrew USA in 2025, citing economic sovereignty. - OECD Two-Pillar Solution: -
Aspect Pillar 1 Pillar 2
Applicability ~100 largest MNCs with over €750 million revenue. Any company with over €750 million revenue.
Implications Reallocates a portion of profits to countries where goods/services are sold for taxation. Requires removal of digital taxes (e.g., India’s Equalisation Levy). Imposes a 15% minimum tax on profits, with Qualified Domestic Minimum Top-up Taxes (QDMTT) to ensure compliance.
- Reforms to Reduce Tax Terrorism/Harassment - Definition: - Tax terrorism refers to undue pressure by tax authorities on honest taxpayers, often through excessive demands or harassment, deterring compliance and investment. - Pre-Filled Online Forms: - The Income Tax Department auto-populates tax returns with data from banks, NBFCs, stock exchanges, and employers’ TDS submissions, saving time and improving accuracy. - Updated Return Form (Budget 2022): - Allows taxpayers to correct errors in filed returns within two years, reducing disputes and penalties. - Faceless Interaction: - Random, computerized allocation of cases to tax officials without disclosing their identity. Assessments and appeals are conducted online (email, video-conference), minimizing harassment and bribery. - Document Identification Number (DIN, 2019): - All tax-related documents (e.g., notices, summons) carry a computer-generated DIN. Invalid without DIN, preventing fake notices and ensuring transparency. Distinct from Director Identification Number (Companies Act). - Additional Measures: - Transparent Taxation Platform (2020): - Includes Taxpayers’ Charter, faceless assessments, and automated case allocation. - Turant Customs (2021): - CBIC’s faceless, paperless, contactless customs processes for importers/exporters. - Taxpayers’ Charter (Budget 2020) - Definition: - A citizens’ charter outlines government commitments to service delivery. The Taxpayers’ Charter, released in August 2020 by CBDT, defines rights and obligations to reduce harassment and enhance trust. - Commitments to Taxpayers: - Impartial, courteous, and professional treatment. - Presumption of honesty unless proven otherwise. - Timely, accurate information on tax rules and grievance redressal. - Collection of correct tax amounts, respecting privacy and confidentiality. - Accountability for officials’ wrongful actions. - Option to appoint representatives (e.g., tax lawyers) for compliance. - Reduced compliance costs (e.g., fewer forms, simplified processes). - Expectations from Taxpayers: - Honesty, timely tax payments, accurate record-keeping, informed compliance, and prompt response to notices. - Tax Ombudsman - Definition: - An ombudsman is an independent authority addressing grievances against government agencies. A tax ombudsman resolves taxpayer complaints against tax authorities. - Indian Experience: - Direct Tax Ombudsman (2003–2019) and Indirect Tax Ombudsman (2011–2019) were abolished. The 2021 Economic Survey recommended reinstating a tax ombudsman with statutory powers. - Online Portals/Apps to Reduce Harassment: - Aaykar Setu (2017): - CBDT’s mobile app for income tax and TDS refunds. - CBIC Portals: - ICEGATE (e-services for customs duty), ICEDASH (real-time customs clearance data), ATITHI (advance customs declarations for travelers). - Transparent Taxation (2020): - Integrates Taxpayers’ Charter, faceless systems, and automated case allocation. - Turant Customs (2021): - Streamlines customs processes for efficiency and transparency. - Income Tax Return (ITR) Forms: - Used to disclose income and pay direct taxes. Types include ITR-1 (Sahaj) for salaried employees, ITR-4 (Sugam) for freelancers, and ITR-2 for complex incomes. In 2022, 7.51 crore ITRs were filed; in 2023, 8.18 crore. - E-Governance Initiatives: - Non-Filers Monitoring System (NMS): - Tracks high-value transactions (e.g., via TDS/TCS, e-commerce) to identify non-filers for notices. - TRACES Portal: - Manages TDS/TCS deposits and refunds, delivered via SBI (Refund Banker). - Project Insight (2017): - Data mining platform by L&T Infotech to track tax evaders. - Project Saksham (2016): - CBIC’s digital re-engineering for GST compliance, not directly for black money. - CASS (Computer Assisted Scrutiny Selection): - Selects cases for raids/prosecution based on data analysis. - Direct Tax Code (DTC) Task Force (2017–2019) - Overview: - Formed under CBDT’s Arbind Modi (later Akhilesh Ranjan), the task force proposed replacing the Income Tax Act, 1961, with a simpler DTC. - Recommendations: - Simplify the Income Tax Act for clarity and compliance. - Equalize tax rates for domestic and foreign companies to enhance ease of doing business. - Increase tax slabs to four (10%, 20%, 30%, 35% for incomes above Rs. 2 crore). - Abolish Dividend Distribution Tax (implemented in Budget 2020). - Establish a Litigation Management Unit for efficient tax dispute resolution. - Provide tax relief for startups to encourage entrepreneurship. - Tax Harassment Measures (Budget 2024): - Reduced TDS rates for certain transactions. - Avoided reopening very old cases. - Relaunched Vivad se Vishwas for out-of-court settlements. - Income Tax Bill 2025 - Overview: - Aims to replace the Income Tax Act, 1961 (5.12 lakh words) with a simpler law (2.6 lakh words) by compressing text into tables, reducing chapters and sections. - Notable Changes: -
Before After
Assessment Year (AY) assesses income from the previous financial year (e.g., FY 2024–25 assessed in AY 2025–26). Simplified to “Tax Year” (12-month period starting April 1).
Old tax regime slabs written in law. Not written, increasing flexibility.
Property defined as land, buildings, shares, gold, etc. Includes virtual digital assets (e.g., bitcoins, NFTs) for 30% CGT.
Limited search/seizure powers for digital assets. Tax officers can search/seize digital spaces (e.g., email servers, cloud, apps).
- Note: - Bill not yet passed; changes may occur. Critics argue it focuses on compression rather than substantial reform. - Taxation → Global Treaties, Agreements, and Indexes - Tax Information Exchange Agreement (TIEA): - Bilateral agreements for sharing financial data to detect tax evasion/avoidance. India signed TIEA with Marshall Islands (2019), with CBDT as the nodal agency. - USA’s Foreign Account Tax Compliance Act (FATCA, 2010): - Requires foreign financial institutions (e.g., Indian banks) to report American-held assets to US tax authorities, curbing tax evasion by US citizens. - Tax Inspectors Without Borders (TIWB): - A UNDP-OECD initiative for training tax inspectors. India trains Eswatini’s tax officials, enhancing global tax enforcement cooperation. - Global Financial Secrecy Index (2022): - Published by Tax Justice Network, ranks countries on financial secrecy and tax avoidance opportunities. USA ranks 1st, India 36th, losing over $10 billion annually due to MNC tax abuse. - Black Money → Demonetisation - Definition: - Demonetisation is the withdrawal of specific currency notes as legal tender, rendering them invalid for transactions. In India, governed by the RBI Act, 1934, with notifications by the Department of Economic Affairs. - Historical Instances: - 1946 (Rs. 500 notes), 1978 (Rs. 1,000, Rs. 5,000, Rs. 10,000 notes), and 2016 (Rs. 500, Rs. 1,000 Mahatma Gandhi series notes). - Demonetisation 2016: - Announced on 8 November 2016, Specified Bank Notes (SBN: Rs. 500, Rs. 1,000) were to be deposited by 30 December 2016. From 31 December 2016, RBI ceased honoring SBN (except for NRIs with relaxed deadlines). - Specified Bank Notes (Cessation of Liabilities) Act, 2017, prohibited holding SBN except for research/numismatics in limited amounts. - Objectives: - Combat corruption, black money, counterfeiting, and terror financing. India’s high cash-to-GDP ratio (12.04%) and low soil rate for Rs. 500/1,000 notes suggested their use for hoarding black money (~Rs. 3 lakh crore). - Outcome: - ~99.3% of SBN returned to banks, destroying only Rs. 10,720 crore of black money, indicating limited success in eliminating hoarded cash. - Supreme Court Verdict (2023): - Upheld demonetisation in a 4:1 verdict, dismissing challenges by petitioners like P. Chidambaram. - Reasons for Low Tax-to-GDP Ratio in India: - Lack of civic awareness about tax payment as a duty. - Informal sector and cash-based economy enable income concealment. - Low per capita income and high poverty concentrate wealth among a few, encouraging tax evasion/avoidance. - Political funding fuels corruption and black money (e.g., politician-builder nexus). - Constitutional loopholes (e.g., no tax on agricultural income) narrow the tax base. - Ineffective direct taxes (e.g., wealth tax, gift tax) due to loopholes and evasion, leading to their abolition. - Revenue Shortfall - Definition: - Revenue shortfall occurs when actual tax collection falls below the budgeted estimate, impacting government spending and fiscal planning. - Data (in Rs. Crore): -
Year Budget Estimate (BE) Revised Estimate (RE) Outcome
2019–20 19,62,761 16,84,059 Shortfall of 2.78 lakh crore
2020–21 20,20,926 20,27,104 Surplus
2025–26 42,70,233 Pending Wait & watch
- Laffer Curve: - Proposed by economist Arthur Laffer, it suggests that excessively high tax rates reduce revenue by discouraging work or encouraging evasion. Tax cuts (e.g., Modi’s 2017 reduction of lowest income tax slab from 10% to 5%, corporate tax from 30% to 25%) can boost compliance and revenue. - Tax Buoyancy and Elasticity: -
Concept Definition Example (Economic Survey 2023)
Tax Elasticity Measures additional revenue from a tax rate increase (e.g., 5% to 15% income tax). Not specified in raw text.
Tax Buoyancy Measures tax collection growth relative to GDP growth (e.g., 11% tax growth with 10% GDP growth = 1.1 buoyancy). GST: 1.12 (high); pre-GST indirect taxes: 0.99 (low).
- Budget → Revenue Part → Non-Tax Receipts - Definition: - Non-tax receipts are government revenues from sources other than taxes, contributing to fiscal resources without direct taxation. - Notable Components: - Interest Receipts: - Earned on loans to states, railways, CPSEs, and foreign countries. Loan principal repayments are capital receipts. - Dividends and Profits: - From CPSEs, public sector banks, and RBI. Share sales (disinvestment) are capital receipts. - Goods and Services Income: - From railways, postal services, publication sales (e.g., India Yearbook), CISF fees for private airport security, spectrum/mining auctions, and commemorative coins. - Grants-in-Aid and Donations: - Contributions received by the Union (loans are capital receipts). - Non-Tax Revenue from Union Territories: - From UTs without legislatures (e.g., Chandigarh, Lakshadweep). - Budget 2025 Estimate: - Gross Tax Revenue (Union’s direct/indirect taxes + cess/surcharge): Rs. 42 lakh crore. - UT Taxes: Rs. 10,133 crore. - Net Tax Revenue (after devolution to states and disaster fund contributions): ~Rs. 28 lakh crore. - Pillar 2B3: Revenue Expenditure - Definition: - Revenue expenditure refers to government spending on the day-to-day operations and maintenance of state functions, which does not create long-term income-generating or permanent assets. It includes expenses like salaries, pensions, subsidies, and interest payments on loans, critical for sustaining administrative, social, and economic services. - Context: - Revenue expenditure forms a significant portion of India’s Union Budget, ensuring the smooth functioning of executive, judiciary, legislature, and statutory bodies while supporting welfare schemes through subsidies and grants. Efficient management is vital to balance fiscal health and public welfare. - Significance for India: - Revenue expenditure supports essential services like defense, education, and healthcare, and funds welfare programs like subsidies for food, fuel, and fertilizers. It is crucial for maintaining governance and social equity but must be controlled to prevent fiscal deficits and ensure sustainable economic growth. - Revenue Expenditure → Overview - Definition: - Revenue expenditure encompasses routine expenses that maintain government operations without creating capital assets, such as salaries, pensions, utilities (e.g., electricity, stationery), and interest payments on loans. - Characteristics: - Does not result in income-generating or permanent assets (unlike capital expenditure, which creates assets like roads or buildings). - Funds the operational costs of executive, judiciary, legislature, constitutional bodies (e.g., Election Commission), and statutory bodies (e.g., SEBI). - Includes payments for social services (health, education), economic services (agriculture, transport), and administrative functions (police, external affairs). - Key Components: - Interest Payments: - Payments on interest for loans (principal repayment is capital expenditure). This is the largest revenue expenditure, reflecting India’s debt servicing obligations. - Subsidies: - Financial assistance to reduce the burden on citizens or industries (e.g., food, fertilizer, fuel subsidies). - Salaries and Pensions: - Compensation for government employees and retirees, including dearness allowances to offset inflation. - Grants-in-Aid: - Funds provided to states, local bodies, or institutions (e.g., disaster management, Panchayati Raj) without repayment obligations. - Defense Expenditure: - Salaries, fuel, and maintenance for armed forces (e.g., fuel for tanks, soldier salaries). - Union Territories without Legislature: - Operational expenses for UTs like Chandigarh and Lakshadweep. -
Component Details
Interest Payments Highest revenue expenditure; e.g., Budget 2024–25 allocated ~Rs. 10.5 lakh crore for interest payments.
Subsidies Includes food (~Rs. 2 lakh crore), fertilizer (~Rs. 1.6 lakh crore), and fuel subsidies as per Budget 2024–25.
Salaries & Pensions Covers central government employees and retirees; e.g., ~Rs. 5 lakh crore in Budget 2024–25.
Grants-in-Aid Finance Commission-recommended grants for states and local bodies; e.g., ~Rs. 2 lakh crore for disaster management and rural development.
Defense Revenue component includes salaries and maintenance; e.g., ~Rs. 3 lakh crore in Budget 2024–25.
- Revenue Expenditure → Subsidies - Definition: - Subsidies are financial benefits provided by the government to individuals, farmers, or industries to reduce costs, promote welfare, or support economic activities. Unlike taxes, subsidies are voluntary and not punishable if refused. - Comparison with Taxes: -
Aspect Tax Subsidy
Nature Compulsory contribution to the state; non-payment is punishable. Voluntary benefit to reduce burden; refusal is not penalized.
Benefit No direct or specific goods/services guaranteed (e.g., Mukesh Ambani’s taxes don’t ensure pothole-free roads). Provides specific benefits (e.g., Rs. 6,000 under PM-KISAN, subsidized LPG).
- Types of Subsidies - Definition: - Subsidies are categorized based on their delivery mechanism and purpose, aimed at promoting affordability, equity, or economic growth. - Types and Examples: - Direct Cash Subsidies: - Transferred directly to beneficiaries’ bank accounts (e.g., PM-KISAN: Rs. 6,000 annually for farmers; DBT-PAHAL: ~Rs. 200 per LPG cylinder). - In-Kind Subsidies: - Non-monetary benefits like free school bags, uniforms, books for poor students, free medicines in public hospitals, or free insurance schemes (e.g., Ayushman Bharat). - Indirect Subsidies: - Government pays organizations to offer goods/services at reduced rates (e.g., low fees in government colleges, subsidized kerosene, urea, or crop insurance premiums). - Implicit Subsidies: - Government policies suppress supply to raise prices for a sector’s benefit (e.g., banning American poultry imports creates a shortage, allowing Indian poultry farmers to charge higher prices, indirectly subsidizing them). - Cross-Subsidization: - One group’s higher payments offset another’s lower costs (e.g., Indian Railways charges higher freight rates to keep passenger fares affordable for the poor). - Regulatory Subsidies: - Mandated price caps by regulators (e.g., State Electricity Regulatory Commission limits electricity rates for farmers to Rs. 2–4 per unit). - Procurement Subsidies: - Government purchases at above-market prices (e.g., Food Corporation of India buys food grains at Minimum Support Price to support farmers). - Interest Subsidies (Subvention): - Government pays part of the interest on loans for farmers, MSMEs, or affordable housing (e.g., LIC’s Vaya Vandana Yojana for senior citizens). -
Type Example
Direct Cash PM-KISAN (Rs. 6,000/year), DBT-PAHAL (LPG subsidy).
In-Kind Free school supplies, Ayushman Bharat insurance.
Indirect Subsidized urea, low-cost government college fees.
Implicit Ban on poultry imports to support local farmers.
Cross-Subsidization Railways’ high freight rates to subsidize passenger fares.
Regulatory Electricity rate caps for farmers.
Procurement FCI’s MSP-based food grain purchases.
Interest Subvention Subsidized loans for farmers, MSMEs, housing.
- Impact of Subsidies - Positive Impacts: - Merit Goods: - Subsidies on healthcare, education, LPG, solar panels, and windmills increase access and generate positive externalities (e.g., LPG subsidies reduce firewood use, preserving trees and reducing indoor air pollution). - Economic Support: - Subsidies like PM-KISAN and fertilizer subsidies stabilize farmers’ incomes, enhancing agricultural productivity and rural welfare. - Social Equity: - In-kind subsidies (e.g., free school supplies) and indirect subsidies (e.g., cheap college fees) promote access to education and healthcare for the poor. - Negative Impacts: - Environmental Harm: - Subsidies on diesel, kerosene, and urea lead to negative externalities like air pollution, soil degradation, water pollution, and algae blooms due to overuse. - Subsidy Leakage: - Inefficiencies like ghost beneficiaries (non-existent recipients created by corrupt officials) and ineligible beneficiaries (e.g., wealthy individuals receiving subsidies) waste resources. - Fiscal Burden: - High subsidy costs strain government budgets, potentially increasing fiscal deficits (e.g., food subsidies alone cost ~Rs. 2 lakh crore in Budget 2024–25). - Market Distortion: - Implicit subsidies (e.g., poultry import bans) and procurement subsidies (e.g., MSP) can distort market prices, affecting economic efficiency. -
Impact Positive Negative
Merit Goods Promotes healthcare, education, clean energy. Limited impact if poorly targeted.
Environment LPG reduces deforestation, pollution. Diesel, urea subsidies harm soil, air, water.
Fiscal Supports welfare and economic stability. High costs increase fiscal deficits.
Leakage Targeted delivery benefits the poor. Ghost/ineligible beneficiaries waste funds.
- Subsidy Delivery → JAM and DBT - Definition: - JAM (Jan Dhan, Aadhaar, Mobile) and Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) are mechanisms to deliver subsidies efficiently by linking bank accounts, Aadhaar, and mobile numbers to reduce leakage and ensure benefits reach intended recipients. - Economic Survey Observations (2015–23): - 2015–16: - JAM introduced to streamline subsidy delivery, reducing middlemen and ghost beneficiaries. - 2016–17: - DBT saved ~Rs. 36,000 crore by eliminating fake LPG connections. - 2018–19: - Highlighted DBT’s role in food subsidies (e.g., PDS) and MGNREGA payments, ensuring transparency. - 2022–23: - DBT expanded to 300+ schemes, transferring ~Rs. 6.3 lakh crore annually, with Aadhaar-based authentication minimizing fraud. - Process: - Beneficiaries’ bank accounts are linked to Aadhaar and mobile numbers. - Subsidies (e.g., PM-KISAN, LPG) are transferred directly to accounts, bypassing intermediaries. - Example: - PAHAL scheme transfers LPG subsidies to consumers’ accounts, reducing black-market sales of cylinders. - Challenges: - Digital divide in rural areas limits access to mobile/internet for DBT. - Aadhaar authentication failures due to biometric mismatches exclude genuine beneficiaries. - High administrative costs for maintaining DBT infrastructure. - National Recruitment Agency (NRA) - Definition: - Established in 2020 under Budget 2020, NRA conducts a Common Eligibility Test (CET) to shortlist candidates for non-gazetted Group B and C posts in central government and public sector banks (PSBs). - Objectives: - Streamline recruitment by replacing multiple agency exams with a single CET, reducing time and cost for candidates and recruiters. - SSC and IBPS conduct subsequent mains exams for specific posts. - Features: - Computerized test centers in every district for accessibility. - CET scores valid for multiple agencies, simplifying the application process. - Focus on aspirational districts (117) to enhance employment opportunities. - Significance: - Reduces duplication of exams (e.g., SSC, IBPS, Railways) and lowers financial burden on candidates. - Enhances transparency and efficiency in government recruitment. - Note: - NRA is not a constitutional body and was not established in 2014, nor does it facilitate private-sector jobs. - Revenue Expenditure → Salaries - Definition: - Salaries and related allowances for central government employees and retirees form a significant portion of revenue expenditure, ensuring workforce compensation and inflation protection. - 7th Pay Commission (2014–16): - Overview: - Constituted by the Ministry of Finance (Department of Expenditure) under Justice A.K. Mathur, effective from 1 January 2016. The first commission was led by Srinivasa Varadachariar (1946). - Key Recommendations: - Introduced Pay Matrix, replacing pay bands and grade pay for simplified salary structures. - Increased minimum salary to Rs. 18,000/month (Group D) and maximum to Rs. 2.25 lakh/month (Apex scale, e.g., Department Secretary) and Rs. 2.5 lakh/month (Cabinet Secretary). - Regulatory body salaries raised (e.g., Chairman: Rs. 4.5 lakh/month). - Adopted Dr. Aykroyd’s formula to adjust wages based on commodity price changes, potentially eliminating the need for future pay commissions. - Strengthened Modified Assured Career Progression (MACP) to ensure merit-based promotions, curbing automatic promotions for underperforming officials. - Reforms for defense and Central Armed Police Forces (CAPF), including better allowances and service conditions. -
Aspect Details
Pay Matrix Replaces pay bands; simplifies salary structure.
Salary Range Rs. 18,000 (Group D) to Rs. 2.5 lakh (Cabinet Secretary).
Aykroyd Formula Adjusts wages based on commodity price changes.
MACP Ensures merit-based promotions.
- 8th Pay Commission (Budget 2025) - Overview: - Announced in Budget 2025, but chairman details were unavailable as of 1 February 2025. Aims to further refine salary structures and address inflation and employee welfare. - Context: - Likely to build on the 7th Pay Commission’s Pay Matrix and Aykroyd formula, ensuring dynamic salary adjustments. - Expected to address concerns like rising living costs and pension disparities. - Dearness Allowance (DA) and Dearness Relief (DR) - Definition: - Dearness Allowance (DA) is a cost-of-living adjustment for serving central government employees to offset inflation. Dearness Relief (DR) is a similar allowance for retired employees. - Mechanism: - Calculated as a percentage of basic salary/pension, based on the All India Consumer Price Index (AICPI). - Revised biannually (January and July) by the Department of Expenditure. - Key Developments: - 2020: - DA/DR frozen due to COVID-19 fiscal constraints. - 2021 (July): - Resumed at 28% (from 17%), with arrears paid to employees and pensioners. - 2024: - DA increased to 50% of basic salary for ~1 crore employees and pensioners, reflecting inflation adjustments. -
Aspect DA DR
Beneficiaries Serving employees Retired employees
Purpose Protect against inflation Protect against inflation
2021 Update Resumed at 28% Resumed at 28%
- Bonus to Government Employees - Definition: - Bonuses are additional payments beyond regular salaries, given occasionally to boost morale or reward performance. - Types: - Productivity-Linked Bonus (PLB): - Tied to measurable output (e.g., files cleared, trees planted, or sales improved in postal/railways departments). - Non-Productivity-Linked (Ad-Hoc) Bonus: - Given regardless of performance, e.g., Diwali bonus (up to Rs. 7,000 for non-gazetted employees in 2024). - Example: - 2024: Productivity-linked bonuses for railway employees based on train operation efficiency; ad-hoc Diwali bonuses for Group C and D staff. - Miscellaneous Terms - House Rent Allowance (HRA): - Provided to cover accommodation costs, varying by city classification (e.g., 27% of basic salary in metro cities like Delhi, 9% in smaller towns under 7th Pay Commission). - Gratuity: - Lump-sum payment for employees with continuous service (typically 5+ years), governed by the Payment of Gratuity Act, 1972. Maximum limit: Rs. 20 lakh (2024). - One Rank One Pension (OROP): - Introduced in 2015 for military personnel to ensure equal pensions for the same rank and service length, regardless of retirement date. Criticisms include disputes over base year and calculation formulas, with ex-servicemen seeking revisions. -
Term Details
HRA 9–27% of basic salary based on city type.
Gratuity Up to Rs. 20 lakh for 5+ years of service.
OROP Equal pensions for same rank/service; implemented 2015.
- Pillar 2D1: Budget → Capital → Receipts & Expenditure - Definition: - Capital receipts and expenditure in the Union Budget involve funds that affect the government’s assets or liabilities, distinct from revenue receipts/expenditure, which support day-to-day operations. Capital receipts include borrowings and disinvestment proceeds, while capital expenditure creates long-term assets or reduces liabilities. - Context: - Capital receipts provide funds for infrastructure, debt repayment, and investments, crucial for economic growth and fiscal stability. Capital expenditure drives development through asset creation (e.g., highways, hospitals) and financial investments, aligning with initiatives like Atmanirbhar Bharat and Gati Shakti. - Significance for India: - Effective management of capital receipts and expenditure enhances infrastructure, boosts economic productivity, and reduces fiscal deficits. Strategic disinvestment and monetization policies under Modi’s regime optimize public sector resources, fostering private sector participation and economic efficiency. - Budget → Capital Receipts - Definition: - Capital receipts are funds that increase the government’s liabilities (e.g., borrowings) or reduce its assets (e.g., disinvestment). They are categorized into debt and non-debt receipts. - Types of Capital Receipts: - Capital Debt Receipts: - Internal Borrowing: - Funds raised from domestic sources like RBI, banks, NBFCs, small savings schemes (e.g., Post Office Savings Accounts, Kisan Vikas Patra), and Provident Funds (e.g., EPFO, PPF). - External Borrowing: - Loans from foreign countries and institutions like IMF, World Bank, and BRICS Bank, typically in foreign currencies (e.g., USD, Euro). - Loan Principal Recovered: - Repayments of principal (not interest) from loans given by the Union to states, foreign countries, or public sector companies. - Capital Non-Debt Receipts: - Disinvestment: - Proceeds from selling government shares in Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) or Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs). -
Type Examples
Internal Borrowing RBI, banks, NBFCs, Post Office Savings, PPF.
External Borrowing IMF, World Bank, BRICS Bank loans.
Loan Principal Recovered Repayments from states, CPSEs, foreign nations.
Disinvestment Sale of shares in CPSEs like ONGC, SAIL.
