- Foreign Borrowing in Foreign Currency - Introduction: - In Budget 2019, Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman announced that India’s sovereign external debt-to-GDP ratio is low (~5%), allowing the government to raise part of its borrowing from external markets in foreign currencies (e.g., USD, Euro). - Arguments in Favor: - Reduces Crowding Out: - Domestic borrowing reduces funds available for private corporates. External borrowing frees up domestic capital for private sector growth, enabling factory expansion, job creation, and GDP growth. - Lower Interest Rates: - Advanced economies (e.g., USA, EU) offer lower interest rates, making foreign loans cheaper than domestic ones. - Manageable Debt Levels: - India’s low external debt ratio suggests that moderate foreign borrowing is unlikely to cause significant fiscal strain. - Arguments Against: - Exchange Rate Risk: - A weakening rupee (e.g., $1 = Rs. 60 to Rs. 70) increases the repayment burden in rupees, making loans more expensive than anticipated. - Risk of Over-Borrowing: - Future governments may excessively borrow abroad to fund populist schemes, risking a debt crisis if the rupee depreciates significantly. - Alternative Approach: - Increasing foreign investment limits in Government Securities (G-Secs) in INR attracts foreign capital without currency risk, preferable to borrowing in foreign currency. - Conclusion: - While foreign borrowing can reduce domestic crowding out and leverage low global interest rates, the exchange rate risk and potential for over-borrowing outweigh benefits. Economists like Raghuram Rajan advocate caution, suggesting more deliberation before expanding foreign currency borrowing. -
Arguments In Favor Against
Economic Impact Reduces crowding out, boosts private sector growth. Exchange rate risk increases repayment costs.
Interest Rates Lower rates in advanced economies. Future over-borrowing risks debt crisis.
Alternative - Increase G-Sec investment limits in INR.
- Foreign/External Borrowing Data - Economic Survey 2023: - Most of India’s external debt is in INR, minimizing currency risk. - Data (in Rs. Crore): -
Year External Debt
2022–23 37,124
2023–24 24,832
2024–25 32,000
2025–26 23,500
- Significance: - India’s external borrowing remains low relative to GDP, reducing fiscal vulnerability. The decline in 2025–26 reflects cautious borrowing strategies. - Budget → Capital Receipts → Disinvestment - Definition: - Disinvestment involves the government selling its shareholding in Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) or Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs) to raise funds, typically retaining at least 51% ownership to maintain control. - Types of Government-Owned Entities: - Departmental Undertakings: - Directly managed by ministries (e.g., Railways, Postal Services). No separate legal entity, high ministerial interference, audited by CAG. - Statutory Corporations: - Created by specific Acts (e.g., RBI, LIC, SBI, EPFO). Some have internal audits, excluding CAG. Moderate operational flexibility. - Government Companies (CPSEs): - Registered under the Companies Act with ≥51% government shareholding (e.g., Coal India, NTPC). Audited by CAG-empaneled private auditors, higher operational flexibility. -
Type Examples Features
Departmental Undertakings Railways, Postal High interference, CAG audit.
Statutory Corporations RBI, LIC, SBI Moderate flexibility, some exclude CAG.
Government Companies Coal India, NTPC High flexibility, private auditors.
- Objectives: - Promote public welfare through affordable services, develop infrastructure, ensure regional balance, and prevent economic concentration in private hands. - Challenges: - Political interference, lack of innovation, employee union resistance, and loss-making operations hinder efficiency. - CPSE vs. PSU vs. PSB - Public Sector Banks (PSBs): - Banks with majority government shareholding (e.g., SBI, PNB). - Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs): - Government companies under the Companies Act with ≥51% Union shareholding, excluding PSBs, public sector insurance companies, and NBFCs (e.g., ONGC, SAIL). - Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs): - Collective term for all government-owned entities, including CPSEs, PSBs, and state/local government companies. -
Entity Definition Examples
PSB Banks with ≥51% government shareholding. SBI, PNB.
CPSE Government companies excluding PSBs, insurance, NBFCs. ONGC, SAIL.
PSU All government-owned entities (Union, state, local). CPSEs, PSBs, state companies.
- Ratna Companies - Definition: - Ratna status, granted by the Department of Public Enterprises (DPE, shifted to Finance Ministry in 2021), provides operational autonomy to high-performing CPSEs (not PSBs, statutory corporations, or private companies). - Categories and Conditions: - Miniratna (Category I & II): - CPSEs with continuous profits for three years, subdivided based on profit levels (e.g., NHPC, Mazagaon Dock). - Navratna: - Miniratna or other CPSEs meeting criteria like manpower cost ratios (e.g., Hindustan Aeronautics, Bharat Electronics). - Maharatna: - Navratna CPSEs with ≥Rs. 5,000 crore annual profit for three years, listed on stock exchanges, and significant global presence (e.g., Coal India, NTPC). - Benefits: - Autonomy in hiring, acquisitions, and decision-making without government approval for minor decisions. - Examples (as of 1/1/2020): - Miniratna: National Film Development Corporation, WAPCOS. - Navratna: Oil India, Container Corporation. - Maharatna: Bharat Petroleum, GAIL, ONGC. -
Category Conditions Examples
Miniratna Profits for 3 years. NHPC, Mazagaon Dock.
Navratna Miniratna or specific criteria. Bharat Electronics, Oil India.
Maharatna Navratna, ≥Rs. 5,000 crore profit, global presence. Coal India, NTPC.
- Government Policy Towards Disinvestment (Pre-2021) - Disinvestment: - Reducing government shareholding in CPSEs while retaining ≥51% to maintain control. - Privatization/Strategic Disinvestment: - Selling ≥50% shareholding with management control transfer, as defined by DIPAM. - Historical Developments: - 1951: - First Constitution Amendment Act allowed nationalization by imposing restrictions on private industries for public interest. - 1953: - Air Corporations Act nationalized nine private airlines into Indian Airlines and Air India International. - 1991: - Interim Budget introduced disinvestment, with PM Narasimha Rao announcing shareholding reduction in government companies. - 1998–99 (Vajpayee Era): - No disinvestment in strategic sectors (e.g., railways, defense, atomic energy). - Phased disinvestment in non-strategic sectors; established Department of Disinvestment (1999), privatizing Maruti Udyog, VSNL, etc. - UPA-1 (2004–09): - No disinvestment due to Leftist coalition pressure; focus on reviving sick government companies. - UPA-2 (2009–14): - Allowed disinvestment up to 49%, retaining 51% control. Proceeds went to National Investment Fund (NIF) for bank recapitalization, metro rail, etc. - Launched CPSE-Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs). - Arguments for Disinvestment: - Private investors enhance efficiency, innovation, and autonomy. - Proceeds fund welfare schemes and reduce fiscal deficits. - Arguments Against: - Risks MNC monopolies, worker exploitation, and job losses. -
Year Policy
1951 First Amendment enabled nationalization.
1991 Disinvestment introduced in Interim Budget.
1998–99 No disinvestment in strategic sectors; phased disinvestment elsewhere.
2004–09 No disinvestment; revive sick companies.
2009–14 Up to 49% disinvestment; NIF and CPSE-ETFs.
- Disinvestment & Privatization in Modi Raj (2014–) - Methods of Disinvestment: - Initial Public Offerings (IPOs): - Converting private limited CPSEs to public companies (e.g., IRCTC, RVNL). - Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs): - CPSE-ETF, Bharat-22-ETF for diversified share sales. - Institutional Placement Programme (IPP): - Shares offered to non-retail investors (e.g., institutions). - Offer for Sale (OFS): - Shares sold to both retail and non-retail investors. - Share Buyback: - CPSEs buy back government-held shares, reducing government shareholding. - Key Actions: - Closed loss-making CPSEs (e.g., HMT Watches, Hindustan Photo Film). - 2016: - Renamed Department of Disinvestment to Department of Investment and Public Asset Management (DIPAM). - Privatization Examples: - Budget 2022: - Neelanchal Ispat Nigam Limited (NINL) privatized. - Economic Survey 2023: - 10 CPSEs privatized, including HPCL, REC, Air India. - 2024: - Ferro Scrap Nigam Limited (FSNL) sold to a Japanese company for Rs. 320 crore. -
Method Example
IPO IRCTC, RVNL.
ETF CPSE-ETF, Bharat-22-ETF.
IPP Shares to institutional investors.
OFS Shares to retail/non-retail investors.
Share Buyback CPSEs buy government shares.
- New Public Sector Enterprise (PSE) Policy (2021) - Strategic Sectors: - Atomic energy, space, defense, transport, telecom, power, petroleum, coal, minerals, banking, insurance, financial services. - Policy: - Retain at least one government company; others may be merged, privatized, or closed. - Non-Strategic Sectors: - All other sectors (e.g., manufacturing, hospitality). - Policy: - All government companies to be privatized or closed. - Terminology: - NITI Aayog prefers “strategic disinvestment” or “strategic sale” over “privatization” to avoid political backlash. -
Sector Policy Examples
Strategic Retain ≥1 company; merge/privatize others. Defense, telecom, banking.
Non-Strategic Privatize/close all companies. Manufacturing, hospitality.
- Disinvestment Targets (Previous Budgets) - Data (in Rs. Crore): -
Budget Year Target Achieved
2018–19 80,000 Yes
2019–24 Big targets Not achieved
2023–24 50,000 33,000
2025–26 47,000 Pending
- Analysis: - Consistent underachievement reflects challenges like market conditions, political opposition, and valuation disputes. - National Monetisation Pipeline (NMP, 2021) - Definition: - A government initiative to monetize assets of CPSEs, National Highways, and Railways by selling, renting, or leasing land, buildings, or machinery to raise Rs. 6 lakh crore by 2025. - Objectives: - Mobilize funds for infrastructure development, reduce fiscal burden, and enhance asset efficiency. - Examples: - Leasing railway stations, toll roads, or CPSE land to private entities. - Related Topics: - National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF). - National Infrastructure Pipeline (NIP) and Gati Shakti initiative (refer to Pillar 5). - National Land Monetization Corporation (NLMC, 2022) - Definition: - Established under the Finance Ministry to monetize surplus land and non-core assets of CPSEs and government departments. - Objectives: - Optimize land use, generate revenue, and support NMP goals. - Functions: - Identify, value, and monetize (sell/lease) surplus land (e.g., unused PSU land for commercial development). - Note: - Refer to Pillar 2A1 for details on Finance Ministry departments. - Minimum Dividend Rule (2024–Nov) - Policy: - Finance Ministry mandates CPSEs (e.g., ONGC) and government NBFCs (e.g., LIC, Power Finance Corp) to pay ≥30% of profits as dividends to shareholders, including the government. - Exclusions: - Public Sector Banks (e.g., SBI, PNB) not explicitly covered, as per available data. - Significance: - Ensures steady revenue for the government, supporting fiscal consolidation. - Note: - Additional conditions related to net worth apply but are not critical for memorization due to low cost-benefit for UPSC. - Budget → Capital Expenditure - Definition: - Capital expenditure involves government spending to create long-term assets or reduce liabilities, such as infrastructure projects, loan repayments, or investments in PSUs. - Notable Components: - Capital Assets: - Expenditure on buildings, vehicles, and equipment for schemes, ministries, and departments. - Debt/Equity Financing: - Investments in PSUs, foreign institutions, or loans to state/foreign governments. - Loan Principal Repayments: - Repaying principal for internal and external debts (interest payments are revenue expenditure). - Indian Development and Economic Assistance Scheme (IDEAS): - Managed by the Department of Economic Affairs (DEA), provides loans to foreign nations for development projects. -
Component Details
Capital Assets Buildings, highways, hospitals, vehicles.
Debt/Equity Financing Loans to states, PSUs, foreign nations.
Loan Repayments Principal for internal/external debts.
IDEAS Loans for foreign development projects.
- State CAPEX Loans - Special Assistance to States for Capital Expenditure (CAPEX Scheme): - Introduced in 2020, provides interest-free, 50-year loans to states for capital projects (e.g., health, rural development, water supply, irrigation, power, transport, education, urban development). - Budget 2020: - Allocated ~Rs. 12,000 crore. - Budget 2025: - Increased to Rs. 1.5 lakh crore. - Linked Reforms (Budget 2023): - Scrapping old government vehicles to reduce emissions. - Urban planning reforms to improve city infrastructure. - Strengthening urban local bodies to issue municipal bonds. - Housing for policemen to enhance security infrastructure. - Constructing Unity Malls to promote regional products (refer to Pillar 3A). - Libraries and digital infrastructure for children and adolescents. - Significance: - Enhances state-level infrastructure, promotes fiscal federalism, and aligns with national development goals. - Effective Capital Expenditure - Definition: - Effective capital expenditure includes direct Union capital expenditure and grants-in-aid to states for capital projects, reflecting the total capital investment impact. - Significance: - Measures the government’s contribution to asset creation and economic growth, critical for infrastructure development and job creation. - Note: - Detailed explanation available in referenced video lectures, focusing on budget allocations and economic impact. - Capital Expenditure for a Company - Definition: - Capital expenditure by a company involves investments in income-generating assets, categorized as tangible or intangible. - Types of Assets: - Tangible Assets: - Land, buildings, vehicles, equipment, raw materials, unsold inventory. - Intangible Assets: - Patents, copyrights, brand logos, trade secrets (e.g., KFC recipe), licenses (e.g., telecom spectrum), investments in shares/bonds, client databases, accounts receivable, goodwill, website domains. - Subcategories: - Current Assets: - Short-term assets like cash, inventory. - Financial Assets: - Investments in shares, bonds. - Fixed Assets: - Long-term assets like buildings, equipment. -
Asset Type Examples
Tangible Land, buildings, vehicles, inventory.
Intangible Patents, copyrights, goodwill, licenses.
- Significance: - Capital expenditure drives company growth, innovation, and competitiveness, contributing to economic development. - Pillar 2D2: Budget → Deficits and Fiscal Policy - Introduction: - Deficits occur when government expenditure exceeds its receipts, impacting fiscal stability and economic growth. Understanding types of deficits, their financing, and related policies is crucial for assessing India’s fiscal health and compliance with the Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act, 2003. - Types of Budget - Surplus Budget: - Occurs when government receipts (revenue and capital) exceed expenditure, allowing debt repayment or reserve creation. - Balanced Budget: - Receipts equal expenditure, ensuring no borrowing is required. - Deficit Budget: - Expenditure exceeds receipts, necessitating borrowing to bridge the gap. -
Budget Type Definition
Surplus Receipts > Expenditure
Balanced Receipts = Expenditure
Deficit Expenditure > Receipts
- Budget Deficit - Definition: - Excess of total expenditure (revenue and capital) over total receipts (revenue and capital). Since 1997–98, the Union Budget has ceased reporting budget deficit, focusing instead on fiscal deficit. - Example Data (Budget 2025): - Not explicitly reported due to discontinuation; fiscal deficit is now the primary metric. - Fiscal Deficit - Definition: - Excess of total expenditure over total receipts, excluding borrowings. It represents the amount the government must borrow to meet its financial obligations. - Formulas: - Tamilnadu Textbook: - Fiscal Deficit = Budget Deficit + Borrowings (internal, RBI, external). - NCERT Textbook: - Fiscal Deficit = Total Expenditure – (Revenue Receipts + Non-Debt Creating Capital Receipts). - Alternatively: Difference between total expenditure and total receipts, excluding borrowings. - Note: Sukhmoy Chakravarti Committee (1997–98) emphasized fiscal deficit as a key indicator. - Example Data (Budget 2025): - Fiscal Deficit Target: 4.5% of GDP (as per 15th Finance Commission recommendation). -
Source Formula
Tamilnadu Textbook Budget Deficit + Borrowings
NCERT Total Expenditure – (Revenue Receipts + Non-Debt Capital Receipts)
- Gross Fiscal Deficit (GFD) vs. Net Fiscal Deficit - Gross Fiscal Deficit (GFD): - Total borrowings (internal, RBI, external). - Net Fiscal Deficit: - GFD minus net lending by the central government (e.g., loans to states or PSUs). - Note: RBI is listed separately in formulas per NCERT, despite being domestic, for clarity in source differentiation. -
Type Definition
Gross Fiscal Deficit Total Borrowings (Internal + RBI + External)
Net Fiscal Deficit GFD – Net Lending
- Primary Deficit - Definition: - Fiscal Deficit minus interest payments on previous loans, indicating borrowing for new programs rather than servicing past debt. - Significance: - High interest payments from accumulated debt increase borrowing needs, creating a vicious cycle. Primary deficit isolates borrowing for current expenditures. - Introduced: 1993 Budget Speech by Finance Minister Manmohan Singh. - Example Data (Budget 2025): - Primary Deficit Target: Shifted to 0% by 2022–23, not achieved due to COVID-19. - Gross Primary Deficit (GPD) - Definition: - Gross Fiscal Deficit minus net interest liabilities. - Net Primary Deficit: - Net Fiscal Deficit minus net interest liabilities. - Note: Formulas vary by context (A-B or B-A), but the focus is on isolating non-interest borrowing. - Revenue Deficit - Definition: - Excess of revenue expenditure (e.g., salaries, subsidies, interest payments) over revenue receipts (e.g., taxes, non-tax revenue). - Formula: - Revenue Deficit = Revenue Expenditure – Revenue Receipts. - Significance: - High revenue deficit forces borrowing for operational expenses, reducing funds for capital expenditure (e.g., schools, hospitals), hindering human development and economic growth. -
Deficit Formula
Revenue Deficit Revenue Expenditure – Revenue Receipts
- Effective Revenue Deficit - Definition: - Revenue Deficit minus grants-in-aid to states/local bodies used for capital asset creation (e.g., panchayat buildings, disaster management infrastructure). - Introduced: Budget 2011 by Finance Minister P. Chidambaram. - Significance: - Adjusts revenue deficit to account for productive expenditure, providing a clearer picture of non-productive borrowing. - Formula: - Effective Revenue Deficit = Revenue Deficit – Grants for Capital Assets. -
Deficit Formula
Effective Revenue Deficit Revenue Deficit – Grants for Capital Assets
- Fiscal Glide - Definition: - Gradual reduction in fiscal deficit over time to achieve economic stability without abrupt cuts. - Example: - Budget 2018: Finance Minister Arun Jaitley aimed for a 3% fiscal deficit by 2020–21, likened to a glider descending smoothly. Temporarily relaxed in 2020 due to structural reforms (FRBM trigger). - Extra-Budgetary Resources (EBR) - Definition: - Loans raised by public sector undertakings (PSUs) or government entities, repaid from the Union Budget, often bypassing immediate fiscal deficit calculations. - Examples: - Food Corporation of India (FCI) borrowing from National Small Savings Fund (NSSF) for food subsidies (discontinued in Budget 2021). - Data (in Rs. Crore): -
Fiscal Year EBR Amount
2020–21 148,000
2021–22 121,000
2022–23 & 2023–24 0
- Criticism: - 15th Finance Commission and Economic Survey 2020 criticized EBR for reducing transparency, as they escape media scrutiny, parliamentary debate, and audits. - Debt Composition - Amount-Wise (Economic Survey 2022, in Rs. Lakh Crore): -
Category 2019–20 2020–21 2021–22
Internal Debt 80 99 115
External Debt 5 6.15 6.59
Public Debt (Internal + External) 85 105 121
Other Liabilities (e.g., Small Savings) 13 12 12
Extra-Budgetary Resources 1.12 1.39 1.39
Total Liability 99 117 134
- Type-Wise: - Union vs. State Debt: - Economic Survey: - States (70%), Union (30%). - Kerala Petition (Supreme Court): - States (40%), Union (60%). - Union Debt Breakdown: - Internal (94%), External (6%). - Repayment Currency: - Mostly Rupee, minimal foreign currency. - Interest Type: - Fixed (95%), Floating (5%, e.g., tied to LIBOR). - Tenure: - Mostly long-term. - Debt-to-GDP Ratio: - External Debt: - <3% of GDP. - Total Public Debt: - Declined since 2003, stable since 2011, among the lowest in G20, OECD, and BRICS. - Financing the Deficit: Negative Consequences - Methods and Issues: -
Method Issues
Increasing Taxes May lead to tax evasion or reduced work motivation (Laffer Curve).
Borrowing Ricardian Equivalence, crowding out, lower sovereign ratings.
Printing Money (Monetization) Hyperinflation, reduced purchasing power.
- Ricardian Equivalence: - Theory: - High deficits lead to increased borrowing, prompting future tax hikes. Citizens save more, reducing consumption, harming the economy. - Invalid in India (Economic Survey 2021): - Citizens not fully rational in consumption decisions (influenced by emotions, e.g., luxury purchases). - Tax evasion and black money reduce tax compliance. - Crowding Out: - Government borrowing reduces funds for private borrowers, limiting factory expansion and job creation. - Financial Repression: - Forcing institutions (e.g., SBI, LIC) to buy G-Secs deprives households of optimal returns. - Economic Survey 2021: - Limited evidence of crowding out (1990–2019) due to increased savings, banking habits, and capital market growth. - Monetization of Deficit: - Printing money to cover deficits causes hyperinflation (e.g., Germany post-1919). FRBM Act prohibits RBI from subscribing to G-Secs in the primary market (since 2006–07) except for Ways and Means Advances. - Sovereign Rating Impact: - High fiscal deficits may lower India’s rating (e.g., Moody’s Baa3 in 2020), increasing borrowing costs and risking capital flight. Economic Survey 2021 suggests minimal immediate concern. - Countercyclical Fiscal Policy - Definition: - Increasing government spending during economic slowdowns to boost employment and GDP, and reducing spending during booms to build reserves. - Examples: - Historical: - Indian kings built infrastructure during famines to create jobs. - Modern: - USA, UK, Chile; India yet to fully adopt (Economic Survey 2021 recommendation). - Crowding-In: - Government spending (e.g., highways) encourages private investment (e.g., petrol pumps), boosting jobs and GDP. - Interest Rate Growth Rate Differential (IRGD): - IRGD = Loan Interest Rate – GDP Growth Rate. - Negative IRGD (e.g., 6% interest – 9% growth = -3%) indicates sustainable debt, as tax revenue growth outpaces interest payments. - Economic Survey 2023: India’s IRGD is positive, ensuring debt sustainability. - Domar Condition: - Debt is sustainable if GDP growth exceeds public debt growth (Evsey Domar, 1944). India meets this condition per Economic Survey 2023. - Automatic Stabilizers - Definition: - Budget mechanisms that automatically increase spending or reduce taxes during slowdowns without new legislation (e.g., income tax, unemployment allowances, food subsidies). - Significance: - Stabilize economy by boosting demand for lower-income groups. - Disposable Income & Marginal Propensity to Consume (MPC): - Disposable Income = Personal Income – Direct Taxes. - MPC: - Proportion of additional income spent on consumption. - Multipliers: - Tax Multiplier: - Negative, as tax increases reduce consumption. - Autonomous Expenditure Multiplier (AEM): - Measures GDP impact of essential spending (e.g., food, shelter). - Government Expenditure Multiplier: - Measures GDP impact of government spending. - Balanced Budget Multiplier: - Equals 1, as increased government spending offsets reduced consumption from higher taxes. - Budget Functions: -
Function Description
Allocation Provides public goods (e.g., defense, roads).
Redistribution Taxes rich to fund welfare for poor.
Stabilization Increases spending during slowdowns.
- Deficit Financing Methods -
Method Description
Redemption Repay principal and interest regularly (Terminal Annuity).
Sinking Fund Regular deposits to repay loans at maturity.
Conversion/Restructuring Modify old loans’ interest/tenure.
Evergreening New loans to repay old loans.
Repudiation Refuse to repay loans (e.g., post-1917 Russia); not viable today.
- Fiscal Consolidation/Prudence - Definition: - Reducing expenditure and deficits to ensure fiscal sustainability. - Measures: - Targeted delivery via Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) using JanDhan-Aadhaar-Mobile. - Subsidy rationalization (e.g., deregulating petrol/diesel prices, limiting LPG subsidies). - Closing loss-making PSUs (e.g., HMT Watches, Hindustan Photo Films). - Privatization (e.g., Air India, IDBI). - Austerity: - Reducing wasteful spending (e.g., banning luxury expenses, 20% cut in travel/conference costs). - Expenditure Management Commission (2014): - Chaired by Bimal Jalan, recommended expenditure reduction and subsidy rationalization. - Fiscal Stimulus - Definition: - Reducing taxes or increasing public spending to boost demand and economic growth. - Manmohan Singh’s Stimulus (2008): - Post-subprime crisis: - Reduced excise/customs duties, tax benefits for commercial vehicle purchases, increased MSP for farmers. - Issue: - Increased fiscal deficit in subsequent years. - Modi’s Atma-Nirbhar Bharat Stimulus (2020): - Response to COVID-19 lockdown; included RBI monetary measures (Rs. 13 lakh crore), PM Garib Kalyan Yojana (Rs. 1.7–1.9 lakh crore), and MSME credit guarantees. - Total: - Rs. 20–30 lakh crore (~10–15% of GDP). - Frontloading Risks: - Excessive stimulus may overheat the economy, causing inflation. - Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act, 2003 - Objectives: - Reduce fiscal deficit to 3% of GDP (Union) and 3% of GSDP (States) by 2008. - Eliminate revenue deficit by 2008 (later relaxed to effective revenue deficit by 2015). - Amendments: - Deadlines extended due to challenges (e.g., 2018: Abandoned revenue deficit targeting). - Budget 2021: - Fiscal deficit targets set at 6.8% (2021–22) and 4.5% (2025–26). - Trigger Mechanism (Section 4(2)): - Allows exceeding deficit targets by 0.5% of GDP/GSDP during: - National security/war. - National calamity. - Agricultural collapse. - GDP growth decline. - Structural reforms with fiscal implications. - Example: - Budget 2020 cited structural reforms to relax targets for 2019–20 (3.8%) and 2020–21 (3.5%). - Documents: -
Document Purpose
Macroeconomic Framework Statement Shows GDP, growth, receipts, expenditure.
Medium-Term Fiscal Policy Statement Outlines deficit control plans.
Fiscal Policy Strategy Statement Explains deviations from targets.
- Fiscal Indicators (Budget 2025): - Central Government Debt: ~56% of GDP (state debt not specified). - Fiscal Federalism - Tax Devolution and Grants: - Despite reduced tax revenue during COVID-19, Union continued devolution and grants as per Finance Commission recommendations. - State Fiscal Deficit Limits: - Constitution Article 293: - States need Union permission to borrow. - Pre-2020: - 3% of GSDP. - Post-2020 (Atmanirbhar Bharat): - Increased to 5% (2020–21), 4.5% (2021–22), 4% (2022–23), 3.5% (2023–24) with reform-linked incentives (e.g., One Nation One Ration Card, power sector reforms). - Total extra borrowing: - Rs. 4.27 lakh crore for states. - Kerala Deficit Issue: - Ongoing Supreme Court case regarding Union restrictions on Kerala’s borrowing; more relevant for Mains. - NK Singh FRBM Review Panel - Constituted: - Budget 2016 by Finance Minister Arun Jaitley to address rigid FRBM targets. - Issue: - Targets (e.g., 3% fiscal deficit) limited flexibility for crises (e.g., farm loan waivers). - Note: - Recommendations outdated post-COVID; not critical for Prelims. - Budget Marksmanship - Definition: - Ability to align actual income and expenditure with budget estimates. - Issue (15th Finance Commission): - Poor marksmanship due to overestimated tax revenue, leading to: - Tax terrorism/litigation (e.g., direct tax disputes: Rs. 3 lakh crore in 2012, Rs. 10 lakh crore in 2020). - Ad-hoc expenditure cuts, reliance on EBR, delayed contractor payments, and substandard infrastructure. - Data (2019–20, in Rs. Lakh Crore): -
Category Budget Estimate Revised Estimate Actual
Tax Receipts 16 15 13
Revenue Expenditure 24.47 23.49 23.50
- Freebies/Revdi Culture - Definition: - Unsustainable freebies announced by political parties to win elections, criticized by PM Modi and Supreme Court. - Status: Developing topic, more relevant for Mains. - Fiscal Responsibility: Miscellaneous Bodies - Expenditure Management Commission (2014): - Chaired by Bimal Jalan, recommended reducing deficits and subsidies. - Public Debt Management Agency (PDMA): - Proposed but not established; shifted to Mains. - Fiscal Council: - Proposed but not established; shifted to Mains. - Corona Ex-Gratia Yojana: - Disaster Management Act, 2005 (Section 12): Requires relief for disaster victims. - 2021: Supreme Court ordered Rs. 50,000 compensation per COVID-19 death, straining fiscal capacity. - Pillar 2D3: Budget → Types of Budget and Schemes - Introduction: - Budgets in India are classified based on their purpose, funding, and expenditure types, guiding resource allocation and fiscal policy. Understanding these classifications and their management is critical for assessing government efficiency and economic impact. - Revenue vs. Capital Budget - Revenue Budget: - Covers short-term (1 year or less), non-asset-creating income and expenditure. - Includes: - Taxes, non-tax revenue (e.g., fees, dividends), and revenue expenditure (e.g., salaries, pensions, subsidies, interest payments). - Capital Budget: - Covers long-term, asset-creating income and expenditure. - Includes: - Borrowings, disinvestment proceeds, and capital expenditure (e.g., infrastructure, machinery, financial assets like bonds). -
Budget Type Nature Examples
Revenue Budget Short-term, non-asset-creating Taxes, salaries, subsidies
Capital Budget Long-term, asset-creating Borrowings, infrastructure, disinvestment
- General Budget vs. Railway Budget - Historical Context: - 1920–21: - Acworth Committee recommended a separate Railway Budget due to railways’ significant revenue contribution during British rule. - 1924: - Separation Convention formalized this practice. - Post-independence, the Railway Budget was presented before the General Budget. - Merger (2017): - Modi government merged the Railway Budget with the General Budget, based on NITI Aayog’s Bibek Debroy Committee recommendations: - No constitutional requirement for a separate budget. - Populist measures (e.g., low fares) during coalition governments eroded railway profitability. - Railway revenue became a small fraction of the General Budget, not justifying separate presentation. - Impact: - Streamlined budgeting, reduced populism, and aligned railway finances with national fiscal goals. - Plan vs. Non-Plan Expenditure Budget - Definition: - Plan Expenditure: - Linked to Five-Year Plans (e.g., development projects). - Non-Plan Expenditure: - Routine expenses (e.g., salaries, maintenance). - Discontinuation (Budget 2017): - Modi government ended the distinction due to: - No constitutional mandate. - Dissolution of the Planning Commission (2014–15). - End of the 12th Five-Year Plan (2012–17). - Impact: - Simplified budget presentation, focusing on revenue and capital classifications. - Budgeting Methods - Traditional/Line-Item Budgeting: - Focuses on listing income and expenditure without evaluating benefits (e.g., Rs. 10,000 for hospital beds). - Lacks performance measurement. - Performance Budgeting: - Ties expenditure to measurable outcomes (e.g., Rs. 50,000 for computers to achieve 30% faster RTI processing). - Enhances cost-benefit analysis and efficiency. - Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB): - Requires departments to justify all expenditure from scratch, not incrementally (e.g., reviewing education schemes’ effectiveness before reallocation). - Prevents automatic budget increases. - Sunset Budgeting: - Schemes have predefined deadlines (e.g., a subsidy for two years), ensuring automatic termination unless renewed. -
Method Description Advantage
Traditional Lists income/expenditure without outcomes Simple but lacks efficiency
Performance Ties expenditure to outcomes Improves efficiency
Zero-Based Justifies all expenditure from zero Eliminates wasteful spending
Sunset Schemes with fixed deadlines Ensures timely review
- Gender Budget - Definition: - A statement within the General Budget (since 2005) highlighting allocations for women-centric schemes, though not constitutionally mandated. - Structure (Budget 2025): - Part A: - 100% women-focused schemes (e.g., SAMBAL, SAMARTHYA under Women and Child Development Ministry) – >Rs. 1 lakh crore. - Part B: - Schemes with at least 30% allocation for women (e.g., school education) – >Rs. 3 lakh crore. - Part C (since Budget 2024–July): - Schemes with less than 30% for women (e.g., PM-KISAN, Rs. 6,000 to farmers) – >Rs. 16,000 crore. - Total: - >Rs. 4 lakh crore. - Criticism: - Three-part system (introduced in 2024) accused of data manipulation to exaggerate women-focused spending. -
Part Allocation Examples Amount (Rs. Crore)
Part A 100% for women SAMBAL, SAMARTHYA >1,00,000
Part B Min. 30% for women School education >3,00,000
Part C <30% for women PM-KISAN >16,000
- Tribal Sub-Plan (TSP) and Scheduled Castes Sub-Plan (SCSP) - Definition: - Since the 1970s, ministries must earmark funds for Scheduled Castes (SC) and Scheduled Tribes (ST) within their budgets. - Monitoring: - SCSP: - Social Justice Ministry via e-utthaan.gov.in. - TSP: - Tribal Affairs Ministry via stcmis.gov.in. - Additional Allocations: - Budget documents also include allocations for children and North Eastern Areas (not constitutionally required). - Output-Outcome Framework for Schemes - Introduced: Budget 2017. - Purpose: - Tracks scheme outlays, outputs (deliverables), and outcomes (impact), monitored by NITI Aayog. - Example: -
Ministry/Scheme Outlay Output Outcome
MEITY/PMGDISHA Rs. 400 crore Computer training for 5 crore rural persons Increased digital literacy in rural areas
- Cash vs. Accrual Budgeting - Note: - Outdated topic, removed from recent handouts. Cash budgeting records transactions when cash changes hands; accrual budgeting records when obligations are incurred. Not relevant for Prelims. - Lapsable Funds and March Rush - Definition: - Appropriation Act allows spending from the Consolidated Fund of India within one financial year (ending March 31). Unspent funds lapse and require fresh parliamentary approval. - March Rush: - Departments rush to spend funds in March to avoid lapse, often leading to inefficient or reckless expenditure. - Mitigation (2017–18): - Finance Ministry capped fourth-quarter spending at 33% and March spending at 15% of annual budget to curb wasteful spending. - Non-Lapsable Funds - Definition: - Funds that do not lapse on March 31, usable without fresh parliamentary approval (e.g., Nirbhaya Fund for women’s safety). - Criticism: - Lax utilization; e.g., Nirbhaya Fund (2013) had ~Rs. 3,000 crore by 2018, with less than 50% utilized. - 2018: - Defence Ministry’s request for a non-lapsable modernization fund rejected due to utilization concerns. - 15th Finance Commission: - Recommended Modernisation Fund for Defence and Internal Security (MFDIS) of Rs. 2.38 lakh crore (2021–26). -
Fund Purpose Issue
Nirbhaya Fund Women’s safety Underutilization
MFDIS Defence modernization Recommended by 15th FC
- Types of Schemes - Classification: - Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS): - Shared funding between Union and States. - Central Sector Schemes: - Fully funded by the Union. - Union Territory Schemes (without legislature): - 100% Union-funded. - Management: - Disbursed and monitored via Public Financial Management System (PFMS) by Controller General of Accounts (CGA), Department of Expenditure. - Budget 2022 CSS Rationalization: - Reduced CSS from 130 to 65 to eliminate duplication and improve efficiency. - Examples: - Stopped: - National Dairy Plan-II, Dairying Through Cooperatives. - Merged: - Women and Child Development Ministry: 19 schemes merged into Mission Shakti, Mission Vatsalya, Saksham Anganwadi, and POSHAN 2.0. - Agriculture Ministry: 20 CSS merged into Krishionati Yojana, Integrated Scheme on Agricultural Cooperative, Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana. - Impact: - Reduced subsidy burden and fiscal deficit through better monitoring. - Ministry-Wise Highest Allocation (Budget 2025) - Note: - Specific data not provided in the prompt; typically, ministries like Defence, Agriculture, and Education receive the highest allocations (in Rs. crore). Refer to Budget 2025 documents for exact figures. - Result-Based Financing - Definition: - Shifts from input-based financing (funding inputs like materials) to result-based financing (funding tied to outcomes, e.g., 15 km road constructed daily or 80% skill trainees employed). - Budget 2023: - Adopted for select schemes to ensure accountability, with penalties for unmet targets. -
Financing Type Description Impact
Input-Based Funds inputs (e.g., materials) Lacks outcome focus
Result-Based Funds tied to outcomes Enhances accountability
- Pillar 3A: Balance of Payment (BoP) - Academic Disclaimer: - Purpose: - Detailed overview of Balance of Payment (BoP) and international trade concepts for competitive exams (UPSC, State PSCs). - Sources: - Economic Surveys (ES 2018-24), RBI Annual Reports, IMF’s BPM-6 manual. - Reference Year: - 2018-19 used as a stable reference due to disruptions in 2020-22 (COVID-19, Russia-Ukraine war). - Recent Data (2024-25): - Partial data (Apr-Dec 2024, ES25) included for trends but less reliable for MCQs due to incomplete availability. - Notes about Data Tables: - 2018-19: - Stable reference year due to disruptions in 2020-22. - 2024-25: - Partial data (Apr-Dec 2024, ES25) less reliable for MCQs but included for trends. - Source: - RBI’s BoP statistics, ES (2018-24), IMF reports. - BoP - Meaning/Definition: - Definition: - Systematic record of all economic transactions (goods, services, capital) between residents and non-residents over a period (usually annual). - Prepared by: - RBI, following IMF’s BPM-6 manual, in USD. - Principle: - Global BoP nets to zero (one country’s debit is another’s credit). - Components: - Current Account: - Trade (goods, services), primary income (wages, dividends), secondary income (remittances, grants). - Capital Account: - FDI, FPI, loans, NRI deposits. - Errors & Omissions: - Adjusts discrepancies (e.g., unreported transactions). - Example: - India’s 2018-19 BoP: Current Account Deficit (-$57B), Capital Account Surplus (+$54B), balanced by forex reserves. -
AccountComponentsExamples
Current AccountGoods, services, primary income, secondary incomePetroleum, IT services, dividends, remittances ($76B)
Capital & Financial AccountFDI, FPI, ECB, NRI deposits, forex reservesWalmart FDI, FPI in Sensex, SBI ECB, FCNR deposits
- RBI’s Method of Classifying BoP: - Current Account: - Goods: - Physical trade (e.g., petroleum, electronics). - Services: - IT, tourism, transport. - Primary Income: - Investment income, wages (e.g., dividends from Indian firms abroad). - Secondary Income: - Remittances, grants (e.g., $76B remittances in 2018-19). - Capital & Financial Account: - FDI: - Long-term investments (>10% equity). - FPI: - Short-term investments (≤10% equity). - External Commercial Borrowings (ECB): - Loans from abroad. - NRI Deposits: - FCNR, NRE, NRO accounts. - RBI Forex Reserves: - Used to balance BoP. - Note: - For exams, focus on traditional classification (current + capital accounts) over IMF’s complex structure. - Balance of Payment - Current Account: - Components: - Visible: - Goods trade (e.g., exports: gems; imports: crude oil). - Invisible: - Services (e.g., IT exports), primary income (e.g., dividends), secondary income (e.g., remittances). - Data (2018-19): - Goods: - -$180B (deficit; exports $330B, imports $510B). - Services: - +$82B (surplus; exports $208B, imports $126B). - Primary Income: - -$28B (deficit; e.g., dividends paid to foreign investors). - Secondary Income: - +$70B (surplus; e.g., remittances from Gulf). - Net Current Account Deficit (CAD): - -$57B (2.1% of GDP). - Data (2020-21): - CAD surplus +$24B (0.9% GDP) due to low imports (lockdowns), strong pharma/IT exports. - Data (2023-24, ES25): - CAD -0.7% GDP, driven by higher oil/gold imports. -

- Exam Note: - CAD = Goods + Services + Primary + Secondary Income. Focus on trends (deficit vs. surplus). - Balance of Trade (BoT): - Definition: Difference between exports and imports. - NCERT: Goods only. - IMF: Goods + services. - Data (2018-19): - Goods Exports: $330B (e.g., petroleum products, gems). - Goods Imports: $510B (e.g., crude oil, electronics). - Services Exports: $208B (e.g., IT, telecom). - Services Imports: $126B (e.g., business services, transport). - BoT Goods: -$180B (deficit). - BoT Services: +$82B (surplus). - BoT Combined: -$98B (deficit). - Trends (2023-24, ES25): - Goods deficit widened due to oil/gold, services surplus grew (IT exports). -

- Terms of Trade: - Net Terms of Trade (NTT): - Formula: - (Value of Exports / Value of Imports) * 100. - India (2018-19): - ~64.7 (exports $330B, imports $510B; <100 due to higher import value). - Exam Note: - NTT <100 indicates unfavorable trade terms (imports costlier than exports). - Gross (Barter) Terms of Trade (GTT): - Formula: - (Quantity of Imports / Quantity of Exports) * 100. - India: - Typically <100 (exports higher in quantity, e.g., bulk agricultural goods vs. high-value oil imports). - Example: - India exports 10M tonnes of rice, imports 5M tonnes of oil; GTT <100 due to value disparity. - Income Terms of Trade (ITT): - Formula: - NTT * Quantum Index of Exports. - Relevance: - Critical for developing nations like India, as higher export volumes (e.g., IT services) improve ITT despite low NTT. - Exam Note: - ITT reflects real purchasing power of exports; less MCQ focus but useful for essays. - Benefits of International Trade: - To Government: - Generates foreign exchange reserves ($670B, 2024), creates jobs (IT sector employs 5.4M), boosts GDP (~40% from trade), promotes efficient resource allocation (labor-intensive exports like textiles). - To Exporting Companies: - Access to global markets (Tata Motors to ASEAN), higher profits via economies of scale, increased capacity utilization (pharma plants at 80%), reduced domestic competition (Indian IT vs. global firms). - To Customers: - Wider product choices (imported electronics like iPhones), better quality (German machinery), competitive pricing (Chinese solar panels). -
StakeholderBenefitsExamples
GovernmentForex reserves, jobs, GDP growth, efficiency$670B reserves, IT jobs (5.4M), ~40% GDP
Exporting CompaniesGlobal markets, profits, capacity, less competitionTata Motors (ASEAN), pharma (80%), IT vs. global firms

- Types of Economies: - Closed Economy: - Minimal trade, high self-sufficiency, restricted capital flows (e.g., North Korea, trade <5% of GDP). - Open Economy: - High trade and capital flows, liberalized markets (e.g., India post-1991, trade-to-GDP ratio ~40%; Singapore, 130%). - Mixed Economy: - Balances trade openness with domestic protection (e.g., India’s partial PSU dominance and private sector growth post-1991). - Developed vs. Developing: - Developed economies (e.g., USA, Germany) have advanced infrastructure, high-value exports (e.g., machinery, tech). Developing economies (e.g., India, Vietnam) focus on agriculture, services, labor-intensive goods. -
Economy TypeDescriptionExamplesTrade-to-GDP Ratio
Closed EconomyMinimal trade, self-sufficientNorth Korea<5%
Open EconomyHigh trade, capital flowsIndia, Singapore~40% (India), 130% (Singapore)
Mixed EconomyBalances openness, protectionIndia post-1991~40%
DevelopedAdvanced infra, high-value exportsUSA, Germany20-30%
DevelopingAgriculture, services, labor-intensiveIndia, Vietnam30-50%
- Circular Flow of Income & Import/Export: - Exports: - Inject foreign income into the economy, increasing household income, business revenues, and government tax collections (e.g., $208B service exports in 2018-19 boosted IT sector wages). - Imports: - Represent leakages, as domestic income is spent abroad (e.g., $510B goods imports in 2018-19 reduced domestic liquidity). - Impact: - Trade balances affect GDP, employment, and inflation. Surplus (exports > imports) stimulates growth; deficit (imports > exports) may weaken currency (e.g., INR depreciation in 2018). - Entry into International Business - Modes/Types: - Import/Export: - Direct trade of goods (e.g., India exporting basmati rice to UAE, importing crude oil from Saudi Arabia). - Contract Manufacturing: - Producing for foreign brands (e.g., Foxconn assembling iPhones in Chennai for Apple). - Outsourcing: - Delegating production/services (e.g., Godrej producing Dettol soaps for Reckitt Benckiser). - Franchising (Services): - Licensing brand and business model (e.g., McDonald’s via Westlife Foodworld in India). - Licensing (Goods): - Allowing production under brand (e.g., Pepsi via Varun Beverages for bottling). - Joint Venture: - Shared ownership (e.g., Maruti Suzuki, 56% Suzuki, 44% Indian stakeholders; Hero Honda ended 2010). - Wholly Owned Subsidiaries: - Full foreign ownership (e.g., Amazon India, 100% owned by Amazon USA). -
TypeMeaning/ExampleChallengesBenefits
Import/ExportTrading goods (basmati rice, crude oil)Transport costs, tariffs, exchange rate risksLow entry barriers, quick market access
Contract ManufacturingFoxconn for Apple iPhonesQuality control, low margins, dependencyTechnology transfer, job creation

LicensingPepsi via Varun BeveragesIP misuse, quality controlRoyalties, low investment
Joint VentureMaruti SuzukiPartner conflicts, profit sharingShared capital, risk, expertise
Wholly Owned SubsidiariesAmazon IndiaHigh investment, political risksFull control, high profits
- FAQ1: - Difference between contract manufacturing, licensing, joint venture? - Contract Manufacturing: Produces for another brand, no IP ownership (e.g., Foxconn for Apple). - Licensing: Grants IP rights for production (e.g., Pepsi formula to bottlers). - Joint Venture: Shared ownership/control (e.g., Maruti Suzuki). - FAQ2: - Benefits of partnerships? Access to capital, technology transfer, risk-sharing, market knowledge, innovation (e.g., Maruti Suzuki’s R&D from Japan). - Documents Related to Import/Export: - Quotation/Proforma Invoice: - Exporter’s offer (price, quantity, delivery date; e.g., tea exporter quoting $5/kg). - Indent/Receipt of Order: - Importer’s acceptance (e.g., UAE firm confirming tea order). - Importer-Exporter Code (IEC): - DGFT-issued license for trade (mandatory since 1993). - Certificate of Inspection: - Issued by Export Inspection Council (EIC) for quality (e.g., marine products). - Certificate of Origin: - Proves product’s origin (e.g., Darjeeling tea for EU markets). - End-User Certificate (EUC): - Ensures sensitive goods (e.g., defense equipment) aren’t resold to restricted nations. - Shipping Documents: - Bill of lading (sea), airway bill (air), packing list. - Demurrages: - Penalties for port delays (e.g., $10,000/day at Mundra Port). -

- Documents Related to Loans: - Bill of Exchange: - Definition: - Exporter’s written order to importer for payment at a future date (e.g., 90 days post-shipment). - Use: - Facilitates trade credit, often bank-guaranteed (e.g., SBI discounting exporter’s bill). - Example: - Indian textile exporter draws $100,000 bill on US importer, payable in 60 days. - Export Credit Guarantee/NIRVIC Scheme: - ECGC covers 90% of bank losses if exporter defaults (e.g., textile firm failing to repay export loan). - Letter of Credit/Undertaking: - Importer’s bank guarantees payment (e.g., HSBC issuing LoC for Nirav Modi’s diamond imports). - FAQ: - Export credit guarantee vs. letter of credit? - ECGC: - Protects exporter’s bank (e.g., SBI covering textile exporter’s default). - LoC: - Protects exporter by ensuring importer’s payment (e.g., HSBC for US buyer). -
DocumentMeaningExample
Export Credit Guarantee/NIRVICCovers bank losses for exporter defaultECGC covers 90% for textile loan
Letter of CreditImporter’s bank guarantees paymentHSBC for Nirav Modi’s imports

- Balance of Payment - Current Account - Import of Oil: - Context: - India imports 85% of crude oil ($140B, 2018-19). - Goal (2015): - Reduce oil imports by 10% by 2022 via domestic production, renewables. - Outcome: - Target missed; imports rose to 88% by 2022 due to limited domestic output. - Strategy: - Enhance exploration (HELP policy), promote ethanol blending (20% target by 2025), green energy (500 GW renewable capacity by 2030). - Domestic Oil Exploration Policies: - Nodal: - Directorate General of Hydrocarbons (DGH), Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas. - Pre-1991: - Only PSUs (ONGC, Oil India) explored oil/gas. - Post-1991: - Private players (Reliance, Cairn) allowed under NELP, HELP policies. -
PeriodPolicyPlayers
Pre-1991PSU MonopolyONGC, Oil India
Post-1991NELP, HELPReliance, Cairn, ONGC
- NELP’s Primary Limitations: - New Exploration Licensing Policy (NELP, 1997-2016): - Auction-based contracts for oil/gas blocks. - Issues: - Separate licenses for each hydrocarbon (oil, gas, shale). - Production-sharing contracts led to cost inflation (e.g., Reliance overstated KG Basin costs). - Reduced government revenue due to profit-sharing model. - High regulatory burden (multiple inspections, approvals). -
IssueDescriptionImpact
Separate LicensesOne license per hydrocarbonIncreased complexity
Cost InflationProducers overstated costsLower government revenue
Regulatory BurdenMultiple inspectionsDelayed projects
- HELP Policy: - Hydrocarbon Exploration and Licensing Policy (2016) replaced NELP. - Features: - Single uniform license for all hydrocarbons (oil, gas, shale, CBM). - Revenue-sharing model (government gets fixed % of revenue, not profits). - Market freedom (no price controls, free marketing). - Open Acreage Licensing Policy (OLAP): Companies choose exploration blocks. - Relaxed offshore norms (e.g., fewer environmental clearances). - Impact: - Increased private participation (e.g., Vedanta in Rajasthan), 50+ blocks awarded by 2023. -
FeatureDescriptionImpact
Single LicenseCovers all hydrocarbonsSimplified exploration
Revenue-SharingFixed % of revenueStable government income
Market FreedomNo price/marketing controlsAttracts private players
OLAPCompanies choose blocks50+ blocks awarded (2023)
- India’s Strategic Oil Reserves: - Objective: - Ensure energy security during crises (war, supply cuts). - Locations: - Visakhapatnam (1.33M tonnes), Chandikhol (1M tonnes), Padur (2.5M tonnes), Mangalore (1.5M tonnes). - Storage: - Underground rock caverns (cost-effective, secure). - Operator: - Indian Strategic Petroleum Reserves Ltd (ISPRL), under Ministry of Petroleum. - Capacity: - 5.33M tonnes (15 days of India’s oil needs, 2024). - Leasing Out: - Feb 2024, ISPRL leased 1M tonnes of vacant storage to private firms (e.g., ADNOC) due to funding constraints, generating revenue. -

- Crude Oil Prices & OPEC: - OPEC: - Established 1961, HQ Vienna, 14 members (Saudi Arabia, UAE, Iran, Iraq; Qatar exited 2019). - Price Drivers: - Supply: - OPEC cuts (e.g., 2022 raised prices to $100/barrel), Houthi attacks (Red Sea disruptions). - Demand: - Global growth (post-COVID travel), slowdowns (2020 lockdown). - India’s Suppliers (2021): - Iraq (23%), USA (15%), Saudi Arabia (14%). - Russia-Ukraine War (2022): - War spiked prices ($120/barrel, Feb 2022); OPEC (UAE, Iraq) increased output, prices fell 17% ($100/barrel, Mar 2022); India imported discounted Russian oil (20% of imports by 2023, $30/barrel savings). - Price Cap (2022): - G7 capped Russian oil at $60/barrel; India rejected, bought at $70/barrel, saved $5B (2022-23). -
FactorPrice UpPrice DownExample
SupplyOPEC cuts, Houthi attacksOPEC increases, US shale2022: $100/barrel
DemandGlobal growth, travel surgeLockdowns, slowdown2020: -$40/barrel (WTI)
- Crude Oil - Theory/GK: - Cartel: - OPEC’s collusion to control prices (e.g., 1973 oil crisis). - Sweet Crude: - Low sulfur (<0.5%; e.g., Brent). - Sour Crude: - High sulfur (>1%; e.g., Middle East oil). - Brent Index: - Global oil price benchmark (North Sea, $80/barrel, 2023). - 1 Barrel: - 159 liters, 42 US gallons. -

Brent IndexOil price benchmarkNorth Sea, $80/barrel (2023)
- Fall of Crude Oil Prices in Negative: - Apr 2020: - WTI prices fell to -$40/barrel due to COVID lockdowns, storage shortages. - India’s Impact: - Limited benefit (imports tied to Brent, not WTI; Middle East focus). - ES21: - India saved $10B on oil imports in 2020 due to low prices. - Balance of Payment - Current Account - Import of Gold: - Context: - India 2nd largest gold consumer ($33B imports, 2018-19) after China. - Drivers (2010-14): - High inflation (8-12%), negative real interest rates, cultural demand (weddings, festivals). - Impacts: - Widens CAD ($10B+ annually), weakens INR, encourages black money (unreported gold purchases). - ES24: - Gold imports rose 30% in 2023-24 due to global uncertainty, festival demand. - RBI’s 80:20 Scheme (2013-14): - Rule: - 20% of imported gold must be exported (e.g., as jewelry). - Objective: - Curb CAD, reduce INR depreciation. - Outcome: - Reduced gold imports by 10% in 2014; scheme ended as demand stabilized. - Sovereign Gold Bond Scheme (2015): - Issued by: - RBI, sold via banks, post offices. - Features: - Interest-bearing (2.5% p.a.), 8-year tenure, redeemable in cash, linked to market prices. - Objective: - Reduce physical gold imports, promote financial savings. - Impact: - ₹1.2 lakh crore issued by 2024, reduced gold imports by 5%. - Gold Monetization Scheme (2015): - Mechanism: - Deposit idle gold (min. 30g) in banks for 1-15 years; used for coins, jewelry, electronics. - Interest Rates: - Short-term (1-3 years, bank-decided), Medium-term (5-7 years, 2.25%), Long-term (12-15 years, 2.5%). - Impact: - Low uptake (20 tonnes deposited by 2023 vs. 1000 tonnes target). - Discontinued (2025): - Mar 2025, medium/long-term deposits stopped due to high gold prices (₹90,000/10g), low participation; short-term deposits optional for banks. -
TypeDurationInterest (%)Authority
Short-Term1-3 yearsBank-decidedBank
Medium-Term5-7 years2.25Govt
Long-Term12-15 years2.5Govt
- Gold Investment Schemes - Taxes: - Sovereign Gold Bonds: - Interest taxable (income tax), capital gains exempt if held till maturity. - Gold Monetization: - Interest and capital gains tax-exempt. -

- Indian (Sovereign) Gold Coins (2015): - Issued by: - MMTC, sold via banks, post offices. - Denominations: - 5g, 10g, 20g (99.9% purity, Ashoka Chakra logo). - Not legal tender (no INR marking). - Benefits: - Assured purity, easy resale, high liquidity. - Impact: - Reduced demand for unverified gold, promoted domestic refining. - Gold Price in Corona: - 2020: - ₹56,000/10g (Aug) due to low bank rates, geopolitical tensions, safe-haven demand. - 2021: - Fell to ₹45,000/10g (Mar) with vaccinations, economic recovery. - 2024-25: - ₹90,000/10g due to weak markets, global uncertainty (e.g., US election, Ukraine war). -

- Lab Grown Diamonds (LGD): - Natural Diamonds: - Formed in Earth’s mantle (1-3B years), costly ($2000/carat), ethical issues (deforestation, child labor in Africa). - LGD: - Produced via CVD/HPHT in labs (2-4 weeks), cheaper ($800/carat), ethical, identical properties. - Budget-2023: - Reduced customs duty (5% to 0%), R&D grants to IITs, seed funding for LGD startups. - Impact: - India’s LGD exports doubled to $1B (2023-24). -

- BoP - Current - Export - GI Tag: - Geographical Indication (GI): - Identifies products with specific origin (e.g., Darjeeling tea, Banarasi saree). - Benefits: - Premium pricing (e.g., Darjeeling tea at $100/kg vs. $10/kg for regular tea), higher export revenue, cultural protection. - Governance: - WTO’s TRIPS Agreement, India’s GI Act (1999, 10-year validity, renewable). - Nodal: - WIPO (global), GI Registry (Chennai, India). - Stats: - 400+ GI tags by 2024 (e.g., Alphonso mango, Basmati rice). - GI Logo: - Commerce Ministry’s logo: “Invaluable Treasures of Incredible India,” used on GI products (e.g., Kancheepuram silk). -

- FDI - Inward vs. Outward Direct Investment (ODI): - Inward: - Foreign firms investing in India (e.g., Foxconn’s $1B iPhone plant). - Outward: - Indian firms investing abroad (e.g., Tata’s $2B acquisition of Jaguar Land Rover). - Stats: - Inward FDI ($44B, 2023), ODI ($15B, 2023). - Gross vs. Net FDI Inflow: - Gross: - Total FDI inflow ($64B, 2018-19). - Net: - Gross - Repatriation ($30B, 2018-19; e.g., Jack Ma selling Paytm stake). - ES24: - Net FDI fell to $25B (2023) due to repatriation, US Fed rate hikes. - ES25 - FDI Performance: - 2000-2024: - Cumulative FDI inflows exceeded $1T, India 9th globally (UNCTAD 2023). - 2024 Challenges: - Global uncertainty (US election tariffs, Ukraine war), high borrowing costs (global rates at 5%), repatriation ($20B, e.g., SoftBank exiting Paytm). - Data (2023-24): - Gross FDI $70B, Net FDI $25B (after $45B repatriation). - Sectors: - IT (25%), manufacturing (20%), services (15%). -

- Balance of Payment - Disequilibrium: - Definition: - Persistent surplus or deficit in BoP, impacting forex reserves, currency stability. - Causes: - Trade Deficit: - High imports (e.g., oil, $140B in 2018-19). - Capital Outflows: - FPI exits ($15B in 2024). - Global Shocks: - Oil price spikes, geopolitical tensions (e.g., Ukraine war). - Types: - Temporary: - Short-term (e.g., 2020 CAD surplus due to low imports). - Structural: - Persistent (e.g., oil import dependence). - Correction Measures: - Monetary: - RBI intervention (e.g., selling USD to stabilize INR). - Fiscal: - Import tariffs (e.g., 20% on gold), export subsidies (RoDTEP). - Structural: - Reduce oil imports (HELP, ethanol blending), boost exports (SEZs, FTP-2023). -

TypesTemporary, structural2020 surplus, oil dependence
CorrectionsMonetary, fiscal, structuralRBI USD sales, gold tariffs, SEZs
- Exchange Rate System: - Definition: - Mechanism to determine currency value in international trade. - Types: - Fixed: - Pegged to USD/gold (e.g., Bretton Woods, 1944-71). - Floating: - Market-driven (e.g., INR since 1993, managed float). - Managed Float: - RBI intervenes to stabilize INR (e.g., 2013 crisis, $10B USD sold). - India’s System: - Managed float since 1993; INR value based on demand/supply, RBI interventions. - Data: - INR depreciated 3% in 2024 (₹83/USD) due to FPI outflows, US Fed rate hikes. -
