- Pillar 3B1: Balance of Payments and Currency Exchange - Definition: - The Balance of Payments (BoP) is a systematic record of all economic transactions between residents of a country and the rest of the world over a specific period, typically a year. It reflects the inflow and outflow of money, influencing exchange rates and economic stability. Currency exchange involves the conversion of one currency into another, determined by exchange rates, which are critical for international trade and financial stability. - Context: - BoP and currency exchange are pivotal for India’s economic policy, affecting trade, investment, and monetary stability. Managed by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), these elements ensure India can meet external obligations, stabilize the rupee, and foster economic growth. - Significance for India: - A stable BoP supports India’s economic resilience, enhances global creditworthiness, and mitigates external shocks, such as oil price volatility or global financial crises. Robust foreign exchange reserves and remittances strengthen India’s position in the global economy. - Balance of Payments (BoP): Overview - Definition: - BoP is divided into two main accounts: the Current Account, which records trade in goods, services, income, and transfers (e.g., remittances), and the Capital Account, which includes capital transfers, foreign investments (FDI, FPI), and external commercial borrowings (ECB). Errors and Omissions account for discrepancies, ensuring the BoP balances to zero. - Components: - Current Account: - Includes trade balance (exports minus imports of goods), services (e.g., IT exports), income (e.g., investment earnings), and transfers (e.g., remittances). - Capital Account: - Covers foreign direct investment (FDI), foreign portfolio investment (FPI), external commercial borrowings, and other capital flows. - Errors and Omissions: - Captures unrecorded transactions to balance the BoP. - Official Reserve Transactions: - Reflects changes in RBI’s foreign exchange reserves to balance surplus or deficit. - BoP Dynamics (2018-19 vs. 2020-21): -
Component 2018-19 (Bn USD) 2020-21 (Bn USD)
Current Account -57 (Deficit) +24 (Surplus)
Capital Account +54 (Surplus) +64 (Surplus)
Errors & Omissions -0.3 -0.486
Overall Balance -3 +87
Official Reserve Transactions +3 (RBI sold dollars) -87 (RBI bought dollars)
Net BoP 0 0
- Transaction Types: - Autonomous Transactions: - Occur independently of RBI intervention (e.g., trade, investments). - Accommodating Transactions: - RBI’s actions (e.g., buying/selling dollars) to balance BoP to zero. - Sterilization - Definition: - Sterilization is the process by which the RBI neutralizes the impact of foreign exchange interventions on the domestic money supply to prevent inflation or deflation. - Mechanism: - When RBI sells dollars to stabilize the rupee, it injects rupees into the market, increasing money supply and risking inflation. To counter this, RBI conducts Open Market Operations (OMO), selling government securities to absorb excess rupees. - Example: In 2018-19, RBI sold $3 billion to balance BoP, requiring sterilization to manage rupee liquidity. - Significance: - Prevents inflationary pressure from excess rupee circulation, ensuring monetary stability while maintaining exchange rate targets. - Mundell-Fleming Trilemma - Definition: - The Mundell-Fleming Trilemma, proposed by economists Robert Mundell and Marcus Fleming in the 1960s, states that a country cannot simultaneously achieve a fixed exchange rate, free capital mobility, and an independent monetary policy. Only two of these objectives can be pursued effectively at the same time. - Application to India (2020): - During the COVID-19 pandemic, RBI prioritized exchange rate stability due to supply-demand mismatches causing potential inflation. It allowed limited capital mobility and adjusted monetary policy (e.g., lowering interest rates) to support economic recovery, illustrating the trilemma’s trade-offs. - Implications: - India’s managed float exchange rate regime balances these constraints, allowing RBI to intervene in forex markets while maintaining partial control over monetary policy. - India’s Balance of Payments Crisis (1991) - Context: - In 1991, India faced a severe BoP crisis due to a high current account deficit, inadequate capital account surplus, and depleted foreign exchange reserves, exacerbated by external shocks like the Gulf War and internal policy inefficiencies. - Causes of Disequilibrium: - Macroeconomic Imbalances: - High fiscal deficit (12.7% of GDP in 1990-91) and current account deficit (3.1% of GDP) due to excessive government spending and import reliance.[](http://indiabefore91.in/1991-crisis) - External Shocks: - Gulf War (1990-91) increased oil import costs by 60%, straining reserves as India relied on Iraq and Kuwait for oil.[](http://indiabefore91.in/1991-crisis) - Policy Constraints: - Pre-1991 Nehruvian socialist policies nationalized key sectors (e.g., banking, insurance), restricted private sector growth via Licence-Quota-Inspector Raj, and limited FDI/FPI due to fears of foreign influence, reducing export competitiveness and capital inflows.[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis) - Structural Issues: - Poor infrastructure and technological backwardness hindered export growth, leading to persistent trade deficits. - Consumerism: - Demand for imported luxury goods (e.g., Swiss watches, sports cars) increased the trade deficit. - Impact: - Foreign exchange reserves fell to $1.2 billion by January 1991, covering only three weeks of imports, risking default on external debt.[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis) - Rupee devaluation in two steps (July 1991) increased import costs, exacerbating inflation (13% in 1991-92).[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis) - India pledged 67 tonnes of gold to IMF for a $2.2 billion emergency loan, with 47 tonnes airlifted to the Bank of England and 20 tonnes to Union Bank of Switzerland.[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis) - Resolution: - The crisis prompted the New Economic Policy (LPG reforms: Liberalization, Privatization, Globalization) under Finance Minister Manmohan Singh, reducing import tariffs, devaluing the rupee, and opening the economy to FDI/FPI, fostering long-term stability.[](https://testbook.com/ias-preparation/balance-payment-crisis-1991) - Additional Factors: - Secular Disequilibrium: - Long-term import reliance in newly independent India (1947-1990s) due to underdevelopment. - Cyclical Disequilibrium: - Mismatches in business cycles with trading partners reduced export demand during global slowdowns. - RBI’s Foreign Exchange Reserves - Definition: - Foreign exchange reserves are assets held by the RBI in foreign currencies, gold, Special Drawing Rights (SDRs), and Reserve Tranche Position (RTP) with the IMF, used to stabilize the rupee, meet external obligations, and buffer economic shocks.[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves) - Components: - Foreign Currency Assets (FCA): - Includes major currencies (e.g., US dollar, euro) and foreign government securities (e.g., US Treasury bonds). Accounts for ~87% of reserves ($616 billion in September 2024).[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_India) - Gold Reserves: - Held as a hedge against inflation and economic uncertainty (695.31 metric tonnes in March 2021, with 10.4% of reserves in December 2024).[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_India)[](https://www.forbesindia.com/article/explainers/indian-forex-reserves/87581/1) - Special Drawing Rights (SDRs): - IMF-allocated reserve assets ($18.547 billion in September 2024).[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_India) - Reserve Tranche Position (RTP): - India’s quota contribution to IMF, accessible as emergency credit ($4.3 billion in September 2024).[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_India) - Current Status (2024-2025): - India’s forex reserves reached $704.89 billion in September 2024, the fourth largest globally after China ($3 trillion), Japan (~$1.2 trillion), and Switzerland.[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_India)[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves) - Covers ~90% of external debt ($700+ billion) and over 11 months of imports, indicating robust adequacy.[](https://www.drishtiias.com/loksabha-rajyasabha-discussions/perspective-india-s-forex-reserves-hit-record-high) - Gold Repatriation (2024): - RBI moved 100 metric tonnes of gold from the Bank of England to India in 2024, reducing reliance on foreign storage due to geopolitical risks (e.g., Western sanctions on Russia in 2022). Total gold: 822 metric tonnes, with 408 tonnes now stored domestically.[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_economic_crisis) - Reserve Dynamics: -
Factor Reserves Increase Reserves Decrease
BoP Transactions RBI buys dollars in surplus scenarios (e.g., high FDI/FPI inflows). RBI sells dollars to cover deficits (e.g., high current account deficit).
RBI Interventions RBI buys dollars to prevent rupee appreciation, aiding exporters. RBI sells dollars to stabilize rupee during depreciation.
Valuation Effect Market value of assets (e.g., gold, euro) rises. Market value of assets falls (e.g., gold price drop in 2023).
- Significance: - Stabilizes exchange rates by enabling RBI interventions to manage rupee volatility.[](https://www.moneyhop.co/blog/role-of-rbi-in-indias-foreign-exchange/) - Absorbs external shocks (e.g., oil price spikes, global crises like Sri Lanka’s 2022 default). - Enhances creditworthiness, attracting FDI and supporting external debt payments. - Adequacy Indicators (IMF): - Reserves to Imports Ratio: - Should cover at least 6 months of imports (India: ~11 months in 2024).[](https://www.drishtiias.com/loksabha-rajyasabha-discussions/perspective-india-s-forex-reserves-hit-record-high) - Reserves to External Debt Ratio: - Should cover short-term debt for 1 year (India: ~90% of total external debt).[](https://www.drishtiias.com/loksabha-rajyasabha-discussions/perspective-india-s-forex-reserves-hit-record-high) - Reserves to Monetary Aggregates (e.g., M3): - Ensures liquidity relative to money supply; specific thresholds vary. - Negative Implications: - Opportunity Costs: - Funds locked in reserves are unavailable for domestic priorities (e.g., health, education, infrastructure).[](https://www.forbesindia.com/article/explainers/indian-forex-reserves/87581/1) - Imported Inflation: - Deliberate currency weakening to boost exports can increase import costs (e.g., crude oil), fueling inflation. - Neglect of Non-Tradable Sectors: - Focus on export-driven tradable sectors (e.g., IT, manufacturing) may underfund non-tradable sectors like healthcare and education, impacting social development. - Remittances and BoP Stability - Definition: - Remittances are funds sent by migrant workers to their home country, classified as unilateral transfers under the Current Account’s “invisibles.” - Role in BoP: - Remittances provide stable foreign currency inflows, narrowing trade and current account deficits, especially for import-dependent countries like India.[](https://www.drishtiias.com/daily-updates/daily-news-analysis/india-s-balance-of-payments) - Unlike FDI/FPI, remittances are less volatile, offering consistent support during economic downturns (Economic Survey 2024 observation). - Relationship with Oil Prices and Exchange Rates: - Oil Prices: - Higher global oil prices increase jobs and remittances from Indian workers in oil-rich Middle Eastern countries. Conversely, falling oil prices reduce jobs and remittances.[](https://www.moneyhop.co/blog/impact-of-balance-of-payment-on-exchange-rate/) - Exchange Rates: - A weaker rupee increases the rupee value of remittances (e.g., $1,000 sent from abroad rises from ₹75,000 to ₹84,000), boosting inflows. - Significance: - India is the world’s largest recipient of remittances (~$100 billion annually), supporting BoP stability and household consumption, particularly in states like Kerala and Punjab. - Additional Insights: - Remittances are more stable than FDI/FPI, which are pro-cyclical (increase during booms, decrease during crises), making them a critical buffer for India’s BoP. - Currency Exchange Rate - Definition: - The exchange rate is the price of one currency in terms of another (e.g., $1 = ₹84), determined by supply and demand in the foreign exchange market.[](https://www.slideshare.net/slideshow/the-relation-between-balance-of-payment-and-foreign-exchange-rate/190798861) - Types of Exchange Rate Regimes: - Fixed Exchange Rate: - Currency is pegged to another (e.g., Bretton Woods system), requiring RBI intervention to maintain the peg. - Floating Exchange Rate: - Market-driven rates with minimal intervention (e.g., US dollar). India follows a managed float, balancing market forces and RBI interventions. - Key Concepts: - Nominal Effective Exchange Rate (NEER): - Weighted average of a country’s currency against a basket of trading partners’ currencies, not adjusted for inflation. A decline in NEER indicates currency depreciation. - Real Effective Exchange Rate (REER): - NEER adjusted for inflation differences. REER > 100 suggests currency overvaluation, reducing export competitiveness; REER < 100 indicates undervaluation.[](https://testbook.com/question-answer/the-balance-of-payments-of-a-country-is-a-systemat--60266b82b512515961f2876a) - Purchasing Power Parity (PPP): - Theory stating that exchange rates should adjust to equalize the purchasing power of currencies across countries in the long run (e.g., a burger costing $5 in the US should cost ₹420 in India at $1 = ₹84). Often fails in the short run due to trade barriers.[](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/37626214_The_Balance_of_Payments_and_the_Exchange_Rate) - Special Drawing Rights (SDRs): - IMF’s international reserve asset, a basket of currencies (USD, EUR, CNY, JPY, GBP), used to supplement member countries’ reserves. India holds $18.547 billion in SDRs (2024).[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_India) - RBI’s Role: - Manages exchange rate volatility through forex interventions, interest rate adjustments, and open market operations to stabilize the rupee and control imported inflation.[](https://www.moneyhop.co/blog/role-of-rbi-in-indias-foreign-exchange/) - Significance: - Exchange rates impact trade competitiveness, import costs, and inflation. A weaker rupee boosts exports but raises import costs (e.g., crude oil), affecting India’s BoP. - Pillar 3B2: Currency Exchange Rate - Definition: - The currency exchange rate is the price of one currency in terms of another, determined by supply and demand in the foreign exchange market. It influences international trade, investment, and economic stability, with the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) managing volatility to stabilize the Indian rupee. - Context: - Exchange rates are critical for India’s Balance of Payments (BoP), affecting export competitiveness, import costs, and foreign investment. India operates a managed float regime, balancing market forces with RBI interventions to ensure economic stability. - Significance for India: - Stable exchange rates enhance India’s global trade competitiveness, reduce imported inflation (e.g., from oil prices), and support financial inflows like remittances and foreign investments, aligning with goals of economic self-reliance (Atmanirbhar Bharat). - Exchange Rate Regimes: Theory - Definition: - An exchange rate regime is the set of rules governing how a country’s currency is exchanged with foreign currencies, influencing economic policy and trade. - Types of Regimes: - Fixed/Pegged Exchange Rate: - Currency value is tied to another currency (e.g., US dollar) or asset (e.g., gold), requiring central bank interventions to maintain the peg. - Floating Exchange Rate: - Currency value is determined by market forces with minimal central bank intervention (e.g., US dollar). - Managed Float: - Hybrid system where the currency fluctuates within a range, with central bank interventions to stabilize excessive volatility (India’s current regime since 1993). - Appreciation vs. Depreciation: - Appreciation: Currency increases in value relative to others (e.g., $1 = ₹60 to ₹40), benefiting importers but hurting exporters. - Depreciation: Currency decreases in value (e.g., $1 = ₹60 to ₹80), boosting exports but increasing import costs. -
Scenario Indian Exporter (e.g., Tea) Indian Importer (e.g., Dell Laptops)
Weaker Rupee ($1 = ₹60 → ₹80) Benefits: Higher rupee earnings from exports Loss: Higher costs for imports
Stronger Rupee ($1 = ₹60 → ₹40) Loss: Lower rupee earnings from exports Benefits: Lower costs for imports
- Foreign Exchange Market: - The market where currencies are traded, operated by Authorized Dealers (ADs) like banks and non-banking financial companies (NBFCs), registered under the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999. - ADs set different buying and selling rates (e.g., ICICI: $1 buy at ₹67.95, sell at ₹72.76) to earn profits, with transactions subject to GST based on the amount exchanged. - Runaway Inflation and Depreciation - Runaway Inflation: - Uncontrollable, rapid increase in prices of goods and services, often triggered by excessive money supply or supply chain disruptions (e.g., Zimbabwe’s hyperinflation in 2008). - Runaway Depreciation: - Rapid decline in a currency’s value relative to others (e.g., Pakistani rupee: $1 = PKR 175 in 2022 to PKR 300 in 2023), exacerbating inflation by increasing import costs. - Interlinkage: - Depreciation fuels inflation by raising import prices (e.g., oil, electronics), while inflation erodes currency value, creating a vicious cycle. - Fixed/Pegged System Challenges: Sri Lanka (2022) - Context: - Sri Lanka’s 2022 economic crisis saw its rupee collapse due to a fixed exchange rate system, high debt, and depleted forex reserves, leading to default on external debt. - Challenges: - Insufficient Reserves: - Sri Lanka’s central bank lacked adequate forex reserves to defend the pegged rate, unable to supply dollars to meet import demands. - External Shocks: - Rising oil prices and tourism collapse post-COVID increased the current account deficit, straining the peg. - Policy Failures: - Excessive borrowing and populist policies (e.g., tax cuts) led to fiscal deficits, undermining the fixed rate’s sustainability. - Outcome: - Rupee devaluation and shift to a floating regime, coupled with IMF bailout conditions, highlighted the risks of fixed systems in volatile economies. - Managed Float System Challenges - Currency Speculation: - Speculators bet on currency movements, increasing volatility. RBI counters this by buying/selling dollars to stabilize the rupee. - Interest Rate Arbitrage: - Higher interest rates in one country (e.g., US Federal Reserve’s tapering) attract capital, strengthening that currency and weakening others like the rupee. - Currency Manipulation: - Central banks may buy dollars to weaken their currency, boosting exports. The US Treasury monitors such actions, listing countries like India and China on its “watch list” for alleged lack of transparency. - Example (2024-2025): - FPI outflows from India’s stock market (October 2024) weakened the rupee to $1 = ₹86.70 by January 2025. RBI’s non-intervention reflected a shift to market-driven rates to conserve forex reserves. - Currency War and Global Dynamics - Currency War (2018): - US accused China, Russia, and Japan of deliberately weakening their currencies to gain trade advantages, making their exports cheaper. China’s Yuan was allegedly kept weak to counter US tariffs. - Mar-a-Lago Accord (2025): - Proposed by US President Trump to weaken the dollar, similar to the 1985 Plaza Accord, to boost US exports. It involves foreign central banks selling dollars from their reserves to reduce dollar strength (deal pending as of April 2025). - Reverse Currency War: - Refers to competitive currency appreciation to attract investment, though less relevant for UPSC unless highlighted in future Economic Surveys. - Soft vs. Hard Currency - Definitions: - Hard Currency: - Stable, widely accepted currencies with low volatility (e.g., US dollar, euro, pound sterling), held as reserves by central banks. - Soft Currency: - High volatility currencies from developing nations (e.g., African, Asian currencies), less trusted globally. - Factors Affecting Soft Currency: - Customer Income: - Lower purchasing power reduces currency demand. - Forex Supply/Demand: - Scarcity of dollars increases depreciation pressure. - Interest Rate Arbitrage: - Capital flows to high-interest countries, weakening soft currencies. - Speculation: - Speculative trading exacerbates volatility. - Soft vs. Hard Dollars: - A technical concept related to US mutual fund commissions, not critical for UPSC exams. - Bad Money Drives Out Good Money (Gresham’s Law) - Gresham’s Law: - When two currencies circulate, the “bad” (less valued) currency drives out the “good” (more valued) currency, as people hoard the good currency and spend the bad. - Thiers’ Law: - Contrarily suggests good money drives out bad money, as people prefer higher-quality currency. Applicability depends on context and MCQ framing. - Modern Examples: - Zimbabwe (2008): - Hyperinflation (e.g., bread costing 10 million Zimbabwe dollars) led to preference for US dollars (good money) over local currency (bad money). - Zimbabwe Gold Coin (2022): - Introduced as Mosi-oa-Tunya ($1,800 per coin) to counter hyperinflation, but lacked foreign acceptance. - Sri Lanka (2022): - Foreigners rejected the fixed-rate Sri Lankan rupee ($1 = LKR 200) due to its perceived low value. - Historical Exchange Rate Regimes - Gold Standard (1870-1914): - Currencies were backed by gold (e.g., $1 = 14g gold, £1 = 73g gold), enabling fixed exchange rates based on gold ratios. Bimetallism (gold/silver backing) was used when gold supplies dwindled. - Collapsed during World War I due to excessive money printing beyond gold reserves. - Bretton Woods System (1946-1971): - US dollar was pegged to gold ($35 = 1 ounce), and other currencies were pegged to the dollar (e.g., ₹1 = $0.30). Managed by the IMF, it enforced devaluation for excessive money printing. - Collapsed in 1971 when the US ended gold convertibility to fund Cold War expenses, shifting to floating/managed float systems. - Currency Exchange in India: - Pre-1991: - Fixed Fiduciary System (1860s): - British India issued rupees backed by government securities (up to ₹10 crore) and gold/silver beyond that. - Proportional Reserve (1935): - RBI required 40% gold backing for currency. - Bretton Woods (1946): - Rupee pegged to the dollar, which was pegged to gold. - Minimum Reserve System (1956): - RBI mandated to hold ₹400 crore in forex/gold, with excess currency backed by government securities. - Post-1991: - Shift to managed float (1993) with minimal reserve requirements (₹115 crore gold), allowing RBI to print currency backed by assets like government securities and forex. - Liberalization of Currency Exchange - Context: - Triggered by the 1991 BoP crisis, India undertook reforms to liberalize exchange rates and boost exports. - Key Reforms: - 1991: - Rupee devalued by 18-19% to enhance export competitiveness. - 1992: - Dual exchange rate system introduced (market rate + RBI-fixed rate for importers). - 1993: - Unified managed float system adopted, discontinuing dual rates. - Outcome: - Rupee depreciation improved exports, attracted FDI, and supported economic recovery post-1991. - IMF Special Drawing Rights (SDR) - Definition: - SDR is an artificial currency created by the IMF to supplement member countries’ reserves, used to settle BoP crises or convert into other currencies. - Composition (2022): -
Currency Weight (2022) Previous Weight Exchange Rate vs. USD
US Dollar 43.38% 41.73% $1 = $1
Euro 29.31% 30.93% 1.13
Chinese Yuan 12.28% 10.92% 6.7
Japanese Yen 7.59% 8.33% -
Pound Sterling 7.44% 8.09% -
- Valuation: - 1 SDR = $1.40 (₹98 at $1 = ₹70). Known as “paper gold” due to its accounting nature, not backed by physical gold. - Usage: - Traded among IMF members to settle BoP deficits or converted into dollars for import payments. Additional SDR loans are available with interest. - Quota Reforms (2016-2021): - India’s IMF quota increased to 2.76% (8th largest), granting ~$17-18 billion in SDRs from the 2021 $650 billion allocation. - Governance: - Voting power is tied to quotas, exercised by India’s Finance Minister (ex-officio IMF Governor) or RBI Governor (Alternate Governor). - Challenges for India: - Indian rupee is not in the SDR basket due to limited export volume and partial capital account convertibility. - Currency Convertibility - Definition: - Convertibility refers to the ease of converting a currency into foreign currencies for current or capital account transactions, regulated by RBI under FEMA, 1999. - Current Account Convertibility: - Fully convertible since 1994 for trade, remittances, and income transfers, with minor restrictions (e.g., 2013-14 gold import 80:20 rule requiring 20% re-export). - Capital Account Convertibility: - Partially convertible with restrictions to prevent volatility: - External Commercial Borrowing (ECB): Capped at $1.5 billion per company annually. - Foreign Portfolio Investment (FPI): Limited to 5% in government securities, 20% in corporate bonds. - Foreign Direct Investment (FDI): Prohibited in sectors like casinos. - Full Convertibility (S.S. Tarapore Committee, 1997): - Recommended gradual adoption when: - Forex reserves cover 6 months of imports. - Fiscal deficit ≤ 3.5% of GDP. - Inflation ≤ 3-5%. - Bank NPAs ≤ 5% of assets. - Risks: East Asian crisis (1997) showed full convertibility led to capital flight, currency depreciation (e.g., Indonesian rupiah: $1 = IDR 2,000 to IDR 18,000), and economic instability. - RBI Reforms (2004-2019): - Liberalized Remittance Scheme (LRS, 2004): Allows $250,000 annual overseas remittances for current/capital transactions. - ECB limits relaxed to $1.5 billion (2022). Restrictions remain on sectors like gambling and real estate. - FEMA Rules (2022): - Prohibits Indian residents from investing in foreign gambling or real estate firms. Wilful defaulters require a No Objection Certificate (NOC) for overseas financial commitments. - Liberalized Remittance Scheme (LRS) and Tax Collection at Source (TCS) - LRS Overview: - Allows Indian residents to remit up to $250,000 annually for current or capital account transactions (e.g., education, investments) without RBI approval. Beyond this, RBI permission is required. - TCS Rates (October 2023): -
Transaction Type Up to ₹7 Lakh Beyond ₹7 Lakh
Foreign Tour/Shopping 5% 20%
Overseas Investments 0% 20%
Education/Medical 0% 5%
Other (e.g., Donations) 0% 20%
- Objectives: - Discourage excessive foreign spending to reduce current account deficits. - Enhance tax surveillance to curb evasion, with TCS adjustable against income tax liability. - FCRA (Foreign Contribution Regulation Act, 2010): - Regulates foreign donations to NGOs/universities to prevent misuse (e.g., by foreign agencies like ISI/CIA). - Requires registration and annual reporting; non-compliance bans foreign donations. - 2022 Reform: Increased remittance limit from relatives abroad to ₹10 lakh (from ₹1 lakh) without reporting, boosting forex inflows. - RBI’s Currency Swaps and Forex Mechanisms - Currency Swaps: - Agreements where RBI exchanges rupees for foreign currency (e.g., dollars) with another central bank or Indian banks to manage liquidity and stabilize the rupee. - Example: - $75 billion swap agreement with Japan allows access to each other’s reserves during crises. - 2022: - RBI’s $5 billion swap with Indian banks increased dollar supply to prevent rupee depreciation. - Nostro/Vostro Accounts: - Nostro: - Indian bank accounts held abroad in foreign currency (e.g., SBI’s dollar account in the US). - Vostro: - Foreign bank accounts held in India in rupees (e.g., Russian bank’s rupee account for oil trade). - Special Rupee Vostro Account (SRVA): - Facilitates trade settlements with countries like Russia and Sri Lanka in rupees, bypassing dollar-based SWIFT sanctions. - Rupee-Ruble Agreement: - India pays for Iranian oil in rupees via Vostro accounts, reducing dollar dependence amid geopolitical tensions. - Bank Realisation Certificate (BRC): - Documents the exchange rate for exporters’ foreign currency earnings, required for tax benefits under Foreign Trade Policy. Electronic BRCs (e-BRC) expedite processes. - Foreign Inward Remittance Certificate (FIRC): - Proof of foreign currency receipts, issued digitally (e-FIRC) since 2016 to combat money laundering. - Bharat Connect FX-Retail Platform (2019): - Developed by NPCI and CCIL, enables banks (e.g., Axis, HDFC) and MSMEs to buy/sell forex efficiently. RBI plans to integrate it with Bharat Bill Payment for broader access. - Internationalization of the Indian Rupee - Definition: - Increasing the rupee’s use in global current and capital account transactions and as a reserve currency in other nations’ forex reserves. - Current Status (ES23): - Rupee’s share in global forex reserves: 1.6% (vs. 88% for US dollar). Minimum 4% required for “international currency” status. - Benefits: - Reduces dollar dependency, mitigating exchange rate volatility and imported inflation (e.g., oil prices). - Facilitates trade with sanctioned countries (e.g., Russia, Iran) outside SWIFT. - Lowers transaction costs by eliminating forex conversion fees. - Challenges: - Limited export diversity restricts rupee acceptance (e.g., Russia may not need Indian goods post-Ukraine war). - FPI panic-selling in Indian markets could crash stock prices, increasing volatility. - Global shocks (e.g., US Fed tapering) may destabilize rupee-based transactions. - Conclusion: - Internationalization aligns with Atmanirbhar Bharat but requires enhanced export competitiveness and economic stability. - Yuan as Global Currency - Progress: - Added to SDR basket in 2015, increasing global acceptance. - China promotes Yuan via One Belt One Road (OBOR), AIIB, BRICS bank, and Panda Bonds for infrastructure lending. - Implications: - Reduces China’s dollar dependency for imports (e.g., oil, metals). - Poses strategic challenges to the US and India if Yuan dominates global trade. - Dollarization - Definition: - Adoption of the US dollar as legal tender by non-US countries (e.g., Panama, Ecuador, El Salvador) or Euro by non-EU countries (e.g., Vatican, Monaco). - Examples: - Argentina (2023): President Javier Milei proposed dollarization to combat 140% inflation due to fiscal deficits and excessive money printing. - Pros: - Prevents deficit monetization, enforces fiscal discipline, and controls inflation by limiting central bank’s currency issuance. - Cons: - Loss of monetary sovereignty, as money supply depends on US Federal Reserve. - Central bank cannot act as lender of last resort, increasing vulnerability to external shocks. - Suitability: - Ideal for small nations (e.g., Marshall Islands), countries with high US exports, or those with significant US remittances (e.g., Ecuador). Large nations like Argentina require substantial dollar reserves ($40 billion). - De-Dollarization - Definition: - Efforts to reduce reliance on the US dollar in global reserves and transactions (e.g., Russia reduced dollar assets to 16% in 2021). - BRICS and Trump’s Threat (2024-2025): - Russia proposed a SWIFT-like system and CBDC-based loans to bypass dollar dominance. - Trump threatened 100% tariffs on BRICS exports if they pursue de-dollarization. - India clarified (March 2025) it does not support undermining the dollar, prioritizing economic ties with the US. - Prospects for Rupee: - Rupee could become a global reserve currency in the long term with full capital account convertibility, controlled inflation, fiscal discipline, and increased exports. - Quantitative Easing and Federal Tapering - Quantitative Easing (QE): - Central banks (e.g., US Federal Reserve) print money to buy assets (e.g., mortgage-backed securities) to increase money supply, as during the 2008 subprime crisis and 2020 COVID-19 pandemic. - Federal Tapering: - Gradual reduction of asset purchases (e.g., 2013 US Fed tapering), reducing dollar supply, raising US interest rates, and triggering capital flight from emerging markets (Taper Tantrum). - Strong Dollar Impact: - Squeezes global credit, as many countries/companies borrow in dollars, increasing borrowing costs during US rate hikes. - Helicopter Money: - Direct cash transfers to citizens to stimulate demand, not widely used in India. - Zero Interest Rate Policy (ZIRP): - Central banks set near-zero interest rates to encourage borrowing and investment, used post-2008 by the US and Japan. - Miscellaneous Exchange Rate Concepts - Currency Depreciation (ES25, 2024): -
Currency Depreciation (%)
Indian Rupee 2.9

- Global Factors: - Dollar movements, FDI/FPI flows, interest rates, gold/oil demand, current account deficits, and geopolitical issues (e.g., Ukraine, Palestine) influence exchange rates. - NEER and REER: - Nominal Effective Exchange Rate (NEER): - Weighted average of rupee against 36 currencies based on trade volume. Increase indicates rupee appreciation. - Real Effective Exchange Rate (REER): - NEER adjusted for inflation differences. REER > 100 suggests overvaluation, reducing export competitiveness; REER < 100 indicates undervaluation. - ES25 (2024): - NEER stable at 90-92; REER appreciated from 103 to 107, indicating stronger rupee purchasing power. - Purchasing Power Parity (PPP): - Compares currencies based on purchasing power for identical goods (e.g., $1 = ₹17 for coffee if ₹20 in India = $2 in US). OECD estimates India’s PPP at $1 = ₹17. - GDP (PPP) Ranking: - US, China, India, Japan, Germany. - Big Mac Index: - Informal PPP measure using McDonald’s burger prices. - Twin Deficit: - High current account and fiscal deficits, increasing economic vulnerability (e.g., India pre-1991). - Pillar 3C1: Bretton Woods Organizations - Definition: - The Bretton Woods Organizations are international institutions established post-World War II to stabilize the global economy, promote development, and facilitate international trade. - Context: - Established at the 1944 Bretton Woods Conference in New Hampshire, USA, with 44 nations, including India, to address post-war economic challenges. These institutions shape global financial and trade policies, critical for India’s economic integration and development goals. - Significance for India: - These organizations provide loans, technical assistance, and trade frameworks, supporting India’s infrastructure, poverty alleviation, and global trade competitiveness, aligning with Atmanirbhar Bharat objectives. - Bretton Woods Conference (1944) - Overview: - Held in Bretton Woods, New Hampshire, USA, post-World War II to restore global economic stability. - Outcome: Proposed three institutions: - International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD, part of World Bank). - International Monetary Fund (IMF). - International Trade Organization (ITO), which failed due to US opposition, leading to GATT and later WTO. - Relationship with United Nations: -
Institution UN Relationship
World Bank (IBRD) Specialized Agency of UN
IMF Specialized Agency of UN
WTO (successor to GATT) Related Organization of UN (not fully compliant with UN charter)
- World Bank (Washington, 1945, July) - Overview: - Initially focused on reconstructing war-torn Europe, shifted post-1950s to development in Asia and Africa. - Comprises International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD) and International Development Association (IDA). - World Bank Group: - Consists of five institutions: -
Institution Role
International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD) Provides development loans with interest to middle-income countries.
International Development Association (IDA) Offers interest-free (concessional) loans to the poorest countries.
International Finance Corporation (IFC) Supports private enterprises in developing countries (e.g., via Masala Bonds).
Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA) Provides insurance against non-commercial risks (e.g., political instability) for foreign investors.
International Centre for Settlement of Investment Disputes (ICSID) Resolves disputes related to foreign investments. India is not a member.
- Voting Power: - Determined by share capital contributions. USA holds the largest share, followed by European nations. ICSID, as a dispute settlement body, does not use voting power. - Presidency: - Traditionally, the US nominates the World Bank President due to its significant shareholding. Current President (2023): Ajay Banga (USA), succeeding David Malpass. - Reports: - World Development Report, Remittance & Migration Report, Global Economic Prospects. - Discontinued: Ease of Doing Business Index (2021) due to data manipulation allegations. - Controversies: - 2019: - US President Trump criticized World Bank loans to China, arguing China’s wealth reduces its need for loans. World Bank clarified it reduces lending as countries grow richer. - 2021: - Allegations that IMF Chief Kristalina Georgieva (former World Bank CEO) pressured staff to improve China’s Ease of Doing Business ranking, leading to the index’s discontinuation. - Non-Bretton Woods Multilateral Development Banks (MDBs) - Definition: - MDBs are institutions created by multiple countries to provide financing and advisory services for development, distinct from Bretton Woods organizations. - Key Examples: -
MDB Established HQ Details
Bank for International Settlements (BIS) 1930 Basel, Switzerland Comprises 60 central banks, sets global banking norms (Basel I, II, III) for financial stability.
African Development Bank (AfDB) 1964 Abidjan, Ivory Coast India is a member and receives loans for development projects.
Asian Development Bank (ADB) 1966 Manila, Philippines India is a member, accessing loans for infrastructure and development.
European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD) 1991 London, UK India joined in 2018 as a shareholder, ineligible for loans but can propose joint projects for soft diplomacy.
Indian Development and Economic Assistance Scheme (IDEAS) 2005 - India’s initiative to provide loans to developing countries, managed by the Ministry of External Affairs and Finance Ministry.
- BRICS New Development Bank (NDB): - Established: - 2014 (6th BRICS Summit, Fortaleza). - Headquarters: - Shanghai, China. - Members: - Brazil, Russia, India, China, South Africa (founding, 18.98% share each as of 2023), plus Bangladesh (1.79%), UAE (1.06%), Egypt (2.27%). Open to all UN members. - Features: - $100 billion Contingent Reserve Arrangement for BoP crises, plans for a BRICS payment system and rating agency. - Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank (AIIB): - Established: - 2015-16. - Headquarters: - Beijing, China. - Members: - Over 100 nations, including China (27% shareholding), India (7%, second-largest), UK, Switzerland. - Shareholding: - 75% Asian countries, 25% non-Asian. - Focus: - Infrastructure financing in Asia, complementing ADB. - International Monetary Fund (IMF, Washington, 1945, December) - Overview: - Promotes global currency exchange stability and assists countries facing Balance of Payments (BoP) crises through loans and Special Drawing Rights (SDR). - Structure: - Acts as a currency reservoir, allowing members to borrow foreign currencies using SDRs (see Pillar 3B2). - Voting Power: - Based on SDR quotas. USA (16.52%) can veto decisions requiring 85% majority. - Leadership: - Chief: - Kristalina Georgieva (2019, Bulgaria, second woman after Christine Lagarde). - Chief Economist: - Gita Gopinath (2019-2022, Indian-American), later promoted to First Deputy Managing Director (2022). - Reports: - Global Financial Stability Report, World Economic Outlook. - Membership: - 189 members (vs. UN’s 195), reflecting near-global participation. - Loan Windows: -
Loan Window Duration Loan Limit Purpose
Rapid Financing Instrument (RFI) 3-6 months 50% of SDR quota Sudden BoP needs
Stand-By Arrangement (SBA) 1-2 years 145% of SDR quota Short-term stabilization
Extended Fund Facility (EFF) 3-5 years 435% of SDR quota Medium-term support
- Other Windows: - Flexible Credit Line (FCL), Catastrophe Containment and Relief (CCR), High-Access Precautionary and Liquidity Line (HPCL). - Bailout Loans: - Pakistan and Sri Lanka received IMF bailouts (e.g., Sri Lanka in 2022 for economic crisis), often with conditions like fiscal reforms and currency devaluation. - Theories of International Trade - Bullionism: - Early mercantilist theory (16th century) defining wealth by precious metal ownership, emphasizing gold and silver accumulation through exports. - Mercantilist Theory (16th-18th Century): - Wealth is static; nations maximize wealth by boosting exports and minimizing imports to accumulate gold/silver. - Colonial powers flooded colonies with goods but restricted imports to protect domestic wealth. - Adam Smith’s Absolute Cost Advantage (1776): - Countries should specialize in goods they produce most efficiently (lower labor input). - Example: India produces 30kg wheat vs. China’s 10kg per laborer; India should focus on wheat, import rice from China (15kg vs. India’s 10kg). - Assumptions: No costs except labor, no transport costs, free trade. - David Ricardo’s Comparative Cost Advantage (1817): - Countries should specialize in goods with relatively lower production costs, even if less efficient overall. - Example: England needs fewer workers for textiles (9 vs. France’s 10) and wine (8 vs. France’s 12). England should focus on textiles (relatively cheaper) and import wine. - Revealed Comparative Advantage (RCA) Index: Identifies export strengths (e.g., India’s RCA in labor-intensive textiles, cotton, carpets, and capital-intensive pharmaceuticals per ES21). - Heckscher-Ohlin Factor Proportions Theory (1919): - Countries export goods intensive in their abundant factors (e.g., capital-abundant USA exports jet planes, labor-abundant India exports cotton). - Trade patterns depend on factor endowments (capital, labor, land). - General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT) and World Trade Organization (WTO) - GATT (1948): - Established post-ITO failure to reduce trade barriers and promote global trade in goods. - Uruguay Round (1986-1993): - Expanded to services and intellectual property rights (IPR). - WTO (1995, Geneva): - Founded via the 1994 Marrakesh Treaty, succeeding GATT. India is a founding member. - Objectives: - Facilitate free trade, reduce tariffs, and enforce trade agreements. - Structure: - Operates as a member-driven organization, with decisions via consensus. - Pillar 3C2: World Trade Organization and Related Trade Agreements - Definition: - The World Trade Organization (WTO) is a global body established in 1995 to regulate international trade, reduce trade barriers, and resolve disputes, succeeding the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT). Related trade agreements include bilateral and multilateral pacts like FTAs, aimed at enhancing trade and economic cooperation. - Context: - The WTO shapes global trade rules, impacting India’s export competitiveness and domestic industry protection. Trade agreements like FTAs and regional pacts (e.g., RCEP, IPEF) influence India’s economic strategy, aligning with goals like Atmanirbhar Bharat and global supply chain integration. - Significance for India: - WTO frameworks and trade agreements provide India market access, protect against unfair trade practices, and support sectors like textiles, pharmaceuticals, and agriculture, while navigating challenges like non-tariff barriers and protectionism. - World Trade Organization (WTO, Geneva, 1995) - Overview: - Established via the 1994 Marrakesh Treaty, succeeding GATT (1948). India is a founding member. - Objectives: Facilitate free trade, reduce tariffs, ensure non-discriminatory trade practices, and resolve trade disputes. - Structure: - Ministerial Conference: - Supreme decision-making body, meets biennially to negotiate trade agreements. - One country, one vote (unlike IMF/World Bank’s shareholding-based voting). - Appoints Director-General (DG) for administrative oversight. - Current DG (2021): - Ngozi Okonjo-Iweala (Nigeria), first woman and African chief, succeeding Roberto Azevêdo. - General Council: - Handles day-to-day operations in Geneva, implements Ministerial Conference decisions. - Comprises representatives from all member countries. - Sub-bodies: - Dispute Settlement Body (DSB) and Trade Policy Review Body. - Committees oversee specific agreements (e.g., anti-dumping, subsidies). - Functions: - Administers trade agreements, monitors trade policies, provides a platform for trade negotiations, and settles disputes. - Promotes trade liberalization while allowing protections for domestic industries through tariff and non-tariff measures. - Tariff Barriers Against International Trade - Definition: - Tariff barriers involve taxes, duties, cess, or surcharges on imported goods/services to protect domestic industries (e.g., Trump’s 25% duty on imported steel). - Types: -
Type Description Example
Countervailing Duty (CVD) Imposed to counter foreign subsidies or high domestic taxes making imported goods cheaper. India’s CVD on subsidized Chinese exports.
Anti-Dumping Duty Applied when foreign goods are sold below normal price or cost of production (dumping). India’s $185/tonne duty on Chinese steel to match domestic prices.
- Implementation in India: - Directorate General of Trade Remedies (DGTR) under Commerce Ministry investigates dumping and recommends anti-dumping duties. - DG Foreign Trade (DGFT) launched ARTIS portal for stakeholders to file anti-dumping complaints. - Non-Tariff Barriers (NTBs) Against International Trade - Definition: - Non-tariff measures (NTMs) are government policies (excluding tariffs) that impact trade, intensified post-COVID-19 and Ukraine war. - Types: - Technical Measures: - Linked to product quality, health, safety, or environment. - Examples: - Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures (SPS): Standards for food/fisheries safety (e.g., India’s ban on US poultry due to avian influenza concerns, contested by the US). - Technical Barriers to Trade (TBT): Product standards (e.g., minimum seatbelts in cars). - Climate-Related NTMs: - Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM, EU, effective 2026): - Levies 20-35% equivalent duty on carbon-intensive imports (steel, aluminum, cement, fertilizers, hydrogen, electricity). - Aims to promote cleaner production but impacts $9.5 billion of India’s EU exports (e.g., $5 billion iron-steel, $2 billion aluminum). - World Bank’s CBAM Exposure Index highlights India’s vulnerability. - 2025-Feb: EU expressed willingness to address India’s concerns amid Trump’s tariff pressures. - EU Deforestation Regulation (EUDR, effective 2025-Dec): - Requires exporters (e.g., palm oil, beef, soy, cocoa, coffee, rubber, timber) to prove products are not from deforested land or violate environmental laws. - Impacts exporters from India, China, Brazil; criticized for EU’s own 15% forest loss (1990-2020). - Non-Technical Measures: - Subsidies/cheap loans to local industries. - Defense offset policies (e.g., India’s requirement for foreign defense firms to invest in Indian companies). - Restrictions on foreign repair/warranty centers. - Government procurement restrictions (e.g., India prioritizing local rice over Chinese). - Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) restrictions (e.g., US semiconductor export ban to China). - Local content requirements and rules of origin. - Import quotas/licenses (e.g., India’s 2023 laptop import licensing). - Impact (ES25): - NTMs affect 67% of global trade ($6.5 trillion), with 26,000+ new NTMs post-COVID. - Indirectly influence FDI: Firms bypass NTMs via local partnerships (e.g., US solar firms investing in India under National Solar Mission subsidies). - NTMs are harder to measure than tariffs due to their granular, product-specific nature. - WTO Notable Agreements - Overview: - WTO agreements address tariff and non-tariff barriers, subsidies, and trade facilitation to promote global trade and protect domestic interests. - Key Agreements: -
Category Agreement Details
Tariff Barriers General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT) Regulates trade in goods, reducing tariffs.
Non-Tariff Barriers General Agreement on Trade in Services (GATS) Governs trade in services.
Non-Tariff Barriers Agreement on Technical Barriers to Trade (TBT) Sets standards for non-food products (e.g., asbestos limits in talcum powder).
Non-Tariff Barriers Agreement on Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures (SPS) Regulates food safety (e.g., pesticide residue limits).
Subsidies Subsidies and Countervailing Measures (SCM) Regulates non-food subsidies.
Subsidies Agreement on Agriculture (AoA) Regulates agricultural subsidies via Green (allowed, e.g., research), Blue (allowed, e.g., cattle vaccination), and Amber (limited, e.g., fertilizers, MSP) boxes.
Miscellaneous Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights (TRIPS) Covers IPR, including compulsory licensing and pharma patents.
Miscellaneous Trade-Related Investment Measures (TRIMs) Regulates foreign investment in goods (not services).
Miscellaneous Agreement on Fisheries Subsidies (AFS, 2022) Prohibits subsidies for illegal, unreported, unregulated (IUU) fishing, over-exploited species, and high-seas fishing.
Plurilateral Information Technology Agreement (ITA), etc. Optional agreements signed by subsets of WTO members (e.g., aircraft, dairy).
- WTO Disputes Involving India - Overview: - India has faced multiple disputes at the WTO’s Dispute Settlement Body (DSB), often losing due to non-compliance with WTO rules. - Key Disputes: -
Complainant Issue Outcome
USA Solar panel subsidies to local producers India lost; subsidies deemed WTO-inconsistent.
USA Ban on American poultry imports India lost; ban ruled as unjustified.
USA Export subsidies/tax benefits (e.g., MEIS, SEZ) India lost; ordered to stop subsidies.
Australia, Brazil, Guatemala Sugar export subsidies India lost; subsidies ruled WTO-inconsistent.
- Appellate Body (AB) Crisis: - The AB, a 7-member body for appeals against DSB rulings, is dysfunctional since 2017 due to US opposition to member appointments. - India’s 2019 appeal against DSB orders remains pending due to AB’s non-functionality. - 2020: - India argued it would not implement DSB orders until AB resolves appeals. - Multiparty Interim Arbitration (MPIA): - Temporary mechanism by 20+ WTO members (e.g., EU, China, Brazil) to address AB crisis. - India is not a member, advocating for AB restoration to protect sovereignty. - WTO Negotiations, Summits, and Packages - Most Favoured Nation (MFN): - Requires non-discriminatory trade treatment among WTO members (e.g., same tariff concessions as the most favored nation). - Exceptions allowed for FTAs or Least Developed Countries (LDCs). - Example: - India granted Pakistan MFN status in 1996, revoked in 2019 post-Pulwama attack, citing non-reciprocity. - Least Developed Countries (LDCs): - Identified by UN ECOSOC (e.g., Somalia, Ethiopia). - WTO allows duty-free, quota-free access for LDC exports without violating MFN norms. - Aid for Trade Initiative: - WTO program to fund trade capacity-building in developing countries and LDCs. - Doha Development Round (2001, Qatar): - Developing countries demanded: - First-world trade liberalization for easier market access. - Flexibility for developing nations to maintain trade barriers. - Financial/technical assistance from developed nations. - Ongoing without conclusion due to first-world opposition; focus shifted to new issues like e-commerce. - Ministerial Conferences (MCs): -
MC Place Year Outcomes
MC1 Singapore 1996 First WTO MC.
MC4 Doha 2001 Doha Development Agenda; first vs. third-world tensions.
MC9 Bali 2013 Bali Package: Trade Facilitation Agreement (TFA), Peace Clause for food subsidies.
MC10 Nairobi 2015 Nairobi Package: Extended Peace Clause, Special Safeguard Mechanism (SSM).
MC11 Buenos Aires 2017 No notable outcomes.
MC12 Geneva 2022 Fisheries Subsidies Agreement (AFS), COVID-19 vaccine patent waiver.
MC13 Abu Dhabi 2024 Added Comoros, Timor-Leste; unresolved food/fisheries subsidies; WEIDE Fund launched.
- Food Subsidies and Peace Clause: - Agreement on Agriculture (AoA) limits subsidies to 5% (developed) and 10% (developing) of agricultural production value. - India’s subsidies (e.g., MSP, NFSA) often exceed 10%, critical for poor farmers and malnourished populations. - 2013 Bali MC: - Peace Clause grants temporary immunity to developing nations’ food subsidies, preventing CVD or WTO complaints. - 2020: - India reported exceeding 10% rice subsidy limit (2018-19), protected by Peace Clause. - Cairns Group: - 20 agricultural exporting nations (e.g., Thailand, Australia, Brazil) advocating subsidy reductions. India is not a member. - Bali Package (2013): - Trade Facilitation Agreement (TFA): - Reduces bureaucratic delays in trade (e.g., online portals for customs, e-way bills). - India ratified in 2016, effective 2017. - National Committee on Trade Facilitation (NCTF) under Cabinet Secretary oversees implementation. - Peace Clause (see above). - Nairobi Package (2015): - Extended Peace Clause. - Special Safeguard Mechanism (SSM): Allows developing nations to temporarily raise tariffs to protect farmers from import surges. - Phased subsidy elimination: Immediate for developed nations, relaxed for developing. - Fisheries Subsidies Agreement (AFS, 2022): - Prohibits subsidies for illegal, unreported, unregulated (IUU) fishing, over-exploited species, and high-seas fishing. - India seeks special and differential treatment (S&DT). - Requires 2/3rd member ratification (110/164); 70 ratified by Feb 2024, not yet effective. - WTO-2024 (Abu Dhabi) Outcomes: - Investment Facilitation for Development (IFD) Agreement: - Proposed by China and 100+ members as a plurilateral agreement. - Rejected by India and South Africa, citing sovereignty concerns and non-trade focus. - Food Subsidies: Thailand accused India of low-price rice exports due to subsidies; unresolved. - Women Exporters in the Digital Economy (WEIDE) Fund: - Launched by WTO and International Trade Centre (ITC) with $50 million (UAE: $5 million). - Aims to support women entrepreneurs in developing/LDC nations for digital trade. - New Members: Comoros, Timor-Leste added. - ES25 Insights: - Positive: WTO reduced tariffs (e.g., India: 48.9% in 2000 to 17.3% in 2024; China: 16.4% to 8.3%). - Negative: 26,000+ new NTMs, regional trade agreements (RTAs) rose from 22 (1990) to 369 (2024), and bilateral trade favoring geopolitical allies, reducing faith in WTO. - Conventional Gravity Model - Overview: - Developed by Jan Tinbergen (1960s), explains trade flows between countries based on economic and geographic factors. - Factors: -
Factor Proportion Example
Economic Size (GDP) Directly proportional Larger GDP (e.g., India) attracts more trade from smaller economies (e.g., Sri Lanka).
Geographical Distance Inversely proportional India-Mexico trade lower than India-Sri Lanka due to transport costs.
Other Factors Varies Exchange rates, income levels, tariff/NTM barriers, infrastructure, skilled manpower.
- Implication (ES23): - FTAs may not yield immediate trade benefits due to these factors requiring time to mature. - Types of Trade Agreements - Overview: - Trade agreements reduce barriers, promote economic cooperation, and vary by scope and membership. - Types: -
Type Description Example
Partial Scope Agreement (PSA) Trade on a limited list of goods with reduced tariffs. India-Sri Lanka PSA.
Preferential/Free Trade Agreement (PTA/FTA) Lower tariffs for members; non-members face regular tariffs. India-Mauritius CECPA.
Comprehensive Economic Partnership/Cooperation Agreement (CEPA/CECA) Extends beyond tariffs to FDI, visas, IPR, e-commerce. India-UAE CEPA.
Customs Union (CU) FTA with a common external tariff (CET) for non-members. East African Community (EAC).
Common Market (CM) CU with free movement of capital and labor. MERCOSUR (South America).
Economic Union (EU) CM with common currency, fiscal/monetary policies, and parliament. European Union.
- Key FTAs Involving India: - India-Mauritius CECPA (2021): - Over 300 Indian goods (agriculture, textiles, electronics) and 600 Mauritian goods receive concessional duties. - India-UAE CEPA (2022): - Covers 90% of goods/services, includes e-commerce chapter, enhances India’s access to Middle East/Africa markets. - India-Australia ECTA (2022): - 96% of Indian exports (e.g., engineering, gems, textiles) get 0% duty; Australia offers coal, wool, LNG. - Excludes sensitive Indian sectors (dairy, wheat, rice). - Features: - Relaxed Australian work visas for Indian STEM graduates, faster medicine approvals, double taxation avoidance for IT firms. - Expected to create 1 million+ jobs and boost bilateral trade to $50 billion in 5 years. - India-EFTA TEPA (2024): - EFTA (Switzerland, Iceland, Norway, Liechtenstein) commits $100 billion FDI in India over 15 years, creating 1 million jobs. - Covers 90% of goods but excludes gold, dairy, soy, coal. - Includes mutual recognition of professional degrees (e.g., nursing, accounting). - Interim (Early Harvest) Agreements: - Cover limited goods/services as a precursor to comprehensive FTAs (e.g., India-UK, India-Canada EPTA targeting 40-65% goods). - Major Free Trade Agreements - Transatlantic Trade and Investment Partnership (TTIP): - Proposed US-EU FTA to reduce tariffs and ease FDI; negotiations ongoing, not signed. - US-EU Trade and Technology Council (TTC, 2021): - Coordinates trade/technology policies in export controls, FDI screening, supply chains (e.g., semiconductors), AI standards, and global trade challenges. - Atlantic Declaration (US-UK, 2023): - Aims to counter authoritarian states (e.g., Russia, China), disruptive technologies (e.g., AI), and transnational challenges (e.g., climate change). - Trans-Pacific Partnership (TPP): - Proposed by US with 12 Pacific nations; abandoned in 2016 by Trump due to job loss fears from cheap Mexican imports. - Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership (CPTPP/TPP-11, 2018): - Signed by 11 nations (Australia, Japan, Vietnam, etc.) after US withdrawal, focuses on tariff reduction and investment norms. - Indo-Pacific Economic Framework (IPEF, 2022): - Launched by US at QUAD Summit (Tokyo) to counter China’s RCEP and Belt-Road Initiative. - 14 members, including India, Australia, Japan, Vietnam. - Four pillars: - Trade (India: observer), Supply Chain Resilience (India ratified), Clean Economy (India signed 2024), Fair Economy (India signed 2024). - Clean Economy Investor Forum (CEIF): - Mobilizes funds for sustainable infrastructure; India’s nodal agency: Invest India. - Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership (RCEP): - Proposed FTA among 10 ASEAN nations and 6 partners (Australia, China, India, Japan, New Zealand, South Korea). - 2019: - India opted out, fearing damage to dairy, spices, MSMEs from Chinese/New Zealand imports. - 2022: - ASEAN-India Trade in Goods Agreement (AITIGA) review ongoing, targeting completion by 2025. - Trade Agreement Dynamics (ES23) - Why FTAs Are Increasing: - WTO summits (e.g., Doha) stalled due to disputes over subsidies and trade barriers. - FTAs are easier to negotiate among friendly nations, offering flexibility. - Benefits: - Access to raw materials, lower consumer prices, expanded markets, protection against unfavorable trade policies, and regional stability. - Rules of Origin: - Ensure goods originate from FTA partner countries for tariff benefits (e.g., India-Sri Lanka FTA prevents Chinese goods relabeled in Sri Lanka). - Require minimum value addition (e.g., x% processing in Sri Lanka). - Certificates of Origin issued by DGFT (India’s Commerce Ministry). - Declining Relevance of Older Agreements: - NAFTA (1994): - Replaced by USMCA (2018) due to Trump’s concerns over US job losses. - SAFTA (2004): - Losing relevance post-India’s 2016 SAARC summit boycott and 2019 duty hikes on Pakistani goods. - AfCFTA (2019): - African Union’s FTA covering 54 nations, enhancing intra-African trade. - Burning Issues in International Trade - Protectionism and Trade Wars (Trump 1.0, 2017-21): - Policies like high tariffs to protect domestic industries sparked retaliatory trade wars (e.g., US-China tariff escalations). - Trump’s Reciprocal Tariff (2025): - Imposed 10% base tariff on all imports, with higher tariffs on countries with trade deficits or high taxes on US goods (e.g., 50% on Lesotho, 125% on China). - 2025-Apr: - 90-day tariff pause for most nations except China (retaliation), Canada, and Mexico (drug smuggling concerns). - Impact on India: - Positive: - Boosts India’s smartphones, solar modules, textiles, and pharma exports due to higher tariffs on competitors (e.g., Vietnam, China). - Negative: - Higher tariffs (e.g., 10-24%) may reduce US demand for Indian goods. - Historical Context (ES25): - Tariff wars date to colonial times (e.g., US 45% tariffs post-British Industrial Revolution, 1870-1913). - Modern strategies include national markets (e.g., GST), stable banking, high tariffs, education, and infrastructure. - Example: - South Korea’s 1970s protection of heavy industries vs. India’s lax export quality control (e.g., 2023 Gambia cough syrup deaths). - Re-shoring, Friend-shoring, Home-shoring: -
Term Definition Example
Re-shoring Relocating factories to home country. Tesla moving from China to US.
Friend-shoring Shifting operations to allied nations. Apple moving factories to India from China.
Home-shoring Allowing remote work from employees’ homes. Companies adopting work-from-home policies.
- China Shock 2.0, China+1 Strategy, Economic Decoupling: - China Shock 2.0: - Surge in Chinese exports disrupting global markets. - China+1: - Firms diversify supply chains to India, Vietnam, etc., to reduce China reliance. - Decoupling: - Reducing economic dependence on China through trade restrictions. - US Trade/IPR Mechanisms: - Special 301 Report: - Annual US report listing countries violating American IPR (e.g., India, China on Priority Watch List for movie piracy, drug patents). - Actions include WTO complaints or capacity-building (e.g., training Russian police). - Generalized System of Preferences (GSP): - Offers developing nations lower US tariffs for select products. - 2019: - India removed from GSP due to high tariffs on US goods (e.g., Harley-Davidson bikes). - Limited impact as most Indian exports (e.g., textiles) are outside GSP. - Pillar 3C3: Other Notable Groupings Related to Economy - Definition: - International economic groupings are alliances of nations formed to promote trade, investment, resource security, and regional cooperation, addressing global challenges like trade barriers, climate change, and geopolitical competition. - Context: - These groupings shape India’s economic diplomacy, enhancing market access, countering China’s influence (e.g., Belt and Road Initiative), and supporting initiatives like Atmanirbhar Bharat and Act East Policy. - Significance for India: - Membership in groups like BRICS, G20, and SCO strengthens India’s global influence, while non-membership in groups like OECD and APEC allows strategic autonomy but limits benefits. - Groupings: Indian Subcontinent - Overview: - Regional groupings foster economic, technical, and cultural cooperation in South Asia, addressing challenges like terrorism, refugee crises, and geopolitical tensions. - Key Groupings: -
Group Details Members Key Developments
SAARC (1985) HQ: Kathmandu, Nepal. Focus: Trade, education, connectivity. 8: Afghanistan, Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Maldives, Nepal, Pakistan, Sri Lanka 2016: Summit cancelled post-Uri attack; SAFTA losing relevance due to India-Pakistan tensions. India’s initiatives: South Asian University (SAU, 2010, Delhi), GSAT-9 (2017) for telemedicine, education, banking, broadcasting.
BIMSTEC (1997) HQ: Dhaka, Bangladesh. Focus: Multi-sectoral technical and economic cooperation. 7: Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Myanmar, Nepal, Sri Lanka, Thailand 2025-Apr: 6th Summit, Bangkok, themed “Prosperous, Resilient, and Open BIMSTEC.” Challenges: Myanmar coup, Rohingya refugee crisis impacting India and Thailand.
- Mekong-Ganga Cooperation (2000) - Overview: - Launched to enhance cooperation in tourism, culture, education, transport, and communications between India and five ASEAN nations (Cambodia, Laos, Myanmar, Thailand, Vietnam). - Geographical Context: - Mekong River originates in China, flows through Myanmar, Laos, Thailand, Vietnam, Cambodia, and drains into the South China Sea. - Significance for India: - Strengthens Act East Policy, counters China’s regional influence, and promotes connectivity projects like the India-Myanmar-Thailand Trilateral Highway. - Recent Developments (Web): - 2024: - India emphasized cultural exchanges and infrastructure projects at the 12th MGC Foreign Ministers’ Meeting, aligning with ASEAN-India Summit goals. - Groupings: ASEAN vs SCO - Overview: - ASEAN and SCO are regional organizations with distinct focuses: ASEAN on economic and cultural integration, SCO on security and economic cooperation. - Comparison: -
Group Details Members Key Developments
ASEAN (1967) HQ: Jakarta, Indonesia. Established via Bangkok Declaration. Focus: Economic integration, regional stability. Celebrates ASEAN Day (Aug 8). 10: Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, Vietnam 2018: 25th Anniversary of ASEAN-India Dialogue; leaders attended India’s Republic Day. Summits: 2024 (Laos), 2025 (Malaysia), 2026 (Philippines).
SCO (2001) HQ: Beijing, China. Focus: Security (via RATS, Tashkent), economic cooperation. Official languages: Russian, Chinese. 9: China, Russia, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Tajikistan, Uzbekistan, India, Pakistan (2017), Iran (2023) 2017: India, Pakistan joined at Astana Summit. 2023: India’s theme “Towards a SECURE SCO.” 2024-Oct: S. Jaishankar attended SCO Heads of Government Summit in Islamabad.
- Significance for India: - ASEAN strengthens India’s Act East Policy; SCO provides a platform to balance China and Russia’s influence while addressing regional security. - Afghanistan 6+2+1 Group for Redevelopment - Overview: - Informal grouping to support Afghanistan’s reconstruction, comprising 6 neighboring countries (China, Iran, Pakistan, Tajikistan, Turkmenistan, Uzbekistan), 2 global powers (USA, Russia), and Afghanistan. - Significance for India: - India, though not a member, supports Afghanistan via projects like the Salma Dam and Chabahar Port, aligning with regional stability goals. - Groupings: Ocean-Rim - Overview: - Ocean-rim groupings promote trade and economic cooperation among coastal nations, with India playing a key role in IORA. - Key Groupings: -
Group Details Members Key Developments
IORA (1997) HQ: Ebene, Mauritius. Focus: Maritime safety, trade, blue economy. 22 Indian Ocean nations (including India); China, USA as dialogue partners 2018: Mandela Scholarship established; Maldives added, Myanmar blocked by South Africa. 2023: Summit in Colombo, Sri Lanka.
APEC (1989) HQ: Singapore. Focus: Economic cooperation among Pacific Rim economies. 21 economies (including China, USA; India not a member) 2019: Summit cancelled in Chile due to unrest. 2020: Virtual summit adopted Putrajaya Vision 2040 for economic cooperation.
- Significance for India: - IORA aligns with India’s Indo-Pacific strategy; APEC non-membership limits India’s Pacific trade benefits but preserves autonomy. - Groupings: BRICS - Overview: - BRICS promotes economic, political, and cultural cooperation among emerging economies, countering Western-dominated institutions. - Timeline: -
Year Event
2001 Term “BRIC” coined by Jim O’Neill.
2009 First summit (Brazil, Russia, India, China).
2011 South Africa joined.
2014 New Development Bank (NDB) formed.
2023 Summit in Johannesburg; theme: “BRICS and Africa.”
2024 Summit in Kazan, Russia.
2025 Summit planned in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.
- Pillars of Cooperation: - Political and Security Cooperation - Financial and Economic Cooperation - Cultural and People-to-People Cooperation - Leadership: - Rotational presidency (e.g., 2022: China, 2023: South Africa, 2024: Russia). - Membership (as of Jan 2025): - 11 Full Members: Brazil, Russia, India, China, South Africa, Saudi Arabia, Egypt, UAE, Ethiopia, Iran, Indonesia (joined Jan 2025). - Partner Countries: Belarus, Bolivia, Cuba, Kazakhstan, Malaysia, Thailand, Uganda, Uzbekistan, Nigeria. - Pending Applications: Turkey, Azerbaijan. - Invited but Rejected: Argentina (2023). - Invited but Unconfirmed: Saudi Arabia. - Significance for India: - NDB funds infrastructure projects (e.g., $1 billion for India’s renewable energy). BRICS enhances India’s global voice and counters China’s dominance. - Groupings: BRICS, OECD, OPEC, JAI - Overview: - These groupings focus on economic cooperation, resource management, and strategic partnerships, with varying Indian involvement. - Key Groupings: -
Group Details Members India’s Status
OPEC (1961) HQ: Vienna, Austria. Focus: Oil production coordination. 14: Saudi Arabia, UAE, Iran, Iraq, Venezuela, etc. (Qatar withdrew 2019; Russia not a member) Not a member; India engages via OPEC+ dialogues.
OECD (1961) HQ: Paris, France. Focus: Economic cooperation, taxation (e.g., BEPS norms). 38 developed nations (e.g., USA, UK, Japan) Not a member; India collaborates on BEPS, G20 frameworks.
NAM (1961) Focus: Non-alignment during Cold War, advocating for developing nations. 120 nations (Asia, Africa, Latin America, Caribbean, Europe) Founding member; aligns with India’s strategic autonomy.
JAI (2018) Trilateral (Japan, America, India) meeting at G20 Buenos Aires. Focus: Economic growth, prosperity. India, Japan, USA Member; strengthens Indo-Pacific cooperation.
QUAD (2007) Strategic dialogue for Indo-Pacific stability. India, USA, Japan, Australia Member; 2024 Summit in Wilmington, Delaware.
AUKUS (2021) Security pact for nuclear-powered submarines to Australia. Australia, UK, USA Not a member; concerns over France’s exclusion.
Paris Club (1956) Informal group aiding debtor nations with loan repayment. 22: Australia, Canada, France, Germany, UK, etc. Observer; engages in G20 Common Framework.
IBSA (2003) Dialogue for democracy, development. India, Brazil, South Africa Member; complements BRICS cooperation.
- Brexit Context: - Post-Brexit (2020), UK’s role in OECD, Paris Club, and AUKUS remains strong, but its exit from the EU affects G20 dynamics (EU as a G20 member). India-UK FTA talks (2025) align with G20’s trade facilitation goals. - Groupings Led by Gora (Whites): G7 - Overview: - G7 comprises seven major developed economies, focusing on global economic and security issues. - Details: - Members: - Canada, France, Germany, Italy, Japan, UK, USA. - History: - Formed in 1975; Russia joined (1997, G8) but was expelled (2014) post-Crimea annexation. - Summits: - 2024 (Italy), 2025 (Canada). - 2020: - Trump proposed inviting India, Australia, South Korea, Russia; UK opposed Russia’s inclusion due to geopolitical tensions. - 2021: - Cornwall, UK Summit; India invited as a guest (PM Modi attended virtually). Outcome: 15% Global Minimum Tax (ref: Pillar 2B, Black Money). - Brexit Context: - Post-Brexit, UK leverages G7 to maintain global influence, opposing Russia’s return and pushing for democratic alliances (e.g., D10). Regime Year Details India’s Status

Australia Group 1984 Prevents export of chemical/biological weapons. Member (2018).
