- Significance for India: - Membership enhances India’s credibility in global non-proliferation; NSG non-membership limits nuclear trade but India secured a 2008 NSG waiver. - Russia-Related Organizations/Agreements - Black Sea Grain Initiative (2022): - Objective: - Allows Ukraine to export grains via Odesa, Chornomorsk, Pivdennyi ports post-inspection to stabilize global food prices. - Mediators: - UN, Turkey. - Controversy: - Russia’s threats to exit due to geopolitical tensions. - CTBT (1996): - Bans all nuclear explosions; Russia ratified (2000) but withdrew (2023) citing USA’s non-ratification. - Non-ratified: India, China, Pakistan, USA, etc. - USA-Related Arms Treaties: -
Treaty Year Details Status
INF Treaty 1987 Prohibited 500-5,500 km range missiles. USA withdrew (2019).
New START 1991 Reduces nuclear weapons. Expired (2021); extension talks stalled.
Open Skies Treaty 1992 Allows unarmed aerial surveillance. USA withdrew (2020).
- Significance for India: - India’s non-ratification of CTBT aligns with strategic autonomy; engages with Russia, USA via bilateral defense ties. - Pillar 4A1: Agriculture and Allied Sectors - Introduction to Agriculture - Definition: - Agriculture is a primary economic activity involving the cultivation of crops, fruits, vegetables, flowers, and the rearing of livestock for food, fiber, and other products essential for human sustenance and economic development. - Significance: - Contributes significantly to India’s GDP and employs approximately 42% of the workforce (World Bank, 2018, down from 55% in 2011). - Supports food security, rural livelihoods, and economic stability, aligning with UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDG) #1 (End Poverty) and #2 (Zero Hunger). - Key Targets and Models: - Government’s goal (2016): Double farmers’ income by 2022-23 compared to 2015-16, through enhanced productivity, diversification, and market access. - Sir Arthur Lewis’ Dual Economy Model: Economic development shifts labor from agriculture to more productive industrial sectors, reducing agriculture’s share in GDP over time. - Production Trends: - Within agriculture and allied sectors, crop production contributes the highest to economic value, followed by livestock, forestry, and fisheries. - Growth rate fluctuates due to climatic factors like El-Niño-induced droughts; the target is a 4% annual growth rate (set during PM Manmohan Singh’s tenure). - Agricultural Production Statistics - Key Data (Economic Survey): - Milk: - India is the world’s largest producer, contributing over 20% of global supply. - Vegetable Oil: - India is the largest importer globally. - Sugar: - Second-largest producer and largest consumer worldwide. - Fisheries: - Second-largest producer, employing 28 million people, with exports exceeding ₹40,000 crore annually. - Eggs: - Third-largest producer; Meat: Eighth-largest producer. - Workforce: - 54.6% of India’s population engaged in agriculture and allied sectors (Census 2011). - Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare - Structure: - Comprises two departments: - Department of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare, and Department of Agricultural Research and Education (DARE). - Implements the National Policy for Farmers (2007) to enhance farmer welfare and agricultural productivity. - Key Organizations: - Statutory Bodies: - Coconut Development Board, National Cooperative Development Corporation (NCDC), Protection of Plant Varieties and Farmers’ Rights Authority. - Attached Offices: - National Rainfed Area Authority. - Public Sector Enterprises: - National Seeds Corporation Ltd. (NSC). - Autonomous/Cooperative Bodies: - National Agricultural Cooperative Marketing Federation of India (NAFED), Small Farmers Agribusiness Consortium (SFAC), and various boards/institutes for horticulture and plant health management. - DARE oversees the Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR) and central agricultural universities in Imphal (Manipur), Pusa (Bihar), and Jhansi (Uttar Pradesh). - Ministry of Fisheries, Animal Husbandry, and Dairying (2019) - Structure: - Established in 2019, with two departments: Department of Fisheries and Department of Animal Husbandry and Dairying. - Previously, these functions were under the Ministry of Agriculture. - Key Organizations (Fisheries): - Fishery Survey of India (Mumbai), Central Institute of Fisheries Nautical and Engineering Training (CIFNET, Kerala), Central Institute of Coastal Engineering for Fishery (CICEF, Bangalore), National Institute of Fisheries Post-Harvest Technology and Training (NIFPHATT, Kochi), National Fisheries Development Board (NFDB, Hyderabad), Coastal Aquaculture Authority (Chennai). - Key Organizations (Animal Husbandry and Dairying): - Statutory Bodies: - National Dairy Development Board (NDDB), Animal Welfare Board of India (under the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act, 1960). - Cooperative Societies and Ministry of Cooperation - Cooperative Society: Definition - A cooperative society is a voluntary association of individuals who collaborate to promote member welfare and prevent exploitation by middlemen, such as in agricultural trade. - Example: - The Kaira District Cooperative Milk Producers’ Union (1946), Gujarat, evolved into AMUL under Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel’s guidance. - Data: - Over 8.5 lakh registered cooperatives with 29 crore members in India. - Features and Benefits - Voluntary Membership: - Individuals can join or leave freely. - Legal Status: - Registered under State laws (for single-state cooperatives) or the Multi-State Cooperative Societies Act, 2002 (for multi-state operations, e.g., NAFED). Operates as a separate legal entity with limited member liability. - Equal Voting: - One member, one vote, regardless of capital contribution. - Welfare Motive: - Focuses on service rather than profit maximization. - Government Support: - Benefits from low taxes, subsidies, and low-interest loans. - Types of Cooperative Societies - Consumer Cooperatives: - Purchase goods in bulk directly from producers, bypassing middlemen to reduce costs for members. - Producer Cooperatives: - Enable small-scale producers (e.g., AMUL for milk) to procure raw materials collectively, enhancing efficiency. - Farmer Cooperatives: - Facilitate collective purchase and sharing of seeds, fertilizers, machinery, and land to address fragmented landholdings. - Marketing Cooperatives: - Assist small producers in selling products, e.g., Tribal Cooperative Marketing Development Federation of India (TRIFED). - Credit Cooperatives: - Collect savings from members and provide affordable loans, often formed by farmers or community groups. - Housing Cooperatives: - Pool resources to provide housing through collective land or loan arrangements. - Ministry of Cooperation (2021) - Establishment: - Formed in July 2021 to strengthen the cooperative movement, previously managed under the Ministry of Agriculture’s Department of Agriculture, Cooperation, and Farmers Welfare. - Oversees administrative, legal, and policy frameworks for cooperatives in agriculture, dairying, sugar, and banking. - Leadership: - Headed by the Home Minister. - Data: - India has 1,94,195 cooperative dairy societies and 330 cooperative sugar mills. - Organizations under Ministry of Cooperation - Registrar of Multi-State Cooperative Societies (Statutory, 2002): - Oversees multi-state cooperatives. - National Cooperative Development Corporation (NCDC, 1963): - Promotes agricultural marketing, seeds, fertilizers, food processing, and cold storage. - National Council for Cooperative Training (Delhi): - Provides training for cooperative management. - Vaikunth Mehta National Institute of Cooperative Management (VAMNICOM, Pune): - Offers postgraduate diploma courses in cooperative management. - Tribhuvan National Cooperative University (2025) - Background: - Initiated by Tribhuvandas Patel, instrumental in forming AMUL with Dr. Verghese Kurien (father of the White Revolution). - Objective: - Transform the Institute of Rural Management Anand (IRMA), Gujarat, into Tribhuvan Sahkari University under the 2025 Act to advance cooperative education. - Multi-State Cooperative Societies Act, 2002 (Amendment 2022) - Historical Context: - 1984: - Multi-State Cooperative Act. - 2002: - Replaced the 1984 Act. - 2011: - 97th Constitutional Amendment added: - Right to form cooperatives as a fundamental right (Article 19). - Directive Principle for cooperative promotion (Article 43B). - Part IX-B in the Constitution for cooperative societies (Articles 243-ZH to 243-ZT). - 2022 Amendment Objectives: - Establish a Co-operative Election Authority for transparent board elections. - Appoint an Ombudsman to address member complaints. - Create a fund, financed by profitable cooperatives, to revive sick multi-state cooperatives. - Facilitate mergers of cooperative societies. - Criticisms: - Encroachment on state powers over single-state cooperatives. - Resistance from profitable cooperatives to fund loss-making ones. - New Cooperative Societies (2022) - Three new multi-state cooperatives under the 2002 Act: - National Multi-State Cooperative Export Society: - Enhances agricultural exports. - National Multi-State Cooperative Organic Society: - Promotes organic farming. - National Multi-State Cooperative Seed Society: - Ensures quality seed production and distribution. - National Cooperative Export Limited (NCEL, 2023) - Registration: - Under the Multi-State Cooperative Societies Act, 2002. - Founders: - AMUL, IFFCO, KRIBHCO, NAFED, NCDC. - Objective: - Ensure at least 50% of export profits reach farmers, addressing the issue of middlemen capturing most gains. - Agri-Input: Land - Challenges: - India has 17% of the global population but only 2.5% of the world’s land, leading to pressure on agricultural land. - Fragmentation of landholdings due to population growth and inheritance practices reduces average farm size (0.725 ha in 2003 to 0.512 ha in 2019). - 86% of agricultural families own 2 hectares or less, limiting mechanization and productivity. - Significance: - Land size and quality directly impact agricultural productivity and farmers’ income. - Land ownership provides social value and serves as collateral for credit. - Solutions: - Encourage small farmers to join manufacturing/service sectors, selling land to larger farmers for consolidation. - Land reforms and modernization to enhance efficiency and reduce disputes. - Types of Land Reforms: - Land Reform: - Government-led redistribution of agricultural land to landless farmers. - Land Acquisition: - Government purchase of private land for industrial or infrastructure development. - Land Reforms Post-Independence - Zamindari Abolition: - Eliminated intermediary landlords to grant ownership to cultivators. - Bhoodan Movement: - Vinoba Bhave’s initiative to redistribute land voluntarily to the landless. - Ceiling on Landholdings: - Fixed maximum land ownership limits to promote equitable distribution. - Tenant Protection Laws: - Safeguard tenant farmers’ rights. - Forest Rights Act, 2006: - Grants land titles to forest dwellers cultivating land for 75 years. - Model Agricultural Land Leasing Act, 2016: - Protects landowners from illegal occupation and enables tenant farmers to access bank loans using lease agreements. - National Land Records Modernization Programme (NLRMP, 2008) - Administered By: - Department of Land Resources, Ministry of Rural Development. - Objectives: - Digitize land records to reduce disputes over sale/inheritance and prevent benami transactions. - Assign Unique Land Parcel Identification Numbers (ULPIN). - Enable translation of land records into other languages. - Funding: - Central Sector Scheme, 100% funded by the Union Government. - Features: - Digitizes cadastral maps and supports land ceiling enforcement. - Types of Farming: Cooperative vs. Collective Farming -
ParameterCooperative FarmingCollective Farming
OwnershipFarmers retain individual land ownership; cooperate for farming activities.Collective or social ownership of land, livestock, and labor; small plots for personal use.
OutputHigher due to motivated farmers with ownership.Lower due to lack of ownership, leading to inefficiency (e.g., USSR’s Kolkhoz model).
ExampleSmall Farmer Large Field (SFLF) in India.Soviet Russia’s Kolkhoz model.
- Cooperative Farming: Small Farmer Large Field (SFLF) - Pilot Project: - Conducted by the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) in Odisha. - Mechanism: - Small landholders synchronize crop selection, agricultural operations, and marketing to gain bargaining power with input suppliers and buyers. - Benefits: - Enhances productivity and income for small farmers. - Types of Farming: Truck Farming/Market Gardening - Definition: - Small-scale farming producing fruits, vegetables, and flowers for urban markets and restaurants. - Features: - Operates in urban/suburban areas; governed by proximity to markets for quick delivery. - Notable Regions: - Netherlands (tulips), Northwest Europe, Northeastern USA, Mediterranean. - Other Types of Farming - Mixed Farming: - Combines crop cultivation and livestock rearing for diversified income. - Primitive Farming: - Subsistence-based, using traditional methods. - Factory Farming: - Intensive, large-scale production of crops or livestock. - Viticulture: - Specialized cultivation of grapes for wine production. - Agri-Input: Seeds - Importance: - Seed quality significantly impacts agricultural yield, as land area expansion is limited. - Optimal seed replacement: - Hybrid seeds annually, non-hybrid every three years. - Challenges: - Low seed replacement rate due to high costs and limited availability. - Government Response: - Approved 100% FDI in seed development (automatic route). - Initiatives: - Seed Village Concept: - Trains farmers to produce seeds locally, meeting village demands. - Seed Banks/Vaults: - Store seeds to preserve genetic diversity and ensure supply during calamities (e.g., Global Seed Vault, Svalbard, Norway). - Green Revolution - Definition: - A significant increase in food production through High-Yielding Variety (HYV) seeds. - Phases: - Phase 1 (1960s–1970s): - Focused on wheat, rice, and affluent states (Punjab, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu). - Phase 2 (1970s–1980s): - Expanded HYV seeds to more states and crops. - Biofortification: - Enhances nutritional quality of crops (e.g., ICAR’s CR Dhan 310 rice with high protein and zinc; Golden Rice for Vitamin A). - Landraces - Definition: - Naturally occurring variants of cultivated crops, developed without artificial intervention. - Features: - Climate-resilient, often nutrient-rich compared to hybrid varieties. - Pepsi vs. Potato Farmers - Legal Framework: - Indian Patents Act (1970): - Does not grant patents for seeds/plant varieties. - Protection of Plant Varieties and Farmers’ Rights Act (2001): - Grants Intellectual Property Rights to breeders, researchers, and farmers for new plant varieties. - Case Study: - Pepsi supplied FC5 potato seeds to Gujarat farmers, who continued growing and selling them. - 2019: - Pepsi filed a complaint but later withdrew it. - 2021: - Government canceled Pepsi’s registration due to incomplete documentation. - Genetically Modified (GM) Crops - Definition: - Crops developed by inserting selected genes from one organism into another (e.g., Bt. Cotton with Bacillus thuringiensis genes for pest resistance). - Regulation: - Governed by the Environment Protection Act, 1986, through the Genetic Engineering Appraisal Committee (GEAC). - Environment Ministry grants final approval after GEAC recommendations. - Status: - Bt. Cotton (non-food crop) approved in 2002; food crop approvals remain contentious. - GM Mustard Approval - Timeline: - 2017: - GEAC approved; Environment Ministry rejected, ordering further studies. - 2022: - GEAC approved again; final approval pending. - Smart Corn GMO (USA) - Features: - Short-height corn (1/3rd of normal) developed by Bayer, resistant to storms due to modified growth hormone (gibberellic acid). - Status: - Approved for cultivation in the USA (2023). - Challenges of GM Crops - Biosafety Concerns: - Potential risks to human/animal health, soil bacteria, bees, and biodiversity. - Cross-Pollination: - GM crops may eliminate indigenous species. - Economic Issues: - Indian companies pay trait fees to foreign firms (e.g., Monsanto for Bt. Cotton), with government-set ceilings causing disputes. - Terminator Gene Technology: - Produces sterile seeds, forcing farmers to repurchase (banned in India). - SATHI Portal - Full Form: - Seed Authentication, Traceability & Holistic Inventory Portal. - Objective: - Centralized online system for seed traceability and quality assurance, managed by the Ministry of Agriculture. - Seed and Fertilizer Quality - Context: - India produces 12% of global cereals but has lower per-hectare yields, emphasizing the need for quality seeds and fertilizers. - Challenges: - Indian soil lacks organic carbon and micronutrients (boron, iron, sulfur). - Global warming necessitates heat-tolerant varieties (e.g., wheat in Northwest India). - Urea-Gold: - Combines urea with sulfur to reduce wastage and enhance nutrient uptake. - Recommendations: - Use drones and fertigation (fertilizer application via drip irrigation) for efficient fertilizer use. - Agri-Input: Water (Irrigation) - Definition: - Supplying water to crops at regular intervals to enhance productivity. - Challenges: - India has 17% of the global population but only 4% of water resources, making it water-stressed. - Over 50% of agriculture depends on monsoon rainfall, vulnerable to El-Niño droughts (e.g., 2014–2015). - High water consumption by crops like paddy and sugarcane due to subsidized inputs. - Key Data: - India leads in groundwater irrigation (39 million hectares), followed by China (19 million) and the USA (17 million). - Indian farmers use 3,000–5,000 liters of water per kg of rice, compared to 350 liters in China. - Projections: - By 2050, India will face severe water insecurity, limiting multi-cropping and income growth. - Jal Shakti Ministry - Structure: Comprises two departments: - Department of Water Resources, River Development, and Ganga Rejuvenation. - Department of Drinking Water and Sanitation. - Department of Water Resources, River Development, and Ganga Rejuvenation - Key Organizations: - Attached Offices: - Central Water Commission (National Water Academy, Pune; Ganga Flood Control Commission; Bansagar Control Board), Central Water and Power Research Station, Central Soil and Materials Research Station. - Statutory Bodies: - Betwa River Board, Brahmaputra Board, National Ganga Council (replaced National Ganga River Basin Authority), Central Ground Water Authority (under Environment Protection Act, 1986). - Government Companies: - Water and Power Consultancy Services Ltd. (WAPCOS), National Projects Construction Corporation Ltd. - Autonomous Bodies: - National Institute of Hydrology, Krishna River Management Board, Godavari River Management Board. - Department of Drinking Water and Sanitation - Focus: - Provides safe drinking water and sanitation facilities, with no major MCQ-worthy organizations. - Water Schemes - National Rural Drinking Water Programme (NRDWP, 2009): - Objective: - Provide safe water for drinking, cooking, and domestic needs in rural areas, addressing contamination (arsenic, fluoride). - Funding: - Centrally Sponsored Scheme (50:50 Union-State share). - Status: - Subsumed under Jal Jeevan Mission (2019). - Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchai Yojana (PMKSY, 2015): - Objective: - Enhance irrigation coverage and water efficiency (valid until 2025-26). - Funding: - Centrally Sponsored Scheme (Union-State cost-sharing). - Components: - Accelerated Irrigation Benefit Programme (AIBP): - Fast-tracks ongoing irrigation projects. - Har Khet Ko Pani: - Distributes water via canal networks. - Watershed Development: - Builds water harvesting structures (e.g., check dams, farm ponds, traditional systems like Jal Mandir in Gujarat). - Per Drop More Crop: - Promotes micro-irrigation (drip, sprinklers); shifted to Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY) in 2022-23. - Related Terms: - Fertigation: - Mixing fertilizers in drip irrigation systems. - Mulching: - Covering soil to reduce evaporation and weed growth. - Challenges: - High costs, equipment damage by animals, and limited repair facilities. - Namami Gange Yojana (2015): - Objective: - Make the Ganga pollution-free within five years via sewage/effluent treatment and awareness campaigns. - Funding: - Central Sector Scheme (100% Union-funded). - Background: - Builds on the 1986 Ganga Action Plan; Ganga declared National River in 2008-09. - Jal Kranti Abhiyan (2015): - Objective: - Designates two water-scarce villages per district as ‘Jal Grams’ to achieve water surplus through existing schemes (PMKSY, MGNREGA). - Jal Shakti Abhiyan (2019 onwards): - Objective: - Raises awareness for water conservation. - Jal Jeevan Mission (Rural, 2019): - Objective: - Provide piped water (Har Ghar Jal) to all rural households by 2024, focusing on rainwater harvesting and groundwater recharge. - Funding: - ₹3.6 lakh crore, converging with other schemes and CAMPA funds. - Status: - Subsumed NRDWP. - Jal Jeevan Mission (Urban, 2021): - Objective: - Provide Functional Household Tap Connections (FHTC) in 4,378 Urban Local Bodies and liquid waste management in 500 AMRUT cities. - ATAL Bhujal Yojana (ATAL JAL, 2019): - Objective: - Promotes panchayat-led groundwater management in seven water-stressed states (Gujarat, Haryana, Karnataka, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Rajasthan, Uttar Pradesh). - Funding: - 50:50 Union-World Bank. - Mechanism: - Incentivizes better-performing gram panchayats and forms Water User Associations. - Miscellaneous Schemes: - Dam Rehabilitation and Improvement Program (DRIP, 2012): - Enhances dam safety via the DHARMA web portal. - National Hydrology Project (2016): - Uses remote sensing for water resource management and flood forecasting. - NABARD Funding (2017): - ₹20,000 crore for long-term irrigation, ₹5,000 crore for micro-irrigation. - Composite Water Management Index - Developed By: - NITI Aayog. - Methodology: - Ranks non-Himalayan states, Northeastern/Himalayan states, and Union Territories on nine themes and 28 indicators (base year: 2017-18). - River Interlinking (Budget 2022) - Objective: - Links rivers to transfer water from surplus to deficit areas (e.g., Ken-Betwa, Damanganga-Pinjal). - Challenges: - Public protests led to the withdrawal of the Par-Tapi-Narmada project. - Irrigation Observations (Economic Survey 2025) - Only 55% of sown area is irrigated; two-thirds of agricultural land faces drought risk. - High irrigation coverage in Punjab, Haryana, Uttar Pradesh, and Telangana reduces drought vulnerability. - Tools like CLART GIS and AVNI Gramin app identify water bodies for rejuvenation. - Rejuvenating water bodies offers environmental, economic, and social benefits. - Agri-Input: Fertilizers - Definition: - Fertilizers are synthetic chemicals that enhance soil nutrients, unlike natural manure/compost from organic waste decomposition. - Key Fertilizers: - Nitrogen (Urea): - Produced via the Haber process using natural gas; India imports natural gas. - Phosphorus: - 80–85% imported; ISRO uses remote sensing to identify phosphate mines. - Potash: - 100% imported (33% from Russia/Belarus, affected by the 2022 Ukraine war). - Nutrient Ratio: - Ideal N:P:K ratio is 4:2:1; India’s is 8:3:1 due to excessive urea use. - Challenges: - Overuse of subsidized urea causes soil/groundwater pollution and smuggling to non-agricultural uses or neighboring countries. - Increases fiscal deficit due to high subsidy burdens. - Nutrient-Based Subsidy (NBS, 2010) - Administered By: - Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers. - Funding: - Central Sector Scheme (100% Union-funded). - Mechanism: - Subsidizes non-urea fertilizers based on nutrient content (e.g., ₹19/kg for Nitrogen, ₹15/kg for Phosphorus). - Challenges: - Urea excluded, leading to overuse; delayed NBS subsidy payments due to fiscal constraints. - Soil Health Card (2015) - Administered By: - Ministry of Agriculture. - Funding: - Centrally Sponsored Scheme (Union-State cost-sharing). - Features: - Tests soil for 12 parameters (macronutrients, micronutrients, physical properties) every three years, advising on suitable crops, seeds, and fertilizers. - Bhu-Vision/Krishi-RASTAA Soil Testing Equipment - Developed By: - ICAR’s Indian Institute of Rice Research with private collaboration. - Features: - Tests soil for 12 parameters in 30 minutes, delivering results to mobile phones. - Neem Coating of Urea (2015) - Administered By: - Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers. - Mandate: - Fertilizer companies must neem-coat urea. - Benefits: - Prevents diversion to non-agricultural uses. - Slows urea dissolution, improving nitrogen absorption and yield. - Reduces pest attacks, lowering pesticide use. - Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) of Fertilizer (2018) - Administered By: - Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers. - Mechanism: - Subsidies paid to companies post-sale via Point of Sale devices, verifying farmer identity (Aadhaar, Kisan Credit Card). - Benefits: - Prevents urea diversion and smuggling. - Nano-Urea - Developed By: - IFFCO (2021). - Features: - Liquid nano-urea (500 ml) replaces 90 kg of solid urea per acre, costing ₹240 vs. ₹500+. - Benefits: - Enhances fertilizer efficiency and protects soil. - Nano-DAP (Interim Budget 2024) - Features: - Liquid di-ammonium phosphate (DAP), high in phosphorus, applied before sowing. - Cost: - ₹600 for 500 ml (vs. ₹1,350 for 50 kg bag). - Objective: - Promote nationwide use to reduce costs and improve efficiency. - Pradhan Mantri Kisan Samridhi Kendra (PMKSK, 2022) - Administered By: - Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers. - Objective: - Converts fertilizer retail shops into one-stop shops for agricultural inputs (seeds, fertilizers, pesticides) and services. - One Nation One Fertiliser (PM-BJP, 2022) - Administered By: - Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers. - Full Name: - Pradhanmantri Bhartiya Janurvarak Pariyojna. - Objective: - Standardizes branding (BHARAT Urea, DAP, etc.) for subsidized fertilizers to prevent smuggling and enhance transparency. - PM PRANAM Scheme (Budget 2023) - Administered By: - Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers. - Objective: - Reduces chemical fertilizer use. - Mechanism: - States reducing fertilizer consumption receive grants (e.g., 50% of subsidy savings). - Context: - Fertilizer subsidy bill reached ₹2.25 lakh crore in 2022-23, straining fiscal resources. - Agri-Input: Pesticides and Weedicides - Definition: - Chemicals used to control pests and weeds, respectively. - Challenges: - India loses 25% of crops to pests, weeds, and diseases, but per-hectare pesticide use is low compared to developed nations. - Excessive use (e.g., Endosulfan) risks contamination of food, land, and water. - Solutions: - Promote organic pesticides and biocontrol agents. - Adopt Integrated Pest Management (IPM) to maintain pest levels at economically tolerable thresholds, preserving biodiversity. - Agri-Input: Organic Farming (PKVY, 2015) - Definition: - Farming without synthetic fertilizers, pesticides, or chemicals, relying on crop rotation, residues, manures, and biofertilizers. - Data: - India has over 44 lakh organic farmers, the highest globally. - Benefits: - Ecological: - Reduces pollution, protects biodiversity, and prevents crop residue burning. - Economic: - Higher export prices, reduced subsidy burdens, and lower fiscal deficits. - Paramparagat Krishi Vikas Yojana (PKVY, 2015): - Administered By: - Ministry of Agriculture. - Funding: - Centrally Sponsored Scheme (60:40 or 90:10 Union-State share). - Mechanism: - Supports clusters of 50 farmers with ₹20,000 per acre for three years. - Tools: - Jaivik Kheti web portal for market linkage; Participatory Guarantee Scheme for organic certification. - Case Study: - Sikkim achieved 100% organic status in 2015; Tripura and Uttarakhand aim to follow (Economic Survey 2023). - Agri-Input: Bhartiya Prakritik Krishi Padhati (BPKP, 2021) - Sub-Scheme: - Under PKVY, promotes natural farming without chemicals, using cow dung, biofertilizers, and biomass recycling. - Funding: - ₹12,200 per hectare every three years. - Similarity with PKVY: - Both focus on chemical-free farming; BPKP emphasizes traditional methods. - Zero Budget Natural Farming (ZBNF) - Definition: - Farming without purchased inputs, using natural resources like cow dung, biofertilizers, and biopesticides (e.g., Jiwamrita, Bijamrita, Neem Astra). - Features: - Originated in Karnataka by Subhash Palekar; promoted in Himachal Pradesh and Andhra Pradesh. - Budget 2022: - Promotes ZBNF along a 5-km Ganga corridor. - Benefits: - Reduces farmer debt, protects soil, and enhances biodiversity. - Limitations of Organic Farming and ZBNF - Lower Yields: - Compared to chemical-based farming. - Case Study: - Sri Lanka’s rapid shift to organic farming (2021) reduced yields, causing food shortages and inflation. - Off-Season Crops: - Difficult to produce, risking food insecurity if scaled nationally. - Market Challenges: - Organic produce has less appealing shelf life, color, and texture, requiring consumer awareness. - Conclusion: Organic Farming and ZBNF - Aligns with SDG #2 and New India Vision 2022 for sustainable agriculture and doubled farmer incomes. - Offers ecological and economic benefits but requires balanced implementation to avoid yield losses. - Sustainability in Agriculture (Economic Survey 2025) - ICAR’s Composite Index of Agricultural Sustainability: - Uses 51 indicators (environmental health, soil/water quality, socioeconomic development). - India’s score: - 0.49 (moderately sustainable). - Top Performers: - Mizoram, Kerala, Madhya Pradesh, Andhra Pradesh, Manipur, West Bengal, Uttarakhand. - Lowest Performer: - Rajasthan. - 100 Million Farmers Initiative (WEF) - Objective: - A World Economic Forum platform for net-zero, nature-positive food systems by 2030. - Agri-Input: Farm Mechanization - Definition: - Use of machinery (tractors, harvesters, reapers) to enhance agricultural productivity. - Significance: - Increases land and labor productivity by improving output per unit time. - Creates rural employment in machine production, operation, and maintenance. - Types of Technological Progress: - Labor-Augmenting: - Enhances worker productivity (e.g., Spinning Jenny, 1770). - Capital-Augmenting: - Improves machinery efficiency (e.g., steel plough vs. wooden plough). - Context: - MGNREGA’s guaranteed wages increased labor costs in Punjab and Haryana, driving mechanization. - Challenges - India’s tractor industry is the largest globally (80,000 exports annually), but farm mechanization is low (40%) compared to China (60%), Brazil (75%), and the USA (95%). - Small farmers lack funds for machinery; diverse soil/climate requires customized equipment. - Solutions: - Establish renting centers, promote R&D, and support Make in India. - Schemes: - Sub-Mission on Agricultural Mechanisation (SMAM, 2017): Addresses mechanization challenges. - FARMS App: - Facilitates machinery renting. - Budget 2022: - Promotes ‘Kisan Drones’ for crop assessment, land record digitization, and spraying. - Subsidies for Agri-Drones - Scheme: - Sub-Mission on Agricultural Mechanisation (SMAM). -
BeneficiarySubsidy
Agriculture graduates (drone hiring centers)50%, max ₹5 lakh
Women/SC/ST/Small Marginal/Northeast Farmers50%, max ₹5 lakh
Women Self-Help Groups (SHGs)80% (e.g., ₹8 lakh for a ₹10 lakh drone package)
- NAMO Drone Didi - Scheme: - Under Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana – National Rural Livelihoods Mission (DAY-NRLM). - Objective: - Provide drones to 14,500 women SHGs (2024–26) for rental services (spraying fertilizers/pesticides). - Features: - Drone package includes spray assembly, battery, charger, camera, and one-year warranty. - Training for SHG members in drone operation and maintenance. - Funding: - Central Sector Scheme (80% Union, 20% SHG contribution). - Coordination: - Involves Ministries of Agriculture, Rural Development, Fertilizers, Civil Aviation, and Women and Child Development. - Agri-Input: Finance/Credit - Context: - Small/marginal farmers produce limited marketable surplus, often receiving low prices, necessitating loans for inputs. - Economic Survey 2016: - Smaller landholdings correlate with higher indebtedness. - Economic Survey 2020: - Northeast states account for <1% of agricultural loans. - Budget Targets: - 2020-21: - ₹15 lakh crore agricultural credit. - 2021: - ₹16.5 lakh crore; 2022: ₹18 lakh crore. - NABARD provides refinancing; PM-KISAN beneficiaries without Kisan Credit Cards are issued them. - Measures to Enhance Credit: - RBI’s Priority Sector Lending norms. - Bank nationalization post-independence. - Establishment of Regional Rural Banks (RRBs), Local Area Banks (LABs), and Small Finance Banks (SFBs). - Kisan Credit Card and interest subvention schemes. - Kisan Rin Portal (KRP) - Objective: - Streamlines Modified Interest Subvention-Kisan Credit Card (MISS-KCC) claims, replacing manual submissions to RBI/NABARD. - Benefits: - Faster subsidy disbursal to banks, reducing delays. - Administered By: - Ministry of Agriculture. - Negotiable Warehouse Receipts (NWR) - Administered By: - Warehousing Development and Regulatory Authority (WDRA), Ministry of Consumer Affairs (2007). - Mechanism: Farmers deposit produce in WDRA-registered warehouses, receiving NWRs to: - Pledge for bank loans. - Trade via commodity exchanges or e-NAM. - Benefits: - Prevents distress sales, allowing farmers to wait for better prices. - PM-Kisan Bhai (2023) - Objective: - Supports small/marginal farmers with warehouse rent subsidies and additional Kisan Credit Card loan interest subsidies. - Administered By: - Ministry of Agriculture. - e-Kisan Upaj Nidhi (2024) - Administered By: - WDRA. - Objective: - Enhances warehousing access for farmers. - Features: - Increases WDRA-registered warehouses (from 5,500 to more). - Reduces warehouse security deposit from 3% to 1%. - Farmers deposit produce, receive e-NWRs, and access 7% interest post-harvest loans without collateral. - World’s Largest Grain Storage Programme - Administered By: - Ministry of Cooperation via NCDC, with NABARD, FCI, and Central Warehousing Corporation support. - Objective: - Build storage capacity at Primary Agricultural Credit Societies (PACS) to reach 100% of production by 2047 (current: 47%). - Features: - FCI pays rent to PACS for storing MSP crops. - Pilot in 11 PACS across 11 states (e.g., Uttar Pradesh, Tamil Nadu). - Benefits: - Reduces post-harvest losses, enables better prices and faster loans. - Warehousing: Flospan and Smart Warehouses - Flospan: - Mobile Storage Units (400 metric tonnes capacity), piloted with UN-World Food Programme in Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal Pradesh. - Smart Warehouses: - Use sensors for real-time monitoring of temperature, humidity, and pests, managed by the Indian Grain Storage Management & Research Institute (IGMRI, Hapur). - Agri-Output: Crop Residue Management - Issue: - Farmers in Punjab, Haryana, and Uttar Pradesh burn paddy stubble (September–October) to clear fields for wheat sowing, causing smog and PM 2.5 pollution in Delhi. - Impacts: - Reduces soil fertility and organic carbon. - Solutions: - In-situ composting and machines (Happy Seeder, Zero Till Seed Drill) for sowing without residue removal. - National Green Tribunal (2015): - Declared residue burning a crime under IPC Section 188 and Air Pollution Control Act, 1981. - Budget 2018: - 100% funding to Punjab, Haryana, and Delhi, with subsidies for machinery. - Biomass depots for residue storage; use in thermal power plants (5–7% biomass pellets, Budget 2022) and biochar briquettes for industries. - Crop Residue Emissions (PM 2.5, g/kg): - Sugarcane (12.0), Maize (11.2), Cotton (9.8), Rice (9.3), Wheat (8.5). - Recommendation: - Promote crops with lower lignocellulosic content (e.g., potato, soybean) to reduce pollution. - Budget 2023 and 2024 Announcements - Cooperative Societies: - Vision: - ‘Sahakar Se Samriddhi’ (Prosperity through Cooperation). - Computerization of 63,000 PACS and a national cooperative database with GPS mapping. - Fertilizer Reduction (PM-PRANAM): - Encourages states to adopt nano-urea, nano-DAP, and organic fertilizers with grants for reduced consumption. - Organic Farming: - Establish 10,000 Bhartiya Prakritik Kheti Bio-Input Resource Centres; train 1 crore farmers in natural farming. - Farm Inputs: - National manufacturing network for pesticides and micro-fertilizers. - Horticulture: - Atmanirbhar Clean Plant Program for disease-free planting material; vegetable clusters near cities; 50 food irradiation units in MSMEs. - Edible Oils: - Self-reliance in mustard, groundnut, sesame, soybean, and sunflower. - Agri-Startups: - Agriculture Accelerator Fund for rural startups. - Millets (Shree Anna): - Funding for the Indian Institute of Millet Research, Hyderabad. - Loans: - Jan Samarth-based Kisan Credit Cards in five states. - Irrigation: - Funding for Upper Bhadra Irrigation Project (Karnataka). - Fisheries: - Support for shrimp farming and exports via NABARD. - - Other Initiatives: - Multi-sectoral ‘Rural Prosperity and Resilience’ programme with states. - Mission for Aatmanirbharta in Pulses. - Prime Minister Dhan-Dhaanya Krishi Yojana (PMDKY, Budget 2025) - Target: - 100 districts with low productivity, moderate crop intensity, and below-average credit access. - Administered By: - Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare. - Benefits: - Enhance agricultural productivity. - Promote crop diversification and sustainable practices. - Develop post-harvest storage at panchayat/block levels. - Improve irrigation facilities. - Provide loans for short- and long-term needs. - Funding: - Converges existing schemes; inspired by NITI Aayog’s Aspirational Districts Programme.
